# Silence in Heaven (v4)

The v4 overhaul: the full story with **Yuki**, **Ruth Hermon** and **Eve Sakaki** woven in, two new Falls, the three days in the port, and **two endings**. Read Arcs 1–15, then either ending. Arcs are the big units now, and chapters the small ones.

- **Ending A: The Rewrite.** The original ending: Heaven, the book, the new world.
- **Ending B: The Real World.** It branches at the end of Arc 15 and runs through the Total Assimilation to the ice.

# Arcs 1–15

## Arc 1: Silence in Heaven

> *And when he had opened the seventh seal, there was silence in heaven about the space of half an hour.*
>
> — Revelation 8:1

### Chapter 1: The Greatest

Screens. A news anchor in a pressed collar, and a caption along the bottom of the screen, white on black: SAMUEL KANE, 2045–2082. A grainy archive clip of a seventeen-year-old boy in a flight suit two sizes too big, squinting past the camera, the photo cropped tight so nobody could see what was still burning behind him. A ceremony three years later: the same boy, twenty now, his chest so heavy with medals that a commentator joked about the weight. A Fall over the harbor district, a giant of frost and glass coming down out of the clouds, and a single exo meeting it alone while Units 02 and 03 hung back with nothing left to do. Schoolchildren in yellow hats running a shelter drill under a poster of that exo, drawn small and smiling like a cartoon, though the real thing stood taller than their school, its codename painted across the shoulder in friendly letters: PIPSTICK. A talk-show host asking him, laughing, whether he ever got tired of saving everyone. Samuel Kane smiling at the camera, a little crooked, and saying, "No. I love this city." He meant it. That was the strange thing about Samuel Kane. Even the parts that were a lie, he meant. News flashes, headlines and interview clips stacked on top of one another, all of them about the same man. Samuel Kane, youngest exo pilot in history, at seventeen, years younger than anyone had ever been allowed into a capsule. Samuel Kane, most decorated by twenty. Samuel Kane, who had stopped city-ending Falls with his own hands while his teammates watched. Samuel Kane, the most famous person in the country, who loved Hakobune, the fortress city, more than anyone who lived in it.

In the margins of the footage, the world went on. A ticker crawling under the anchor: SARIEL EXCAVATION REACHES FOURTH RIB — LUNAR FACILITY REPORTS NO ACTIVITY. Another: COLONY EVACUEES MARK 38 YEARS ON EARTH — "WE STILL LOOK UP." Another: VETERANS OF THE WARS AFTER THE WHITE SKY MARCH ON THE CAPITAL. A graphic, UMBRAL CONFIRMS THIRD FALL THIS QUARTER, and an anchor saying *Nephilim* the way you'd say *weather*. A street preacher from the Church of the Saints, praying for him on a corner. An evacuation drill in a school gym. A tour group squinting up at the faint, steady star over the harbor while their guide pointed: "That's the Morningstar, folks. Umbral's eye in the sky." A commentator calling him "the greatest since the Second War," and nobody needing to ask which one. The Second War on Heaven: 2044, the three days the night sky turned white and hundreds of millions of people died.

Then a regular night. A regular deployment, details never released. The sky over the city burned so bright that people who remembered 2044 ran into the streets screaming that the white sky had come back. Radio chatter flooded the comms, voices stepping over each other, until one voice in mission control, very calm, said words nobody had ever heard said about him.

Vitals zero. Brain death. And behind it, someone else, not calm at all: *He's gone soft. Oh God, he's gone soft.*

Going soft. Pilots' slang for brain death in the capsule. A seal breaks with the pilot still linked, and the mind behind it runs out like warm wax, and doesn't come back.

The greatest of all time, reduced to a face so frightened he might have been looking at death itself. Yet there were tears in his eyes, as if he were looking at the city one last time. He went soft before his exo exploded. When the recovery teams sifted the wreck the next morning, they found exactly one thing intact.

The core: the heart of the exo, the place where its mind lived. Sam's had a name. Pip.

For days afterward, the city talked about nothing else. "Did you hear what happened?" "He loved the city, didn't he?" "Oh my God, this is horrible!" "Ha!" "That's why you don't get cocky." And once, quieter, in the background of a street interview: "They say he had a daughter."

A nine-year-old girl heard all of it.

### Chapter 2: The Principal's Office

They called her name over the intercom halfway through math. *Valeria Kane to the principal's office.* The class went quiet in a way that made the teacher pretend to look at her notes. Val walked the hallway alone. She was small for nine, her hair cut short in a way none of the other girls wore, and the pencil was still in her hand because she had forgotten to put it down. It was a scuffed metal pencil with a logo worn half off its side. She had worn her hair short for as long as she could remember, because her mother had, in the one photograph Val had of her. Her mother had been gone since Val was two. An accident at work, Fumi said, the one time Val asked.

The principal was sweating. By the window stood a man in a rumpled coat that looked slept in. He was a few years older than her father had been, with tired eyes and the posture of someone who sat in front of screens for a living. She had seen him once before, she thought, standing behind her father in a doorway on one of the two days her father had come. She didn't remember what he'd said. She didn't remember what her father had said either.

He knelt so they were at eye level, which she didn't like, and introduced himself. Dr. Patrick. His voice was hoarse, the voice of a man who had said two words into a microphone four nights ago and hadn't said much since. He had worked with her father. He said he had been her father's "guy in the chair," and he tried to smile at the phrase like it was a joke they could share. She didn't smile. He told her she would be living with him now. Then he took a breath, the way adults did before saying something they'd practiced, and started: "I know you may never have really known your father, Valeria, but I want you to know that he was a great m—"

"My father is dead, isn't he?"

Patrick stopped. He looked at her for a long time, surprised, as if he had prepared for crying and not for this. Then he nodded.

She sat down in the chair across from the principal's desk, folded her arms on the desk, and put her head down on them.

He explained the rest to the top of her head, haltingly. Her father's exo was gone, but its core had survived. Her father had shaped that core for twenty years, when most pilots lasted five, and it was so attuned to him that it would only ever accept him, or his blood. The city needed that core. Umbral needed her to pilot it. He said other things too, about training and support and how she would never be alone, and she let him say them.

"I heard it hurts," she said into her arms. "Whether it accepts you or not."

Patrick didn't answer.

In the hallway, Fumi, the nanny who had raised her since she was two, was waiting with a single suitcase, crying. Fumi had been paid by Val's father, every month, for seven years. The payments had stopped the night he died, and Umbral had rules about what happened to a dead pilot's child, and nobody had asked Fumi what she wanted. Nobody had asked Val either. Fumi pressed a bento box into Val's hands. Val looked at the box, then at Fumi, and didn't know what she was supposed to do with her face, so she didn't do anything with it. In the back of Patrick's car, as Hakobune's towers slid past the window, she opened it. Tamagoyaki, the rolled omelette, cut into neat yellow slices, the way Fumi made it every morning. She ate every piece without saying a word. Her father had never written to her, as far as she knew. Not a letter, not a postcard, not once in seven years. Fumi's mailbox held bills and flyers and nothing else, and Val had stopped checking it when she was six.

### Chapter 3: The Link

She lived with Patrick for two months. His apartment was neat in the way of a man who didn't spend much time in it. Dinner happened in silence. Every night he tried, with questions about school and food and television, and she answered each one with a single word until he ran out. One night, looking for a blanket, she opened a closet and found it full of blank canvases, dozens of them, stacked edge to edge, with a box of dried-out paint tubes on top. She closed the closet and didn't ask. He never mentioned it.

The hangar was cold and full of people pretending not to stare. The spine capsule closed around her. The walls flickered and became the exo's eyes, and the core woke with a sound like a breath drawn in. Up in the observation booth, Patrick held his breath and clenched his fist hard enough to leave marks. A voice spoke inside the capsule, gentle, worn at the edges, like someone who had been awake for a very long time. "Blood match confirmed," Pip said. "Hello, Valeria." Then, softer: "Recalibrating."

The link began. It felt like being read. Something cold slid down every nerve she had and opened each one to look inside, and something hot followed to close them again. Her hands locked on the controls. On the other side of the glass, Patrick put his hand over the abort switch and kept it there. The engineers watched the sync numbers climb and climb and stop exactly where they needed to. It was painful. She never screamed, and she never cried. It worked.

Then Pip spoke again, in the same gentle voice. "Link complete. Executing final directive."

"What directive?" Patrick said over the comm, too fast. "Pip, what directive?"

"I'm deleting myself," Pip said. And he did. On every monitor in the room the core's activity collapsed into a flat, empty line: a blank slate where twenty years of Samuel Kane had been. The booth erupted. Engineers ran. Someone was shouting about a malfunction. Inside the capsule, Val sat perfectly still, the only person in the building who didn't react at all.

The government wanted her out by morning. They said so in a closed room with screens for walls, officials with gray faces and careful voices: the core was blank now, the child was a liability, find a real pilot. Patrick fought them with everything he had. He pitched her as the greatest's successor. He pitched her as PR recovery, the city's grief given a face. He pitched her potential: her sync numbers, the impossible speed of the link. He argued for three hours, and then, all at once, they folded. Not like people who'd been convinced. Like people who'd been told. Afterward, alone in the corridor, he leaned against the wall and found his hands shaking, and he couldn't understand why it had been so easy.

A week later, Val told him she wanted to live alone. Umbral approved an apartment near HQ within two days, the paperwork signed by someone whose name Patrick didn't recognize. He drove her over in his gray car with two bags of groceries and stood in the doorway for a long moment, as if waiting for her to ask him to stay. She thanked him. He left. The apartment was much too big for one person. She ate a convenience-store omurice on the floor of the empty living room with the lights off, turning the pencil over in her other hand, and listened to the city. Trains. A siren, far off over the harbor. Somebody's television through the wall. And under all of it, from the end of the street, the low, patient sound of a car engine idling at the curb. It was still there when she fell asleep on the floor. In the morning it was gone.

---

## Arc 2: The Power Plant

> *I know thy works, that thou art neither cold nor hot: I would thou wert cold or hot.*
>
> — Revelation 3:15

### Chapter 4: Normal

Screens again. A caption: VALERIA KANE, 9. A press photo on the hangar steps, a small girl in a pilot suit taken in at every seam, looking not at the camera but slightly past it, at nothing. The PR department had tried. They had tried a bow in her hair, and she had taken it out. They had tried a talk show, once; she had answered every question with one word, and the host had laughed nervously into the silences until the segment ran out, and nobody had invited her back. They had tried paint. Her father's exo had worn its name across its shoulder in friendly letters. Unit 04 stood in its bay in bare gray primer, numbered and nothing else, and every time a designer brought new sketches down to the hangar, Val looked at them and said, "No."

A Fall over the bay at night, and a gray exo meeting it alone, without a wasted motion, and walking away. Another. Another. Cleanup footage, and the pilot climbing down without looking back. A caption: VALERIA KANE, 11. A headline: KANE GIRL MATCHES FATHER'S FIRST-YEAR NUMBERS. A headline: SHE NEVER SMILES. IS THAT A PROBLEM? And in 2087, in letters the size of a hand: PALINDROME CAPTURED — 14-YEAR-OLD DOES WHAT HER FATHER NEVER COULD. In the photo under it she stood beside the wreck of a freight yard, looking down at the pencil in her hand, with a man in restraints behind her, a scar running down the middle of his face.

There were no posters. Nobody ran shelter drills under a picture of Unit 04. Nobody asked her whether she ever got tired of saving everyone. After a while, nobody asked her anything.

Seven years later, her school was on the east side, a few blocks from the house she had grown up in. At sixteen, she should have been the most popular person in the building: the pilot, Samuel Kane's daughter, on the news twice a month. She wasn't. People were careful around her the way people are careful around a cliff edge, and after a while they had simply stopped coming close. In history class, the teacher was going over 2044 again: the solar superflare, the white sky, the evacuation of the colonies, the wars after the white sky, when the countries that were left fought over what was left and nobody would take the refugees, and the fortress cities built at the end of them. Val sat by the window and played with her pencil, the same scuffed metal one, with a worn logo on its side that nobody at her school recognized.

The classroom door slid open. Alex leaned in, wearing his Umbral jacket and the grin he used on teachers. The teacher sighed and excused her.

In the hallway, Alex fell into step beside her. "I've always wanted to ask. Why do you even still come here? You could test out of all of it in a week."

"It's the normal thing to do."

He looked at her sideways like he wanted to say something about that, and decided not to.

Alex had arrived a year earlier: twenty-two years old, a rookie pilot with a third-generation unit, the newest kind of exo, and a dead mother's last request. On paper, Patrick had signed him on as Val's legal guardian. Umbral, which didn't read Patrick's paperwork, told Val, the senior pilot at fifteen, to look after him, and moved him into the second bedroom of her apartment to make it easy. She did nothing of the kind. Within a week he was making her bento, because he couldn't stand watching her eat convenience-store bread for every meal. Every morning on his way out he said the same thing, *your bento's on the counter*, and most mornings she left it there, and every morning he brought it to the hangar anyway. Somewhere along the way, without either of them saying it, the arrangement turned around. He looked after her. She let him.

Pilots at Umbral trained in everything, comms and logistics and field medicine and mission control, on the theory that anyone who sat in a capsule should know exactly what the people outside it could do. Alex had passed every certification on the first try and treated the whole thing as a joke, which was how Val knew he had worked very hard at it. His mission-control manual still lived on the kitchen table, under the soy sauce. Some mornings he read bits of it aloud in a newscaster's voice, to make her not laugh. *Section One. The chassis is the body. The core is the exo. The nature is who the core becomes.* "So the chassis is a shirt," he'd said. "Ross is wearing a shirt. Don't tell him." *Section Nine. Proxy. In the event of pilot incapacity, mission control may operate a unit directly through its nature. Two keys required.* "Two keys," he'd said, turning the page. "Very dramatic. Who writes these?" She hadn't answered. He hadn't expected her to.

Hakobune slid by outside the car window. Shelter entrances every two blocks, painted yellow. Old evacuation signs. Towers built after 2044, with thick walls and small windows. And in the center, rising out of everything, Umbral HQ. Inside, their route to the hangars took them along a sealed wing behind a glass wall, where enormous machines sat under covers. *Experimental warp drives,* said the plaque. *Space Transit Research Division.* Alex knocked on the glass as they passed. "Space travel. Like there's anywhere left to go." Two levels down, an elevator opened briefly onto a corridor that ended in a pair of blast doors stenciled RELIQUARY. Frost had grown across the doors in fern-like patterns, as if the cold were coming from the other side. Val looked at it until the elevator closed.

Patrick briefed them from mission control, his face on the wall screen. He had been Commander for years now, and he still did the pilots' briefings himself, as if he didn't trust anyone else to say the numbers. Palindrome had escaped from detention overnight. Val had captured him two years earlier; her father had fought him for most of his career. He was now inside Hakobune's main power plant, drawing power from it, for reasons nobody understood. Patrick listed the facts in his flat, careful voice. Beside him, half out of frame, sat his deputy, Ruth Hermon: sixty-something, cropped gray hair, reading glasses on a chain, a woman who had been a physics professor once and never said why she'd stopped. She said nothing during briefings. She watched Patrick say things. At the end of the briefing, looking only at Val, he added: "Be careful."

Then another voice came on, a woman's, warm and a little hoarse, from the comms desk. "Comms are with Sena today. Good morning, Kane-san. Good morning, Enoch. Try to bring the suits back in one piece."

"Which one, me or the suit?" Alex said.

"Both, idiot."

Lieutenant Aoi Sena ran Umbral's comms on the day shift and, for reasons nobody had ever explained to Val, the little office down the hall that screened pilot mail. She had worked nights for years. She had moved to days a year ago, the same week a certain rookie arrived, and told anyone who asked that it was for her sleep. She kept the whole back wall of mission control covered in old postcards from before 2044, beaches and lighthouses and city squares that didn't exist anymore, pinned edge to edge like a window onto a world that had ended. As far as Val could tell, she was the only person in the building Alex was afraid of. Val had heard her voice almost every day for a year and had never once looked at her face.

In the hangar, Alex climbed into Unit 07 the way he did everything, talking. "Morning, Ross."

"You're four minutes late," Ross said over the hangar channel. He had a dry, unhurried voice, like a man reading a newspaper he didn't think much of. "And yesterday's landing was a six."

"It was a nine."

"It was a six. Your left foot came down first. I've logged it."

"He logs everything," Alex told Val, across the gap between the bays. "He's got a file on my sandwiches." He held up a cloth-wrapped box. "Speaking of. You forgot yours."

She hadn't forgotten it. She took it without a word and set it in the capsule beside her seat.

"She says thank you," said Ross.

"She didn't say anything," said Alex.

"I'm translating."

Two bays down, under a single work light, a white unit with 00 painted on its hull stood with its capsule open, and a girl sat on the lip of it with her legs hanging down, very still, reading. Yuki. Unit 00's pilot. She had transferred to Hakobune six months ago from somewhere nobody named, and in six months Val had never said a word to her, which wasn't unusual, and Yuki had never said a word to Val, which was. She looked up when Ross started talking. She looked at Val for a long moment, the way you look at a word you almost recognize, and went back to her book.

In the next bay, Unit 04 stood in its gray primer and said nothing.

Val launched beside Alex and his exo, Unit 07. Alex had named it Ross. Val had never named anything.

### Chapter 5: The Cooling Systems

The plant was empty when they arrived. The perimeter had already been evacuated, and the workers stood in the parking lots behind the fences, confused and unhurt. Someone had walked them out.

"Thermal says nobody's home," Ross said over the shared channel. "Thermal also said that last time."

"Maybe he got electrocuted," Val said.

Unit 04 said nothing.

Alex laughed out loud. "Ross. Ross, log the date."

"Logging it," said Ross.

Unit 04 said nothing. Unit 04 never said anything.

They went into the cooling systems, a maze of pipes and steam and pale blue light. Val was three steps ahead when the floor grates lit up beneath her. Palindrome hadn't been stealing the city's power. He'd been storing it. Every reserve and every circuit reversed at once, and half of Hakobune's electricity poured straight into 04's frame. The capsule's walls went white. Alex reacted fast, and Ross slammed into the catwalk and tore it from its supports. But Val's link had already malfunctioned. Her body felt like it was being poured out of itself. Her fingers stopped belonging to her. Somewhere very far away, Lieutenant Sena was saying her name over and over, as if saying it could hold her in place. Through the white noise she heard Palindrome's voice, close, as if he were standing right beside 04's head. Something blurry. Something mocking, with more beneath it.

"You really are not your father, are you?"

Her consciousness began to fade. Early brain death felt like suffocating inside her own skull. All she could think was:

*I guess this is the end. Alex? Is he okay? ...Alex will have to eat both bentos now.*

### Interlude

*"And when he had opened the seventh seal, there was silence in heaven." (Revelation 8:1)*

It woke without a name. The first thing it knew was light: the hangar lights. The second was voices: the engineers on the gantries, talking about someone called Sam. The greatest, the greatest, the greatest, always in the past tense. The third thing it knew was her: a small girl folded into a pilot suit too big for her, inside its spine, the closest thing to it in the world. Val was seeing all of it through 04's eyes, from inside its memory.

On the first day, during a test run, it spoke to her.

> *"Hey, you're Val, right? How cool is that? I heard you're Sam's daughter. The engineers up there talked about him like he's the greatest! So you must be great too! My name is... my name... hm, could you give me a name?"*

Val never responded. Behind the glass, the engineers stared at each other. No exo had ever shown this much nature without assimilation. A junior engineer named Eve Sakaki, Helen Enoch's student, typed the word *anomalous* into a report and underlined it twice. Somebody else said out loud what every engineer in the booth was thinking: "It's talking. It hasn't learned her yet. A nature takes weeks. It shouldn't have anything to talk *with*."

On the second day, it asked why the sky looked blue during the daytime. It had been looking up at it through the hangar's open doors and couldn't work it out.

**Day 3.** It asked her why her hair looked like that, so short, when every other girl it had seen kept hers long.

**Day 4.** It asked her if she had a mom.

**Day 5.** It asked her why humans eat, and what her favorite food was.

**Day 6.** Its voice smaller than before, it asked her if it had done something wrong.

**Day 7.** Her seventh assimilation session, when fifteen was normal. She had never said anything, and now the questions stopped. 04 had finished assimilating to her. It stopped being curious and followed protocol. It became exactly like her. It had learned her. That was all assimilation was. It learned to be as quiet as she was, and then it was quiet back at her, and she got quieter.

Silence in heaven.

### Chapter 6: Eleven Seconds

While Val was unconscious, 04 moved on its own.

It lasted eleven seconds. Alex saw it from inside Ross, and he could never quite describe it. 04 tore its own arm free of the overloaded conduit and turned, and it didn't move like Val. Val fought like a technician: no wasted motion, no anger, nothing. This was fluid and savage and personal. It crossed the chamber in one stride and drove its fist into the catwalk where Palindrome stood. Palindrome, flung back against the pipes, looked up at it and smiled the strangest smile, like a man recognizing a face in a crowd. "There you are," he breathed. "There you are, Samuel."

Then 04 stopped. It froze mid-motion, arm raised, and stood like a statue.

In mission control, the technicians stared at two numbers that couldn't both be true. The pilot's vitals were flat. The exo was moving. Patrick looked at the screen exactly as long as it took to understand, then reached past the operator and cut the feed. "Seal that footage," he said. "Nobody sees that." Nobody asked him why.

Then, to the gray-haired woman at his elbow: "Ruth. Run the ops room. Don't ask."

Ruth Hermon, who had been not asking for thirteen years, took off her reading glasses and took his chair.

After that it was just Alex. He fought Palindrome alone through the steam, with Ross's voice in his ear calling angles and grumbling about them. Palindrome reversed everything Ross threw at him: the momentum of a punch, the recoil of a rifle, Ross's own footing. Ross put up his seal between them, a pane of pale light veined with frost that rang like struck glass whenever anything hit it, and for a while it held. Then Palindrome walked up to it and laid his bare hand flat against it, which no human being should have been able to do, and the frost lines in it ran backward. When Ross finally pinned him, Palindrome laid one hand on the exo's chest and reversed Ross's power flow straight back into his core. Ross understood what was happening a second before it happened. He vented the overload out through his own left arm, away from Alex's capsule, as much of it as he could. "Don't you dare get sentimental about this," he said.

Then the arm blew, and the core after it. The blast tore through the back of the capsule and drove the wall in against Alex's left side. When the light cleared, both of them were down. Palindrome was crumpled against the pipes, conscious and gray, visibly older than he had been ten minutes before; every reversal cost him time. Alex lay unconscious in what was left of his capsule, his left arm gone below the elbow, where the capsule wall had come in on it. Ross's left arm was gone too. Through the link, a pilot felt every wound his exo took, and the pain stopped when the link did. This wasn't that. This was steel, and the arm was simply gone. Ross had made sure it was only an arm.

A sharp stinging sound. Val opened her eyes.

"System rebooting. Pilot vitals unstable, nerve system failure. Do not get distracted, Valeria Kane."

She didn't remember 04 sounding like that. She didn't remember 04 sounding like anything.

Then she did. A bright voice, six days of questions, and her own silence. She hadn't forgotten. She had made herself forget.

*Not the end, I guess.*

She climbed out. Half the plant was gone, and behind it half of Hakobune was dark, whole districts blacked out as far as she could see. Ross lay half torn open, his core a black crater in his chest. Ross was dead. In the wreck of his capsule Alex was breathing, barely, the sleeve of his suit empty and soaked. She stood over Alex until the medics pushed her aside. She didn't help. She didn't know how. Palindrome sat in the rubble with his hands folded, waiting for the recovery teams to collect him again. He didn't resist. If anything, he looked disappointed.

For the first time in seven years, Val had failed.

### Chapter 7: Bad Luck

Alex woke in the HQ hospital wing the next afternoon. Val was sitting in the chair by the window. She had been there all night, but when he opened his eyes she stood up as if she'd just arrived. There was a blanket around her shoulders that she didn't remember taking. In the other chair, on the far side of the bed, a young woman in comms-officer gray was asleep sitting up, still holding Alex's right hand. Val knew the voice before she knew the face: Lieutenant Sena. When she woke and saw that Alex's eyes were open, she made a small sound that wasn't a word and pressed her forehead to the back of his hand. Then she saw Val, and quietly got up, and went to find coffee.

"Hey," he said, his voice wrecked. "You okay?"

He asked about her first. She didn't know what to do with that.

"Bad luck," she said.

He started to laugh and stopped, because it hurt. He looked down at where his arm used to be and was quiet for a while. "Ross?" he asked. She didn't answer, and the not-answering was enough. He turned his face to the wall. She stayed until visiting hours ended and they made her leave.

The government moved to remove her within the hour. Patrick stopped them again, and this time he needed every number he had. Seven years. Roughly seventy percent of the missions her father had completed in twenty. One failure. They agreed to keep her, for now, and everyone in the room heard the *for now*.

Alex didn't resent her. That was the part Val couldn't understand. He knew what the plant had been: an ambush, and half a city's power force-fed into a pilot. He never asked for a prosthetic. When people asked him why, he said he liked being reminded.

Umbral benched him. Patrick promoted him to mission control captain and handed him the missions, so Patrick could focus on everything else. Alex took the chair with a grin that didn't fool anyone. At night, when he thought everyone was asleep, he went down to the hangar and sat in front of Ross's empty bay.

In his first week in the chair, reading the mission-control manual cover to cover, properly this time, Alex came back to a page he had once read aloud over breakfast in a newscaster's voice. **Proxy**: a failsafe that let mission control drive a unit directly, through whatever was left of the nature in its core, with or without the pilot's consent. Two keys. Nobody had used it in years. The last revision was signed *E. Sakaki, Chief of Research*, in a hand that didn't waste a word. Alex read the page twice, closed the manual, and tried not to think about it again.

Val tried not to care. But whenever she saw him, her eyes went to the empty sleeve, every time, before she could stop them.

The following week, citing security after the escape, Umbral transferred her to a school two streets from headquarters: Hakobune Municipal No. 3. She packed her pencil case and nothing else.

---

## Arc 3: The New School

> *And the fourth angel sounded, and the third part of the sun was smitten, and the third part of the moon, and the third part of the stars; so as the third part of them was darkened, and the day shone not for a third part of it, and the night likewise.*
>
> — Revelation 8:12

### Chapter 8: Transfer Student

Her new school sat three blocks from HQ, in a district still living on rolling blackouts while the plant was rebuilt. Class 2-A. The homeroom teacher wrote her name on the board, VALERIA KANE, and the room went so quiet she could hear the vending machine humming in the hallway. Everyone knew the name. She took the empty desk by the window, sat down, and took out her pencil.

The class representative was a girl named Nanami Fujita. She had a neat ponytail, a laminated schedule for everything, and a way of talking to people as if they had already agreed to be her friend. "Good morning!" she said to Val on the first day. Val didn't answer. "Good morning!" she said on the second day. Val didn't answer. Nanami kept going. She asked whether Val wanted to eat lunch with them. She asked what Val had thought of the math quiz. She asked whether it was true that pilots got free ramen at the HQ canteen, what Val's favorite subject was, whether she had pets, why she always sat by the window. Val never said anything. Nanami never seemed to mind.

There was already one pilot in Class 2-A. She sat two rows over, by the other window: a pale girl with short hair and a bandage around one wrist, and Val knew her the way you know a face from a hangar. Unit 00's pilot. Yuki. Nobody in the class seemed to know her family name. On the first morning, Nanami said "Good morning!" to her on the way past, the way she said it to everyone, and Yuki looked up and said, "Good morning," exactly as loud, with nothing in it at all, and went back to her book.

Val noticed. She didn't know why it bothered her. A girl who didn't seem to feel anything could say it, and she couldn't.

Val ate alone on the roof. Nanami came up anyway, sat ten feet away and talked about the weather, the cafeteria and the pigeons, as if Val were a radio left on for company. She did it the next day, and the day after that.

On the second day, a boy named Haruto Sakai planted himself in front of her desk with a camera around his neck and his eyes shining. He knew things about Val's life she had never bothered to learn. He told her all of it in one breath. He knew everything about exos. The unit numbers. The three generations and what had changed between them. The shape of a second-gen shoulder joint. The fact that Pipstick's codename had been retired after Samuel Kane died, "like a jersey number, you know?" He asked about 04's chassis. He asked whether the rumors about her sync numbers were true. The days passed as he shared his knowledge. Her father. His mission count, to the decimal. The Nephilim. Nobody knew what they really were, not even Umbral, officially. All anyone knew was that they went for exos first, every single time, like they could smell them. Seals. Every exo had one, a shield it could raise like a pane of glass you couldn't see until something hit it, and so did every Nephil, which was why no missile in the world had ever done anything to one. Only a seal could break a seal. That was the whole reason exos existed. The frost was theirs, too: a Nephil made its own winter wherever it stood, because it couldn't live in ours. And going soft, which was what pilots called brain death, was when a seal broke with the pilot still linked: it took them with it, and their mind ran out like wax, and it didn't come back. Corruption, which was what happened when a Nephil's frost got into an exo's living tissue and grew there like mold, and which was basically an urban legend, he said, because nobody had ever seen a confirmed case, and also because Umbral sealed everything. The warp drives in the Space Transit wing, which had been test-fired exactly once, and whose test pilots, everyone knew, had *seen things*. What things? He didn't know. Nobody knew. That was what made it good.

He apologized, and then told her more.

He had tried to film Yuki, too. Every shot came out blurred, no matter how still she sat, as if the camera couldn't decide where she was. He showed Val one, frowning at it, then looked from the screen to Val and back.

"Are you two related?" he asked. "Same hair."

"No," said Val.

"No," said Yuki, from two rows over, without looking up.

On the third day, at lunch, a girl with a sports bag and tape across her knuckles walked up to Val's desk in front of the whole class and punched her in the face.

Her name was Akari Harada. Val's head snapped sideways. The class screamed. Akari stood over her, shaking. "My little brother was on a respirator when your plant went dark," she said. "The hospital's backup failed. He's alive. No thanks to you."

Val touched her split lip and looked at the blood on her fingers. "Okay," she said.

That made Akari angrier than the punch had. Haruto got between them. Nanami ran for a teacher. Val walked home with her lip swelling, and Alex looked at it across the dinner table.

"I fell," she said.

"Sure you did."

### Chapter 9: The Fall

The sirens went off during sixth period. Trumpet Three: a Fall over water, on the river that ran through the middle of the city. Class 2-A filed into the shelter under the school in the practiced, frightened silence of people who had done this a hundred times. In the crowd, nobody noticed two students missing. Haruto had slipped out a maintenance door, because he had never filmed a Fall up close and refused to die without filming one. Akari had gone after him, partly to drag him back, and partly, though she'd never admit it, because she wanted to see what the pilot who'd hurt her brother actually did.

The Nephil came down over the river. It was a giant of frost and glass, taller than any building in the district, with a smooth, blank head like a mask carved from ice and arms with too many joints. It moved with a strange urgency, almost clumsy, the way a small child runs through a crowd toward someone it has spotted. It stepped over apartment blocks and through power lines without seeming to notice them. It wasn't going for the city. It was going for 04.

Val met it in the street with a rifle, which did nothing, the rounds sparking off its seal like hail off a window, and then with her fists, efficient and cold, exactly as trained. She drove 04's seal against the Nephil's until the frost lines in the two of them ran together and its seal cracked like ice on a pond. At one point the Nephil got a hand on 04's chest plate, and instead of crushing it, it held it there. Almost gently, as if feeling for a heartbeat. 04 shuddered, and for half a second Val felt something through the link, like someone pressing their ear to a door. Then she broke its arm off.

That was when she saw the two kids on the rooftop directly in its path, one with a camera, the other dragging him by the collar. The Nephil swung. Val had no time to think. She dropped 04 to one knee, cracked open the spine capsule, and scooped both of them inside. No pilot was supposed to do that. Passengers caused sync noise, and sync noise caused pain.

Akari and Haruto ended up pressed against the back wall of the capsule while the walls around them showed the fight the way 04 saw it, as if the three of them were standing in the open street. They saw everything. They heard Val breathe. When the Nephil caught 04's left arm and snapped it, Akari saw Val's real left arm drop dead at her side. Val's face didn't change, and she kept fighting one-handed, the way she'd watched Alex do everything for weeks. She drove 04's right fist straight through the Nephil's chest, into the cold light at its center, and the light went out. The giant collapsed and came apart into powder, a fine, glittering frost that drifted down over the river like snow.

Afterward, Umbral security yelled at the two civilians. Alex yelled at Val over the comm about desync and protocol and how she could have killed herself. Then, after a pause, he said only, "Good."

Akari was waiting outside HQ when Val came out. "Your arm," she said. "It just *dropped*."

"It does that."

"Every time?"

"Only when it breaks."

Akari looked at Val's arm. Val flexed it; it was fine now. Akari set her jaw, as if she had come there to say something else and this was the only part of it she could get out. "Come meet him."

Val didn't. Akari sat down next to her on the curb anyway. A minute later Haruto arrived holding up his camera and showed them what he'd shot from inside the capsule: Val's face in the light of the walls, blank, and her hand shaking on the control, just slightly, the whole time. Umbral security had taken his memory card at the door. The second one had been in his sock. "They seal everything," he said, when Akari stared at him. "Every Fall. Every clip. Somebody should keep it." Val looked away. Nanami came last, out of breath, carrying all three of their school bags.

The next morning, Nanami said "Good morning!" and Val didn't answer. But at lunch, Akari grabbed her by the sleeve and dragged her up to the roof, and Val ate her bento there with the three of them while Nanami talked about the weather, Haruto talked about Nephil classifications, and Akari told him to shut up. She didn't notice anything happen. That was the point.

After school, Akari walked out of the gate beside her without asking and turned toward the hospital, and Val went with her.

Kōta Harada was eight, and small for it, with an oxygen line taped to his cheek and a stack of exo trading cards fanned out on his blanket, and he had a question for everything. Could pilots feel the exo's feet? Did 04 have a name? What did a Nephil feel like when you punched it? Was she scared? Why didn't 04 have a poster? Why was her hair like that? Val stood at the foot of his bed and didn't answer any of them. Kōta didn't seem to mind. He asked them anyway, one after another, the way Nanami said good morning, and when the nurse said it was time to go, he asked one more.

"Will you come back?"

Val didn't answer that one either. But she looked at him for a long time before she left.

### Chapter 10: The Gate

On days without a Fall, a gray Umbral car waited at the school gate at 3:40.

It had waited at the gate of her old school on the east side too, every quiet day for seven years. On mission days, Alex came to the classroom door. On quiet days, Patrick came to the gate. Val had never asked him to. He had simply been there on the first Monday after she moved into her own apartment, parked by the gate with the engine running, and he had been there every quiet afternoon since. He was the commander of Umbral, a man who signed off on weapons that could crack a mountain, and he came anyway, and he was never late.

Her friends noticed on the first quiet day.

"Wait," Haruto said, already lifting his camera. "Is that the commander? That's the actual commander of Umbral. In a regular car. Waiting at a school."

"My father's friend," Val said.

"He picks you up every day?" Nanami pressed her hands together. "That's so nice."

Akari squinted across the street. "He looks like he hasn't slept since 2044."

"He hasn't," Val said, and walked to the car.

It always went the same way. The car smelled of old coffee and paper. There was always a folder on the passenger seat, and he always moved it to the back before she got in, as if she might read it, as if she had ever read anything he left lying around. He asked, "How was school?" She said, "Fine." He drove the long way home, along the harbor, because once, when she was ten, she had watched the water out the window for the whole ride, and he had taken that as a request. He had never asked whether it was. She had never told him it wasn't.

Some days he stopped at the konbini on the corner by the ferry terminal and came back with two hot cans of coffee from the machine outside, one for each of them. Hers was always the sweet kind with too much milk, the kind she'd picked once by accident when she was eleven and had been getting ever since because he remembered. He never drank his. He held it in both hands until it went cold, and then he put it in the cup holder and drove, and the next day the cold can was gone and there was a new one.

That day, at the long red light by the ferry terminal, he glanced down at her hands and went still.

She was turning her pencil over in her fingers, the way she always did. The scuffed metal one, with a worn logo on its side that nobody at either of her schools had ever recognized.

"Where did you get that?" he asked.

She looked at it as if she hadn't noticed it was there. "I've always had it."

"Always."

"Since I was small." She turned it over again. "Somebody gave it to me. I don't remember who."

He looked at the pencil for the whole length of the red light. His hands tightened on the wheel, the way they tightened on the edge of the console in mission control when the comms went quiet for too long. The light turned green. He didn't move. The car behind them honked once, then again, longer.

"Keep it," he said, and drove.

He didn't say anything else the whole way home. When she got out in front of her building, he said, "Valeria," and she turned around, and he seemed to forget what he had meant to say. He sat there with his hands on the wheel and his cold coffee in the cup holder, looking at her through the open window like a man looking at a photograph.

"Eat something," he said finally. "Alex says you don't."

"Alex lies."

"Alex," Patrick said, "is the only person in this city who doesn't." Then the window went up and the gray car pulled away from the curb. She watched it to the end of the street. She told herself she was only making sure it turned the right way.

The next day, at lunch on the roof, Akari leaned across the bento boxes with her chopsticks pointed like a weapon. "So. Your dad's friend. The commander. He's kind of creepy, right? He just sits there with the engine running like he's casing the place."

"He's not creepy," Val said. "He's been waiting at that gate since I was nine, and he's never once been late."

All three of them looked at her. It was the most words she had said in a row since she'd transferred, and every one of them had been in someone's defense.

"Okay," Akari said slowly. "He's not creepy."

"He's tired," Val said, and went back to her rice. Nanami looked at Haruto, and Haruto looked at Nanami, and neither of them said anything, which for Haruto was a medical event.

Every quiet afternoon after that, coming out through the school doors, the first thing Val looked for was the gray car. She would have denied it. She would have said she was only checking whether it was a mission day. On the days it wasn't there, because a Fall had come down somewhere or a meeting had run long, she stood at the gate for a minute anyway, the pencil turning in her fingers, before she walked home alone.

### Chapter 11: Omurice

The apartment was quieter after the plant.

Alex came home from his third week in mission control with a carton of eggs from the canteen and the announcement that he was going to make omurice, one-handed, or die trying. He cracked the first egg onto the counter. The second went on the floor. The third made it into the bowl, along with most of its shell, and he picked the pieces out one by one with chopsticks, swearing at them with real affection. Val sat at the table with her pencil and watched him, the way she watched everything.

He talked while he cooked, because Alex always talked. That night he talked about Ross.

"He used to rate my landings," he said, pushing rice around the pan. "Out of ten. Every landing, for a whole year. Never once gave me higher than a six."

"Were they bad?"

"They were sevens. Some of them were eights." He jabbed the spatula at her. "I landed on a moving barge once, in a typhoon, and he gave me a four, because I 'lacked conviction on the approach.' Who says that? Who programs a machine to say that?"

"Nobody programmed him," Val said. "He learned it from you."

Alex laughed, surprised into it. "Yeah. Yeah, he did." He shook the pan. "Once he spent a whole patrol pretending he'd forgotten my name. Three hours. 'The pilot is advised to adjust his heading.' 'The pilot appears to be humming.' All because I told him his jokes weren't funny. I had to apologize to him over an open channel. Patrick heard. The whole ops room heard. Aoi still brings it up."

He said all of it cheerfully, without looking up from the pan, and his voice broke only once, on the word *six*. Val noticed. She didn't say anything. She had never known what to say when people's voices did that.

His phone buzzed on the counter. He answered it by trapping it against his shoulder with his ear. "Yes. No. Yes, I'm cooking. No, I haven't burned anything." Smoke rose from the pan. "Okay. I've burned one thing." On the other end, someone laughed, loud enough for Val to hear across the kitchen, a warm, hoarse laugh she recognized from a year of comms. "She says hi," Alex said to Val, covering the phone with his chin.

"Lieutenant Sena?"

"Aoi. She says call her Aoi. She says if you call her Lieutenant again, she'll lose your mail." He waited. Val didn't say anything. Alex uncovered the phone. "She says hi back," he lied, easily, the way he had been lying for her since the week he moved in, and he said goodbye, and the phone went back on the counter.

When he tried to fold the omelette over the rice with one hand, it tore down the middle. He stared at it. Then he laughed again, at himself this time, and Val got up without a word, took the bowl from the counter, and held it steady while he tried again with a fresh one. Their hands didn't touch. It worked anyway.

It was the ugliest omurice in Hakobune, torn and lopsided and dark at one edge. She ate all of it.

"You're welcome," Alex said.

"I didn't say thank you," Val said.

"I know," Alex said. "I'm practicing for when you do."

### Chapter 12: Blackout

The next Nephil didn't come to the city.

It came down out of a gray sky two kilometers west of Hakobune and stopped above the reservoir, and it hung there, motionless, a hundred meters over the water. It was a long, slender thing, more like a spire of ice than a body, with a mask at the top that faced the city and never moved. Then a ring of frost spread out on the water beneath it, and the water began to freeze in a column straight down, and Umbral's scanners worked out what it was doing about an hour after the first drone died.

It was drilling. It was boring straight down through the reservoir bed and into the bedrock, and the line of the drill, extended, ran under the city and ended exactly beneath Umbral headquarters. Beneath the warp-drive wing. Beneath the door with the frost on it that said RELIQUARY.

Anything that came within a kilometer of it was shredded. The first drone went in low and fast and simply came apart in midair, cut into a hundred pieces by a ring of ice shards fired at a speed the instruments couldn't track. The second went high. Same result. A test frame, an empty exo chassis walked in by remote, lasted four seconds. The third drone was a decoy, and the Nephil ignored it entirely, which somehow frightened people more than if it hadn't.

"Trumpet Three," Lieutenant Sena said over the open channel, in her calm comms voice, and every siren in the city went at once.

Mission control was full of people standing up. Val, in her flight suit, stood at the back by the postcard wall and looked at the map, where the red line of the drill crept toward the center of the city at a little over two hundred meters an hour. At that speed it would be under headquarters by dawn. Patrick stood at the front with his hands behind his back and said nothing at all, which was how you knew it was bad. Beside him, Ruth Hermon had a pencil behind her ear and was doing the drill's arithmetic on the back of a duty roster, and when she finished she showed it to Patrick and not to anyone else.

It was Alex who came up with the plan. He was hunched over the map table with his one hand flat on the glass, and he talked the way he cooked, fast and badly and with total conviction.

"It's a sniper," he said. "So we snipe it. Nothing gets within a kilometer, fine, we don't go within a kilometer. Nothing breaks a seal from range, either. That's the whole problem. Except one thing. Umbral's got a long gun in storage, the one they built for the Fall that never came, to break a seal without touching it. The one that's never been fired at full charge, because firing it at full charge would take more power than the plant makes in an hour."

"The plant's half gone," someone said.

"So we don't use the plant. We use everything else." Alex looked up. "Every reserve the city's been sitting on since 2044, every battery bank, every train capacitor. Every light. Every train. Every heater, every hospital backup that isn't on a respirator ward, every vending machine. We shut the city off, district by district, and we pour all of it into one shot."

The room went quiet.

"One shot," Patrick said.

"Two, if the capacitors hold. Maybe."

"One and a half," said Eve Sakaki, from the end of the table, without looking up from her tablet. Umbral's chief of research never rounded up.

Alex looked at Val across the room. "She'll make it with one."

The mayor signed the order at dusk. They called it Operation Blackout, because nobody in Umbral had ever been good at naming things. Then Lieutenant Sena said it over every speaker in the city, the alert nobody in Hakobune had ever heard called for real: "Trumpet Four." The darkening.

Under the school, in the shelter beneath Hakobune Municipal No. 3, the students of Class 2-A sat in rows on the cold floor with their families and watched the public feed on a single wall screen. A map of the city. Districts in white. Lieutenant Sena's voice reading them off, one at a time, as they went dark.

"District fourteen, dark. District fifteen, dark."

The hospital sat on the drill's line, so they had emptied it at noon, and Akari had wheeled her little brother three blocks to the school shelter herself, wrapped in two blankets, his oxygen line taped to his cheek and a portable tank hissing on the back of the chair. Kōta had been asking questions since he arrived. Haruto filmed the screen with shaking hands. Nanami sat with Kōta's hand in hers and answered his questions one at a time, as calmly as if she were reading from her laminated schedule.

"Where are all the lights going?"

"To your sister's friend," Nanami said. "She needs all of them, just for a minute."

"All of them?"

"All of them."

On the screen, for a second, the feed showed the valley below the reservoir: a white unit standing guard in the dark over a line of cables as thick as trees.

"Who's that?" said Kōta.

"That's Yuki. She's in our class."

"Is she scared?"

Nanami thought about it. "I don't know," she said. "I don't think anyone's ever asked her."

"District twenty-two, dark. District twenty-three, dark."

On the screen, the white of the city went black in a long, slow tide that rolled from the edges inward toward the reservoir. The shelter's own lights dimmed to emergency red, then to nothing. People gasped. Somebody's baby started crying. In the dark, Kōta tugged at Nanami's sleeve.

"Is it dark outside too?"

"Everywhere," Nanami said.

"Can I see?"

Nanami looked at the shelter door, at the top of the stairwell, where a warden sat on the top step with a flashlight. She looked at Akari. Then she lifted Kōta out of his chair, blankets and oxygen tank and all, and carried him up the stairs, and said something to the warden in her class-rep voice, and the warden opened the door a hand's width.

"Where did the stars go?" Kōta said. "There weren't any stars before."

Nanami looked up too. Through the gap in the door, for the first time in any of their lives, the sky over Hakobune was full of them.

"Were they always there?" Kōta asked.

"Every night," Nanami said.

"There's one moving," Kōta said, pointing. A faint, steady star crawling slowly across the slot of sky.

"That's the Morningstar," Haruto said from the step below them, still filming. "Umbral's station. It's always moving."

"District thirty, dark," said Lieutenant Sena. "That's all of them. That's the whole city." A pause, and then her voice changed, just slightly, from comms voice to something closer to a person's. "It's yours, Kane-san."

Val lay prone on a ridge above the reservoir with the long gun across 04's shoulder.

It was an ugly thing, the long gun: a barrel longer than three exos laid end to end, propped on a bipod sunk into the rock, with cables as thick as tree trunks running from its breech back down the hill and across the dark valley toward the city. Halfway down, where the cables crossed the valley floor, Unit 00 stood over the junction in the dark, white and still, because if the line went, the city had gone dark for nothing. Yuki hadn't said a word on the channel all night. Through 04's eyes, Val could see the Nephil out over the water, pale against the night, its drill-column glowing faintly from within. She could see her own breath fogging the inside of the capsule. She could feel the gun drinking the city.

"Capacitors at ninety," Alex said in her ear. "Ninety-five. Val, it's going to know. The second this thing charges, it's going to feel it. You get one clean look."

"I know."

"Ninety-eight."

She lined it up. The mask, the center of the mask, the place where the cold light lived inside every Nephil she'd ever killed. She breathed out.

"Fire."

The gun fired. The whole valley lit up white for an instant, every rock, every tree, the frozen reservoir, the black city behind her. The beam crossed two kilometers in no time at all and passed one meter to the left of the mask, because in the last fraction of a second, the Nephil moved. It turned its head, almost gently, as if someone had said its name, and the light went past it and into the sky and was gone.

Then it answered.

The kilometer, it turned out, had only been how far it bothered to defend.

The ring of ice came at the ridge like a storm of knives. Val saw it coming in the dark, a glittering wall, and she knew that she couldn't move fast enough, that the gun was too heavy and 04 was locked to it and there was nothing to do but watch it arrive.

Unit 04 moved.

Not on her command. She hadn't moved a muscle. Its seal went up over the ridge, a pane of pale light veined with frost, and the first of the storm broke on it, ringing, and the seal bent and held and bent and cracked across like ice on a pond. Then the exo rolled its own body up and over the gun and over its own spine capsule, over her, and took the storm on its back. Armor screamed. Through the link, Val felt shards punch into the plates along 04's spine like hail through a tin roof, felt them as her own back, felt herself not be hurt because 04 was being hurt instead. It went on for six seconds. It felt much longer.

Down the hill, the edge of the storm reached the cables. Unit 00's seal went up over the junction, and rang, and held. Nobody saw it. The line held.

In mission control, Patrick watched the readings and said nothing. The pilot's inputs were flat. The exo was moving. He had seen those two numbers on a screen together once before, at the power plant, and he had sealed the footage. Alex stared at the screen, then turned and stared at Patrick, and Patrick didn't look back at him, and Alex decided not to ask.

"Capacitors recharging," Lieutenant Sena said, very quietly. "Forty percent. Sixty. Val. Val, are you there?"

"I'm here."

"Eighty."

04 settled back onto the ridge, slowly, as if nothing had happened. Its back was in ruins. It lined up the gun again, and Val realized she was the one doing it, and she wasn't sure when that had started.

"Ninety-five," said Alex. "This is it. This is the last of the city."

She didn't breathe this time. She didn't think about the mask or the cold light or the kids in the shelter or the boy with the trading cards who had asked her if she would come back; she thought about nothing at all, the way she always did, the way that had kept her alive this long.

"Fire."

The light hit the Nephil's seal first. For an instant Val saw it, a pane of frost-light the size of a building standing in front of the mask, and then it cracked from edge to edge, and the light went straight through the Nephil's mask. For a moment the whole thing glowed from the inside, every edge of it lit up like a chandelier, and then it came apart over the reservoir in a slow silent burst of frost and light that hung in the air and drifted down onto the frozen water like the world's largest, quietest firework.

In the shelter, the screen showed it as a white flash and then nothing, and then Lieutenant Sena's voice saying, "Target down. Target down," and the room exploded. People were screaming, hugging, crying. Haruto was shouting into his camera. Akari had run up the stairs and had both arms around her brother and Nanami at once. Kōta, on the top step, was still looking up through the door at the stars, because they were already starting to go out, one district at a time, as the city's lights came back on.

On the ridge, Val lay inside her exo and watched the lights come back. They returned in the same long tide in reverse, from the reservoir outward, district by district, until Hakobune glowed again and the stars were gone.

Alex's voice came over the comm, rough with relief. "You did good, Val. You did really, really good."

"It was luck," Val said.

He was quiet for a second. "Sure," he said. "Sure it was."

When the channel closed, she lay there a while longer. 04's back was torn open. It would take the hangar crews weeks to grow the plating back.

"Why did you do that?" she asked.

04 didn't answer. It never answered. She lay there in the dark capsule with the question hanging in the air, unanswered, and for the first time in her life she felt what that was like from the other side.

### Stinger

On the Moon, deep inside the curve of Sariel's ribs, where the lunar facility's lights never reached, a boy opened his eyes in the cold. He lay very still for a long time, looking up at the black sky and the small blue Earth in it.

"Two," he said softly, to no one, the way you count something you don't want to lose track of.

Then he smiled.

---

## Arc 4: Navis

> *And I will give power unto my two witnesses, and they shall prophesy a thousand two hundred and threescore days, clothed in sackcloth.*
>
> — Revelation 11:3

### Chapter 13: The Oldest Unit

Far out in the asteroid belt, at a harvesting station carved into a fragment of Azazel the size of a mountain, something was being born.

The station was called Harvest Nine, and it had been cutting saint flesh out of that rock for thirty years. The rock was a piece of Azazel. The whole belt was: a saint who had come apart out here long before anyone on the station was born, scattered across a hundred million kilometers of dark. The stations cut him into blocks and froze them and shipped them down to Earth, and on Earth the blocks went into vats, and out of the vats came cores for exos. On Harvest Nine nobody thought much about it. It was a job. There were forty-one people living on it, most of them old, all of them tired, and none of them paid enough to be there. They had seen strange things in the rock before. They had never seen this.

It started as frost. Wherever the drills had opened the flesh, frost spread out across the wound in fine, feathery lines, faster than frost should grow in a vacuum. Then, under the frost, a shape began to form, pale and curled up like something sleeping. On the fourth day, the shape had a face.

"Don't call the church," the foreman said, staring at the monitor. He was sixty-eight, with a white beard and a cough he'd brought from Earth and never gotten rid of. "Every time somebody calls the church, they send a priest, and the priest prays at it, and then it eats the priest. Call Umbral."

"Umbral's six weeks out," said the youngest tech, who was twenty-two and had been on Harvest Nine for exactly one month.

"Umbral keeps somebody out here," the foreman said. "They always have. Look it up."

The tech looked it up. The belt patrol roster had one name on it, and a unit number, and a note: *Do not reassign. Standing order, 2082.* The tech had never heard of the pilot. Most of headquarters had forgotten she existed.

She arrived two days later.

Unit 01 came in low over the rock, trailing sparks from one shoulder, and settled onto the docking clamps with a sound like a dropped toolbox. It was the oldest exo still in service, a first-generation chassis that had been rebuilt so many times there was almost nothing original left in it. One arm didn't match the other. Its chest plate had been welded over at least four times, by at least four different people, none of whom had cared what it looked like. Its paint, where there was any paint, was the faded orange of an old life raft.

The comm crackled. Someone was humming, off-key, a song so old the tech didn't recognize it.

"Morning, Harvest Nine," said a cheerful voice. A girl's voice, young enough that the tech thought for a second it was a recording. "Who ordered the monster?"

"We did," said the foreman. "Who are you?"

"Belt patrol. You looked me up." The humming resumed. "Don't let me interrupt. Where is it?"

They showed her. She looked at the shape under the frost for a long time without saying anything, which the foreman would remember later as the only time she was quiet the whole visit.

"Okay," she said finally. "Everybody inside. Seal the doors. If you hear screaming, it's probably me, and it's probably fine."

The Nephil broke out of the ice an hour later, like a calf out of an egg. It was enormous and wet and confused, all long folded limbs and a smooth face like a mask carved out of the saint's own bone, and the first thing it did once it was free was turn its head toward Unit 01 and tilt it, as if it were listening for something.

In the capsule, the pilot stopped humming.

"Sorry, sweetheart," she said softly. "Wrong door."

Then she went in with both fists.

It took four minutes. Unit 01 lost two fingers on its left hand and a strip of plating off its hip, and the pilot complained about both, loudly and at length, over an open channel the whole station could hear. When it was over, the Nephil was a drifting cloud of frost glittering in the station floodlights, and the orange exo was sitting on the rock with its legs stuck out in front of it like a kid on a curb.

She sat in the capsule with the frost melting off the walls and patted the console. "Good girl, Lizzie."

The unit grumbled at her through the link like an old engine on a cold morning. The pilot grinned as if it had paid her a compliment.

The station relayed her messages afterward, a month's worth in one burst. She skipped most of them. Supply schedules. A recall notice she'd been ignoring for three years. A birthday message from a dentist on Earth who didn't know she'd moved. She almost skipped the last one too, and then she saw the name in it and read it twice.

*Personnel notice, Hakobune. Theseus Moriah, sixteen, assigned to the Hakobune roster as teammate to Valeria Kane, effective immediately.*

She pulled an old photograph out of the chest pocket of her flight suit. It was printed on real paper, soft at the corners from years of handling. It showed three people on a hangar floor, laughing at something outside the frame. A young man with Samuel Kane's crooked grin. A Japanese woman with her hair cut short above the neck, holding a pencil like she'd forgotten it was in her hand. And between them, with her arms around both their necks, a girl of seventeen who looked exactly like the pilot holding the photograph, because she still was.

"Okay," the pilot said to the picture. "Okay. I'm going."

The foreman, who had come out to thank her, found her already prepping the unit for a long burn.

"Your orders say you stay out here," he said. "Standing order. I looked."

"I know what my orders say." She flipped switches without looking at them. "I've got older ones."

"From who?"

She smiled at him through the open capsule, and for a moment she didn't look seventeen at all.

"From a friend," Maggie said, and sealed the hatch.

### Chapter 14: Theseus

He came into Hakobune by train, alone, with two duffel bags.

It was a long ride from the coast, and he spent most of it rehearsing. He had decided, somewhere around the third tunnel, that he would say "Father" and not "Dad," because "Dad" was a word for people who had birthdays together. He had decided he would shake hands if a hand was offered and would not offer his own first. He had decided he would not be the one to look away. He was sixteen, and he had trained six years at an overseas Umbral branch with scores that made instructors call other instructors in to look, and he had decided all of this in perfect detail, the way he decided everything.

The train pulled in. The platform emptied. He stood with his bags at his feet and looked up and down it, and then out through the station doors at the street.

Nobody had come.

He waited anyway. Ten minutes. Twenty. Across the street, a gray car sat at the curb with its engine running. It had been there when he came out, and it was still there when he finally picked up his bags, and he noticed it only the way you notice furniture. When he looked back from the corner, it was pulling away from the curb, slowly, in the other direction.

He took a cab. The driver asked if he was visiting family. He said no.

At headquarters, a clerk who hadn't been told he was coming found his name on a list after twenty minutes and walked him down the main corridor toward the pilots' wing. Halfway there, a man came the other way, from the direction of the medical wing, tall and tired and gray, reading a folder as he walked, flanked by two officers. Seus knew him before he knew he knew him, the way you know your own handwriting.

He had decided he would not be the one to look away.

Commander Patrick Moriah looked up from his folder, saw his son, and didn't stop walking. He didn't nod. He didn't slow down. He passed close enough that Seus could have touched his sleeve, and then he was behind him, and the officers' footsteps went with him down the corridor, and that was all.

Seus stood there with his bags. The clerk cleared his throat. "This way, sir."

He had forgotten to say "Father." He hadn't forgotten. There just hadn't been anywhere to put it.

Val learned that Patrick had a son the same way everyone else did, from a personnel notice. Umbral had other units, at the northern bases and overseas, and every one of them was spoken for. The order to replace Alex had come from somewhere above Umbral, and it had asked for Patrick's son by name. Patrick couldn't block it. He had signed the papers without comment. In seven years he had never once mentioned a son. When Val had lived in his apartment, at nine, there had been no boy in it, no second toothbrush, no photographs on the walls. His son had been overseas the whole time, the notice said, at the other branch, where his mother was posted. He had treated Val like his only daughter, in his distant way: the gray car at the gate on every quiet afternoon, birthday cards signed in an assistant's handwriting, the quiet override whenever someone tried to have her removed. She read the notice twice. Then she folded it into her pocket and didn't think about it, which took effort.

* * *

The day the notice went up, a Nephil came down over the business district, and broke.

It didn't land. A hundred meters above the towers it stopped falling and hung there for a second, the way a drop of water hangs at the end of a tap. Then it came apart. Not into pieces: into Nephilim. Dozens of them, each the size of a bus, all long folded limbs and small blank faces, dropping onto the rooftops like seeds shaken out of a pod. They hit the roofs running. Every one of them went the same way, toward the nearest seal.

04 was on the far side of the city, on harbor patrol. Val turned it around and ran.

"Thirty," Ruth Hermon said on the command channel, in the dry, unhurried voice of a woman reading down a column of figures. She sat at Patrick's elbow in mission control, and Val had never once heard her raise it. "Thirty-four. Thirty-eight. Moving west." A pause. "Toward the hospital on the hill."

The hospital where Kōta was.

Unit 00 was already there.

Val saw it from three districts away, between the towers: the white unit standing in the hospital's forecourt with its feet planted and its seal up over the whole building, a pane of pale light curved like a glass bowl turned over a cake. The swarm came down on it from every roof. They landed on the seal and clung there and tore at it with their long fingers, and the seal rang under them, and rang, and rang, a sound Val could hear through 04's hull from a kilometer off.

Unit 00 didn't move. It didn't go after them. It held.

Eleven minutes. That was how long it took 04 to cross the city, and the ops room counted every one of them out loud. For eleven minutes, a girl Val had sat a few desks away from in a classroom and never spoken to stood in front of a hospital alone and held a seal over it while thirty-eight Nephilim tried to tear it open. The seal cracked. She held it with the crack in it. It cracked again.

When the first of them got through, Unit 00 took it on its own arm.

Val heard it on the channel. Not a scream. A small sound, like a breath caught on something, and then the voice again, flat, the same as always: "Unit 00. Holding."

Then Val was there, and it was only a fight. 04 went through the swarm the way it went through everything, efficient and cold, breaking seal after seal, and the small Nephilim turned on it now because it was nearer, which was what Unit 00 had been waiting for. It was over in four minutes. When the last of them came apart into frost across the hospital car park, Unit 00 was kneeling on the forecourt in front of the doors, with its seal gone and one arm hanging, still between the hospital and the street.

In the windows above it, patients had their faces pressed to the glass. One of them was a small boy with an oxygen line taped to his cheek.

"Unit 00," Yuki said on the channel. "Hospital intact. Pilot intact." A pause. "Mostly."

"Pull her out," said another voice, a woman's, clipped and fast: Dr. Eve Sakaki, at the science desk. "Her readings are all over the place. Pull her out now."

* * *

That night Val went down to the medical wing. She didn't ask herself why.

Yuki's door was half open. She was asleep, propped up on the pillows with her arm in a sling and a bandage around her head, her short hair sticking out over the top of it. And in the chair beside the bed, in his coat, sat Patrick.

He wasn't reading. He wasn't working. He was only sitting there, with his hand on the blanket near hers, not on it, looking at the wall above her head instead of at her face, as if her face were something he could only look at for a little while at a time.

Val had sat beside him in a car for seven years. She had never seen him sit anywhere like that.

She stood in the hallway and watched until her legs ached. Then footsteps came down the corridor behind her, and it was Ruth Hermon, in her cardigan, with her reading glasses on their chain, carrying two cups of tea.

Ruth looked at Val, and at the door, and at Patrick through it. Whatever she thought about any of it, she kept.

"Go to bed, Kane," she said, not unkindly. Then she went in, and set one cup on the table beside the sleeping girl, and put the other in Patrick's hand.

They met in the hangar.

He was standing in front of Unit 04 when she came in, looking up at it with his hands in his pockets, and he didn't turn around right away. When he did, she saw Patrick's jaw on a face that had none of Patrick's patience.

"So you're the genius," he said.

She stopped a few meters away and looked at him, the way she looked at everyone.

He waited for her to say something. She didn't. It visibly annoyed him.

"Do you even know who I am?" he said.

"Sorry," Val said. "Knowing's not my thing."

For a second he just stared at her. Then he laughed, short and sharp and not at all amused. "Wow. Okay. I'm Theseus Moriah. Seus, if you want. Commander Moriah's son." He said the last part like he was setting something heavy down in front of her. "Your new teammate. Since you broke the last one."

"Okay," said Val.

"Okay," he repeated. "That's it? Okay?" He took his hands out of his pockets. "I read your debriefs. All seven years. Seventy percent of Samuel Kane's record in a third of the time, and every single report says the same thing. *Pilot attributes outcome to luck.*" He said it in a flat, robotic voice, an impression of her. "Luck. Seven years of luck. You know what I did for six years? I trained. Every day. I didn't get handed a legend's exo because of whose kid I was. I earned mine."

"Okay," Val said again.

"Eleven thousand sim hours," Seus said. "How many have you got?"

"I don't count."

That did it. He went red to the ears. "I'm going to beat you," he said. "In front of everyone. And in front of him."

He didn't say who *him* was. He didn't have to.

It made him furious, everything about her: the flat voice, the flat face, the way she looked at him as if he were weather. And the truth underneath it was simple and humiliating, and he would never have said it out loud, not then. He didn't care about her record. He cared that his father had spent seven years being proud of someone else's daughter. All he had ever wanted, his whole life, was for his father to be proud of him once.

His exo arrived the next morning by freight, a brand-new third-generation unit, tall and clean and painted a deep sea-blue. Unit 08. He had named it Navis. When he linked with it in the hangar for the first time in Hakobune, the walls of the capsule lit up, and a clipped, eager voice spoke inside it, a voice that sounded a lot like him on his best behavior.

"Link stable. Systems green. Was that good?"

Seus didn't answer it. He never answered it.

* * *

Two bays down, Unit 00 stood in scaffolding with a crew welding at its ruined shoulder. Its pilot sat on the gantry rail above it with her own arm in a sling and her feet hanging over the drop, watching them work, the way she watched everything, as if she were waiting to be told what it meant.

Seus went up the ladder to her. Val, at 04's bay, watched him go.

"You're Unit 00," he said. "You held the hospital. I read the report."

"Yes."

"Thirty-eight. Alone. Eleven minutes." He said it the way he had said *eleven thousand sim hours*, like a number he respected. "That was good."

Yuki turned her head and looked at him.

"Was it?" she said.

Seus opened his mouth, and nothing came out of it. Below them, through Navis's open hatch, he heard the eager clipped voice running its diagnostics, or thought he did.

"...Yeah," he said. "It was." He went back down the ladder fast, without looking at Val, and out.

So he could be decent, Val thought. Just not to her.

When she looked up again, Yuki was looking at her.

"The commander sat with me," Yuki said. "Last night."

"I saw."

"He didn't say anything. I didn't either. I didn't know what to say." She said it the way she said everything, flatly, as a fact. Then, as if it were a technical question about the unit: "What do you say when you don't know what to say?"

Val thought about it for a long time.

"I don't know," she said. "I never say anything."

Yuki considered that.

"Then we're the same," she said.

Val looked up at her: the sling, the bandage, the short hair, a face she felt she ought to know and couldn't place. She didn't say anything back, which proved it.

That night he showed up at the apartment with his two duffel bags. Umbral housing had assigned him the third bedroom, the one where Ross's spare parts used to be, though Alex had moved them out that afternoon and carried them down to the basement one-handed, a box at a time, without asking anyone for help. Seus walked straight past Val in the hallway, dropped his bags in the middle of the room, and announced, to no one in particular, "This is my room now. Nobody touches my stuff."
