"Wouldn't dream of it," said Alex, beaming at both of them from the kitchen doorway like a man who had just adopted two cats that hated each other.

There was a woman in the kitchen, too. She was stirring a pot, in a gray sweater with the sleeves pushed up, and she turned around when Seus came in and smiled at him like she had been expecting him all day.

"You must be Theseus," she said. "I'm Aoi. Sit down. Alex made curry, and I'm so sorry about that, but I fixed most of it."

"Most of it," Alex said.

"The parts I could reach." She set a bowl in front of Seus before he had decided whether to sit. "Eat. You look like you came off a train."

He had. He didn't know how she knew. He sat down, because the bowl was already there, and ate, because it smelled better than anything he'd eaten in a week, and for the length of the meal he forgot to look angry. Aoi talked to him the whole time, about the train line he'd come in on and whether they still sold the good bento at the coast station and what his scores had been at the overseas branch, which she had somehow already read. Val sat at the far end of the table and said nothing. Alex sat between them and said everything.

Later, lying on a bare mattress in a room that still smelled faintly of machine oil, Seus listened to the apartment settle. Through the thin wall, he could hear that Val wasn't sleeping either. He could hear her not sleeping, which was a strange thing to be able to hear. It sounded like silence that was being held on purpose.

He turned over and faced the other wall.

### Chapter 15: In Sync

The Nephil that fell on the eastern harbor split in two the moment Navis cut it down.

It was the first real fight Seus had in Hakobune, and he had been magnificent in it, and he knew it. He'd broken its seal on the first pass, a pane of pale light cracking like ice on a pond, and taken the Nephil apart from the shoulder to the hip in one stroke while Val was still repositioning, and he'd turned around in the shallows with seawater pouring off Navis's armor, already composing the debrief in his head. *Pilot attributes outcome to training.* Then the two halves of the Nephil sat up. Each of them grew back what it was missing, in about a minute and a half. Now there were two.

He cut them both down. They split again. Now there were four, and then there were two again, because the four had folded back into each other the way water folds back together, and by then the whole harbor district was frozen to the waist and Umbral was calling them both back.

"Both of you, back," Alex said. "Seus, *back*. That's the commander's son disobeying an order on an open channel. Want that in your debrief?"

Eve Sakaki's analysts figured it out within the hour. The two halves shared one core, swapping it back and forth between them faster than any instrument could track, and they shared one seal: break it on one half, and it simply closed again over the other. The only way to destroy it was to break both seals and hit both halves at exactly the same moment. Not close. Not near. The same instant, to within a few hundredths of a second, so the core had nowhere to run.

"Two pilots," Patrick said in the briefing, looking at the screen instead of at either of them. "Perfectly synchronized."

For the first time, Seus looked across the briefing room at Val. She was already looking at the screen.

The Nephil had settled in the harbor and gone still, slowly freezing the bay around itself into a white plain. The analysts gave it six days before the ice reached the shelters on the eastern shore.

Alex gave them the six days.

He went home that night and moved all the furniture in the living room against the walls, one-handed, while Val and Seus watched from the doorway. He drew two lines on the floor in tape. Then he took out his phone and played a song through the kitchen speaker, a bright, silly, desperate pop song from about thirty years before any of them were born.

"Aoi picked it," he said. "She says it's the most synchronized song ever recorded. It's got a dance. You're going to learn the dance."

"No," said Seus.

"You're going to eat at the same time," Alex went on, as if Seus hadn't said anything. "Sleep at the same time. Brush your teeth at the same time. Every hour you're not sleeping, you're in this room learning the same sequence of movements until your bodies do it without you. In six days you go out there and you move like one person."

"I have a perfect score in close combat," Seus said. "I am not learning a *dance*."

"Then you'll have a perfect score in the dance."

"Who made you the instructor?"

"Umbral. Same people who made you a pilot. They certify us in everything." Alex ticked them off on his one hand. "Comms. Logistics. Field medicine. Morale. This is morale." He pressed play again.

Haruto, when he heard about it the next morning, said it was the funniest thing he had ever been allowed to film, and he was allowed to film it, because Alex said the more people watching, the better. By the second evening there were five people in the living room, and two of them were dancing.

Seus hated every second of it. He was good at it, which was worse. He learned the sequence in a single evening, every step of it, and then he did it faster, cleaner, better than the recording. Val learned it more slowly, and then she did it exactly, every single time, the same way, at the same speed, like a machine stamping out the same part forever.

They could not do it together.

"You're dragging," Seus snapped on the third night, out of breath, his hair stuck to his forehead. "You're doing it on purpose."

"You're early," said Val.

"I'm on the beat!"

"You're ahead of it."

"Nobody can match you," he said. "That's the problem. You don't move like a person. You move like a metronome."

"Then match the metronome," said Val.

On the fifth day, Akari came over to watch. She stood in the doorway with her arms folded and took one long look at Seus's form.

"You dance," she said, "like a refrigerator falling down a flight of stairs."

Seus spun around. "Who are you?"

"Akari Harada. Class 2-A. I punched her." She nodded at Val. "I could punch you too, if it'd help."

"You," Seus said, "have the grace of a parking ticket."

They argued for forty minutes. It ranged over dancing, refrigerators, the overseas training branch, the relative stupidity of people who punch pilots, the relative stupidity of people who move into apartments with two bags and act like they own them, and whether a parking ticket could be said to have any qualities at all, graceful or otherwise. Nanami tried to mediate for the first ten minutes and then gave up and started taking notes. Haruto filmed the whole thing and narrated it under his breath like a nature documentary. Val kept practicing, alone, in her taped square, to the song on repeat.

Somewhere in the middle of the argument, Seus stopped speeding up. He didn't notice. He was too busy yelling at Akari. He fell into step beside Val almost by accident, still facing the wrong way and still shouting, and for eight bars of a stupid old song they moved exactly together.

Alex, in the kitchen, saw it and didn't say a word.

On the sixth day they went out and did it.

Two exos crossed the frozen harbor side by side. The sea-blue one and the gray one, moving the same way at the same time, as if a single person were wearing both of them. The halves of the Nephil rose to meet them, each behind its seal, two panes of pale light in the freezing fog. Val and Seus didn't speak. They didn't need to. Both seals rang and broke in the same instant, the same strike landed on both halves, the core had nowhere to go, and the whole frozen thing shattered like a plate dropped on stone.

In the ops room, people cheered. Aoi put both hands over her mouth.

Seus stood in Navis's capsule afterward with his heart pounding, waiting to feel triumphant. Instead he felt something worse, something he didn't have a word for: the strange, unwelcome warmth of having been, for ninety seconds, perfectly in step with someone. Of having not been alone inside his own exo.

"Was that good?" Navis asked.

"Shut up," said Seus.

On the way back to the hangar, walking side by side across the ice in their exos, he spoke to Val on the private channel for the first time.

"How do you do that? Not think?"

She was quiet for a long time. "I don't know."

"Must be nice."

Another silence. Then: "It isn't."

He looked over at 04, the gray exo walking next to his. It had never said a word in the whole six days. Neither, mostly, had she.

He didn't know what to say to that, so he didn't say anything, and the two of them walked the rest of the way home in the cold.

### Chapter 16: The Warm Chapters

For a while, it was almost good.

Seus transferred into Class 2-A the week after the harbor, over his own loud objections, and his feud with Akari became the central fact of it. They argued about everything. Lunch, and specifically whose fried chicken it was, homework, the correct way to tie a shoe, whether a person who had never been punched could be trusted. Once, in gym, they argued so hard over a volleyball serve that the ball went through the gymnasium window, and they spent the following week arguing over whose fault that had been, in front of the vice principal, who eventually ruled that it had been the window's fault for being there. Haruto filmed all of it and announced that he was making a documentary called *Two Idiots*. Nanami kept the peace, mostly by threatening to report both of them, and somehow, every afternoon, she ended up walking home with Haruto, though neither of them ever explained why, and nobody ever asked.

Yuki came back to class the same week, with her arm still in its sling. On her first morning back, somebody in the back row said, not quietly enough, that the doll was back. Akari turned around in her chair.

"She held the hospital," Akari said. "Thirty-eight of them. My brother watched her do it from the window." She looked at him until he looked at his desk. "Say it again."

He didn't. At lunch Akari dragged her desk over next to Yuki's without asking and ate there, and argued with Seus across the top of Yuki's head, and Yuki ate in the middle of it, one dish at a time, in order, as if she were sitting out a rain shower. Her lunch came wrapped in a different cloth every day. Aoi left it at the comms desk each morning, and each morning Yuki collected it and said, "Thank you, Lieutenant Sena," and Aoi said, "Aoi," and Yuki said, "Lieutenant Sena," and went.

After night patrols, there was ramen on the headquarters roof.

It was Aoi's idea, and nobody could remember agreeing to it. She brought four bowls up from the canteen in a cardboard box, and they sat on the edge of the roof with their legs hanging over the city, Alex slurping one-handed and complaining that chopsticks were a fundamentally two-handed technology and the whole country should have switched to spoons in 2044 when it had the chance.

"You just don't want to admit you're bad at them," said Aoi.

"I was bad at them with two hands. It's a consistent level of skill."

Aoi laughed, the warm, hoarse laugh that came over comms every day. Up close she was younger than Val had thought, maybe twenty-four, with a round, tired, friendly face and her hair always coming loose from a clip. She had a way of looking at Alex when he wasn't looking at her that Val noticed and then made herself stop noticing.

"Does he still make you bento?" Aoi asked her, out of nowhere.

Val considered not answering. "Yes."

"Does he still burn the tamagoyaki?"

"Yes."

"Good." Aoi pointed her chopsticks at Alex. "He's practicing on you so he can cook for me. Someday it's going to be edible, and that day I'm marrying him."

Alex choked on a noodle. Seus, sitting on the other side of Val, made a noise of deep disgust that was at least forty percent delight.

On the way down, Aoi stopped Val in mission control, by the wall of postcards. Up close, there were hundreds of them, edge to edge, from places with names Val had only seen in history class: Marseille, Okinawa, Valparaíso, a beach in a country that no longer had a coast. Aoi took one down and handed it to her. It was a photograph of a sea so blue it looked fake, under a sky the same color, with a white lighthouse at the end of a pier.

"Okinawa," Aoi said. "2031. Before the sky. Somebody wrote it to somebody else, and it never got delivered, and it ended up in a box in my office decades later. I keep the ones nobody claims." She tapped the lighthouse. "Somebody's still keeping that light on."

Val looked at the card for a long time.

"Keep it if you want," said Aoi.

Val pinned it back on the wall, exactly where it had been. "It belongs here," she said, and Aoi smiled at her like she'd said something much bigger.

In the summer there was a festival by the river. The whole class went. There were paper lanterns strung over the stalls, and goldfish scooping and fried octopus and a man selling shaved ice with syrup in colors that didn't exist in nature, and at the end of the night, at the river's edge, there was tōrō nagashi: the old custom of writing a name on a paper lantern, lighting the candle inside it, and setting it on the water to drift downstream, for the dead.

Akari wrote her grandmother's name. Haruto wrote his father's, without saying anything about it. Nanami wrote four names in small careful handwriting, her whole family's side from before 2044, people she had never met. Seus wrote something on his lantern and turned it toward the water before anyone could read it, and set it down, and watched it go with his hands in his pockets.

Val stood at the edge of the river with a blank lantern in her hands.

"You don't have to write anything," Nanami said beside her. "It's for whoever you want."

Val thought about it for a long time. She thought about her father, and found nothing there to write. She thought about her mother, and found even less, a short haircut in a single photograph. She thought about Ross, who wasn't hers to grieve. In the end she lit the candle and set the lantern down on the water with nothing written on it at all, and watched it drift away down the black river among the others, a small blank light, going wherever the others were going.

On the way back, Seus won a goldfish at the scooping stall on his first try, which made him insufferable for a full minute. Then he looked at the little plastic bag with the fish in it, as if he had no idea what he had been thinking.

"I don't want this," he announced.

"Then give it to someone," said Nanami.

He looked around at the four of them, and his gaze landed on Akari, possibly by accident. He held the bag out at arm's length. "Here."

Akari took it. She held it up to the light of the lanterns and studied the fish with great seriousness.

"I'm naming it Seus," she said.

"You can't name it Seus."

"I already did. Look at him. He's got your face. Look at his stupid little frown." She tapped the bag. "Hi, Seus. You're mine now. I'm going to feed you every day and you're going to be grateful about it."

Seus opened his mouth, and closed it, and walked the rest of the way home three steps behind everyone else with his ears red, and Haruto got all of it on film.

There were other things. Konbini runs at midnight, when nobody could sleep, for melon bread and canned coffee, Seus complaining the whole way there and buying the most food. Sunday dinners, which Alex forced on all of them and which nobody admitted to looking forward to, Aoi showing up every week with something she'd fixed in Alex's kitchen before he could ruin it, Seus pretending he had somewhere better to be and never once leaving early, and, after the second week, Yuki, because Nanami had started telling her the time and the address every Saturday as if it were homework, sitting at the end of the table eating exactly what was put in front of her. Alex on the mats in the sim room on slow afternoons, one-armed, teaching Yuki to fall: how to go down with the link when it dropped you instead of fighting it, over and over, until she could land without bracing. "You're allowed to land," he kept telling her. She didn't seem to believe him. She got better at it anyway.

And some afternoons, the gray car was waiting at the school gate.

Seus saw it in his first week. His father's car, his father behind the wheel, engine running, waiting for Val. He stood by the bike racks and watched her walk across the street and get in. The car pulled away. Patrick never looked over. Not once, not in all the afternoons Seus stood by the bike racks and watched, telling himself he was only waiting for the crowd to thin out.

Akari noticed him noticing. She never said anything about it. On those afternoons she just fell into step beside him and walked him home the long way, talking loudly the whole time about nothing at all, about her brother and her coach and the goldfish named Seus and the deep stupidity of volleyball, until he forgot to look angry, or until he could pretend he had.

And once, in the ops room, during a briefing, Seus tripped over a cable.

He caught himself on a rolling chair. The chair rolled. He went with it, slowly, almost gracefully, across six meters of polished floor and directly into a trash can, which he sat down in with great dignity and stayed in.

Val made a sound.

It was very small. A breath through the nose, sharp, involuntary, like a cough that had changed its mind halfway out. The whole room went silent, because every single person in it knew what it was.

Val Kane had almost laughed.

Nobody said anything. Patrick stopped in the middle of a sentence. Alex had both hands, well, the one hand, pressed flat on the console as if he was afraid the room would tip over. Seus, still in the trash can, stared at her with an expression of pure outraged wonder.

"Somebody write down the date," Aoi said softly from the comms desk. Then, louder, to the whole silent room, in her comms voice: "Okay. Let's get him out of the bin."

### Stinger

In a room with no windows, seven old men and women sat at a long dark table, and one chair at it was empty.

There was a single lamp, set low, so that the light fell on the table and not on the faces around it. You could see hands. Sleeves. A pair of reading glasses. In the middle of the table a book lay open, very old, its spine cracked so many times that it lay flat on its own. Its margins were full of dates, penciled in a dozen different hands. Some of them had been crossed out.

They read from it the way you read a train timetable.

"The two witnesses," said a voice, and a finger in a gray sleeve moved down the page. "In the harbor. Six days."

"Done. On schedule."

A pencil crossed something out.

"The Moriah boy is placed."

"Placed."

"And the spare?"

"In place. Damaged in the swarm. Recovering."

"The book only says two witnesses," said someone at the far end. "It never says who."

"It never does. That's what we're for."

"The father objected."

"The father signed." The reading glasses came off, and were folded, and were set down beside the book. "He always signs."

"And the Kane girl?"

Somebody turned a page. "Almost laughed. In the ops room. It was reported."

"That isn't in the book."

"No," said the first voice. "It isn't."

Nobody said anything for a while. The lamp hummed. Then the finger moved on, down the column, to the next date.

"The fifth trumpet," it said, and they went on reading.

---

## Arc 5: The Dinner

> *And in those days shall men seek death, and shall not find it; and shall desire to die, and death shall flee from them.*
>
> — Revelation 9:6

### Chapter 17: Palindrome

Val went to see Palindrome alone. She had been less numb lately. Almost warm. But she still couldn't shake what he had said at the plant.

*You really are not your father, are you?*

They kept him nine levels under headquarters, in a cell lined with dampeners that hummed at a pitch just below hearing and made her teeth ache. They were there so that nothing in that room could be turned back. The guard who walked her down didn't ask why she was there. People had stopped asking Val Kane why she was anywhere.

He was sitting on the edge of his bunk with his hands folded, exactly the way he had sat in the rubble of the plant. He looked older than he had there. When she came in, he looked up and smiled the same polite, apologetic smile, and the scar running straight down the center of his face pulled it slightly crooked on both sides at once.

"Every reversal costs time," he said, before she could ask. "I used a great many at the plant. I'm twenty-one. I look about sixty, I'm told. The guards are very honest about it."

She stood just inside the door. She didn't sit in the chair they'd left for her.

"You came for your father," he said. It wasn't a question.

She hadn't decided that until he said it. "Yes."

"Of course you did." He looked at the ceiling for a while, as if the words were written up there. "Do you know what jinn are, Valeria?"

"Street slang."

"Street slang," he agreed. "For people like me. People with pieces of dead saints in them. It started as a program, you know. Not slang. A real program, with a budget and a name and a lot of very serious people in very nice offices." He turned his hands over and looked at his palms. "They called it the Graft Program. They wanted pilots who didn't need exos: children whose seals were strong enough to stand in front of a Nephil on their own. They took children, mostly orphans from the Falls, nobody anyone would miss, and they sewed relics into them. Fragments of saint. Mine went in here." He touched the scar, forehead to chin. "Some of us got strong. Some of us got fast. One girl could see through walls for about a week, and then she couldn't see anything. I got this." He opened his hand, and the air above his palm went faintly glassy, a pane no bigger than a coin with frost lines in it, and the dust above it stopped falling and drifted slowly back up toward the ceiling. "Reversal. Everything that touches me goes back the way it came. Even time, a little. Even me."

He closed his hand. The dust fell.

"That was a seal," Val said. She had seen enough of them over the harbor, pale panes of light ringing when something hit them.

"It's mine," he said. "Turned inside out. That's what the relic does. It takes the thing that keeps you in and points it the other way." He smiled at her face. "You think only the machines have one?"

She didn't answer. He didn't explain.

"The program was shut down," he said, "on orders from people above Umbral, by a pilot they sent personally to do it. You can guess which pilot. There were twenty-six of us in that facility. I was fourteen. Most of us were younger. He didn't leave anyone alive to testify." The polite smile again. "Except me. I don't think he meant to leave me. I reversed the shot." He folded his hands again. "The program died in that facility. The relics didn't. They still turn up in the harbor district, sewn in by people with no license and very dirty hands, and the street needed a word for what walks out afterward. So. Jinn."

Val said nothing.

"Your father had beautiful manners," Palindrome said. "He apologized before he fired."

The dampeners hummed. Somewhere far above them, a door opened and closed.

"Why did you evacuate the plant?" Val asked.

He looked at her with real surprise, the first real expression she had seen on him.

"The workers never did anything to me," he said. "Your father never once evacuated anything. He fought in whatever district the fight was in, with whoever was standing in it. Fewer people know that than you'd think." He was quiet for a moment. "If I'd killed them, I'd be him." He tilted his head. "That's the question you came down here with? Not why I hated him?"

"I know why you hated him."

"Yes," he said. "I suppose you do now." He watched her for a while, the way she watched everyone. "I waited years to see whether you'd be like him. I sat in a cell a lot like this one and thought about nothing else. And you aren't. I was disappointed. Then I was relieved." He leaned forward, and the scar caught the light. "But I'll tell you something, Valeria, because I don't think anyone else will. I didn't see your father at that plant. Then your machine moved."

She stood very still.

"Eleven seconds," Palindrome said. "I counted. I've been counting that man's fights since I was old enough to count. That was him."

She turned to leave. She had her hand on the door when he spoke again, almost gently, as if he were telling her where she'd dropped something.

"Umbral isn't the top of the ladder. It answers to the government, and the government answers to someone too. Ask yourself who your commander takes his calls from. They call themselves the Conclave. They keep a book older than Umbral, and they read it like a schedule."

She didn't turn around. The guard opened the door, and she walked out, and the dampeners stopped humming in her teeth halfway up the stairs.

That night she lay awake on her bed and stared at the ceiling, and for the first time she let herself think it all the way through. Her father had been a murderer, and maybe something worse than the public had ever known: a man who'd fought in districts without clearing them, who'd shot children on orders and called it a mission. And whatever he had been, part of him was still inside the thing she climbed into every day. The thing that had moved for eleven seconds without her. The thing that had rolled over the long gun and taken a storm of ice on its back. She didn't know what that part of him was. She didn't know if it was the part that had shot those children, or some other part. She didn't know if there was another part.

Three days later, Palindrome died in his cell.

The official report said heart failure. The two guards on duty that night told their shift supervisor they had heard a single gunshot through the dampeners, and they were transferred to the belt the following week. In a room built so that nothing in it could go back the way it came, he hadn't been able to reverse that one. Val heard about it six days later, from a single line in a security digest she wasn't supposed to be reading, and she sat with the page in her hands for a long time, until the light outside changed.

### Chapter 18: The Discovery

The Fall came down in the schoolyard.

There was no warning worth the name. The sirens started in the middle of third period, and by the time Nanami had the class lined up at the door, something was already standing on the running track outside the window: a Nephil, narrow and tall, frost-white, with a mask for a face, looking at the building the way a child looks into a fish tank. Where its feet touched the track, the track turned to glass. Clear, smooth, all at once, with the white lane lines still showing underneath like something frozen in a pond.

It took a step toward the school. The glass went with it.

Val was already running. Headquarters was two streets away. She ran the whole way with her bag still on her shoulder, and she was in the capsule before the techs had finished the launch call, and 04 came out of the hangar doors at a run and didn't slow down.

The Nephil was at the gym wall when she reached it. Glass had climbed the wall to the second floor. Behind the windows, in the basement shelter, she knew exactly who was sitting on the floor: Nanami with her clipboard, Akari with her arms folded, Haruto filming, and Yuki, one arm still in its sling, standing at the little high window, watching.

She didn't decide anything.

04 moved before she thought it. It moved before she could have thought it. She felt its hand close on the Nephil's arm, and felt its seal slam against the Nephil's seal so hard they rang together like two bells, and felt the Nephil's seal crack, and felt her own hands go into the cold light at the center of it, and none of it was a choice. It was like falling. It was over in nine seconds. The Nephil came apart on the running track in a shower of frost, and the glass it had made stayed where it was, a long clear road from the fence to the gym wall, and 04 stood in the middle of it, breathing, if exos breathed.

In the ops room, Eve Sakaki had stopped writing.

"Ninety-eight," she said. "A hundred and four. A hundred and eleven." She looked up at the commander's chair. "Past a hundred, the seal can't tell where she ends. Pilots who go past it don't come out the same."

Patrick didn't answer. Beside him, Ruth Hermon took off her reading glasses and let them fall on their chain, and said nothing either, in a different way.

Seus came out of the launch bay in Navis forty seconds after it was over, and stood in the schoolyard looking at the glass road and the girl in the exo at the end of it, and didn't say anything on the channel at all.

That night he pulled the sync record. *A hundred and eleven.* No pilot on record had ever held that number for nine seconds and come out talking. He went looking for why.

* * *

Seus found it by accident, which made it worse.

He hadn't been looking for anything about Samuel Kane. He had been looking for proof. For three weeks he had been staying late in the records room, digging through 04's maintenance logs in the hours after training, trying to prove that Val Kane's "luck" was really an unfair technical advantage. The glass in the schoolyard had given him a number to chase. Some secret calibration. Some forbidden firmware. Anything that would let him say *it isn't her, it's the machine,* and mean it.

What he found instead was a timestamp.

04's first recorded nature activity, the moment the core had first spoken, was the childlike voice on Val's first test run asking for a name. He had seen that in the official record; everybody had. What he hadn't seen before was the raw log underneath it, a layer down, in a directory that wasn't supposed to be visible to a pilot's credentials and had been left visible anyway by somebody's mistake. In the raw log, the nature had been active before Val's first session. Hours before. It had been sitting in the core, fully formed, already attuned to her, before she had ever said a word to it.

Somebody had buried this information. The official record had been edited, cleanly and carefully, to move that first timestamp forward by six hours. He could see the edit, and the authorization code that had made it, though he couldn't see whose code it was.

Seus sat back in his chair in the dark records room and felt cold all the way down.

That was the oldest taboo in the exo program. Every trainee learned it the first week. An exo must never influence its pilot. A core is supposed to begin blank and become whatever its pilot makes of it, never the other way around, because the other way around was how people stopped being people. A nature that was already there before the pilot's first session meant only one thing: someone had tampered with the core. Someone had put a mind in there for her on purpose. If anyone found out, 04 would be confiscated, and Val with it. And Seus had been in Umbral long enough to know that "removal" didn't always mean retirement.

The only person who could have put it there was Samuel Kane.

But Samuel Kane had been dead for weeks when that log was edited. Whoever had buried it was still alive.

He sat with the logs for a long time. If Val's father had broken the most sacred rule in the program to do this, then it had to mean something. People didn't risk that for nothing. He couldn't tell her, not until he knew what it meant, and who else knew, and whose code it was that had buried it.

He told Alex, the only person at headquarters he trusted. He did it on the roof, after ramen, after Aoi had gone back down to the night shift, standing at the rail with his hands jammed in his pockets, saying it all very fast so he couldn't change his mind.

Alex read the logs on Seus's tablet twice. By the second time, he had gone pale.

"Who else has seen this?" he said.

"Nobody."

"Keep it that way." Alex handed the tablet back. His hand wasn't quite steady. "Say nothing. To anyone."

"Not even Val?"

Alex looked out at the city for a long moment. "Especially not Val," he said. "Not yet. Not until we know who buried it."

It was the first secret Alex Enoch ever kept from Valeria Kane. It would not be the last. He didn't know that yet. He thought, standing there, that it was the right thing, the careful thing, and that there would be time later to tell her properly.

### Chapter 19: The Dinner Plan

It was Seus's idea. He did not want to admit it, though.

He wanted his father to sit at a table with him. Just once. Not a briefing, not a corridor, not a hangar with a hundred people pretending not to listen, but a dinner. A table, with food on it and plates and chairs, and the possibility, however small, of conversation. He had never once, in sixteen years, eaten a meal across a table from his father. He had counted.

He told no one. He didn't ask for help.

Val helped anyway. She had heard him through the wall, the night after the harbor, saying something to himself in the dark, the same sentence over and over in slightly different ways, practicing. She hadn't caught all of it. She had caught enough.

She asked Alex which nights the commander left headquarters before nine. She asked Aoi, which was harder, because it meant walking up to the comms desk and saying words to a person she had spent a year not looking at. Aoi listened to the whole thing without interrupting, and then said, "Oh, Val," in a voice that made Val want to leave, and then took her down to Alex's kitchen three nights in a row to teach her to make three dishes. Curry, because it was hard to ruin. Miso soup, because everyone could make miso soup. Tamagoyaki, because Val asked.

They were all bad. Val's tamagoyaki tore every single time, no matter how slowly she rolled it.

"That's fine," Aoi said, leaning on the counter. "Torn ones taste the same. My mother's always tore. Alex's always burn. Yours tears. That's how people will know it's yours."

On the third night, Val went to a department store on her own and bought a tablecloth with her own money. It was plain and white and cost more than it should have. It was the first thing anyone who knew her had ever seen her buy for someone else, and Haruto, who saw her carrying it out of the store, talked about it for a week.

Seus found out, of course. He found out because Val was terrible at keeping things from people she lived with, and because he came home to the smell of torn tamagoyaki and Aoi laughing in the kitchen.

He stood in the kitchen doorway with flour on his face from something he'd been trying in secret himself, and stared at the two of them.

"Why are you helping?" he demanded.

Val didn't look up from the pan. "It's the normal thing to do."

He opened his mouth. He closed it. Aoi put a hand over her own mouth and turned to the window very suddenly.

The hardest part was the asking. Seus couldn't do it. He had tried twice, in the corridor, and both times his father had walked past him reading a folder, and both times he had lost his nerve somewhere between the third step and the fourth. So Val did it.

She did it in the gray car, at the long red light by the ferry terminal, with two cans of coffee in the cup holders.

"Seus wants you to come to dinner," she said. "Thursday. At the apartment."

Patrick didn't say anything for the whole length of the red light. His hands were on the wheel. His cold coffee sat untouched. Outside, the ferry horn blew, long and low across the harbor.

"I'll come," he said.

When she told Seus, that evening, he looked at her as if she had told him the sea was dry. Then he said, "Fine," very casually, and went into his room and shut the door. Through the wall, she heard him not making any noise at all for a long time. The next day, he walked through the halls of Hakobune Municipal No. 3 as if the floor were made of something soft, and Akari asked him four times what was wrong with his face.

It was Seus who said to ask Yuki, not looking at Val when he said it. "Four's better," he said. "If there's four people at a table, he can't just get up and leave."

So Val asked her, at the shoe lockers, the next morning.

"Do you eat dinner?"

Yuki considered it, the way she considered everything, as if it might be a trick question. "Yes."

"Thursday. At our apartment."

Yuki looked at her for a moment with that face Val could never place.

"I'll come," she said.

The night before the dinner, Seus decided he couldn't keep the secret any longer.

She deserved to know what her father had done to 04. He had been carrying it for two weeks, and every time she came home from the hangar he looked at her and felt like a liar. He found her on the apartment balcony after midnight, sitting with her knees up, looking at the harbor lights.

He tried to explain. It came out wrong.

He didn't know how to say it carefully; he had never said anything carefully in his life. So it came out the way he said everything, fast and hard and sharp at the edges. It came out sounding like an accusation. *Your father tampered with your core. Before you ever touched it. There was a mind in there waiting for you. Your exo's a fraud. Your whole record is a lie. It was never luck, Kane, it was him, it was always him.*

Val didn't hear the part where he was trying to protect her. She didn't hear the part where he was scared for her. She heard the part where he was right.

She stood up. She said something to him, very quietly, that he would remember for years: "You'd love that, wouldn't you. If it was never me."

He said something back that was worse.

They had the worst fight of their lives on that balcony, over the harbor, in voices low enough not to wake the building, which somehow made it more vicious. Doors slammed. Alex came home at one in the morning to two silent bedrooms, two cold cups of tea on the balcony rail, and a white tablecloth folded neatly on the kitchen counter, waiting for tomorrow.

The next morning, there was a mission.

### Chapter 20: Proxy

The alarm came during second period. Val was at her desk by the window with her pencil in her hand, not listening to a lecture about land reform, when the classroom door slid open and Alex leaned in, and his face wasn't doing the grin he used on teachers.

She dropped the pencil into her desk and stood up. She didn't take it with her. She would think about that later: that she had dropped it there without a thought, because she would be back that afternoon.

The Fall came down over the industrial port, and something was riding inside it.

Val felt it before she saw it. The Nephil came out of the low clouds over the cranes like any of the others, frost-shrouded and wrong-jointed, with a mask for a face. But there was something in it that didn't belong to it, a presence threaded through its body like rot through wood. When it turned its mask toward her, Val felt something look at her out of it that was older than the Nephil and much, much angrier.

Seus was on her left in Navis, sea-blue against the gray port. They hadn't spoken since the balcony. On the private channel there was only static.

The Nephil caught 04 by the shoulders. Its grip was cold, and then it was worse than cold, because the presence came across. Black lines spread over 04's chassis from the Nephil's hands, frost-veined and branching, crawling across the armor like cracks in ice. The oldest reports from the first years of the Falls had a word for this. Corruption. Exos were living tissue, and living tissue could be infected.

Inside the capsule, a voice spoke to Val from the walls, close and calm and full of something worse than hate. The voice sounded old.

"Hello, Sam's girl," it said. "This was mine once."

04 seized. Val lost her arms first. They didn't hurt; they simply stopped belonging to her, the way her arm had in the river district, except it didn't stop there. Her legs went. Her sight went gray at the edges. The black lines crawled across the walls of the capsule toward the place where the core was, and she understood, distantly, that when they reached it, she would go soft, and 04 would belong to whatever was speaking.

Navis moved before Seus did.

Living things have instincts. Navis's instinct, its own, the saint's, the thing in it that was not Seus at all, was to tear the sickness off its teammate. Its hands closed on 04's shoulders, right over the black lines, and pulled. The corruption came with it. All of it. It poured out of 04 and into Navis like water finding a lower place.

Val got her arms back. She got her eyes back. She saw Navis stagger back three steps with the black lines racing across its blue armor.

Seus understood what had happened half a second later, when Navis stopped answering him.

He went for the eject. It didn't fire. The corruption had grown through the capsule's joints like roots, and the spine capsule was fused shut. On the ops-room screens, mission control's link to Navis had gone dark.

His exo went rogue. The corruption drove it through the port, through the stacked containers and the crane gantries, through a row of warehouses and into a residential block that hadn't finished evacuating. People died. Val watched them die, small dark shapes in the street, and couldn't do anything, because she was still on one knee in the water getting her legs back. Seus was screaming. He was screaming at the controls, at Navis, at the voice that now lived in his walls, at himself.

Mission control, no, Patrick, declared him lost. He did it from the command chair in an even voice, and then he ordered Val to engage.

Alex, at the mission desk, turned around in his chair and screamed at him. Not words. Just his name, and then a sound with no words in it at all.

Patrick didn't raise his voice. He looked at Alex across the ops room, steadily, with something in his face that might have been sympathy and might have been nothing.

"Four hundred and twelve people in that block, Captain. Would you sacrifice the city for one life?" he asked.

Alex stood there with his one hand clenched on the back of his chair. The room was silent except for the screaming coming over the speakers from the port. At the comms desk, Aoi had both hands flat on her console and her eyes closed.

Alex stood down.

Then Seus's voice came over the comm, on the private channel, and it was impossibly calm. The screaming had stopped. He sounded like someone talking across a kitchen table.

"It's okay, Val, it's okay. Don't worry about me. There's something in 04 more important than me. I read the logs. Don't let them take it." A breath, crackling. "And... thanks for the dinner. I wish it had happened."

He had accepted it. She could hear it in his voice: he had already decided, somewhere in the last thirty seconds, and he was at peace with it, and he was only waiting for her to do it.

Inside, alone, in the capsule of an exo that wasn't his anymore, Seus asked, very quietly, "Was that good?"

Navis never answered.

Mission control ordered Val to engage a second time.

She didn't. She stood 04 up in the middle of the port, in the shallow water, and let its hands hang at its sides. She would not fight an exo with Seus inside it. She would not. She stood there with her hands at her sides, and she didn't choose, and the rogue exo tore through another building while she didn't choose, and the seconds went by.

"Lieutenant," Patrick said. "Proxy authorization."

At the comms desk, Aoi didn't move.

"Lieutenant Sena."

Umbral's engineers had built Proxy to take two keys: the commander's, and a second officer's, so that no one person could ever do it alone. Today the second officer on duty was Aoi.

She opened her eyes. She looked across the room at Alex, who didn't look back at her. Then she typed in the code, one key at a time, and pressed enter, and took her hands off the console as if it had burned her.

So Patrick engaged Proxy.

He did it from the command chair, across the city, without raising his voice. Proxy let mission control drive a unit directly, through whatever was left of the nature in its core, without the pilot's consent, and what was left in 04 was nothing like Val. It was rage. It came up out of the core like something that had been waiting at the bottom of a well, and the exo came alive around her with a fury she had never felt from it. She felt every movement. She felt 04's legs drive it out of the water, felt its hands come up, felt it cross the port toward Navis in three long strides, and she had no power to stop any of it. She pulled at the controls and nothing happened. She screamed at it and nothing happened.

04 tore Navis apart with Val trapped inside, screaming.

She hadn't screamed at nine, when the link had felt like being poured into a mold too small for her. She hadn't screamed at the plant, suffocating inside her own skull. She screamed now, the whole time, until there was nothing left of her voice, while her hands, 04's hands, did what Patrick told them to.

On the ops-room wall, her sync climbed. Ninety. A hundred. A hundred and ten.

"Pull her out," Eve said. "Commander, pull her out, she's past—"

Patrick didn't move.

Val felt it go. Not the link: her. The rage was all around her and the hands were not hers and the screaming was not doing anything, and somewhere under all of it, the edge of her, the thing that had always kept her in, simply opened, the way a hand opens when it can't hold on anymore. She didn't feel it break. It was worse than breaking. It just wasn't there.

In mission control, Alex watched Seus's vitals collapse. Then, a second before the cockpit telemetry cut out, they flickered. Once. Just once. A single spike on the line that should have been flat.

Nobody else was looking. Nobody except Aoi, who was looking at Alex, and saw his face change, and didn't say anything at all.

Afterward, the port was quiet. Navis lay in pieces in the water, sea-blue plating scattered across the gray, steaming. 04 stood over it with its hands hanging down.

The recovery team got the capsule open forty minutes later.

There was nobody in it. There was a flight suit in the seat, still buckled in, and it was full of something that wasn't a body anymore: a soft pale light, like frost with a lamp inside it, filling the suit to the collar and the cuffs and moving, very slowly, the way breath moves. The tech who opened the hatch sat down on the gantry and didn't get up for a while.

"She's gone soft," somebody said in the ops room.

"No." Eve was already on her feet, already pulling the capsule feed onto the main wall. She looked at it for a long time. "Her seal isn't broken. It's open." She touched the screen with two fingers, the way you touch something to see if it's hot. "Going soft doesn't come back. It never has. This should be gone already." She was quiet for a moment. "04 is holding her together. It's never done that for anyone."

They tried for three days.

Eve ran the salvage herself. She didn't go home. She ran it from the gantry beside 04's spine with cables trailing across the hangar floor, with every procedure Helen Enoch had ever written and two she made up, and each time the light in the suit flickered and settled and stayed exactly as it was. On the second night she sat down on the floor of the gantry with a can of the commander's sweet coffee in her hands, the kind he left on the lab counter and never came back for, and didn't drink it.

Yuki sat beside the capsule the whole time.

Nobody told her to. She came down to the hangar the afternoon of the port, with her arm still in its sling, and sat on the floor with her back against 04's ankle, and didn't leave. She didn't eat much. Aoi brought her a lunch box each morning, the same one she always packed for her, and Yuki ate exactly half of it and put the lid back on. Ruth came down at night with two cups of tea and sat on an upturned crate and didn't say anything, and Yuki didn't either, and after an hour Ruth took both cups back up. Alex came and stood at the bottom of the gantry ladder with his one hand on the rail and looked up at the hatch for a long time and went away again.

On the second night, very late, Patrick came to the hangar door. He stood there in his coat. He didn't come in. Yuki looked at him across the dark hangar, and he looked at her, or at her face, and then he turned around and went back up.

* * *

Inside, there was no inside.

There was no capsule and no port and no Val, exactly. There was warm, and soft, and no edges anywhere, and in the warm, voices. All of them. Everyone she knew. Alex and Aoi and Patrick and Nanami and Akari and Haruto and Seus and Yuki and Ruth and Eve and Fumi, and under them, faint, one voice she didn't know at all. They came from everywhere. They didn't wait.

*Do you want to live?*

"No."

*Why do you pilot?*

"It's the normal thing to do."

*Who are you?*

"Nobody."

*What do you want?*

"Nothing."

*Is anyone waiting for you?*

"No."

*Do you love anyone?*

"No."

*Does anyone love you?*

"No."

*Should you have been born?*

There was a long silence, there in the warm, longer than any of the others.

"...No."

*Will you say it back?*

She didn't answer that one at all.

And they started again. From the top, all of them, faster, the same questions in the same order, as if she had gotten them wrong and had to do them over. *Do you want to live? Why do you pilot? Who are you?* And again. And again. She answered and answered, the same answers, until the answers weren't words anymore, and she began to understand that this was what the warm was for: you answered until there was nobody left to answer, and then you didn't have to anymore.

It would have been easy. It was the easiest thing she had ever been offered.

But somewhere in the third time through, or the thirtieth, she noticed whose voices they were. Patrick's, from the command chair, deciding. Aoi's, typing a code one key at a time. Seus's, on the private channel, at peace, already decided, waiting for her to do it. All of them, every one, asking her what she wanted, now, after they had already decided everything for her.

Something in her that wasn't a word yet got very hot.

It came up out of the warm the way the rage had come up out of 04, except it was hers. It was the only thing in there that was. It had edges. It had a shape. It had a mouth.

"Who the fuck decides what's important for me?"

She screamed it. She screamed it at all of them, at the warm, at the questions, and the warm didn't have an answer, and the scream did what no answer could have: it was hers, and you can't scream if you're nobody.

On the third morning, the light in the flight suit went out.

The hatch was open. Eve was asleep against the gantry rail. Yuki was the first one up the ladder, sling and all, and she looked into the capsule and saw Val curled on the capsule floor in what was left of her flight suit, knees to her chest, hair wet, shaking, breathing.

Nobody had heard the scream. Her comms had been off for three days.

Val looked up at the face in the hatch, the face she could never place, and then she cried. Not one tear, the way her father had. All of them. She cried the way she hadn't since before she could remember, with her whole body, on the floor of the capsule, and Yuki knelt on the edge of the hatch and didn't touch her, and didn't leave, and didn't say anything, until she was done.

Umbral had cleaned up the site while she was gone. Somehow, under all of it, Navis's core was still intact. The chassis was totaled beyond salvage, every plate and bone of it, but the core had come through untouched, the one thing in the whole wreck that hadn't been broken. The recovery team logged it and took it away to storage without a word to anyone.

The recovery team's report listed the pilot's remains as unrecoverable. The report was filed by the records office. The officer who signed it was Lieutenant A. Sena.

That evening, somewhere in an apartment across the city, a girl fed a goldfish, and it felt wrong.

### Chapter 21: Show Something

Yuki walked her home. Nobody asked her to. She walked a step behind, the way she walked everywhere, all the way to the apartment door, and when Val opened it, Yuki came in after her as if she had been expected.

She had been. It was Sunday. The dinner had been Thursday.

The table was still set. Four plates, four glasses, chopsticks laid neatly on their rests. The white tablecloth she had bought with her own money. Three dishes in the fridge, covered in plastic wrap, three days old. The torn tamagoyaki on a plate by itself, gone cold and a little dry at the edges.

She didn't turn on the lights. She stood in the doorway and looked at it.

Yuki went past her to the table. She looked at the four chairs for a while, the way she looked at a problem in class, and then she sat down in the one at the head of the table, the one Val had set for the commander, because nobody else was going to sit in it. She picked up her chopsticks with her good hand and took a piece of the torn tamagoyaki, cold, and ate it.

"It tastes the same," Yuki said.

Val didn't know the same as what. She didn't ask. She stood in the doorway in the dark and watched the only guest who had come eat a three-day-old omelette at a table set for people who weren't coming, slowly, all of it, as if it were important.

Then she turned around and went to headquarters, because she couldn't think of anywhere else to go, and because she had decided, on the walk home, that she was going to see a grieving father, and she needed to see it with her own eyes.

She found Patrick at his desk, exactly as he always was. Lamp on. Reports stacked at his left hand. Pen moving. When she came in, he looked up and his face did what it always did for her: softened, slightly, the way a cold room softens when someone opens a door.

"Valeria," he said. "You should be resting."

"He is your son, damn it!"

The pen stopped.

"Show something!" Her voice cracked on it. It was already wrecked from the capsule, from three days of screaming nobody heard; it came out as half a word. "You... you can't do that..."

Patrick looked at her across the desk and said nothing. Not a word. His face didn't change. His hands rested on the reports.

She stood there in front of him, shaking. *Was my father like this to me?* she thought. *Was Samuel like you?* She didn't say it out loud. She didn't need to. It was on her face, and he could read her face; he had been reading it at red lights for seven years.

She stormed out. She didn't close the door behind her.

Patrick sat alone in his office until dawn and didn't move. The lamp stayed on. The pen stayed where it had stopped.

That night, Alex didn't come home at all. He came back the next afternoon, with dark circles under his eyes and his uniform rumpled as if he had slept in a chair, and said he'd been at the wreck site. Aoi wasn't with him. Aoi didn't come to Sunday dinner that week, or the week after, and when Val heard her voice on comms it sounded like someone who hadn't slept either.

Val didn't ask.

In class, she never took the pencil out of her desk again. She could feel it there, under the lid, all day, every day, like something left burning on a stove in an empty house. She didn't open the desk. She borrowed pencils from Nanami, who gave them to her without a word and never once asked for them back.

The next quiet afternoon, the gray car was waiting at the school gate as if nothing had happened. Val came out through the school doors and saw it there, engine running, two cans of coffee in the cup holders.

She walked past it.

It was the first time in seven years she hadn't gotten in. She walked to the end of the street without looking back, and turned the corner, and kept walking.

* * *

Someone was sitting on the wall outside the shuttered bakery at the next corner, swinging her boots.

It was a girl in an old flight suit, orange at the shoulders where the paint hadn't worn off, with a duffel bag at her feet. She looked about seventeen. She had freckles and a chipped front tooth and eyes that didn't match the rest of her face; they looked much older, the way the eyes in very old photographs look older than the people in them.

She hopped down when she saw Val, and grinned, and then the grin went somewhere.

"Hey," she said. "You're the Kane kid. Where's the Moriah kid?"

Val didn't answer.

She didn't mean it as an answer. She just didn't have one. She stood on the pavement with her bag on her shoulder and said nothing, and the nothing went on.

The girl understood it. Val watched her understand it. Her face didn't fall; it went still all at once, the way a radio goes still when the power cuts, and for a long moment she didn't say anything either, and the street was very quiet around both of them.

"Late," the girl said finally.

It didn't sound like a joke. It sounded like an apology for something much bigger than being late.

"Who are you?"

"Maggie." She stepped closer, and before Val could move, she bent and put her ear against Val's collarbone, lightly, the way you'd listen at a door to find out if anyone was home. Val went rigid. Maggie listened for a long moment.

"...Still in there," she said, and straightened up.

"What is?"

Maggie didn't answer that. She looked at Val's face instead, and then at her hair, short above the neck, and something in her own face went soft and old.

"You've got your mom's hair," she said.

Val touched it without meaning to. She didn't know why she kept it short. She had always kept it short.

"You knew my mother."

"I knew a lot of people." Maggie picked up her duffel. "I've been out in the belt since 2082. Standing order. Stay out there, keep your head down, don't come home. There was an older one under it." She said it the way you'd say something you'd memorized a long time ago. "*When they put the Moriah boy next to the Kane kid, they're starting. Go.*" She slung the bag over her shoulder. "So I went. I burned the whole way. Lizzie's parked somewhere she shouldn't be, and she's sulking."

"Whose orders?"

"A friend's," said Maggie.

They walked. Val didn't ask her to come, and Maggie didn't ask if she could. She just fell in a step behind, humming something very old under her breath, off-key, all the way home.

Behind them, three streets back, the gray car stayed where it was, at the gate, with its engine running, until long after dark.

---

## Arc 6: Ask Me Later

> *And when the seven thunders had uttered their voices, I was about to write: and I heard a voice from heaven saying unto me, Seal up those things which the seven thunders uttered, and write them not.*
>
> — Revelation 10:4

### Chapter 22: What Seus Knew

The funeral was small, because there was nothing to bury. Umbral held it in a gray room on the fourth floor, with a photograph of Seus in his flight suit on an easel and a row of folding chairs that nobody sat in but one. Commander Moriah stood at the front and read a statement about duty in the same voice he used for briefings, with Deputy Commander Hermon a step behind him and to the left, her hands folded, looking at the photograph instead of at him. In the front row, in the one chair anybody had taken, Yuki sat with her back very straight and a fresh bandage on her wrist. She had asked Dr. Sakaki what you do at a funeral. Dr. Sakaki had said *sit.* So she sat, the whole time, and when it was over she stayed sitting, looking at the photograph, until a technician came to fold up the chairs around her. Patrick didn't look at the front row once. Nobody from Class 2-A was allowed in, because Seus's death was classified. They stood outside the gates of headquarters in their school uniforms instead, the three of them, holding a paper bag, and when Val came out, Akari held the bag out to her.

It was the goldfish. In a plastic bag full of water, with a twist tie at the top.

"I can't keep him," Akari said. She said it to the pavement. "I keep looking at him and he's got that stupid frown and I can't. You take him. You lived with him."

Val took the bag. The goldfish looked at her through the plastic.

"Was it quick?" Akari asked. Still to the pavement.

Val thought about the port. About her own voice screaming until there was no voice left. About her hands, 04's hands, doing what someone else told them.

"Yes," she said.

It was the first lie she had ever told any of them. Nanami looked at her for a long moment, as if she knew, and didn't say anything. Haruto had his camera in his hand and didn't raise it.

Maggie was outside the gates too. She hadn't been allowed in either. She stood a little apart from the three of them in her old flight jacket, with her hands in her pockets, and when Val came past her she held out a hand without a word. Val looked at it. Then she gave her the folded card they had handed out at the door, the one with Seus's photograph on the front.

Maggie looked at it for a long time. Seus in his flight suit, not smiling, with his jaw set, the way he had stood in the hangar on the first day.

"You've got your dad's frown," she told the photograph. "Don't let it set. It'll stick."

She gave the card back.

Alex told her a week later. He did it at the kitchen table, the same table, because he didn't know where else to do it. She had folded the tablecloth away into a drawer the morning after, and the table was bare wood again, and the goldfish sat in a glass bowl in the middle of it, where a vase might have been in a different kind of apartment.

He told her what Seus had found in the logs. That 04's nature had been there before her first session, before she ever touched it. That someone had edited the record to hide it. That her father had tampered with the core in secret, breaking the oldest rule in the program, and that if anyone found out, they would take 04 away, and maybe her too. That was all Seus had known. That was what he'd been trying to tell her, the night on the balcony.

Then, because he'd decided she deserved all of it, Alex told her who had run Proxy.

She had heard Patrick give the order over the channel, at the port. She had thought that was all he'd done. She had thought someone else had done the rest: an operator at a console somewhere in that room, following orders, with their hands on the keys. Nobody had. Patrick had engaged Proxy himself, from the command chair, and driven 04 with his own hands.

Val went very still. She didn't cry, and she didn't storm out. She looked at the goldfish for a long time.

"Did he know it would kill him?" she asked finally. "Patrick. When he pushed it."

"I don't know," Alex said. That was the truth.

He opened his mouth to say something else. She saw him do it: saw the breath go in, saw him lean forward a little in his chair, as if there was one more thing on the table between them, bigger than the rest. Then he looked at her face, at whatever was on it, and he closed his mouth again, and leaned back, and said, "You should eat something," instead.

She didn't ask what the other thing was.

At the debrief the next day, in a conference room with bad coffee and worse lighting, a junior officer from the analysis section, meaning well, called the incident a tragic stroke of bad luck. "Nobody could have predicted a corruption vector in a standard Fall," he said. "It's just bad luck. Terrible luck."

Val looked at him until he stopped talking.

At the end of the table, Dr. Sakaki didn't look up from her tablet. "It wasn't luck," she said. "It was a corruption vector nobody modeled. My section didn't model it." She went on scrolling. Nobody said anything after that, either.

Val didn't say anything. She just couldn't make the word come out of her own mouth anymore. When it was her turn to give her statement, she tried, out of habit, the way she always had: *Pilot attributes outcome to.* And there was nothing at the end of the sentence. The word was gone. Luck had held her life together since she was nine, the way tape holds a broken cup, and it came apart in a conference room on the fifth floor, over a cold cup of coffee, while a junior officer studied his hands.

"Pilot has no comment," the stenographer typed, after a while.

* * *

Nanami gave her the printouts on Monday.

"Yuki-san's been out all week," Nanami said. "Maintenance, she said. Whatever that means. You live closest." She held out a folder with a laminated label on it, HOMEWORK — YUKI, in her best handwriting, as if it would have been rude to give it any other way.

Nobody knew how to say no to Nanami.

Yuki lived two streets from headquarters, in a concrete block where half the windows were dark, on the fourth floor, at the end of the hall. The buzzer was broken. Val knocked, and waited, and after a while the door opened.

Yuki looked at her without surprise. She was in her school uniform, though it was evening. "Kane-san," she said.

"Homework," said Val, and held out the folder.

Yuki took it. Then she stepped back from the door, and Val understood, after a second, that she was being let in.

The apartment was one room. A bed with a gray blanket pulled perfectly flat. A chair. A refrigerator, humming, with nothing in it but bottled water and a row of white pill packets labeled in Dr. Sakaki's handwriting. No pictures. No plants. No rug. A plastic bag of bandages on the windowsill, and beside it, folded on a clean square of cloth, a pair of reading glasses with one lens cracked across, set down carefully, the way you'd set something down in a shrine.

Val knew the frames. She had seen them on the corner of a desk every night for two months, when she was nine.

"Those are the commander's," she said.

"He was sitting with me," Yuki said. "In the medical wing. He took them off and put his hand over his eyes, and they fell." She looked at them. "He didn't ask for them back."

Val looked around the room for a long time. She had lived in a room like this. The first week after Patrick's apartment, before Alex, before the bento, she had eaten convenience-store omurice on a floor like this one, in the dark, and listened to the city, and there had been nothing on the walls and nothing in the refrigerator, and nobody had come.

"You should eat something," she heard herself say.

It was his line. She heard whose voice it was the moment it was out of her mouth, and she left before Yuki could answer.

### Chapter 23: Why Won't You Tell Me

The night of the funeral, she hadn't been able to sleep, so she had gone down to the hangar at three in the morning. Someone was already there.

The hangar at night was a cathedral. The work lights were off and the only light came from the status panels along the gantries, small and green and blinking, and from the high windows, where the city's glow came in sideways and lay across the floor in long pale stripes. The exos stood in their bays in the dark like saints in alcoves. Lizzie stood in a borrowed bay at the far end, her orange gone gray in the dark. Ross's bay was still empty. Navis's bay was empty now too.

Patrick stood in front of Unit 04, alone.

His tie was pulled loose. His coat was on the floor behind him, where he'd dropped it. He was holding a can of coffee from the vending machine by the door, and his hands were shaking so badly she could hear the liquid slosh from where she stood in the shadows by the entrance.

"You said hold," he said. His voice was hoarse and very quiet. "You gave me a folder in your own handwriting, every step, and not one page of it said why. And you said hold. You said get her in, whatever it costs. I got her in. You said don't ask. I never asked. Not once in seven years did I ask you what it was for. I didn't ask when they took the arm off Enoch. I didn't ask when they wanted her out and I had to lie to their faces to keep her in. I didn't ask."

The can shook. Coffee spilled over his knuckles. He didn't seem to notice.

"I gave up the brushes," he said. "Did you know that? I was going to paint. I was twenty-five and I was going to go to the mountains and paint, and you came to my apartment with that look on your face and you said, Patrick, I need you, and I put the brushes in a closet and I never took them out again. I gave up my name. Do you know what they call me in the corridors? They call me Kane's dog. They've called me that for twenty years. I gave up..."

He stopped. He looked down at the can in his hands as if he didn't know how it had got there.

"I gave you my son today," he said. "On your orders. On orders you never even gave me. I gave him to you because I thought, he must have known. Sam must have known it would come to this, and he must have had a reason, and if I just hold, if I just do what he said and hold, it will turn out that there was a reason." He laughed, once, and it was a terrible sound. "Twenty-two years, Sam. If I stop now, it was for nothing. All of it. Theseus, for nothing."

His voice broke.

"Why won't you tell me everything?!"

The shout went up into the dark of the hangar ceiling and came back down as an echo, smaller, and then smaller, and then nothing.

04 didn't answer. It stood in its bay, gray and silent, the way it always had.

Silence in heaven.

Val watched from the shadows by the door. What she felt wasn't pity. It was disgust, sharp and immediate, at the pathetic, broken thing he was, this man who had driven her hands through his own son. And then, underneath the disgust, slower and worse, she felt something she recognized. A person who had decided that if they just didn't stop, didn't think, didn't feel, didn't ask, it would all turn out to have meant something. She knew that person. She had been that person every morning for seven years.

When Patrick finally walked out, he passed within two meters of her and didn't see her. His eyes were open and fixed on something that wasn't in the room.

Val didn't move for a long time after the door closed behind him.

"He doesn't know," Maggie said.

Her voice came out of the dark at the far end of the hangar. She was sitting on Lizzie's foot with her knees drawn up, and Val didn't know how long she had been there. Long enough.

"Sam never told him," Maggie said. "Not what it was for. Not any of it."

"And you?"

Maggie looked at the door Patrick had gone through. "He told me some of it," she said. "Where to be. When to come. Never why." She got up and dusted off her knees. "Go to bed, kiddo. It's three in the morning."

She hummed something old on her way out, and then the hangar was quiet again.

By morning he was the same man he'd always been. Lamp on, reports stacked at his left hand, pen moving. He had cried himself empty in the dark, and then he had put it away, all of it, in the same closet as the brushes.

### Chapter 24: "I do not."

On a routine patrol over the outer wall, she finally said it.

It was a gray afternoon, cold, with low clouds dragging their bellies across the tops of the shelter towers. 04 walked the wall slowly, the way patrols walked, one long stride at a time, and the city lay quiet on one side and the empty hills on the other. Alex was on the channel, but he wasn't talking. He hadn't been talking much, lately.

"Seus found something in your logs," Val said to 04. She said it out loud, in the capsule, on the internal channel, which only the exo could hear. She had never used the internal channel before. She had not known, until she pressed it, whether she would be able to make herself speak. "Your nature was there before my first session. Before I ever touched you. My father did something to you." She hesitated. "Do you remember anything? Anything from before me?"

The answer came after a long pause, in the same clipped protocol voice 04 had always used.

"I do not."

It was the truth. She could feel that it was the truth, through the link, the way you can feel that a floor is solid before you put your weight on it. Whatever Sam had built into the core was buried too deep for 04 to reach. As far as she could feel, it didn't know why it had been made. It didn't know who had made it. It only knew her.

Val looked out over the city for a while.

"Okay," she said.

Then, because she didn't know what else to do, she stayed on the link a little longer than the patrol needed. The patrol ended. Alex called her back. She acknowledged and didn't move. She sat in the capsule on top of the outer wall with the cold coming through the walls, and she didn't say anything, and neither did 04. The clouds dragged past. A flock of birds went over, low, toward the harbor.

It was the first silence between them in seven years that didn't feel empty.

### Chapter 25: Ask Me Later

Something changed between them after that.

Val started talking to 04. Not much, at first. A comment about the weather on patrol. A complaint about Haruto, who had started following her to the hangar gates every morning to film the exos coming out and kept narrating her walk like she was a rare bird. "He calls you a 'magnificent machine,'" she told 04 once. "He calls me 'the subject.'" A pause. "You're the magnificent one."

04 didn't answer. But there was something in the link afterward, a faint warmth, like the warmth a stone keeps after the sun has gone down.

Once, half asleep after a night mission, she said "Goodnight" out loud before she disconnected, and then lay in bed afterward with her face hot, feeling stupid, staring at the ceiling, waiting for someone to laugh at her. Nobody did. Nobody had heard. The goldfish in its bowl on the kitchen table turned slowly in the dark.

04's strict, professional nature, the mirror she had made of it when she was nine, began letting her new moods in. She could feel it happening. Its voice got a little warmer. Its pauses got a little shorter. When she was angry in a fight, it fought a little harder, and when she was tired on patrol, it walked a little slower, without being told. On missions they moved together better than they ever had, and she couldn't tell anymore where her movements ended and its began.

Around the same time, she started getting into the gray car at the gate again.

She didn't decide to. One afternoon she came out through the school doors, and it was there, engine running, the way it had been there every quiet day for weeks while she walked past it, and her feet took her across the street and she opened the door and got in. Patrick looked at her. She looked at the windshield.

"How was school?" he asked.

"Fine," she said.

He drove the long way, along the harbor. At the konbini by the ferry terminal he went in and came back with two cans of coffee and put the sweet one in her cup holder. Neither of them mentioned the weeks she hadn't gotten in. Neither of them mentioned the port. Neither of them mentioned anything at all, and the silence in the car was the same silence it had always been, and it was not the same at all.

Alex noticed it in the logs. After Seus died, 04 had generated a question internally and never asked it. It had happened the night of the port, a few hours after, while the hangar crews were hosing the seawater out of its joints: a flagged entry in the nature's activity record, marked QUERY, with no content attached. A question, formed and then held. The flag sat in the data for weeks, and Alex looked at it every morning when he ran the overnight diagnostics, and he never told anyone, and he never deleted it either.

Then one evening, on a quiet patrol over the harbor, where the lanterns had floated during the festival, it asked.

"Valeria Kane. Do I have a name?"

Val stopped walking. 04 stopped with her.

The question was seven years old, and she knew it. She had heard it on the very first day, in a child's voice, too loud and too bright, in a hangar full of strangers. She had said nothing. Everything after had followed from that nothing: the seven days, the silence, the mirror, the seven years. She could trace every one of them back to that first nothing, like a crack in a windshield that starts at a single point.

She opened her mouth. Nothing came.

She tried to think of a name and couldn't. Every name she thought of felt too small, or too much, or like something she'd have to keep. And something else, under that, colder: if she named it, it would be hers. And then she would have something to lose.

"...Ask me later," she said finally. "I'll think about it."

"Acknowledged," 04 said, in its clipped protocol voice.

But Val could have sworn that, for the first time in seven years, it sounded happy.

### Stinger

The supply shuttle from the lunar facility docked with the Morningstar at three in the morning, station time, on schedule, and only one passenger got off.

He was a boy with pale hair, in a white flight suit a size too big for him, and he had no luggage. The crew had been told to expect him. They had not been told what he was. They lined the docking corridor in their gray station uniforms and watched him come through the lock, and he smiled at each of them as he passed, as if he were glad to see them, as if he had known them a long time.

The station commander walked him down the spine of the station himself, to a sealed bay near its heart, where something silver stood in a cradle under frost. Then on, to a second door, and behind it a case as long as a unit was tall. It had come up from the lunar dig a month before him, on a shuttle with no other cargo, from under Sariel's fourth rib. It hadn't been driven into the saint like the others. Sariel had been holding it against its chest, the way you hold on to something.

"Your orders come from the Conclave," the commander said. "Not from Umbral. Not from anyone on the ground."

"I know," said the boy.

The case opened. Cold white light came out of it, steady, like a flame that had forgotten how to flicker. The commander stepped back from it without meaning to. The boy didn't. He looked at the Sword for a long time, the way you look at something you lost once and have found again.

"There it is," he said softly.

Then he went to the window, where the Earth hung blue below the station, and found one city on it, on the coast, and watched it.

---

## Arc 7: The Door

> *After this I looked, and, behold, a door was opened in heaven: and the first voice which I heard was as it were of a trumpet talking with me; which said, Come up hither, and I will shew thee things which must be hereafter.*
>
> — Revelation 4:1

### Chapter 26: The First Attack

The intruder came for headquarters on a Tuesday.

He didn't bring an army. He came alone, at eleven in the morning, walking up the main approach in an old pilot's flight suit with a faded unit patch on the shoulder that nobody at the gate recognized, because the unit had been decommissioned before most of them were born. Someone had drawn on the patch, a long time ago, in ballpoint pen: a stick figure with a square head, and under it, in a small child's wobbly capitals, PIP. His skin was pale and veined with frost, the way windows get in winter. He walked straight through the vehicle barrier without slowing down. The barrier was a solid block of reinforced concrete. He walked through it the way you walk through fog.

Every security system that touched him died. The ones with saint tissue in them died first: the door locks with their little grown sensors, the biometric scanners, the gun turrets on the inner wall with their targeting cores. They went dark one after another, as if he were blowing out candles, and the frost spread out from his footprints across the floor tiles in fine, feathery lines. He didn't look at anyone. He didn't hurry. He walked through Umbral's defenses the way a ghost walks through a wall, heading down, always down, toward the warp-drive wing and the sealed door marked RELIQUARY.
