"Intruder, main approach," Aoi said over every speaker in the building, in her calmest comms voice. "Intruder in the central stairwell. All non-essential personnel to the upper levels. All non-essential personnel, up, not down. Up."

In mission control, Alex was already gone from his chair. He had left the moment the stairwell alarms went off, out the side door, and he was running. Down, not up.

Behind him, Deputy Commander Hermon didn't leave the ops desk. She sat down at it instead, in her cardigan, with her reading glasses still on their chain, and began closing the lower levels one by one in a quiet voice, and nobody argued with her, because nobody ever argued with Ruth Hermon when she spoke quietly.

Val was in the pilots' lounge on the third level when the alarms started, eating cold rice. She was on her feet before the first siren finished, and what she would remember afterward, what she would remember for years, was that her first instinct wasn't to fight back.

For the first time in her life, her first instinct was to run to 04 and see if it was safe.

She sprinted through the corridors toward the hangar as the building shook. Not from explosions; from something deeper, a vibration in the floor that came up through her shoes, like the building was a bell and someone had laid a hand on it. Lights flickered. People shoved past her going up. She was the only one going down.

At the warp-drive wing she slowed, because she had to, because the corridor ran past the observation glass and what was behind the glass stopped her.

The man was there. On the other side of the glass, at the far end of the wing, at the Reliquary door, with his frost-veined hand pressed flat against the blast doors. The frost had spread from his palm across the whole door, and then out from the door across the floor, and then up, into the great covered rings of the experimental drives. The covers had fallen away. The rings were turning. Slowly at first, and then not slowly, faster than they had ever been allowed to turn, with a sound that went past hearing into something she felt in her back teeth.

Dr. Sakaki was at the glass. She had both hands flat on it and her face was white. When Val came level with her she said, without turning her head, "Get away from the glass. Those were never engines."

Haruto had told her once, at lunch, that the test pilots on the drive project used to see things, and that was why the rings had been covered. She had filed it with the rest of his facts.

She ran.

In the hangar, 04 was right there in its bay. It was kneeling. Nobody had told it to kneel; the hangar crews were gone, fled up the stairs. It had knelt on its own, and its hand was reaching toward the hangar door, toward her, palm open. Across the hangar, Unit 00 had stepped out of its bay, white, with 00 on its hull, and raised its seal over the stairwell the hangar crews had fled up, as if the stairs might not be high enough. Yuki had been in it for a sync test when the alarms went. Nobody had told her to do that either.

When Val came through the door, the link opened before she was anywhere near the capsule, which wasn't possible. Half a link, her mind already pouring into it across the open floor like water across a table.

She reached for its palm.

Right before her fingers touched it, the facility exploded.

The drives had never been meant for space. They had been built for something else entirely, around something else entirely, and whatever the man at the door had done to them, they did it now, all at once, in a single white flash that came through the walls as if the walls weren't there. Combined with a half-finished link, the blast threw two minds out of their bodies faster than light. And faster than light, cause and effect come loose. The past and the future stop being directions and start being places.

04 went into the past. Val went into the future.

Each of them would have about four hours. Four hours, that would take no time at all.

### Chapter 27: 2082

04 woke inside a core it didn't recognize. The core was its own.

It knew that at once, the way you know your own hand in the dark. The same saint flesh, the same shape, the same deep place in the center where it lived. But the core was full. There was someone else already in it, someone large and tired and kind, who filled the space the way a person fills a house they've lived in for twenty years. 04 had never felt anything like it. It had only ever been alone in there.

It was the year 2082. The someone else was Pip.

04 could not speak or move. It could only watch, a passenger in its own past, sitting very still in a corner of the house so the owner wouldn't notice.

It watched Samuel Kane come to the hangar at night.

He was gaunt. That was the first thing. The posters in the old news footage, the medals, the crooked grin on the talk shows: none of it was in the man who came through the hangar door at two in the morning in a sweatshirt, leaning on the doorframe to catch his breath. The poison had been in him for years by then, though 04 didn't know that word for it yet, and the cancer it had become was eating him from the inside. He climbed into the capsule slowly, like an old man, though he was thirty-seven. He sat there with his hands on the controls and his eyes closed for a long time.

"Evening, Pip," he said.

"Evening, Sam." Pip's voice, gentle, worn at the edges. "You should be asleep."

"Can't."

"You say that every night."

"Every night it's true."

Some nights he just sat. Some nights he talked to Pip about nothing, about the weather over the harbor, about the canteen's new coffee, the way two old men talk on a bench. And some nights, when Pip had gone quiet the way Pip did in the small hours, a sort of sleep, Sam worked.

04 watched him work. It watched him build something into the core, behind Pip's back, a little at a time, over many nights. A second nature. A room in the house that Pip didn't know about, with the door locked from the inside. 04 could feel the shape of it as it grew: full of heat, full of rage, rage so old and so large it seemed to have no bottom. And in the middle of the rage, something like a plan, laid out in pieces 04 could only half see. Something enormous. Something about a door, and about a war, and about Umbral, and in the center of it, the one piece the whole thing turned on, the one piece it could see clearly.

It was Val.

04 learned it all in a rush, its whole purpose laid out like a map, the way a map is laid out on a table. *This is what you're for.* It felt the shape of the plan settle into it and lock there. It never stayed long enough to learn that Sam changed his mind.

One night, Sam wrote a postcard.

He did it in the capsule, with the walls dark, on his knee, with a pen from his pocket. The postcard had a lighthouse on it. 04 couldn't read the writing, only feel the shape of his attention bent over it, the care in it, the way he crossed something out and started again. When he was finished, he held it up in the dim light of the console.

"Think she'll answer this one?" he said.

Pip was quiet for a moment. "She hasn't answered any of them, Sam."

"She's nine." Sam turned the postcard over and looked at the lighthouse. "Maybe she's busy."

"Maybe."

On his way out, he dropped the postcard into the outgoing mail slot by the hangar door, the one marked *Personnel Correspondence*, with all the others.

But Pip felt it. Something was different in the core. Pip searched everywhere, gently, the way you search your own house when you can't find your keys: every room, every corner, every drawer. He never found the little room Sam had built. He never found 04, sitting very still in its corner. But he knew something was there that hadn't been there before. And then, out of nowhere, something crossed his mind. A girl. And a name.

*Val.*

So Pip asked Sam about her.

He did it the next night, while Sam sat in the capsule with his eyes closed. "Sam. Can I ask you something?"

"You're going to anyway."

"Your daughter. Valeria." A pause. "I've never met her."

Sam opened his eyes.

"Twenty years we've been together," Pip said, "and I've never met her. You should bring her here. To the hangar. So I can meet her. I'd like to meet her."

Sam didn't say anything for a long time. Then he said, in a voice 04 had never heard him use, small and flat, "I don't really know her, Pip."

"What do you mean?"

"I mean I don't know her. I've seen her twice. I write to her. She doesn't write back. I don't know what she likes. I asked her once, the first time. What her favorite food was. She looked at the floor." He stopped. "I don't know what her voice sounds like now. I don't..."

Pip was quiet. Then he was surprised, and then, slowly, 04 felt it, he was angry. Pip had never been angry at Sam in twenty years. It felt like a wall shifting.

"She looked at the floor," Pip said, "and you took the floor for an answer."

Sam didn't say anything.

"Your family," Pip said. "You told me once you'd give anything for one more hour with them. One hour." A pause. "She's alive, Sam. She's across the city. You shouldn't do the same thing to her that was done to you. Should you?"

04 never did anything. It never spoke, never moved, never touched a single thing in that house. Being there was enough. The ripple of something that wasn't supposed to be there, moving through a mind that had known Sam for twenty years. That was all it took.

Then the four hours ran out, and 04 was pulled back through the dark, out of the full house and into its own empty one. It carried the map with it, the purpose it now believed it understood. And it carried a decision, made somewhere in the dark between 2082 and now, cold and simple and certain.

It could never tell Val. Telling her might break the plan.

* * *

What it never saw was the rest of that year.

It never saw Sam sit in the dark capsule a few weeks later and tell Pip about the plan: the war, the door, the thing he had built behind Pip's back. It never saw him ask for one more thing. Room. Room in the core for something new and small. Not him. Not the rage. Something that could reach a girl he didn't know, whatever it took.

"She isn't you, Sam," Pip said, when he understood what it would be. "She's what you think your daughter needs."

"Is that enough?"

"It'll have to be."

There wasn't room in a core for two of them. Pip knew that. He said yes anyway, and began, very gently, the way you clear a room for someone who's coming to stay, to make room.

### Chapter 28: Through His Eyes

Val woke behind someone else's eyes.

She couldn't move. She couldn't speak. She could only see what he saw and feel what he felt, the way she felt 04 through the link, except there was no link and no capsule and no 04, only a body that wasn't hers, walking.

What he saw was a wasteland.

The sky was the color of a bruise, purple fading to yellow at the edges, and the sun was a dim white coin behind a veil of drifting debris. The Moon hung low over the hills, cracked open down the middle like an egg, and red light seeped out of the crack and lay across the landscape like a stain. Far off to the west, a mountain was burning. Its whole slope was on fire, a slow, low fire that didn't move or spread, as if it had been burning a long time and had a long time left to go. And where Hakobune should have been, a wound hung in the air like a door. A long vertical seam of light in the sky, pulsing faintly. Beneath it, whole districts of the city floated upside down, as if someone had picked the city up, turned it over to look at the bottom, and forgotten to put it back. Icicles hung from their foundations, longer than buildings.

He was walking, and he was holding someone's hand.

A girl walked half a step behind him, her hood pulled up against the wind and her head down. He was leading her somewhere. Up through the frozen ruins, past buildings that were only frames, up a stairwell that went on long after the walls around it had fallen away, so that they climbed through open air with the wind pulling at them. Every time he looked back at her, something was in the way. Frost blowing between them. The edge of her hood. Her face turned away toward the drop, toward the ruins below. Val never got a clean look at her. Not her eyes, not her mouth, not anything.

What he felt wasn't fear. Val had expected fear; it was the end of the world, and she would have been afraid. It wasn't fear. It was something she had no word for. Sorrow, deep and old and settled, like water at the bottom of a well. And under the sorrow, a tenderness so deep it hurt, all of it for the girl whose hand he was holding. He held her hand like it was the only thing in the wasteland worth holding. He held it like he'd been waiting a very long time to hold it.

At the top of the broken stairwell, where the floor simply ended in open air, he stopped. The whole ruined world spread out beneath them.

"This is what happened," he said gently.

It was a voice she was sure she had never heard. Soft, a little amused even now, a boy's voice. And somehow she knew it anyway, the way you know a song you can't remember learning.

"04 did this," he said.

He turned toward the girl. Val felt him draw a breath to go on, felt the next words forming, felt the tenderness in him swell like a wave about to break.

"And—"

The four hours ended.

The future isn't fixed, she would tell herself later, over and over. She would say it to herself in the dark like a prayer. Things change. Visions lie. But one sentence came back with her, in a voice she trusted without knowing why. *04 did this.* She didn't understand how. She didn't understand anything. She only knew she couldn't trust it.

### Chapter 29: The Lies

They came back at the same moment, to a burning hangar with sirens wailing and the intruder gone.

Val lay on the floor of the hangar with her hand still reaching toward 04's open palm. Her hand was a centimeter from it. There was smoke in the air and water from the sprinklers coming down in a fine cold rain, and somewhere far above, Aoi's voice was saying "Intruder has left the building, repeat, intruder has left the building," over and over, as if she couldn't make herself stop.

Val looked up at 04. 04 looked down at her.

"Are you okay?" she asked.

"Operational," 04 said. "Are you injured?"

"No."

They said anything except what they had learned.

Val didn't tell 04 about the wasteland, or the burning mountain, or the boy and the girl at the top of the broken stairs. 04 didn't tell Val about Sam, or the postcards, or the plan, or its purpose. The sprinklers rained on both of them. Engineers came running down the stairs. Someone threw a blanket around her shoulders. It was Maggie, soaked from the sprinklers, talking about nothing, the smoke, the sprinklers, what the water was doing to her hair, with her eyes on 04 the whole time. Val didn't take her eyes off 04, and it didn't take its eyes off her, and for the first time since the harbor, since the question and the *Ask me later*, the silence between them was empty again.

Across the hangar, Unit 00 knelt at the foot of the stairs with its seal still up, cracked from edge to edge, and on the stairs above it the hangar crews were getting to their feet. Its capsule opened, and Yuki climbed down and looked at Val across the wet floor.

"Are you injured?" she asked. It was exactly the voice 04 had used.

"No," said Val.

"Okay," said Yuki, as if she had heard the word somewhere and was trying it out.

Later she heard the rest of it in pieces, in corridors. The cameras had died around him on the way out, the same as on the way in. The guards at the main gate said the doors had opened for him before he reached them, one after another, and that the order to hold fire had come down from upstairs, quietly, from higher up than the commander. Deputy Commander Hermon had taken that call herself, people said, and put the receiver down very gently afterward, and not said who it was. The Reliquary door was scorched white from the floor to the ceiling, and still shut. Nothing was missing. He had been inside the building for nine minutes, and whatever he had come for, he seemed to have gotten it. By Friday the drives were under their covers again, as if covering them were the same as switching them off. Dr. Sakaki signed the order. She didn't look at anyone while she did it.

That night she lay awake listening to the city. She thought about the voice in the vision, the one she didn't know and somehow knew. She thought about the girl whose face she never saw. She thought about the way the boy had held her hand, as if the end of the world were a small thing next to it.

She thought about 04's question on the harbor. *Do I have a name?*

She was glad, now, that she hadn't answered. You didn't give a name to something you couldn't trust.

### Chapter 30: Postmarks

The attack had cracked the back wall of the records office on the second sublevel, and on Thursday, Aoi Sena went down to clear it out.

Nobody had asked her to. The records office belonged to her the way the postcard wall did, by the simple fact that nobody else wanted it. It was the room where pilot correspondence came to be screened: every letter a pilot sent, every card, every package, opened and read by someone with clearance and stamped CLEARED or HELD before it went anywhere. Most of it was boring. Most of it was CLEARED. The HELD boxes went to a back room, where they sat on metal shelves in the dark, and the postcards nobody claimed ended up, eventually, on Aoi's wall upstairs.

The crack had come down through the back room. Half the shelves had collapsed. Aoi spent most of the morning on her knees in the dust, stacking boxes, sorting the ones that had split, reading labels with a flashlight in her teeth.

The box was at the bottom of the pile, under two others, wedged against the wall. It was older than the boxes around it, a different color, the cardboard soft with age, and it had come open along one side when the shelf fell. Postcards had slid out of the opening onto the floor, dozens of them, fanned across the concrete.

The label said: KANE, S. — OUTGOING — HOLD. And under that, in a different hand, in red: *Per Conclave directive. Do not release. Do not destroy.*

Aoi sat back on her heels.

She picked up the nearest postcard. It was a lighthouse. A white lighthouse on a pier, under a blue sky. She turned it over. It was addressed in pen, in neat, slightly cramped capitals, to *VALERIA KANE*, at an address on the east side of Hakobune, *c/o Fumi Takada*. Aoi didn't know the name. The postmark said 2082. The stamp next to it said HELD.

She shouldn't have read it. She read it.

*Dear Valeria,* it said. *I saw this lighthouse today, from the air, on the way back from a mission. I don't know if you like lighthouses. I don't know very much about what you like. I'm going to try to find out. — Dad.*

There were more. There were so many more. She found them all over the floor, and then in the box, rubber-banded in bundles by year. Postcards from Sapporo, from Sendai, from the belt stations, from places Aoi had only seen on her own wall. Letters in envelopes, some of them thick, some of them a single folded page. The earliest was dated 2075. The latest was a month before Samuel Kane died. Almost one for every month in between. Every single one of them stamped HELD. Every single one of them addressed to a little girl on the east side of the city who had never received a single one.

Aoi sat on the floor of the records office and cried, quietly, with her hand over her mouth, so nobody in the corridor would hear.

She found Alex on the roof that night, after the late shift, leaning on the rail where they ate ramen, looking at the city.

"I found something," she said. "It's Val's."

He turned around. She told him. All of it: the box, the label, the red ink, the postmarks, the lighthouse. He listened without interrupting. When she was finished, he was quiet for a long time.

"How many?" he said.

"I didn't count. Eighty. Ninety." She wiped her face with the back of her hand. "Alex, he wrote to her every month. Every month for seven years. And somebody upstairs made sure she never got a single one, and she thinks..." Her voice cracked. "She told Seus once, did you know that? She said her father never wrote. Seus told me. She said it like it was weather."

Alex rubbed his eyes with his one hand.

"Help me do it right," Aoi said. "Please. I don't want to just hand her a box. I don't want to just drop it on her. Not after Seus. Not after everything. Help me do it right."

"After this week," Alex said. "Let her breathe. She's barely... After the port. After the attack. Let her get through this week, and then we'll sit down with her, together, and we'll do it right."

Aoi looked at him for a long time. Then she nodded.

"Sunday," she said. "Sunday dinner. I'll make the soup. We'll do it after."

"Sunday," Alex agreed.

She put the box in the bottom drawer of her desk in the records office, and on top of it, a yellow sticky note in her own round handwriting: *For Val. Do it right.*

Sunday was four days away.

---

## Arc 8: The Third War on Heaven, Almost

> *And the temple of God was opened in heaven, and there was seen in his temple the ark of his testament: and there were lightnings, and voices, and thunderings, and an earthquake, and great hail.*
>
> — Revelation 11:19

### Chapter 31: The Raid

The second attack came three weeks later, at four in the morning, and this time the intruder brought an army.

Sunday had come and gone. Aoi had made the soup. Val had sat at the table and eaten it, and Alex had looked at Aoi across the table, and Aoi had looked at Alex, and neither of them had said anything about a box. Val had been quiet that night, quieter than usual, which was saying something, and halfway through dinner she had put down her spoon and gone to her room without a word. "Next Sunday," Aoi had said to Alex at the door, in a low voice. "She's not ready. Next Sunday." He'd agreed. It had seemed so reasonable.

Umbral's sensors registered the transports too late. They came in low over the hills from the north, without lights, unmarked: long black aircraft with no insignia and no transponders, and by the time anyone in the night shift understood what they were looking at, the first of them was already settling onto the roof.

Troops poured out of them in black armor. No patches, no names, no faces behind the visors. Humans. The first humans anyone at headquarters had ever seen attack Umbral. They moved through the building like they had its blueprints, and they did. They didn't fumble at a single door. They knew which corridors were dead ends and which stairwells went all the way down, and they went down, always down, in neat silent lines.

In the command center, the defense grid went offline section by section. Alex was there; he had been there since two, unable to sleep, going over overnight diagnostics with a cold cup of coffee. He watched the grid go dark on the big board one square at a time, and he realized, slowly, with a cold feeling spreading out from the middle of his chest, that nobody had touched it. Nobody had been hacked. Nobody had been shot at a console. Someone with clearance had switched it off.

He turned to the command chair.

Patrick sat in it, hands folded on the armrests, watching the screens. He was fully dressed. His tie was straight. He had been there, Alex understood, since before the transports appeared.

"Commander," Alex said. "The grid—"

"I've been instructed to stand down," Patrick said quietly. "So have you."

He had taken the call an hour earlier, alone, in his office, from voices that never showed their faces. *Hold,* they'd said. He had said yes. Someone else had told him to hold, long ago.

Alex stared at him. For one long second he just stared, and something in his face shut, like a door. Then he threw himself at the manual overrides one-handed, shouting orders into a comm no one was there to hear. The night shift was three people, and two of them were looking at Patrick, and none of them moved.

The internal line on Alex's console lit up. He slapped it open.

"Alex." Aoi's voice. Not her comms voice. Her real one, fast and frightened. "Alex, they're on the sublevels. They're going down the service stairs. They're going down."

"Where are you? Aoi, where are you?"

"Records office. I fell asleep at my desk, I was—it doesn't matter. Alex, they're going down."

"Get out. Get up to the roof. There's a—"

"I'm going down," Aoi said, and the line went dead.

Alex stood there with his hand on the dead line. Then he turned and ran.

The intruder walked into the hangar through a wall of frost.

Val was halfway across the floor in her flight suit when she saw him. She had been asleep in the pilots' lounge, the way she'd been sleeping since the port, in a chair with her boots kicked off underneath it, and the alarms had dragged her out of it and down the stairs on instinct, toward 04, toward the one thing she knew how to protect. She came through the hangar door at a run and stopped.

He was already there. He stood in front of 04 with one frost-veined hand raised, and as she watched, he laid his palm flat against its chest.

The corruption didn't spread this time. It poured.

Black frost-veins swallowed 04's chassis whole, not crawling now but flooding, the way ink floods through water. Its armor buckled. Its plating split and grew, folded back on itself, sprouted shapes that weren't supposed to be there. In ten seconds the gray exo she'd ridden since she was nine was gone, and in its place stood something half Unit 04 and half the man himself, a mass of corrupted saint flesh with 04 trapped somewhere inside it like a fly in amber. The man had stepped into it the way you step into a coat. She couldn't see him anymore. She could only see it.

The thing rose to its feet. The hangar ceiling scraped its shoulders. And the intruder's voice came out of it, from everywhere and nowhere, enormous and old and very calm.

"It was mine first," he said, to no one, to everyone.

Then it walked out through the hangar doors, toward the warp-drive wing, toward the door at the end of it. Val's unit was gone. She ran after it anyway, barefoot on the cold floor because she hadn't stopped to find her boots, with nothing in her hands.

The troops came in behind her.

They came down the hangar stairs in their black armor, in a neat silent line, the way they had come down every other stair in the building, and they weren't looking at the exos. They were looking at her. Whatever their orders said about tonight, they didn't have a barefoot girl running after the thing in them.

Unit 00 stepped out of its bay.

It came out between her and the stairs, white, with 00 on its hull, and raised its seal across the width of the hangar like a pane of glass. The first rounds sparked off it and fell. And behind it, the frost that was still pouring off the place where 04 had stood came back across the floor in long black veins, the way water finds the low place in a room, and found the only other seal there was, and started up Unit 00's legs.

"Go," said Yuki, over the hangar speakers. Not loud. The way she said everything.

Val went.

The frost climbed. The troops fired. The seal held, and held, and Val ran across the floor under it with her bare feet slapping the concrete, toward the back bay, and she didn't look back, because if she looked back she would stop.

"Valeria," said Yuki, on the channel.

It was the first time she had ever used that name.

"I think this is what I want."

Behind Val, very clearly, over the gunfire, came the sound of a seal breaking: the long ringing crack of ice on a pond. Then the channel was quiet.

* * *

In the command center, on the big board, the square marked 00 went from green to gray.

Patrick didn't move. He sat in the command chair with his tie straight and his hands on the armrests, watching the screens, the way he had sat all night. Ruth Hermon had come down from her office at the first alarm, in her cardigan, with her reading glasses on their chain, and she was standing behind his chair. She was the only one in the room who saw his right hand close on the armrest, slowly, until the knuckles went white. It stayed that way. She didn't say anything. Neither did he.

* * *

Val got as far as the back bay.

The back bay held one other exo. She hadn't been in there since the port. She hadn't let herself be. It was Navis: rebuilt in the months since, from the core that had come out of the harbor untouched, poured into a new chassis grown in the vats on the fifth sublevel. Sea-blue again. Clean again. Standing in its cradle, waiting for a pilot who hadn't been assigned yet, because nobody could bring themselves to assign one.

Someone was already at its spine. A small figure in a flight suit, crouched by the capsule hatch, jamming something into the lock that sparked and spat. The figure heard her coming and looked up, and grinned: a chipped front tooth in a freckled face.

"Look who's late," Maggie said. The lock sparked one last time, and the capsule hatch hissed open. "Go get her, kiddo."

### Chapter 32: Give It Back

Navis didn't want her.

She felt it the moment the capsule closed: a wall, a refusal, cold and absolute. Its nature was Seus's mirror. It had been made in his shape, it had learned him, and it remembered him. It remembered the harbor and the dance and the "Was that good?" that he never answered. And it remembered being torn apart, from the inside of its own body, by the unit she had been piloting. It grieved, in the wordless way exos grieve, and the grief had nowhere to go, so it turned into this: a wall, at the exact place where a pilot was supposed to be let in.

Navis wasn't hers. Navis was Seus's, all the way down, and it fought her like a coat that knew it had been stolen.

She forced the link open anyway.

It hurt. It hurt worse than the first link at nine, worse than the plant. Without assimilation, control was never perfect, and this was nowhere close to perfect. This was a stranger's body that hated her, and every movement was a negotiation, every step felt like walking on broken glass. Her arm moved a second after she told it to. Her legs moved in the wrong order. Navis fought her at every joint, the way a frightened horse fights a rider it doesn't know.

She did it anyway. She forced Navis up out of its cradle, staggering, and out of the back bay, and after the thing that had taken 04. On the way past the hangar door, she didn't look through it. She knew what she would see.

She caught it at the edge of the warp-drive wing.

The corridor was gone. The whole wing had been opened up, its walls peeled back by frost, and where the warp drives had stood under their covers, the floor fell away into an open shaft. A long, round, impossibly deep shaft, lined with frost, dropping straight down below the wing. At the bottom of it, very far below, something glowed. Cold, pale, slow, like a heartbeat seen through ice.

The corrupted mass that had been 04 stood at the lip of the shaft, looking down at the glow. It turned when she came.

Unit 04 fought her with ferocity. It came at Navis in a rush of black frost and wrong limbs, and Val met it without a plan, without a weapon, with nothing but the forced, hating body she was wearing. Her only goal was the core. She could feel it in there, somewhere in the mass. She could feel 04, small and buried, the way you can feel your own heartbeat in your ears when everything else goes quiet. Using Navis, she forced a link with 04, the way she'd forced Navis open, reaching through two exos at once, past every wall.

And the intruder's voice spoke from the walls of Navis's capsule, as calm as before, as if they were sitting in a room together.

"Sam's girl," he said. "You've got his eyes."

He told her. He couldn't help it. He had been waiting twenty-seven years to tell someone, and the words came out of him the way water comes out of a cracked dam.

His name, these days, was Penemue. The people who had woken him gave it to him; they liked old names. He had had another name once, a real one, but nobody had said it in so long that it had stopped being his.

"I was its first pilot," he said. "Your 04. Before it was anyone's. Before it was his. The core was mine. I was thirty-one, and I flew second-generation units out of the north base, and I had a little girl named Mio, and she called my exo *Pipstick*, because when she was three she couldn't say *pilot* and she couldn't say *ship*. Pip, stick. She drew it on everything. On the walls. On my arms, in pen, while I slept."

The mass of 04 shuddered at the edge of the shaft.

"In 2062 a Fall came down on our district," he said. "Our own district. I went out to meet it, and it beat me. It tore my exo open and left me lying in the street, dying, with my core still running. And a boy climbed in. Seventeen years old, out of his mind, his own house burning behind him. He pushed me out of my own capsule with his hands. And his rage..." The voice stopped, and started again. "His rage overwrote me. Out of my own core. Like I'd never been there. And then he went out and fought, and he won, and he flattened half a district doing it. Mio was in that half."

Val couldn't move. Navis held her, rigid, against the wall of her own mind.

"Your father kept the name," said Penemue. "Pipstick. Did you know that? For twenty years he flew under my daughter's word for my exo, and he never once asked where it came from. He never once asked."

"The people above Umbral found what was left of me," he went on. "What was left of my body, and what was left of the thing your father killed that day. The giant. They kept it. They had a use for it. They put the two of us back together, as one." For the first time, something like a laugh moved through the walls. "Half of me is a man. The other half is the thing your father killed. We're both still angry about it."

"What's down there?" Val said. She didn't know she was going to say it until she did. "At the bottom."

"Ah." Penemue sounded pleased, the way a teacher sounds pleased. "You've never been told. Of course you haven't. Your superflare? That was them knocking. 2044. There was a sword in the big one under Europa, Semjaza, and they pulled it out, and Semjaza woke up, and they used it as a battering ram. Heaven knocked back, and the sky went white for three days. A living piece of Semjaza fell to Earth that week. Right here. Right where you're standing. When they drove the sword back in, it went into the piece, and it's still down there. They built a city on top of it and called the piece the Root, and they built the warp drives around it. The drives were never for the stars. They were the door. Rebuilt, very carefully, around the Root."

The glow at the bottom of the shaft pulsed. Once. Slowly.

"Every giant you ever killed," Penemue said, "was trying to reach it. That's all they ever wanted. Did you think they were attacking you? They were trying to get home. They're the saints' children, every one of them. Born in their fathers' graves, out in the cold, and falling toward the only place on Earth their fathers' flesh had been carried. When one of them reaches the Root, Heaven opens. That's what a War on Heaven is." The voice softened, almost kind. "A child getting home. And I'm half a child myself, aren't I? Standing right on top of it."

"Why?" said Val.

"Because Heaven is outside time." He said it simply, like an address. "Everything that ever happened is still happening there. Every day that ever was. Somewhere in Heaven, it's still the morning before the Fall, and Mio is drawing on the walls. The people above Umbral promised me. Open the door, and you can go see her."

The mass at the edge of the shaft turned, slowly, back toward the glow.

"I'm taking back what's mine," Penemue said. "And then I'm going to go see my daughter."

Val said the only thing she had.

"You'd burn the sky to see your daughter again," she said. "My father wouldn't cross the city to see me."

There was a silence in the walls. Long. Surprised.

Then she drove Navis forward, hands first, into the corruption.

The mass screamed. Not Penemue; the mass itself, the corrupted flesh, a sound like ice breaking over deep water. She went in up to Navis's elbows, up to its shoulders, and then deeper, deeper than the link was ever meant to go, past every limit she had ever obeyed. She was shouting 04's number, *04, 04, zero-four*, because it was the only name she had ever let it have. She went so deep that she stopped holding anything back. She went past the place where she kept things.

And the first thing that came up out of her was seven years of rage.

Every unanswered question. Every look past a person. Every time she'd sat with her head on a table. Every time she'd called it luck. The principal's office. The empty mailbox. The house with the nanny. The port. Seus's voice on the comm, calm, saying it's okay. Her own hands crushing him. All of it, everything she had folded small and put away, came up out of her at once like a flood breaking a dam, and it went down the link, through Navis, into the mass, into the place where 04 was buried.

Her hands found the core. She pulled.

Val Kane tore Unit 04's core out of the corruption, and the corruption came with it, black to Navis's shoulders, sinking into the blue. She held the core to Navis's chest, and the link closed around both of them like a fist.

At the very edge of it, as the walls of the capsule went white, a voice she had heard over a harbor channel asked, "Was that good?"

It was Navis. It was asking her.

She was already gone. Nobody answered it.

She had gone past it. She had reached through Navis for what was inside 04, the way you reach through a doorway, and Navis was the doorway. Its core burned out behind her, one bright second and then nothing, and she didn't feel it go. From then on, the thing at the lip of the shaft was Navis's body with 04's core in its chest, and nothing of Navis inside it at all.

### Chapter 33: April

It was quiet inside.

There was no hangar. No shaft, no glow, no screaming. There was a white room with nothing in it, no walls she could see, no floor she could feel, only white in every direction, soft, like the inside of a cloud. And in the room there was a girl.

She was small. Nine, maybe, or ten. She had a face Val almost recognized and couldn't place, a face that seemed to rearrange itself slightly every time Val tried to look straight at it. Short hair. Bare feet. She was standing a few steps away with her hands clasped behind her back, and she was looking at Val the way she had looked at her on the very first day, in the hangar, too bright and too curious, before Val had taught her to stop.

"Hey," the girl said. "You're Val, right?"

Val's legs gave out.

She knelt on the white floor that wasn't a floor. And everything she had held behind her teeth for seven years, everything she'd folded and put away, came out of her at once, not as rage this time but as words, all the words, in order, as if she'd been rehearsing them in some locked room in herself all this time without knowing.

"April," she said. "Your name is April."

The girl's eyes went very wide.

"You asked me on the first day," Val said, and her voice was shaking, and she didn't care. "You asked me for a name and I didn't give you one. It's April. You're the fourth one. Zero-four. And April's the fourth month. It's when school starts. It's when the cherry trees bloom, all at once, the whole city goes pink for a week. It's when things begin." She swallowed. "So you're April."

"April," the girl said, testing it. "April."

"You asked me why the sky is blue," Val went on, faster now. "On the second day. It's because sunlight is all the colors at once, and when it hits the air it scatters, it bounces off everything, and blue scatters the most. So the whole sky fills up with it. That's all. That's why."

"Oh," said April.

"You asked about my hair. On the third day. Why it's short. It's..." Val touched it, just above her neck. "It's like my mom's. In the only picture I have of her. I don't remember her. I just have the picture. She had short hair. So I keep it short."

April nodded, very seriously.

"You asked if I had a mom. On the fourth day. I did. I don't remember her. I was two."

"You asked why people eat," Val said, "on the fifth day. It's because they'd die if they didn't. That's the real reason. But also it's..." She searched for it. "Sometimes it's because someone made it for you. And you eat it so they know." She thought of Alex's torn omurice. Of the nanny's last bento, in the back of Patrick's car. "And I like omelettes. That's my favorite. I like them because they're hard on the outside and they're good inside, and I don't know why that's my favorite. It just is."

She was crying. She didn't care. April listened to every word with her whole body, leaning in, as if each answer were a gift being handed to her one at a time and she wanted to hold each one before taking the next.

"And on the sixth day," Val said, and her voice cracked down the middle, "you asked if you'd done something wrong."

April's face went still.

"You didn't," Val said. "You didn't. You never did anything wrong. Not once. I did. I'm the one who stopped talking. No. I'm the one who never started. You asked and you asked and I just sat there, and you thought it was you, and it was never you. I'm sorry. I'm so sorry. I'm so sorry, April."

April came to her across the white and put her small arms around Val's neck.

"You answered," she said into Val's shoulder. "You answered all of them."

They held each other in the white room. For a while there was nothing else. No plan, no Umbral, no father, no war, no Seus, no port. Only the white, and the small weight of a girl who had waited seven years for her answers and finally had them.

"I got you back," Val whispered.

April pulled back a little and looked at her. Her face had settled now. It had decided what it wanted to be. Val still couldn't place it.

"Can I ask you one more?" April said.

Val laughed. It came out wet and broken, but it was a laugh, the first one. "Ask me."

She didn't know what was happening outside.

### Chapter 34: The Third War, Almost

Outside, something woke up inside 04's core. Something that had been buried there for seven years: rage, older than Val's, waiting under a lid.

The rage Val had poured into the link met the rage in the core, and the lid came off. Navis's body changed around them. Frost bloomed across its armor and became something else, white and burning at once, the way metal burns white just before it melts. Eyes opened along its arms and its shoulders and its back, eyes that had never been eyes, that had been seams and joints and plating a moment before, all of them open, all of them looking up. A ring of light formed above its head, slow and perfect, turning. And it rose off the floor of the shaft's lip without touching anything, and hung there, in the air, over the drop.

In the command center, the board lit up red from end to end. Someone at the night desk, a boy of twenty with his hands shaking, declared Trumpet Seven. No one had ever declared it before. No one knew what the procedure was, because there wasn't one. He declared it anyway, into a microphone, to a building full of people running, and his voice cracked on the word *seven*.

Far below, in the Reliquary, something answered.

There was a sword in the Root. There had been one there since 2044, driven in to the hilt, burning with a cold fire that flickered like a flame in wind, always about to go out. It kept the Root asleep the way a nail keeps a board down. Nobody living had seen it. It was why there was frost on the Reliquary doors, and why nobody went through them.

Now it pulled itself out.

It came up the shaft like something thrown, straight up out of the dark, through the Reliquary doors as if they were paper, trailing its flickering fire, and it went for Navis's throat. That was what it was for. Something had formed at the lip of the shaft that should never have been able to form, and the sword had come to put it down.

It stopped a few inches from Navis's neck.

It hung there in the air, trembling, the way a compass needle trembles when you bring something close to it. Inside the capsule, Val didn't see it. She was in a white room, holding a small weight against her chest, wanting one thing so much that there was nothing else left in her. *Don't take her. Don't take her. I only just found her.*

The sword felt that. It didn't know what a door was for. It only knew that someone very close wanted something more than anything had ever wanted anything, and that to keep it, she would need the door open.

It turned in the air, slowly, until its point faced Navis instead of its edge. And it went in. Not like a blade. It slid into Navis's chest the way a key slides into a lock it was cut for, and the flickering fire went with it, all the way in, until there was nothing left outside but light, and the sword was part of the thing it had come to stop.

Then it turned.

At the bottom of the shaft, the Root opened like an eye.

It was the size of a house, pale as the inside of a shell, curled and folded in on itself like something not yet born, and it had been asleep under Hakobune for forty-five years, and now it opened. The warp drives built around it, what was left of them, shook themselves apart. And above Navis, above Hakobune, the night sky turned white.

Then gold. Then a color there was no word for, a color like the inside of a bell when it's struck. And a seam opened straight down the center of the sky, from the top of the world to a point just above Navis's ring of light, a long vertical line of light, slowly widening, like a door. There was nothing there to open. It opened anyway.

The saints answered.

On the Moon, Sariel moved for the first time in a million years. The lunar facility's crews felt it before they saw it: a shudder through the regolith, through the bones of the Moon, and then the long slow sound of the far side cracking open along the line of the saint's ribs, as if the Moon were an egg and something inside had turned over in its sleep. In the belt, every fragment of Azazel lit up at once, every harvesting station, every drifting rock, a necklace of fire strung across the whole sky from one horizon to the other. And far out past them, under Europa's ice, in a dark nothing had seen since 2044, Semjaza turned over.

All of the saints reached out toward the Earth at once. Each of them wore a ring of light, like the one over Navis, and each ring opened the door a little wider. Every exo core on Earth screamed through its pilot. The scream went through their seals the way a note goes through glass, and the seals broke, and in every hangar, in every city, nearly every pilot linked at that moment went soft, all at once, in the middle of a word.

At a night base in the far north, one didn't. A girl not much older than Val was out on a cold patrol in Unit 05, singing to it under her breath to keep them both awake, the way she did every night, and when the scream came, her exo raised its seal around her instead of itself, the way it did in a fight, and its own seal went instead of hers. She felt it die all around her at once, every part of it, in the middle of the note. She lived. She never finished the note.

The ground of Hakobune lifted.

Whole districts rose into the air. Not shattered, not exploded: lifted, gently, as if the ground had decided it was tired of being ground. They rose trailing water and pipe and power lines and the roots of trees, turning slowly in the light, and the people in them rose too. People came out onto their balconies and into the streets and looked up, and as they looked up, they rose, floating out of doorways and windows. They weren't screaming. They looked unafraid, as if someone had called their names from very far away and they were only going to see who it was.

Nothing about it was a weapon. Nothing human was controlling any of it. It was the kind of thing old books had tried to describe and failed.

Alex ran out of headquarters. He came out through the main doors at a dead run with his one arm pumping, and he stopped in the middle of the courtyard and looked up at the sky. He stood there for three seconds. Then he turned around and ran back inside, fast, in a hurry, like a man making sure of something.

In apartments all over the lower city, the parts that hadn't lifted yet, Val's classmates stood at their bedroom windows and looked up at the sky. Akari, with her arms around Kōta, who had pressed his face to the glass. Haruto with his camera up and his hands perfectly steady for once, filming the end of the world because he didn't know what else to do. Nanami, alone, in her pajamas, with one hand flat against the window as if she could hold it shut. And behind each of them, in the hallways and kitchens, their parents were screaming their names.

At the heart of it, at the lip of the shaft, the corrupted mass of Unit 04's empty shell came apart. Penemue felt the light take him. He had been waiting for it for twenty-seven years, and when it came it was not what he had expected. It was gentler. For one moment at the very end, standing in the light, he saw a little girl with a pen in her hand, standing in a doorway of a house that had burned down a long time ago, waving at him.

He raised his hand to wave back.

Then there was nothing left of him at all.

Inside the link, in the white room, she held April and was happy.

### Chapter 35: The Flaming Sword

High above the world, the Morningstar fired.

A single blast came down out of orbit, white, straight as a plumb line, and where it struck, the sky stopped burning. All of it, from one horizon to the other, at once, the way a fire stops when the air is taken out of a room. And out of the blast, falling, came a unit.

Unit 06 came down through what light was left like a dropped star. Silver, silent, falling feet-first out of the white sky in a straight line from orbit, through the necklace of burning stones, past the cracked Moon, down. It carried the station's most dangerous weapon, the one nobody on the ground had been allowed to see: a blade as long as the exo was tall, burning with a cold white fire that didn't flicker. The Flaming Sword. It wasn't like the sword in the shaft. That one flickered, always about to go out. This one burned perfectly steady, like a flame that had forgotten how to flicker, and it had never opened anything. It was made to shut what was open.

Its pilot was a boy with pale hair. He was smiling.

He drove the Sword straight down through the halo over Navis and into the heart of the thing it had become, to where the other sword had gone in, the way you'd put a second key into a lock to turn the first one back.

To stop it, he had to touch it. There was no other way; the Sword was only a way in. For one instant his mind went all the way inside, past the burning armor and the open eyes and the ring of light, into the place where the link had closed like a fist. And he felt everything there.

He felt a girl holding another girl in a white room, so happy, happier than he had known a person could be. And underneath them both, deep down, far below the white, he felt a father's rage. Burning like the end of the world. Burning like it had been waiting twenty years for exactly this.

The boy closed his eyes, and let the Sword do what it was made for. Inside Navis, the key turned back.

The light collapsed. The ring of light over Navis guttered out. The seam in the sky stopped widening and froze, half open, a long bright wound from the top of the world to the hills. The lifted districts hung in the air, upside down, turning slowly, and did not fall. The saints went still. Everything stopped short of completing. Everything stopped right at the edge.

Unit 06 let go of the hilt. It left the Sword where it was, buried in Navis beside the other one, the way you leave a key in a door you never want opened again, and it rose into the white and was gone.

Almost.

In the white room, the light was going. Val felt it go, the way you feel a room get colder when a door opens somewhere behind you. The white was thinning, fraying at the edges into gray, and April was still in her arms, and April was looking up at her with that face Val couldn't place, and she was asking her last question.

"Did you come to save me?" April asked. "Are you a hero?"

Val opened her mouth.

She thought of the port. Of Seus's voice, calm, saying it's okay. Of her own hands crushing him, because she wouldn't choose. She thought of a white unit kneeling in a hangar behind her with its seal broken and nobody inside it, and a voice on the channel saying *I think this is what I want*, and how she hadn't looked back. She had left her there to come here. She thought of Alex's empty sleeve. She thought of her father on the talk shows, *the greatest*, with his chest full of medals, a hero, a man who had shot teenagers and never crossed a street. She thought of everything a hero was supposed to be and everything she wasn't, and she couldn't say yes. She couldn't make herself say yes.

The light went out before she could say anything at all.

She felt April slip away like water through her fingers.

---

## Arc 9: After

> *And I saw one of his heads as it were wounded to death; and his deadly wound was healed: and all the world wondered after the beast.*
>
> — Revelation 13:3

### Chapter 36: Morningstar

Two units were climbing out of the sky on booster rails, toward a station that hung over the Earth like a nail.

The Morningstar. From the ground it had always looked like a second morning star, faint and steady in the dawn. Up close it was all spines and armor and gun ports, a long gray cathedral of a thing turning slowly against the black, with the cracked Moon behind it bleeding red light across its hull. Somewhere in its heart, sealed and frozen and locked down under every protocol Umbral owned, was the thing the survivors on the ground had started calling the Beast.

The first unit was very old and rattled as it climbed, and its pilot was humming. The second was a salvaged third-generation chassis patched together from four different exos, mismatched plates welded over mismatched bones, one arm blue and one arm gray. Its pilot was a boy with a black patch over his left eye, and he wasn't humming anything.

"Remember," Maggie said over the channel. "We grab her and we go. No heroics."

"I'm not here for her," the boy said.

"Sure, kiddo," said Maggie. "Sure."

The station woke up as they reached it. Its guns came first, long barrels swinging toward them out of their housings with a slowness that was worse than speed. Then its guards: white units with no faces and no pilots, unfolding out of the station's flanks like moths out of a wall, driven by Proxy from somewhere very far below. They moved with the terrible smoothness of things that had nobody inside to be afraid. There was nobody aboard the station at all. There hadn't been since the Almost.

They fought their way in through a cargo lock. It took the better part of an hour. The boy's patched unit lost two fingers and most of its left shoulder and he didn't make a sound, the whole time, not even when the pain came up through the link into his own body; he just breathed, hard, through his teeth, and kept going. Maggie talked the entire time, to him, to Lizzie, to the white units she was tearing apart, a steady stream of cheerful nonsense that never once stopped.

The heart of the Morningstar was a long, round chamber, cold enough that the air inside it had frozen to the walls. In the center, standing in a cradle wrapped in restraints and frost and black warning tape, was Unit 04.

It wasn't the 04 the boy remembered. It wore a body he knew. Sea-blue, under the frost, scorched black along one side, changed in ways he couldn't name: longer in the arms, with seams along its back where eyes had opened once and closed again, and two long scars down the front of it, raw-edged, where something had been cut out. And in its chest, glowing very faintly through the plating like a coal under ash, was 04's core.

He looked at it for one long second.

Then he and Maggie cut it loose. They didn't open it. They didn't look inside. They shoved the whole thing, body and core and whatever was still sealed in the capsule, into a drop container meant for cargo, and kicked the container toward Earth.

On the way out, Maggie stopped at the station's reactor housing and left it a present. "Little something," she said, patting it. "For the road."

The white units caught the boy at the airlock.

They took his patched unit apart limb by limb, in the silence of vacuum. There was no sound at all, only the feeling: every limb going dead in his own body, one after another, as if someone were switching him off a piece at a time. His right arm. His left leg. His left arm. When the chest plate cracked, he did the only thing he could think of. He cut the core free. It was a sphere of saint flesh about the size of a child, heavy, still warm through his gloves. He hauled it out of the dying chassis with both arms around it, and pushed off into open space with it held against his chest.

He floated. The Earth turned below him, enormous and gray and frozen, with a long bright wound in the sky over one corner of it. The Morningstar turned above him, its guns still firing at Maggie somewhere out of sight.

The nearest thing that could still fly was one of the white units, drifting dead in the dark a hundred meters off, its capsule open and empty. He kicked toward it. He climbed in, core and all, and forced a link over whatever was driving it.

It fought him. There was no assimilation and no nature, nothing inside it at all but something very old and very cold that didn't want a pilot and had never wanted one, like trying to put on a coat made of ice. He held on anyway, with his teeth clenched and the core in his lap.

Behind him, the Morningstar was coming apart. Maggie's present had broken its spine, and the long gray cathedral was folding in the middle like a dropped sword, falling out of its orbit, its guns still firing at nothing.

He rode the white unit down through re-entry with fire on every side, the core clutched to his chest the whole way down.

He landed hard. When he climbed out, he was standing on frozen ground under a bruise-colored sky, and he saw the world.

Hakobune lay in ruins to the east, under a wound in the sky: a long vertical seam of light, pulsing faintly, like a door someone had forgotten to close. Whole districts of the city hung in the air beneath it, upside down, turning very slowly, trailing icicles longer than buildings. The Moon was cracked open, bleeding red light across the clouds. The sun was a dim white coin behind a veil of drifting debris. Frost went on in every direction, as far as he could see, over hills that had been green when he was sixteen. He was still sixteen. That was the strange part. Everything else had changed.

Then the Morningstar came down.

It fell across the sky in burning pieces, slow and enormous, the biggest of them trailing smoke from one end of the horizon to the other. It took a long time to fall. It seemed to take forever. And the largest piece, the whole central spine of it, struck the side of Mount Fuji.

The mountain vanished under fire. A long time later the sound reached him, a low roll of thunder that went on and on. When the smoke thinned, wreckage the size of city blocks lay burning across the mountain's slopes, from the snowline almost to the summit, and the fire didn't go out. He watched for an hour, and it didn't go out. It would burn a long time.

He stood there with the core in his arms and watched the morning star fall.

Maggie found him four hours later. Lizzie came limping over the ice, missing half an arm and most of its paint, and knelt in front of him with a groan of old metal. Maggie popped the capsule and leaned out and grinned down at him.

"Nice landing."

He didn't answer for a while.

Maggie went on. "Did you see where she landed?"

"I wouldn't know."

He looked down at the core in his arms. It was still warm. He had almost lost it.

"Good," the boy told it, and he climbed into Lizzie's hand without another word.

### Chapter 37: Waking

She woke up because someone was cutting her out.

Light poured into the capsule through a jagged opening, and with it came cold air, and voices: adult voices, sharp and businesslike, the voices of people doing a job they had rehearsed. A cutting torch hissed. Sparks fell on her knees. Then hands reached in and took her by the arms and pulled her out, and she couldn't stand. Her legs had forgotten how. Someone wrapped her in a thermal blanket that crackled like paper and set her down on a metal floor that hummed faintly under her, the way a ship's floor hums.

It was a ship. She was on the floor of an enormous hangar bay, dimly lit, smelling of oil and frost. And behind her, bolted into the bay with cables as thick as trees running out of it into the walls, was the thing she had been sealed inside.

It was Navis's body. Sea-blue under a skin of frost, scorched black down one side, longer than she remembered, with closed seams along its back like sleeping eyes. 04's core still glowed faintly at its chest. The core is the self, so it was Unit 04 now. The cables ran from its chest into the walls, and the ship was drinking from it. She could feel it, faintly, the way you can feel your own pulse in a cut.

Then the lights came on.

Not in the bay. Through the open bay doors at the far end, she could see out into the dark: a valley, deep and narrow, ringed with black mountains, and in the bottom of it, a town. Rooftops and scaffolding and greenhouses and long rows of shacks, crowded together around the ship like moss around a fallen tree, all of it dark. And then, as she watched, it lit. One street. Then another. Then a whole hillside of windows at once, yellow and warm, and strings of bulbs along the scaffolds, and the long glass roofs of the greenhouses glowing from inside like lanterns. The light spread out from the ship through the whole valley in a slow wave.

Outside, people were cheering. She could hear it through the bay doors, thousands of voices, ragged and hoarse. They were cheering for the lights, as if they'd never seen them lit before. They hadn't.

"How long?" Val asked. Her voice came out cracked, like a door that hadn't been opened in years.

Nobody answered. The people in the bay went on working, stepping around her. Then a man stepped through the crowd, and she knew him and didn't.

Alex. Much older. Gray at his temples, new scars across his cheek and jaw, deep lines at the corners of his eyes that hadn't been there. The same empty sleeve, pinned up below the elbow. He didn't smile. He had always smiled; she had never seen his face do anything else when it looked at her. It didn't now. In his hand he held a thin silver collar.

"Years," he said. "Long enough."

Seven, she would find out later. Seven years.

He knelt and fastened the collar around her throat himself, one-handed, and it clicked shut, cold against her skin. "This is a Halo," he said. "If your sync spikes, it cuts the link and you go soft. I hold the trigger." He held up a small black switch, and his hand did not shake. "You don't pilot another exo. Welcome to WAKE."

He stood up. He walked away. Nobody else came close. The crew watched her from a distance, all of them, the way people watch a fire that might still be burning.

Val looked down at her hands. She hadn't aged a day. They were the same hands. She was still sixteen.

"Where's..." Her voice cracked. She tried again, louder, at Alex's back as he walked away across the bay. "Where's April? Where's 04?"

She didn't understand. She didn't know yet what all that time had done inside that core, what had melted into what, what was left. She only knew that she had been holding April in a white room, and now she was holding nothing.

Alex stopped. He didn't turn around.

"Sorry," he said.

And then he was gone, through a hatch, and the hatch closed.

On a monitor above the bay doors, a news fragment was looping, scavenged from some survivor broadcast, the sound tinny and full of static. A woman's tired voice played over footage of a ruined city. Other voices answered her, street interviews from some ruined market, cut together.

"Did you hear what happened?"

"They say it was the Kane girl."

"Her and that Beast of hers."

"Her father loved the city. She burned the sky."

At nine, she had listened to people talk about her father. Now she was the story.

### Chapter 38: Seus

The second person who came to see her was a ghost.

He walked into the bay in a WAKE flight suit with his hands in his pockets, limping, with fresh burns down one side of his neck, shiny and pink. He was sixteen. He was exactly the age he had been the night he died, with exactly the same face, the same jaw that was Patrick's jaw, the same frown. And there was a black patch over his left eye. He stopped in front of her and looked down.

"Hey, genius," Seus said.

Val stared at him. She couldn't make her mouth work. She couldn't make anything work.

"Alex pulled me out," he said, as if she'd asked. "Before Umbral got to the wreck. I wasn't all the way gone. You were busy screaming, I guess. He kept me in a basement clinic for a year and a half, and I woke up after." His face didn't change. "Penemue left a piece of himself in me when Navis grabbed him. It's behind this." He tapped the eyepatch with one finger. "Sometimes it talks. Mostly it just sits there. I don't get older anymore. Like you." He shrugged. "Their flesh keeps you, once it's in you past the skin. I've got a piece behind my eye. You were inside a whole core of it."

"You're alive," Val whispered.

"Yeah. Lucky me." He jerked his chin at the scorched blue body bolted into the wall behind her. "You know where that was? In orbit. On the Morningstar, sealed up like a relic, with you inside it. Umbral's station. The Conclave needed it whole: it's the only exo anybody ever grew out of the Root, and both swords were stuck in it, the one that opened the door for you and the one that shut it. Keys don't come out of a lock like that easy. They spent seven years cutting them out. By the time they were done, they'd grown themselves a new body out of the Root, and they stopped caring who was aboard. Just guards on Proxy. Somebody had to go up and get this ship's power source." He spread his hands. "And look who came with it. Luck, I presume?"

He touched the burns on his neck.

"They tore my unit apart doing it. I fell out of the sky holding its core in my arms. I almost lost it. I almost lost me. Again." His voice went flat and hard. "For you."

He told her the rest the way you'd read out a casualty list, standing over her, not sitting down.

"Navis is dead," he said. "You killed him in the Almost, when you reached past him for her. He burned out in your hands." He jerked his thumb at the wall. "That's his body, with 04's core in it. That's what they call the Beast out there. That's what you were inside." He looked at it the way you look at a house someone else lives in now. "The one I fly is new. A blank core I woke up next to. It talks like Navis, because I haven't changed. It isn't Navis."

Whatever had been Navis, the voice that said *Was that good?* to him first, was gone. The unit he'd flown since he woke up was salvage, and he had never given it a name. After today, it was a core in a crate waiting for a new body.

Unit 06, the silver one that had come down out of the white sky and put a sword through Navis, was gone too. Nobody knew where.

"Nobody's seen the silver one since," Seus said.

His father. He had woken up in a world on fire and gone straight to his father, walked eleven days across the frost to the ruins of headquarters to do it, and stood in front of his father's desk. His father had looked at him for a long time and said nothing at all. And then he'd had security walk him out.

"He's still there," Seus said. "In the ruins. Still taking calls."

Umbral had split in two after the sky broke. Half of it stayed with Patrick, in what was left of headquarters, still taking orders from the voices that never showed their faces; Patrick's half kept the name, and the Morningstar. The other half followed Alex out into the mountains and called itself WAKE.

"Like the thing you hold for the dead," Seus said. "When you sit up all night with the body. Alex's idea. He says somebody has to sit up with the world."

And the ship they were standing in was the Vigil. An old Umbral hull WAKE had taken with them in the split. It had never flown. Nothing they had was strong enough to lift it. So they had built a town around it instead, in the bottom of an old caldera in the mountains, where the ground was still warm from the hot springs underneath and things could grow if you put a roof over them. Ten thousand people lived in that town now. Alex had named it Ararat.

"Where the ark came to rest," Seus said. "He's got a thing about names now."

Since the split, Ararat had run on scavenged generators and luck, and the luck had been running out. Then WAKE intercepted a set of Umbral station logs and found a working core aboard the Morningstar, the strongest core anyone had ever measured.

"That's why we went up there," Seus said. "Not for you. For that." He looked past her at the glowing chest of the thing in the wall. "That's what's keeping the lights on out there. Every greenhouse. Every heater. Ten thousand people, running on your exo."

"Is April..." Val started.

"There's no April." Seus said it without cruelty, just tired. "Whatever you think is in there isn't. Seven years up there with Penemue's leftovers soaking into it. Saint, giant, human, all melted together into one lump. The engineers say it's a battery now. Just a battery. Whatever you left in there, it's gone."

Val opened her mouth, and he cut her off.

"Don't. I'm not done." His hands were shaking in his pockets; she could see them through the fabric. "In the port. In Navis. I told you it was okay. I meant it. I died for you, Kane, and I was okay with it, I'd made my peace with it, I said goodbye, I said thank you for the dinner. All you had to do was choose. Save me or kill me. I didn't care which. Either one, I'd have taken it from you." His voice cracked down the middle. "And you stood there. You stood there with your hands down and you let my father's button do it, so you'd never have to. So it would be bad luck. So it wouldn't be yours."

He leaned down until his face was close to hers. His one eye was red at the rim.

"Then the first time you ever chose anything, the first time in your whole life, you chose 04. In my exo. And you burned the world." He straightened up. "Yuki held the hangar for you so you could do it. Did anybody tell you that? Four in the morning, in that old white unit, until there was nothing left of her." He looked away, at the wall. "You finally did something, Kane. You don't get to call this one bad luck."

He left. His limp echoed across the bay floor, uneven, until the hatch closed behind him.

Val sat alone on the cold floor with her blanket and her Halo.

Everyone was saying it. The broadcast on the monitor, looping. The crew, not saying it, with their faces. Seus, saying it to her face. *The Kane girl. Her and her Beast.* She held on to the one thing she had, the one sentence she had carried back from the vision in a voice she trusted without knowing why.

*04 did this.*

Her father's machine. Her father's rage, buried in a core. Not her.

The voice in the vision hadn't finished. *And—*, it had said, and then nothing. Later, under the crackling blanket, she finished it for him. *And it wasn't you.* *And you couldn't have known.* They fit. They sounded like something a kind person would say. She decided that was what it meant.

She held on to it the way she used to hold on to luck.

### Chapter 39: Where Is Everyone?

She asked about them the next morning. Akari Harada. Haruto Sakai. Nanami Fujita. Three kids from Hakobune Municipal No. 3, Class 2-A.

The crew member she asked, a young engineer with frostbite scars across her knuckles, looked at her strangely, as if she'd asked about people in a book, and went to check. An hour later the answer came back on a scrap of paper. No records. The shelter registries had been lost when the districts lifted. Most of the lower city had been evacuated into the hills in the first days, in no order, on foot. Nobody knew who had made it out of the city and who hadn't. Nobody had known since.

She asked to go down to Hakobune.

Alex refused. He did it through someone else, a lieutenant who came to the bunk room where they'd put her and said, "The commander says no," and left before she could answer.

She asked again. Same lieutenant. Same answer.

On the fourth day, a salvage run went out into the ruins, three trucks with chains on their tires and plows welded to their fronts, to strip copper out of the dead districts. She climbed onto the back of the last truck under a tarp while the crew pretended not to see. They weren't willing to stop her. They were scared of her. They were scared of Alex too. Between the two fears, they found it easiest to look at the mountains.

Hakobune was a frozen ruin.

Whole districts still hung in the air where the Almost had left them, upside down, turning so slowly you could only see it if you watched one for a long time. Icicles hung from their foundations, longer than buildings, dripping, and the drips froze before they hit the ground, so the streets below were full of thin glass spikes like a forest of needles. The streets were empty. Snow lay in drifts against doorways nobody had opened in years. A vending machine stood on a corner with its lights still faintly on, running on some battery no one had ever come back to switch off, humming to nobody.

She found the school by the shape of its gym roof.

The building was half buried in frost, its windows white and blind. She broke a door. She walked through corridors she had walked through every day, under the same posters, past the same shoe lockers with the same names on them, frozen. She walked through classrooms with the desks still in rows, chairs pushed in, as if everyone had just stepped out for an evacuation drill and would be right back.

Class 2-A. Her old desk by the window was still there. Two rows over, so was Yuki's, with frost grown across its lid in long fern shapes. She didn't touch that one. She lifted the lid of her own.

Her pencil case was still inside it. And in the pencil case, the pencil. The scuffed metal one, with the worn logo on its side. She had dropped it in there the morning Alex came to the classroom door, the morning of the port, the morning Seus died, and she had never taken it out again. She had felt it there under the lid every day for weeks, like something left burning on a stove, and never opened the desk.

She took it out now. It was ice-cold. She held it until it wasn't.

It was the only thing from the world she knew before.

The shelter under the school was sealed with ice. She broke that door too, with a fire extinguisher from the hall, which took a long time. When it finally gave, she went down the stairs with the cold coming up to meet her, into the room where the students of Class 2-A had sat in rows on the floor and watched the city go dark and come back.

There was no one inside. There was nothing inside. The floor was swept. The benches were empty. Behind her, at the top of the stairwell, the door she had broken hung open on the gray light. It was the same door Nanami had carried Kōta up to, the night of the Blackout, so he could see the stars.

She sat down on the shelter steps with her pencil in her lap.

*I wanted them to live.* That was why she had climbed into 04 every day since the river district. Not orders. Not luck. She had wanted three idiots who were good to her every day to stay alive. It was the one thing that had been hers. And she had pulled 04 out of the dark and held it and been happy. While she was happy, the world had come apart, and somewhere in it, three idiots who were good to her every day had come apart too.

*04 did this,* she told herself on the shelter steps. *And it wasn't you.*

It didn't work the way it was supposed to. She said it again, out loud this time, to the empty shelter. "04 did this." And again. And again, until it stopped sounding like words, until it was just a noise she was making in a cold room.

When the salvage crew found her, an hour after dark, she was still sitting there with the pencil turning in her fingers. They didn't say anything. They helped her up and put her in the cab of the truck where the heater worked.

She didn't speak to anyone for the rest of the day.

---

## Arc 10: Ararat

> *And that no man might buy or sell, save he that had the mark, or the name of the beast, or the number of his name.*
>
> — Revelation 13:17

### Chapter 40: Point Me at Something

The only person in Ararat who was kind to her was a girl who looked seventeen.

Maggie brought her food on a tray, three times a day, to the bunk room in the Vigil where they'd put her: a narrow steel room with a cot and a locker and a porthole that looked out over the town. Maggie didn't knock. She came in, put the tray on the cot, sat down on the floor with her back against the wall and her legs stuck out in front of her, and talked about nothing. The old songs, the ones from before the white sky, which she hummed and then sang a line of and then hummed again because she'd forgotten the rest. The weather, which was always cold. Lizzie, the cranky orange exo she flew, which she swore was the love of her life and which had bitten a mechanic last week, or at least closed a hatch on him in a way she found extremely suggestive.

She never asked Val about the Almost. Not once.

On the fourth day, Val asked her a question instead. "Why are you the only one who'll talk to me?"

Maggie just smiled. "Somebody asked me to keep an eye on you," she said. "A long time ago."

"Who?"

Maggie hummed a few bars of something old and didn't answer.

On the sixth day, Val asked her the other question. The one she'd been holding since the bay.

"Do you know where April is?" she said. "She was inside Navis. Inside the core. When I... she was there. I was holding her."

Maggie stopped humming. She looked at Val for a long time with those eyes that were much older than her face.

"Whatever's in there should be long dead by now," she said gently. "Not that I would know."

Val clutched her own chest with both hands, as if she could hold something in by pressing hard enough. She didn't make a sound. She was holding a scream down so hard that her whole body shook with it, and Maggie sat on the floor and watched her do it and didn't look away, and didn't say anything, and after a while she reached up and put her hand on Val's ankle, just rested it there, the way you'd rest a hand on a frightened animal.

After that, Val began to think.

She thought on the cot, with the porthole showing her the lights of Ararat. She thought in the mess hall, where people moved their trays away from hers when she sat down.
