She thought at the top of the Vigil's hull, where she went at night because nobody else did, and from where she could see the whole valley: the greenhouses glowing under their heat lamps, the steam rising from the hot-spring vents between the shacks, the long dark curve of the mountains, and far off to the west, Fuji, still burning on the horizon like a candle someone had forgotten to blow out.

Some nights she tried new endings on the vision's sentence, the way you try keys in a lock. *04 did this. And you were only holding her.* *04 did this. And it would have happened anyway.* None of them turned.

She never tried any on the other one. A white unit at the hangar doors at four in the morning, and a flat voice on the channel saying her whole name, and Val going past it, toward a girl in a white room she had then lost anyway. There was no sentence for that. She didn't let herself start one.

At nine, she had felt nothing. She had decided, somewhere before she could remember deciding anything, that feeling was dangerous, and she had stopped. That was what people had always seen in her: the flat face, the flat voice, the head on the table. A girl who was empty because empty was safe.

She wasn't empty now. That was the problem. She felt everything. She felt Seus's voice, calm on the comm. She felt April's arms around her neck. She felt the white room going gray. She felt the three empty desks in Class 2-A, the shelter with nobody in it, the pencil in her fist. She felt all of it all the time, and it was like living with her hand on a stove.

And she knew one thing, whatever the vision said, whatever she told herself on the shelter steps. The one time in her life she had done something because she wanted to, really wanted to, wanted it so much she stopped holding anything back, she had reached into the dark for April, and the sky had opened. That was what wanting did. When she wanted something, people died.

So she would stop wanting.

Not the old way. She couldn't go back to the old way; she'd tried, on the cot, and it didn't work anymore, the door to that room was gone. The new way. She would feel everything, and she would do nothing with any of it. She would let someone else decide. Someone else could point, and she would go where she was pointed, and whatever happened after that wouldn't be because she wanted it.

On the eighth day, she walked across Ararat to the command post.

It was an old inn on the hill above the caldera, a rambling wooden building from before 2044, with paper screens and sliding doors and a view of the whole valley. Alex had his office in what had been the best guest room, a long tatami room with the low table pushed against the wall and maps pinned over the paper screens. He was sitting at the table when she came in, with a tablet in his hand and a cold cup of tea, and he looked up, and his face did nothing at all.

Above the table, among the maps, someone had pinned a single postcard: a lighthouse, white against a blue sea, its corners gone soft with handling. Val had seen it before, on a wall of hundreds in mission control, under Aoi Sena's tapping finger. She didn't ask. Nobody in Ararat said that name, she had noticed. Nobody said it at all.

"Point me at something," Val said.

He looked at her for a long moment. "No."

"You said I don't pilot. I heard. I'm not asking to pilot. I'm asking you to use me."

"No."

"You need pilots," Val said. "I know you do. I can hear the hangar crews talking. You lost two units last month. You're losing convoys. I'm the best pilot you have."

"You're the most dangerous thing in this valley," Alex said, very evenly. "I'm not giving you a weapon."

"Then I'll be the weapon."

He looked at her. She looked back. Neither of them said anything for a long time.

"Get out, Val," he said finally.

She went.

She came back the next day. She stood in the doorway of the long tatami room and said the same thing, in the same voice. "Point me at something." He said no. She came back the day after that. And the day after that. She came every day for eleven days, at the same hour, and stood in the same place and said the same four words, and every day he said no without looking up, and every day she turned around and walked back across Ararat to the Vigil, and the people in the street moved out of her way.

On the twelfth day, WAKE lost a convoy.

Three trucks, coming back from the coast with the winter's grain. Umbral's white units caught them in a pass. Twelve dead. The grain gone. Ararat would be on half rations by midwinter. Val heard about it in the mess hall, from the silence more than the words. Then a lieutenant came to her bunk room and said, "The commander wants you," and left.

The tatami room was full of people this time, and then it emptied, one by one, until it was just the two of them and the maps. On the low table, where the tea usually was, there was a set of technical drawings she didn't recognize.

"We've got eleven pilots," Alex said, without preamble. "We've got nine units, and two of them are scrap. Umbral has hundreds. Umbral has the white ones." He pushed the drawings toward her across the table with his one hand. "When the Morningstar came down on Fuji, it brought its guards down with it. Some of them came down whole. We've been pulling them off the mountain for months, the ones the fire didn't get. There's no pilot in them. No nature, either. Dasha guts them. She cuts out the Proxy receivers and whatever the saint left behind, and she wires in a capsule. We call them hollows."

Val looked at the drawings. "That's an exo."

"That's not an exo," Alex said. "It's a gun with a seat. There's nothing in it. Nothing to learn you, nothing to wake up, nothing to grow. It does what you tell it and that's all it does."

He picked up the Halo trigger from beside the drawings. It was the first time she'd seen it since the bay. A small black switch in his palm.

"This stays armed," he said. "Every mission. I hold it. Your sync goes one point over where I say, you go soft. No warning. No second chances. You understand?"

"Yes."

"You do exactly what you're told. You don't improvise. You don't go back for anything. You don't..." He stopped. He started again. "You do what you're told."

"Yes."

He looked at her for a long time across the table, with the trigger in his hand.

"Why are you doing this?" he asked.

She didn't answer.

He didn't push. He looked down at the drawings instead. "Lantern Team," he said. "Hangar three. Tomorrow at six. Captain Kuze. Do what he says."

She went. When the door slid shut behind her, Alex sat with his hand over his eyes for a long time, and the tea went cold, and the maps on the paper screens rustled in the draft from the valley.

### Chapter 41: Lantern

Hangar three was a converted greenhouse at the edge of the caldera, glass roof painted black, and at six in the morning there were five people in it.

Captain Hisashi Kuze stood at the front, in his fifties, with gray stubble and a WAKE jacket worn soft at the elbows. His hands had a tremor in them that stopped when he was holding a cup of coffee, so he was always holding a cup of coffee. He had a small card in his breast pocket, and as Val came in he took it out and looked at it and put it back, the way someone checks a watch.

"You're the Kane girl," he said.

"Yes."

"Good. Stand there." He pointed at an empty space in the line. She stood there.

The others were looking at her. A woman in her thirties with her hair tied up in a scarf and a ginger candy in her cheek, chewing it like she was angry at it. An enormous man, over two meters tall, with a rice cooker under one arm, and frost on him: a fine white crust that started at his left wrist and ran up his arm and across his collarbone and up the side of his neck to his jaw, glittering faintly in the hangar lights. A small, wiry girl in a sniper's coat with a notebook clipped to her belt and a pencil behind her ear, who looked at Val once, steadily, and then went back to looking at the floor. And a young man in his twenties with a string of plain wooden beads wrapped twice around his wrist, who looked at Val the way people look at something they've stepped in.

"Lantern Team," said Kuze. "WAKE's sabotage unit. Five of us, and now six. I'm going to tell you our callsigns now. That's all you need to know. You'll learn the rest if you live."

He took out the card again and read off it, without embarrassment, the way you'd read a grocery list.

"One. That's me. Two." He nodded at the woman with the scarf. "Dasha Orlova. Three." The giant. "Tomoki Oda. Big Tom. Five." The girl with the notebook. "Sae Kirishima. Six." The young man with the beads. "Gage Hart."

He looked at Val. "I'm a retired pilot," he said. "One of the lucky ones. You hear about us. We get out after the five years and we get to keep breathing, and the price of that is, things fall out of our heads. Names, mostly. Faces. I flew Unit 02, off your father's wing. I remember the unit. Some days I remember him. I'll know you're on my team. I won't always know what you're called. So I use numbers. Don't take it personally."

"I won't," said Val.

"Nobody's flown as Four since Ikeda," Kuze said. "Nobody wanted it."

"Give her Four," said Gage.

The hangar went quiet.

"It's what she is." Gage didn't raise his voice. He turned the beads on his wrist, slowly, one at a time, like a man praying. "Unit 04. The Beast's girl. My priest says she rode it down out of the sky with her own hands. Let everybody hear it every time she checks in. Lantern Four. Let it be her name."

Kuze looked at Val. "You want something else?"

Val looked at Gage. Gage looked back.

"Four's fine," she said.

Kuze took the card out of his pocket and wrote on it with a pencil stub. When he turned it over, Val saw the back. It was covered in names, in different inks and different years, most of them crossed out. The oldest, at the top, had faded almost to nothing: HAYASHI. The newest line through a name was IKEDA.

"Four," he said, and put it away.

Dasha took her to see the hollow. It stood in a bay at the far end of the hangar, white, faceless, with a fresh capsule welded into its back like a patch on a coat. Up close, Val could see where the Proxy receivers had been cut out of its skull: neat round holes, plugged with resin. Dasha talked the whole time they walked around it, fast, chewing her candy, and unwrapped a fresh one one-handed without looking, the way other people light cigarettes.

"I used to put minds in these things," she said. "Before the Almost. I worked core suppression. Third-generation design team, under Dr. Enoch, you know that name? The commander's mother. Brilliant woman. Terrible human being. We spent ten years figuring out how to quiet the saint in a core so the pilot's nature had room to grow. Now I take what's left of the saint and I just cut it out. Like coring an apple." She slapped the hollow's leg. "There's nobody home in here, Four. I made sure. No saint, no seal, either; a rifle goes straight through it, so do try not to get shot. You link, you'll feel nothing. It'll do what you tell it. It won't learn you. It won't talk to you. It won't love you." She looked at Val sideways. "That's the point, right?"

"Yes," said Val.

She climbed into the capsule. It was cold, and it smelled of resin and burned wire. The walls lit up gray. She reached for the link the way she'd reached for 04 a thousand times, the way she'd forced Navis open, and found nothing. Not a wall. Not a refusal. Nothing at all. Like reaching into a dark room for a hand and finding only air. It was like sitting inside a held breath.

She moved its arm. It moved. She moved its leg. It moved. Nothing answered. Nothing learned. Nothing was there.

*Perfect,* she thought.

At noon Big Tom came over with the rice cooker and opened it and took out a rice ball wrapped in seaweed and held it out to her. The rice cooker had been his mother's, Val found out later. He had carried it out of the city on the first day of the Almost, and nothing else.

"I'm not hungry," said Val.

"You don't have to be hungry," said Tom. He had a deep, slow voice, the kind that sounded like it was coming from the bottom of a well. "It's lunch. It's not a test."

She didn't take it. He set it down on the hangar step beside her, on a paper napkin, and went back to the others without another word. An hour later, when nobody was looking, it was gone.

In the afternoon, Seus came through hangar three with a crate of parts on his shoulder, on his way somewhere else. Kuze was reading the card again, and said "Four," and Val looked up.

Seus stopped in the doorway.

He looked at her, and at Kuze, and at the card. Then back at her.

"Four," he said.

"It's my callsign."

"I know what it is." His one eye didn't move off her. "You gave that thing a name. April. So it wouldn't have to be a number." He shifted the crate on his shoulder. "And now you're wearing its number."

"It's fine."

"Sure," said Seus. "It's fine."

He went out into the cold. Through the black glass she heard him say it once more, outside, to nobody, the way you press on a bruise to see if it still hurts. "Four."

That evening, she sat alone on the hangar steps, looking out at the valley. The greenhouses were lighting up one by one.

Someone sat down beside her. Sae, the girl with the notebook. She didn't say anything. She sat about a meter away, with her knees up and her coat around them, and looked at the same valley Val was looking at.

They sat there for an hour. Neither of them said a word.

It was the easiest hour Val had spent since she woke up.

When the briefing came, Kuze gave it in the hangar with a coffee cup in his hand and the whole team sitting on crates in front of him.

"Here's what we're for," he said. "Everybody in this valley knows Ararat runs on that core. What everybody doesn't know is that the Vigil could fly. Alex won't lift it while the town needs the power, and he's right not to. But one of these days Umbral is going to come for us. They always come. And when they do, that ship is the only way ten thousand people get out of this valley alive."

He held up three fingers.

"For the Vigil to fly, it needs three things we don't have. One: a cradle. A suppression cradle, so the core can run at full burn without waking up whatever's still in there." He didn't look at Val when he said it. Everybody else did. "Two: fresh tissue for the flight surfaces. Ours died years ago, sitting in the ground. Three: the command codes. It's an Umbral hull. It won't answer anybody without them."

He lowered his hand.

"Umbral has all three. We're going to take them. Three missions. And then this town has a way out." He looked around at them, one by one, checking the card in his head. "That's our job. That's all it is. Questions?"

There were no questions.

### Chapter 42: The Yard

The first mission was a manufacturing yard on the old coast road, in the ruins of a shipbuilding port, where Umbral grew exo chassis in long glass vats along the waterfront.

Before they went in, Kuze called the roll, reading it off the card the way he would before every mission. "One. Two. Three. Five. Six. Four." Four came last, because she was last on the card. Each of them answered. Sae answered with three clicks on the channel. Her unit had gone soft instead of her, Dasha had told her, the night of the Almost, and she hadn't said a word since. Three clicks meant *I'm here*. Two meant *contact*.

They went in at night, five hollows in a line along the shore, white against the black water. Kuze's voice was in Val's ear the whole time, calm, a little slow, calling them by number. "One moving. Two, left. Three, hold. Five, you have the high ground. Six, point." And then: "Four. With me."

She went with him. She did exactly what she was told.

The yard was full of vats, each one glowing faintly green, each one with a half-grown exo floating inside it like a fetus in an egg. Some of them had faces. Some of them were turning slowly in the fluid, and Val didn't look at them. She looked at Kuze's back, and at the target marker in the corner of her hollow's eyes, and at nothing else.

Halfway through the yard, a white unit came around the end of a row of vats. One. A patrol. Val saw it a full second before anyone else. She saw the gap behind it, too, the line through the vats she could take to get behind it and end it in two moves, faster than the plan, cleaner than the plan.

She didn't take it. She waited for orders.

"Contact," said Sae's tap on the channel, two quick clicks. And then Kuze's voice: "Six, take it." And Gage took it, a half second slower than Val would have, and it went down, and they went on.

On the far side of the yard, in a sealed warehouse, they found the cradle. It was a frame the size of a house, folded in on itself like a closed hand, gray metal and something that wasn't metal, with a stenciled plate on its side. Dasha read it aloud over the channel while she cut the restraints.

"Enoch-pattern suppression cradle, mark four." She whistled. "Huh. Hey, Commander, any relation?"

On the channel, from the command post on the hill, two hundred kilometers away, there was a silence. Long enough that Kuze looked up.

"Stay on task, Two," Alex's voice said finally.

"Staying on task," said Dasha cheerfully, and cut the last restraint.

They got it out. That was the thing that surprised everyone afterward; they got it out clean. Big Tom carried most of it on his hollow's back, staggering under the weight, singing something under his breath in a voice so low the channel turned it into a hum. Sae covered them from a crane two hundred meters up, and twice there was a single shot and something stopped moving in the dark. Gage walked point, and walked it well. Val did what she was told, every time, the moment she was told, and not one moment sooner. And at three in the morning a WAKE transport lifted off the beach with the cradle in its belly and six hollows clinging to its frame like barnacles, and nobody was dead.

On the flight home, Dasha told a joke about a priest and a saint and a vending machine that was so bad Gage laughed out loud and then looked furious about it. Tom sang the rest of his song, all the way through, and it turned out to be a lullaby. Kuze sat by the hatch with his coffee and his card and looked at each of them in turn, the way a man counts his children in a crowd.

"Four didn't screw up," Gage said to nobody, to the hull, as they came down over the mountains. It sounded like it cost him something.

In the mess hall the next morning, there was tamagoyaki on Val's tray.

She looked at it. Two slices of rolled omelette, yellow and neat, a little uneven at the edges, beside the gray rice and the pickled radish that everyone got. Nobody else's tray had any. Eggs were rationed in Ararat; there were sixty hens in the long greenhouse at the north end of town, and the eggs went to the clinic and the children.

"Eat," said Big Tom, sitting down across from her with his own tray.

"Where did you get eggs?"

He looked at her gravely. "I'm a jinn. I have powers."

She ate it. It was good. It was soft and sweet, the way Fumi used to make it, the way it had been in the box in the back of Patrick's car the day her father died. She ate both slices slowly and didn't say anything, and Tom didn't say anything either, and when she was done she sat there with the empty tray for a while.

She thought Tom had made it for her. She thought about that for a long time.

That night, in the corridor outside the mess kitchen, Alex stopped Big Tom with a hand on his arm and pressed a ration chit into his palm. A week's worth of eggs. His own.

"Mission days," Alex said. "Don't tell her."

Tom looked down at the chit. "Wasn't going to."

"Good."

Tom looked at him. He had a slow way of looking at people, as if he had all the time in the world, which was the opposite of true. "You could tell her yourself, Commander."

"No," said Alex. "I couldn't."

He walked away down the corridor. Tom watched him go, and then put the chit in his pocket.

The afternoon after the yard, Maggie was sitting on Lizzie's foot in the hangar, as if it were a park bench, when Val came through.

"Kuze says you held formation all night," she said. "Never broke once. Waited for every call."

"That's the job."

"Mm." Maggie looked up at the black glass roof. "Sam always broke formation. Always. You'd think I'd have learned." She hummed a bar of something, and stopped, and hopped down off Lizzie's foot and went off to swear at a mechanic.

Val didn't ask what she meant. She was getting good at not asking.

That evening, Val sat on the hangar steps again. Sae came and sat beside her again, a meter away, knees up. After a while, Sae unclipped her notebook and wrote something in it with the pencil from behind her ear, small and neat, and turned it so Val could see.

*You did good.*

"It was..." Val started, and stopped. The word wasn't there. It hadn't been there since the conference room. She tried another one. "It was the plan."

Sae looked at that for a moment. Then she wrote again, underneath.

*Same thing.*

Val looked at the words for a long time. Something happened at the corner of her mouth, very small, that she didn't quite let finish.

### Chapter 43: Two

It happened on a quiet day, which was the worst part.

A week after the yard, on a gray afternoon with no missions scheduled, the cradle was being installed in the Vigil's core housing, and Dasha was out on the hangar apron at the edge of the caldera with Val and a hollow, teaching her its quirks. Every hollow had them; you cut the mind out of a thing, she said, and it leaves scars, little habits in the meat. This one dropped its left shoulder before it turned. This one hesitated a fraction of a second before a jump, as if it remembered being afraid of heights.

"Grab me the torque driver," Dasha said, waving vaguely at the tool cart parked a few meters back by the hangar door. "The big one. Red handle."

Val went. Three meters. She turned her back to get it.

"And the thing nobody tells you about hollows," Dasha was saying behind her, cheerful, chewing her candy, "the thing they leave out of every manual, is that they—"

There was a sound like a branch breaking very far away.

Val turned around with the torque driver in her hand.

Dasha was on the ground, on the apron, in the spot where Val had been standing a second before. She was lying on her side with her scarf come loose and her eyes open and one hand still raised a little, as if she were about to point at something on the hollow. There was a ginger candy on the concrete next to her mouth. She didn't finish the sentence.

Nobody heard the end of it.

Sae was there before Val had finished turning around, out of nowhere, slamming into her and dragging her down behind the tool cart with a strength that didn't fit her size. Up on the apron, Big Tom roared, a sound with no words in it, and his frost cracked across his neck. Gage was firing at the hills, the far ridge, where something white had glinted and was already gone. Kuze's voice came over every channel at once, calm, slow: "Sniper. North ridge. Everyone down. Everyone down. Six, stop shooting, it's gone."

It was gone. One shot, from a long-range white unit on a ridge four kilometers away, and then it was gone over the crest, and the hills were empty.

Val lay behind the cart with Sae's arm across her chest, holding the torque driver.

Afterward, they stood around Dasha's body in a loose circle while the medics came, though there was nothing for the medics to do. Val was still holding the torque driver. She didn't know how to put it down. She kept thinking, very clearly, very calmly: *If she hadn't asked for it. If I hadn't turned around. If I'd been standing where I was standing.*

"Everybody who stands next to you dies," Gage said. He said it quietly. He wasn't shouting. That was worse. "You know that, right? Everybody. Every single one."

"Six," said Kuze. "Enough."

Gage turned and walked away toward the hangar, turning his beads.

The cradle installation went on without Dasha. It had to; the Vigil didn't care who was dead. But nobody else in Ararat knew how to gut a hollow. They had four left, and they would have to make them last.

That night Val didn't go to the hangar steps. She stayed in her bunk, in the narrow steel room, with the porthole showing her the lights of the town, and she didn't sit next to anyone. She decided, lying there, that she wouldn't anymore. That was the lesson, and Gage had said it out loud, and it was true. Dasha, on the apron. Yuki, at the hangar doors, seven years ago, holding them for her. *Everybody who stands next to you dies.* So she wouldn't let anyone stand next to her.

In the morning, Kuze called the roll in hangar three, reading off the card in his palm.

"One," he said. "Two—"

He stopped. He looked at the card for a long time. Then he took out the pencil stub and drew a line through something, carefully, and went on.

"Three. Five. Six. Four."

Nobody said anything about the gap. Sae clicked three times on the clip of her notebook. Gage said "Here" to the floor. There was a new bead on his wrist, paler than the others, the wood still raw where he had cut it.

Val said "Here," and went to work.

She went to the hangar steps at dusk anyway, because it was where she went, and sat at the very end of them, as far from anywhere as she could get.

Sae came and sat beside her anyway. A meter away. Knees up. Looking at the valley.

Val didn't tell her to leave. She didn't know how.

---

## Arc 11: Lanterns

> *And I heard a voice from heaven saying unto me, Write, Blessed are the dead which die in the Lord from henceforth: Yea, saith the Spirit, that they may rest from their labours; and their works do follow them.*
>
> — Revelation 14:13

### Chapter 44: The Depot

Big Tom sat down across from her at breakfast three days before the second mission, the way he always did, with his tray and his rice cooker, and this time he talked.

"The relic's in here," he said, and tapped his chest, right over the sternum, where the frost was thickest. It made a sound like knocking on a window. "Got it when I was nine. Graft Program, the last batch, the year before they shut it down. I was in the facility when your father came." He said it plainly, without any weight on it. "I was in the basement, in a tank. He didn't know I was there. That's the only reason."

Val put down her chopsticks.

"Every year the frost gets a little higher," Tom went on. "Doc says by thirty it'll reach my heart, and that'll be that. I'm twenty-four." He took a bite of rice. "People in this valley don't like to sit near me. They think it's catching. It's not catching. Sae sits with me. Kuze sits with me, when he remembers who I am. Dasha sat with me."

"Why are you telling me?" said Val.

"Because nobody else in this valley will sit at my table," Tom said. "Except Sae, and Sae doesn't talk."

"I'm not sitting at your table."

Tom looked at her with his slow, patient look. "You're sitting at my table right now."

She was. She looked down at it. At some point in the last two weeks, without deciding to, she had started sitting there.

"I know what they say about you," Tom said. "The Beast's girl. The one who burned the sky. They used to say worse about me. In the facility, they called us the specimens." He shrugged his enormous frosted shoulder. "People need somebody to be the monster, Four. It's easier than being scared of the sky. Doesn't mean you have to agree with them."

He pushed the rice cooker across the table toward her. There was one rice ball left in it, wrapped in seaweed.

She took it.

The second mission was an Umbral depot in the north, built on what had been an airport, where the belt shipments came down. Crates of saint tissue from the harvesting stations, packed in cryo, landed on the old runways by heavy shuttles and stacked in long refrigerated warehouses to wait for the vats. The Vigil's flight surfaces were grown tissue, the way an exo's skin was, and years in the ground had killed them. They needed living flesh to graft in. They needed a lot of it.

They went in at dawn, through the snow, four hollows and Sae's long-barreled rifle on a ridge above the runways. Kuze on the channel, calm. "One moving. Three, the warehouse. Four, with Three. Six, the door. Five, eyes up."

Val went with Tom. They got into the warehouse through the cargo doors, which were enormous and slow, and they found the tissue: rows of steel crates, frosted white, humming, stacked to the ceiling. Tom started loading his hollow's arms. Val started loading hers. The transport was coming down onto the apron outside, rotors thumping.

Then the alarms went.

Conclave troops in black armor, coming out of a hangar at the far end of the runway, more of them than there should have been. White units behind them, three, four, unfolding off a shuttle like moths. And the main cargo door, the one the transport needed, the only door wide enough, began to close. The mechanism was automatic. Somebody, somewhere, had pressed a button, and the door was coming down, a slab of steel a meter thick, sliding down out of its housing like a guillotine.

Val saw the mechanism before anyone else did. A gray steel box on the wall beside her, at her end of the warehouse, where the door's cables ran up into the ceiling. Ten meters from her hollow's hand. Two steps. She could have torn it out of the wall before the door was halfway down.

Nobody had told her to.

"Door," said Kuze. "Somebody get the door."

*Somebody* wasn't a number. Val waited for hers.

Tom got the door.

He drove his hollow into the gap and caught the slab on its shoulders. The hollow's knees buckled. Steel screamed. The door kept coming, slowly, pressing the hollow down toward the floor. Its back cracked. Its arms began to fold.

So Tom climbed out of it.

Val watched him do it through her hollow's eyes, from the far end of the warehouse, where she was still holding two crates of tissue. He climbed out of the capsule onto the hollow's buckling shoulders, enormous, glittering, and he put both his hands flat against the underside of the door, and he pushed.

The frost went out of him all at once. It spread from his chest down his arms and into the steel, white and fast, and across the door and into the mechanism and down into the tracks, everywhere at once, until the whole doorway was furred with ice. The door shuddered. It stopped. Tom stood under it with his arms up, holding it, the frost climbing his neck, his jaw, his face.

"Four," said Kuze. "On the transport. Now."

"No—"

"Four. Now."

She went. She did what she was told. She carried the crates out under the door, past Tom, close enough to see that his eyes were still open and his breath was still smoking in the cold, and onto the transport, and she turned around in the hatch.

"Go on," Tom said on the channel. His voice was very slow now, slower than usual, as if it were coming from the bottom of a well that was filling up. "I'll catch up."

"Tom—"

"Four." It was the only time he ever used her callsign instead of not using anything. "Don't waste it."

The transport lifted. Val stood in the open hatch with the wind tearing at her and watched the depot fall away below. The door was still open. The troops were coming down the runway toward it. And under the door, holding it, was a statue of a man, white from head to foot, frozen solid, arms raised, like something carved out of ice for a festival and left standing in the snow.

He didn't catch up.

The tissue was grafted into the Vigil's flight surfaces over the next week. Val didn't go to see it.

At night, on the cot, she tried the sentence again. She had been trying endings on it for weeks, the way you try keys in a lock. *04 did this. And it wasn't you.* *04 did this. And you couldn't have known.* Tonight she tried a new one.

*And you did what you were told.*

It was true. She had. She lay in the dark and waited for it to fit.

She kept seeing a gray box on a wall, ten meters from her hand.

In the mess hall, three days later, there was tamagoyaki on her tray again.

She looked at it for a long time. It was different. It was burnt along one edge, black and bitter, and it was torn down the middle, as if whoever had rolled it had done it with one hand and given up halfway. It was nothing like Tom's. She thought the mess must have a new cook. She thought it must be someone who didn't know how. She thought, *Tom's gone, and so the eggs should have stopped, and somebody in the kitchen just hasn't been told yet.*

She ate it anyway. Every bite. It was bad. She ate all of it.

### Chapter 45: The Archive

The third mission was the one that mattered most, and everyone knew it.

The command codes. Without them, the Vigil was ten thousand tons of grown steel that would never leave the ground. After the Almost, Patrick's Umbral had moved everything that survived of headquarters' records into a hardened archive in the hills east of the ruins, an old government vault dug into the side of a mountain, and the codes were in there. So was everything else Umbral had ever wanted to keep and hide: personnel files, test records, decades of paper.

Kuze briefed it in the hangar with his coffee cup and his card. He read the card first, the way he always did now.

"One." A pause, where Two used to be. Another, longer, where Three used to be. "Five. Six. Four."

"Two transports. A and B. A takes the low route out, through the pass, fast, with half the haul. B takes the high route, slow, with the codes. One, Five and Six on B. Four rides A." He checked his card again and frowned at it, as if something on it had moved. "A. Four on A."

Gage looked at Val and didn't say anything.

Sae didn't look at anyone. She sat on her crate with her rifle across her knees and her notebook in her lap, and she was looking at the map on the wall, at the low route through the pass, at a ridge halfway along it marked with a small red circle. The circle was in her handwriting. She had scouted the pass three days before, and she had written it up that same night, the way she wrote everything up, in her small neat hand: *Battery on the north ridge. Two guns, maybe three. Low route not safe.* She had given the page to Kuze. Kuze had read it, and nodded, and put it in the tray on his desk with the others.

It wasn't on his card. It had fallen out of his head, the way names did. He stood in front of her circle and briefed the low route as if it weren't there.

That night, Sae went to the operations office in the command post while it was empty. She found the transport manifest on the duty officer's desk. She crossed out one callsign and wrote in another, in her small neat hand, and initialed it the way Kuze initialed things, badly, with a shaky line. Then she went back to her bunk.

She didn't tell anyone. She couldn't have told anyone, even if she'd wanted to; she didn't have the voice to. She'd lost it seven years ago, in the Almost, when her exo's nature had screamed through the link and died while she was still inside it. Nearly every pilot linked that night had gone soft. Her unit had gone soft instead of her, and she had come back missing something, and the something was her voice. She could have written it down again. She wrote everything down. She had written the ridge down, and it hadn't stayed. She didn't write this down.

At the boarding ramp the next morning, Kuze read the manifest off the clipboard, squinting. "Four. Transport B."

Val went to transport B. She went where she was told. She didn't look at the clipboard. She didn't ask why it had changed. She got on board and strapped in and looked at the floor.

Across the apron, Sae was climbing into transport A. She looked back once, at B, at the hatch where Val had just gone in. Then she turned and went inside.

The archive was a long fight in the dark. It took the four of them six hours to get through the outer doors, and two more to find the vault, and when they found it, it was bigger than they'd thought: a cavern, cut into the rock, with shelves running off into blackness farther than their lights could reach, and on every shelf, boxes. Thousands of boxes. Paper, in a world that had mostly forgotten paper.

The codes were in a steel case on a shelf marked *VESSEL REGISTRY*, exactly where the intelligence said they'd be. Kuze took them. Then he looked down the shelf, at the boxes on either side of the case, a whole row of them, unlabeled and labeled and old and new.

"Take everything on this shelf," he said. "All of it. We'll sort it at home. Whatever's next to the codes, somebody wanted it kept quiet."

So they took everything on that shelf. Gage loaded the boxes into his hollow's arms without looking at them. One of them was older than the rest, a different color, soft with age, with a split down one side that somebody had taped shut. Its label said KANE, S. Gage didn't read it. Val, loading the box beside it, didn't see it.

They split at the vault mouth. Transport A went low, through the pass, fast, with Sae on board and half the haul. Transport B went high, slow, with the codes and the rest of the boxes, and Val, and Kuze, and Gage.

Halfway through the pass, at the ridge with the small red circle, transport A met the anti-aircraft battery Sae had found three days before.

On the channel, from transport A, there were three clicks. Small and even, unhurried. Sae's tap for *I'm here.*

Val saw it from the high route. A line of light rising out of the ridge, and then another, and then a flower of fire in the pass below, very bright against the snow, and then a long column of black smoke going straight up into the still air, and then nothing. The fire burned in the pass for a long time. Nobody on transport B said anything. Kuze sat by the hatch with his coffee and his card and didn't look at either of them. On his desk in the command post, under three days of other paper, there was a page in Sae's small neat hand.

"Another one," said Gage, at last, to the hull. "Another one." He was turning his beads. There were two new ones on the string, paler than the rest, and by morning there would be three.

When she got back to the Vigil that night, there was a page from a notebook folded under the thin pillow on her cot. Small, neat handwriting, pencil.

*Your turn.*

Val sat on the cot with the page in her hands. She read it over and over. *Your turn.* She went to the operations office in the dark and found the manifest, and saw her own callsign crossed out on A, and Sae's written in, in that small neat hand, with Kuze's initials forged badly underneath.

Sae had known. Sae had seen the ridge. Sae had taken her seat.

It was the only message anyone had ever left her that she actually received. It came from the one person who couldn't speak.

### Chapter 46: Rice

Nobody scheduled it. Four nights after the pass, Kuze came to the door of her bunk room and looked at her, and at his card, and back at her.

"Four," he said. "Dinner. Hangar three."

Tom's rice cooker was still on the hangar step where he had left it the morning of the depot. Nobody had moved it. Nobody had wanted to be the one. Gage was crouched beside it when she came in, frowning at the buttons as if they had insulted him. Maggie took it away from him and washed the rice in a bucket of hot-spring water, humming. When Gage asked how she knew how, she said she'd been making rice since before he was born, which was true.

Seus came in halfway through, said he wasn't staying, and sat down on a crate by the door with his arms folded. He stayed.

There were three empty crates. Nobody sat on them.

Before they ate, Kuze took out his card. "One," he said. Then he stopped, and looked at the card for a long time, at the lines on it, at the spaces. "Six," he said. "Four." He put it away.

Gage said the names. Not the numbers: the names. *Dasha. Tom. Sae.* He said them to the rice, with his head down and his beads in his fingers, in the voice he used for his priest, and nobody laughed at him. Kuze said them after him, slowly, one at a time, like a man copying something down.

Val said one more, under her breath, a name nobody at the table had ever known. *Yuki.*

The rice was burnt on the bottom. Tom had had a trick for that, and he had never told anyone what it was. They ate it anyway. The burnt part too.

Maggie watched Val eat with her chin on her knees. "You fly like him, you know," she said. "Your dad. Same hands. Same stupid posture." She pointed her chopsticks at Val. "Except you hold formation. He never held it. Not once." She rubbed her shoulder, the way you touch an old scar without noticing.

"So why'd you keep flying next to him?" said Gage.

"Good question." She grinned at him. "Yet here I am, next to another one."

Gage looked at her, and then at Val, and then down at his beads, and didn't say anything.

When the pot was nearly empty, Val pressed the last of the rice into a ball the way Tom had, badly, and set it on a paper napkin on the crate beside Seus. He looked at it. He looked at her. He didn't take it.

When he'd gone, an hour later, so had the rice ball.

Kuze fell asleep sitting up on his crate with his coffee cup in both hands. Maggie put her own jacket over him. Val sat on the hangar step with the rice cooker in her lap, still warm, and listened to Gage in the dark, turning his beads, one by one, three new ones among the old.

---

## Arc 12: Ararat Falls

> *And the ark rested in the seventh month, on the seventeenth day of the month, upon the mountains of Ararat.*
>
> — Genesis 8:4

### Chapter 47: The Chair

Alex sorted the archive haul himself.

He did it at night, in the long tatami room in the command post on the hill, with the maps rustling on the paper screens and a lamp on the low table and the valley dark below the windows except for the greenhouses. Nobody asked him to. Nobody else wanted to. It was the kind of job he gave himself these days, alone, after midnight, so he wouldn't have to sleep.

Most of it was nothing. Personnel files for people long dead. Maintenance logs. Requisition forms for things that hadn't existed in years. He read each one anyway, slowly, one-handed, turning the pages with his thumb.

The box was at the bottom of the third crate.

He knew it before he'd fully turned it over. He knew the color, the softness of the cardboard, the split down one side taped shut with records-office tape. He knew the label: KANE, S. — OUTGOING — HOLD. And the red ink underneath it: *Per Conclave directive. Do not release. Do not destroy.*

There was a filing tag tied around it with archive string, newer than the box: *VESSEL REGISTRY,* the shelf the codes had been on, and under that a set of initials in small upright pen. *P.M.* Somebody had walked this box down into a mountain and put it on the one shelf in that vault WAKE was sure to empty.

And on top of the lid, faded almost to white, curled at one corner, still stuck there after seven years and a war and a vault in a mountain, was a yellow sticky note in round handwriting he'd have known anywhere.

*For Val. Do it right.*

He sat back on his heels on the tatami. He sat there for a long time.

He had last seen that handwriting on a grocery list stuck to his refrigerator, the morning of the raid.

He remembered the night of the Almost the way he remembered his arm. Not as a memory. As a shape that was missing.

He had run out of headquarters into the courtyard, and looked up, and seen the sky open. And then he had turned and run back inside, because Aoi had said *I'm going down* on the internal line and then the line had gone dead. He had taken the service stairs down past the last lit floor, down past where the maps said the building ended, to a door that wasn't on any maps. He had a key to it that wasn't on any key ring. They both did. They had made the room together, in the week after the port: an old sublevel medical bay that nobody used anymore, a bed, a monitor, an IV stand. Aoi had signed the recovery report that said *unrecoverable* so nobody would come looking. And every night for months, one of them had gone down there and sat beside the bed, and held the hand of a boy who never woke up, and told him about their day.

When Alex got there, Aoi was already inside, unhooking Seus's lines one by one with steady hands, trying to get his bed to the freight lift. The whole building was shaking. Dust was coming down from the ceiling in long gray sheets.

"Help me," she said, without looking up.

He helped. They got the lines off. They got the bed moving. And then the ceiling came down.

Not all of it. Just one beam, one long steel beam from the floor above, coming down at an angle across the room and pinning Aoi's legs to the floor beside the bed. She didn't scream. She made a sound like the wind going out of her. Alex was on his knees beside her before the dust had settled, pulling at the beam with his one hand, and it didn't move. It didn't move at all.

"Take him," Aoi said.

"No. I'm not leaving you."

"Alex." She took his face in her hands, both hands, filthy with dust, and made him look at her. "He's sixteen. You can carry one of us. You can carry him. You can't carry me."

"I'll come back."

"Then come back," she said. "Take him."

Then, softer, with something almost like a smile: "You chose the city over him once. Choose him over me. Say no to me, Alex. One more time. Take him."

He carried Seus up eleven flights of stairs with one arm. Seus over his shoulder, limp, heavy as wet sand, the IV port still taped to his arm. Alex didn't remember most of the climb. He remembered the stairwell lights going out on the seventh floor and climbing the last four in the dark by feel. He remembered coming out into the courtyard and laying Seus down on the frozen ground under a white sky. He remembered turning around.

He went back down. The stairwell was gone below the fourth floor. Just gone. Fire, and the sound of the building folding in on itself, floor by floor, like a fist closing.

He stood in the courtyard with Seus at his feet and looked up at the sky, at the seam of light opening down the middle of it, at the districts lifting, at the boy-shaped thing falling out of the Morningstar with a burning sword. And that was what he had been making sure of. That was the whole of it.

He had never told Seus. Seus thought Alex had dragged him out of the port, before the wreck was recovered. Seus didn't know about the room, or the beam, or who had held his hand for all those months. Alex had never found a way to say it that didn't sound like a bill.

He opened the box.

Postcards. Letters. Bundled by year in rubber bands that had gone brittle and snapped when he touched them. A lighthouse on top. He didn't read them. He couldn't. He turned the bundles over in the lamplight, one by one, eighty or ninety of them, 2075 to 2082, every one addressed to a little girl on the east side of a city that didn't exist anymore, every one stamped HELD.

At the bottom of the box, under the last bundle, there was a folder.

It wasn't a letter. It was a test record, gray Umbral stationery, the kind with a crest on every page. *Door Project, Test 2075-11. Subject: Dr. S. Yukimura.* A list of procedures. A list of readings. A single line at the end: *Subject lost. Mind not recovered.* And at the bottom, in the approval box, a signature he knew better than his own, because he had been forging it on permission slips since he was eight.

*Approved: Dr. H. Enoch.*

And under that, in the same red ink as the label on the box: *Per Conclave directive 4-17.*

His mother had signed off on the test that killed Val's mother.

He closed the folder. He put it back in the box. He put the bundles back on top of it, in order, by year. He put the lid on. He put the yellow note back on the lid and smoothed it flat with his thumb. Then he picked up the Halo trigger from where it sat on the corner of the table, where it always sat, and turned it over in his palm.

He knew her sync numbers better than he knew his own pulse. He watched them every mission. When Dasha died, her sync had spiked so high, so fast, that his thumb had gone to the switch on its own and stopped a millimeter above it. When Tom froze in the depot door, it had happened again. And again, in the pass, watching transport A burn. Three times. Three times he had held her life a millimeter from his thumb.

Strong feeling spiked sync. Everybody knew that. It was the first thing they taught you. And here, in this box, were eighty letters from her father, who she thought had never written to her, who she thought was a murderer. And under them, her mother's death, with his own mother's name on it.

He didn't know what it would do to her. He knew what it would do to her numbers.

*When the Halo comes off,* he told himself. *Then.* He had told himself that for months. WAKE's council would never vote the Halo off. He knew that too.

And there was Aoi's note. *Do it right.* There was no right way anymore. Aoi had wanted to sit Val down at Sunday dinner with soup, together, gently, and Aoi was under four floors of rubble in the ruins of headquarters, and every time Alex looked at Val's face, he saw the sky that had come down on Aoi.

He hated that. He hated himself for it. Val was sixteen. She had been sixteen for seven years. None of it was her fault, and all of it was, and he couldn't look at her without seeing both of those at once.

The screen slid open. Maggie came in without knocking, the way she came into everywhere, with two cups of tea. She saw the box on the table and stopped.

She knew it. Alex could see her know it. She put the tea down.

"Tell her," Maggie said.

"I can't."

"You can. You just won't."

"If I tell her about the letters," Alex said, "I have to tell her where they were. I have to tell her about the box. About Aoi. About Seus, and who got him out. About my mother." He laughed, and it wasn't a laugh. "And every time I look at her, Mags, I see the sky that killed Aoi. I don't want to see it. I hate that I see it. She's a kid."

"She's been a kid for seven years," Maggie said. "So's he. So am I, kind of." She picked up her tea and looked at him over it. "Somebody has to talk to her eventually, Alex. Somebody has to actually say something. Out loud. With words."

"Go to bed, Mags."

She went. At the screen, she stopped and looked back.

"Sam couldn't cross a street either, you know," she said. "To see her. Not once. He stood on the other side of the road and watched her come out of school and he never crossed it." She slid the screen shut behind her. "Just so you know whose footsteps you're in."

At four in the morning, Alex was in the Vigil's mess kitchen, alone, in the dark, with one light on over the stove. He cracked eggs one-handed into a bowl, picking out the shell with chopsticks, swearing at it under his breath. He poured the egg into the pan in thin layers and tried to roll it, the way Aoi had shown him years ago, the way the nanny had done it in a house on the east side every morning of Val's childhood, the way Tom had done it every mission day for weeks. It tore. It always tore. He did it anyway.

The night cook came in at five and found him plating it.

"Mission day," Alex said. "Her tray."

He put the box in the bottom drawer of his desk in the command post on the hill, with the note on top. After the Vigil flies, he told himself. When this is over. When the Halo comes off. When there's time.

### Chapter 48: Ararat Falls

They ran the Vigil's first systems test on a Thursday, at dusk.

Everything was in. The Enoch cradle sat around 04's core in the heart of the ship like a closed hand. The new tissue had taken in the flight surfaces, pink and raw under the old gray plating, and the engineers said it would hold. And the codes went into the command system one line at a time, read off a steel case by a lieutenant with a shaking voice, while half of Ararat stood in the caldera and watched.

The Vigil woke.

Every console on the bridge lit at once. Every light along the hull. A low sound came up out of the ship, a hum, deep enough to feel in your teeth, the sound of something very large taking its first breath in seven years. The crowd in the caldera cheered. Alex, on the bridge, put his hand flat on the command console and didn't say anything.

The codes called home.

Nobody knew it then. They found out afterward, the few who were left to find out: there had been something else folded inside the codes, a ghost, a single line that waited for the ship to wake and then reached out across the mountains and told someone exactly where it was. The people above Umbral had let the codes be stolen. Or they hadn't cared whether they were stolen, because either way, the moment the Vigil woke, it would tell them where to find the core.

The bombers came over the mountains three hours later, just after full dark.

Long black shapes, low, in a line, with white units falling out of them like seeds. Conclave transports behind them. The first bombs hit the greenhouses at the north end of the town, the long glass roofs that glowed at night, because they were the easiest thing to see from the air. The glass went up in a single long flash. Then the shacks. Then the scaffolds. Then the old inn on the hill.

"All hands," Alex said, on every channel in the valley. His voice was perfectly calm. It was the calmest Val had ever heard it. "All hands. This is the commander. Everybody to the Vigil. Everybody aboard. Leave everything. We're lifting."

The lights of Ararat went out. All of them at once, the whole valley, as every scrap of the core's power went to the ship. In the sudden dark, there was only the fire from the north end, and the shapes of people running toward the Vigil's ramps in a long ragged river.

Kuze took his jacket off at the top of the main ramp, the way he did before every mission, because the capsules ran hot. He hung it on the rail inside the bay doors with his card in the breast pocket, set his coffee cup on the deck under it, and went down the ramp to his hollow in his shirtsleeves.

Lantern held the ramps. Kuze, Gage and Val, in the last three hollows, standing in the dark at the bottom of the ship's three main loading ramps, while ten thousand people ran past their legs. Maggie flew cover overhead in Lizzie, orange against the black, swearing and singing. Seus was at the top of the main ramp, in the bay doors, with no unit of his own; the core he had carried down from orbit was still in a crate in the hold, waiting for a body that wasn't finished. He had a pistol in one hand and he was hauling people aboard with the other, grabbing them by the coat collars, children, old men, a woman with a crate of chickens, shouting at them to move.

The white units came down into the valley.

Val fought. She did what she was told. Kuze's voice was in her ear, calm, slow: "Four, left. Four, hold. Four, down." She held. The hollow answered every command and felt nothing about any of them, and that was the point. A white unit came at the ramp and she put it down. Another. Another. They kept coming.

Then something hit her hollow from the side, hard, a shot from the ridge, and the world turned over.

She came to in the capsule with the walls dark and red warning lights everywhere and the hollow lying on its side in the street, between two burning shacks, a hundred meters from the ramp. Its legs were gone. Its left arm was gone. It didn't feel anything about it. There was nobody in there to feel it. It was just a corpse with her inside it.

A white unit was coming down the street toward her, unhurried.

Then Gage was there.

His hollow came down out of the dark and landed astride hers, one foot on either side of her capsule, and he met the white unit head-on with both fists. It went down. Another came behind it. He met that one too.

"Get out of it, Four!" he shouted on the channel. "Get out!"

She got the capsule hatch open. She climbed out into the cold and the smoke and the burning. Gage's hollow stood over her, holding the street, white units coming at it from both ends now, three, four. Its plating was coming off in pieces. He wasn't going to be able to hold it. He knew it. She knew it.

"Gage—"

"I still hate you," Gage said. His voice was perfectly steady. "You hear me? I still hate you. Go."

She went.

She ran down the burning street toward the ramp, and behind her she heard his hollow go down, and she didn't look back. She didn't look back. She heard the beads on his wrist in her head, the click of them turning, one by one, like a man praying.

Alex's voice came over the channel. Not on Lantern's frequency. On hers. The private one.

"Val," he said. Not *Four.* "Val, get on the ship."

She made it to the ramp. She made it up the ramp, with Seus's hand closing on her collar at the top and hauling her the last meter into the bay, and the ramp was already rising behind her as she fell through the doors. The deck lurched. The whole ship groaned, a long deep sound from the bottom of its grown steel, and then, slowly, impossibly, the Vigil lifted.

For the first time, the Vigil flew.

It rose out of the caldera with ten thousand people aboard and Ararat burning underneath it, and it climbed. Through the open bay doors, Val could see the town falling away below them. The greenhouses gone. The shacks gone. The old inn on the hill burning, the long tatami room with its maps and its low table and its desk, and in the bottom drawer of the desk, a box with a yellow note on it.

Alex, on the bridge, watched it burn.

The bombers turned for a second pass. The Vigil was slow, too slow, still climbing, and they were coming around in a long curve to meet it head-on.

"Lantern One," said Kuze's voice on the channel. Calm. Slow. "Going up."

His hollow lifted off the ruins of Ararat on its flight jets. Exos could fly, but it burned everything they had, and it was slow, and no pilot ever did it for long. He didn't need it to last long. He didn't need to come back down. He climbed toward the bombers in a long straight line, a small white shape against the black, with fire trailing behind him.

"Lantern One to Lantern Four," he said.

Val stood in the bay doors, holding the frame, with the wind tearing at her.

There was a pause on the channel. A long one. As if he were looking for something in his pocket, a card, and not finding it, and then finding it somewhere else.

"Val," Kuze said. "Your name's Val." A breath, and something in it that might have been a laugh. "I remembered."

He hit the lead bomber head-on. The flash lit up the whole valley, white, and then the bomber was falling, and the ones behind it scattered, and the Vigil climbed through the gap where they had been, up and up, into the dark.

His jacket was still on the rail inside the bay doors, where he'd hung it. One sleeve moved in the wind. His coffee cup had rolled away somewhere across the deck.

Val took the card out of the breast pocket.

It was soft from handling, its corners worn round. On one side was the team: six callsigns in Kuze's careful capitals, with a name beside every one but the last. Two, Three and Five had a single straight line through them, the way he crossed out everyone. At the bottom, *FOUR* had been crossed out too. Beside it, in pencil, in letters that shook so badly they barely held together, pressed so hard they had dented the card, was *Val.*

He had written it down so he wouldn't forget. He had left it on a rail. He had remembered anyway.

She put the card in her pocket.

### Chapter 49: Zero

The alarms went off twenty minutes later, as the Vigil cleared the mountains.

An unmarked unit was approaching. It came up out of the dark behind them, climbing slowly on its flight jets, all white, with no number painted on it anywhere. The crew scrambled for the guns. Everyone took it for one of the pilotless units, one of the white ones, come to finish the job. It came alongside the open hangar bay, where the doors still stood open from the loading, where the refugees were crowded in their thousands on the deck, and it reached out one white hand and caught the edge of the bay doors, and swung itself in, and landed.

The refugees scattered back from it, screaming.

Val didn't. She knew the unit. She had stood in the bay beside it every day for months: the narrow shoulders, the old first-generation joints, the way it held its head a little down, as if it were listening for something. Unit 00. Yuki's. Somebody had painted the numbers off its shoulders, carefully, so you could only see where they had been if you knew to look: two pale ovals under the white.

For one second, before the capsule opened, something in her chest that she had kept still for seven years lifted its head.

Then its capsule opened, and a pilot stood up in the wind.

She was a girl, maybe sixteen. She had April's face.

Not a resemblance. Her face. The face from the white room, the face that had rearranged itself every time Val tried to look straight at it and then settled, at the very end, into something Val couldn't place. Val could place it now. It was her father's face. The jaw, the brow, the shape of the eyes, softened the way a daughter's face is softened from her father's. It was so close to Val's own face that the crew on the bay floor actually looked back and forth between them.

Everyone had said April didn't exist. Everyone had said 04 was just a power source now. A battery. Long dead. *Whatever's in there should be long dead by now.* There's no April, Seus had said. And Alex had said *Sorry*, and walked away, and never said anything else, never once, in all those months.

*They lied,* she thought.

She started to walk toward the girl.

"Valeria Kane," the girl said, in a voice with no inflection at all. "Commander Moriah requests your presence. Come with me."

"Val," Alex said over the bay speakers, from the bridge. "Stop."

She kept walking. Past the refugees, past the crates, past the scorched place on the deck where something had burned during the takeoff.

"Val, if you go, I'll—"

She kept walking. She was tired. She was tired of WAKE, of the people who moved their trays away from hers in the mess, of a commander who wouldn't look at her and only talked to her through other people. She was tired of Umbral, too, of all of it, of everyone who had ever decided anything for her. She was tired of the deaths. Dasha, mid-sentence. Tom, under the door. Sae, in the pass. Gage, in the street. Kuze, in the sky, finding her name. And before any of them, Yuki, at the hangar doors at four in the morning. She didn't understand any of them. She didn't understand why any of them had done it. She only knew that every one of them had died standing next to her, and that she was the reason, and that there was no word left for that, not luck, not anything.

And standing in front of her, in the wind, was the only one she truly knew. The only one who truly knew her. She had held her in a white room and answered every question she'd ever asked. It was April. It had April's face.

She wasn't going to Umbral. She was going to April.

Through the bridge window, high above the bay, she could see him. Alex. Standing at the glass with the trigger in his hand, and his thumb on it. She could see his face from here, and he could see hers. She understood that he would do it. That he had to. That it was his job. She had watched him hold that trigger for months. She understood all of that, and she kept walking anyway, and she found she didn't mind.

She stopped at the foot of the white unit. She looked up at the bridge.

"Do it, Alex," she said. Not loudly. The bay speakers carried it. "So you won't feel sorry anymore."

She had read it on his face for months, every time he looked at her, every time he didn't. Regret, written all over it. She didn't know why. She had never asked. She thought, maybe, if he pressed it, it would go away, and he would be able to look at someone again.

His thumb stayed on the switch.

Alex didn't press it.

Seus came out of the crowd at a run. He had the pistol in his hand. He stopped at the edge of the open deck, ten meters from the white unit, and raised it, and emptied the magazine at the white unit's capsule, every round, the shots ringing off the armor like stones off a bell, and he was screaming the whole time. Not at Val. Up at the bridge.

"Press it! Alex! Press it! Press it, press it, PRESS IT!"

The pistol clicked empty. He kept pulling the trigger.

Maggie stood at the bay door with her arms folded and didn't move to stop anyone. She only said, very quietly, as Val climbed up the white unit's leg toward the capsule, "Careful, kiddo."

Val climbed into the capsule beside the girl with her father's face. The hatch closed. The designation flickered on the inside of it, in small white numbers: 00. Somebody had painted it off the outside. They had left it in here.

The white unit let go of the bay doors and fell away into the dark, and the Vigil went on climbing without her.

All Val could stare at was April.

---

## Arc 13: Not the Same

> *To him that overcometh will I give to eat of the hidden manna, and will give him a white stone, and in the stone a new name written, which no man knoweth saving he that receiveth it.*
>
> — Revelation 2:17

### Chapter 50: Who Is April?

Unit 00 flew all night, and the girl who flew it never said a word.

The capsule was built for one. It had been built for Yuki. Val sat wedged in behind the pilot's seat with her knees against the curved wall and the Halo cold against her throat, and the girl sat in front of her with her hands on the controls, perfectly still, flying the way 04 used to walk patrols in the first years, by protocol, without a single wasted motion. The walls showed the night outside: black mountains, then black plains, then frost, white and endless under the red Moon. Every so often a light fell slowly across the sky, a piece of the Morningstar still coming down, months after the station fell, burning up on its way.

Val didn't look at any of it. She looked at the back of the girl's head. The short hair. The shape of the ear. The line of the jaw when the girl turned, once, to check something on the wall.

She had a thousand things to say. She had said all of them once, in a white room, in order, kneeling, with a small weight in her arms. Now she couldn't find the first one. She was afraid that if she said anything at all, the girl would turn around and be someone else, or would turn around and not be there, and Val would wake up on the floor of the Vigil's bay under a crackling blanket with Alex walking away from her.

She had answered six questions in a white room. Now she couldn't ask one.

So she didn't. She sat behind the girl with April's face and watched her fly, and that was the whole night.

They came down at dawn into the ruins of Hakobune.

Val had seen the city from the back of a salvage truck, from the street, looking up. She had never seen it from the air. The districts that hung over it were bigger than she had understood, whole neighborhoods turned upside down and frozen in place, trailing icicles hundreds of meters long, and under them the lower city lay buried in white like something sleeping under a sheet. The seam in the sky pulsed overhead, slow and faint. In the middle of the ruins, where headquarters had stood, there was a hole.

It was round. It went straight down. It was lined with frost, and at the bottom of it, so far down that the dawn light didn't reach, something glowed: cold, pale, slow, like a heartbeat seen through ice.

Val knew that glow. She had seen it the night the world ended, from the lip of the same hole, with Navis's hands full of her own exo.

Unit 00 dropped past the lip of the shaft and down along its frozen side to a ledge where the old levels of headquarters broke off into open air, and landed in what had once been a hangar. The capsule opened. Cold air came in, and voices.

Patrick's Umbral lived in the ruins of headquarters, in the lower levels that had survived. It was cold and quiet and full of people who didn't look up.

They didn't look up when Val came down off Unit 00's leg. They didn't look up as two guards in gray walked her through the hangar and into the corridors. The corridors were patched with plastic sheeting where the walls had cracked, and the sheeting breathed in and out with the draft from the shaft. The lights ran at half power. Technicians in heavy coats sat at consoles with their breath smoking in the air, typing, and none of them turned their heads. Frost grew on the inside of the walls in long fern shapes, like the frost that used to grow on the Reliquary doors, as if the cold were coming from inside the building now, from everywhere.

Somebody had pinned a shift schedule to a pillar. Somebody had taped a child's drawing to a door. Those were the only signs that people lived here.

Patrick met her in what was left of his office.

It had been on the forty-first floor once, with a window over the harbor. Now it was in the basement, in a room that had been a records vault, with the walls lined in empty steel shelves and a desk that didn't belong to it. There was one lamp. There were reports stacked at his left hand, the way there always had been, and a pen. Behind the desk, leaning against the wall where a window would have been, was a single blank canvas, white, untouched, with a fine skin of frost across it.

In the corner, at a folding table with a go board on it, an old woman in a cardigan sat playing against no one. Ruth Hermon. She had sat beside Patrick in mission control every day of Val's last year in Hakobune, with her reading glasses on their chain and her hands folded, saying almost nothing. She looked up when Val came in and looked at her for a long moment, the way she always had, as if Val reminded her of someone. Then she went back to her stones.

He had aged too, though less than Alex, as if he had decided aging was a waste of time. His hair had gone gray at the sides. His coat was the same coat, or another one exactly like it. He looked up when she came in, and his face did the thing it had always done for her and for no one else, softening slightly, the way a cold room softens when someone opens a door.

"Valeria," he said.

She didn't say anything.

He was exactly the same as before: warm to her, polite, unreadable. He told her she was needed. He told her nothing about what for. When she asked, he said, "You'll be told," in the same mild voice he used to use for *the car's outside* and *your bento's on the counter*, and went back to his reports.

Then, the way he always had at the gate, he asked if she had eaten.

Val stood in front of his desk with her hands at her sides and looked at him. Seven years. The sky. The port. Seus. And he wanted to know if she'd had breakfast.

"The girl in Unit 00," she said.

"She's useful," Patrick said.

"You did it again," Val said.

She didn't know she was going to say it until it was out. She didn't entirely know what she meant by it. Only that there had been a white unit once, in a hangar under this building, with *00* on its hull and a girl in it who answered everything literally and had held a hospital alone, and he had sat beside that girl's bed in the medical wing without saying a word. And now the numbers were painted off, and there was another girl standing under it, and he had said *useful*.

In the corner, a go stone clicked onto the board, and stayed there.

Patrick looked at her for a long moment. Then he moved on, to a report about the shaft's temperature, which he read aloud to her for some reason, the way he used to read her the weather.

Val realized, with a chill, that he didn't care about this one at all. He didn't say it coldly. That was the worst part. He said *she's useful* the way you'd say *the car runs* or *the lights work*, a fact about equipment, and then he turned the page.

"What about Seus?" she said.

Patrick's pen stopped.

"He came to you," Val said. "After he woke up. He walked eleven days across the ice to get here. He told me. He stood right here, and you had security walk him out."

Patrick looked at the pen for a while. Then he capped it, and set it down beside the reports, very precisely, parallel to the edge of the desk.

"He's an adult now," he said. "He can think for himself."

And he stood, and picked up his coat, and left her standing in the vault.

Seus was sixteen. He would be sixteen forever, with his eye behind a patch and his burns still shiny on his neck. Patrick knew that better than anyone alive. Val stood in front of the empty desk and the blank canvas and understood that he had said it anyway, on purpose, the way you'd close a door.

It was no different from before. It was worse, because now Val knew what he had done to his own son, and he still treated her like the one daughter he had.

But at least April was here.

They gave her a room on the level below, a narrow concrete box with a cot and a lamp and a vent that breathed cold air from the shaft. They gave her rations, the same gray rice and pickled radish as Ararat, and a heavy gray parka with a hood, Umbral issue, too big for her, smelling of storage. Nobody mentioned the Halo. A technician looked at it once in the corridor, said, "WAKE hardware," to no one in particular, and walked on. Nobody offered to take it off, and Val didn't ask. It was still live. Alex had told her the range once, in his flat captain's voice: sixty kilometers from the trigger. Anywhere inside that, he could press it, and she would go soft. The Vigil was a long way from Hakobune now. It wouldn't always be.

She kept the hood up everywhere she went, so nobody had to look at her face, and so she didn't have to watch them looking.

She found the girl in the hangar, standing in front of Unit 00, as still as a statue.

The white unit stood in its cradle with its capsule open, and the girl stood below it with her hands at her sides and her face turned up toward it, not doing anything. Not working on it. Not looking at anything on it. Just standing, the way a machine stands in a corner when it's switched off.

Val came up beside her and pushed back her hood.

"Hey," she said. Her voice came out wrong, too soft. She tried again. "Hey... April."

The girl turned her head.

"Who... is April?"

Val swallowed. "Your name is April, right?"

The girl looked at her. Up close, in the half light of the hangar, the face was even closer to Val's own than it had seemed in the wind on the Vigil: the same brow, the same eyes, the jaw that Val had seen on talk shows and old posters, softened. A face that had been made out of two faces.

"My designation is Zero-Four," the girl said. "I was recovered from the frost after the Almost. I serve Commander Moriah."

"No," Val said. "No. You're April. I named you. In the white room, you asked me, on the first day you asked me for a name and I didn't give you one, and then I did. I gave it to you. You said it back to me. April. You said it twice."

"I have no record of a white room."

"You asked me why the sky was blue." Val heard her own voice climbing and couldn't stop it. "You asked me about my hair. You asked me if I had a mom. You asked if you'd done something wrong. You asked me if I was a hero. You had one more question. You always had one more question."

The girl considered this. She tilted her head very slightly, the way a camera adjusts its focus.

"I do not ask questions," she said.

She looked at Val with Sam's eyes, without recognition, without anything.

"April isn't my designation."

Val put out her hand. She didn't know what she meant to do with it. Touch the girl's face, maybe. Touch her hair, the short hair, cut the way Val had once told a girl in a white room that her mother wore it. The girl didn't step back. She didn't lean away. She didn't do anything at all. She let Val's hand stop in the air a few centimeters from her cheek, and waited, politely, for whatever this was to be over.

That was worse than anything. Worse than if she'd flinched.

Val took her hand back.

So that was it. Val had failed after all. She thought she understood, finally, what had happened in the white room. She had wanted 04 back with everything in her, and the door had heard. It had made a body out of what she wanted, and the body had come out wrong: a copy with her face and her father's, who had never asked her a single question. The April she'd named, the one she'd held, wasn't here. Val didn't know where she was.

"Is there something you require, Valeria Kane?" the girl asked.

"No," Val said.

"Then I will continue to stand by."

She turned back to Unit 00 and lifted her face to it again, and stood.

Val went back to the room they'd given her, lay on the bed, and stared at the ceiling. The vent breathed cold air down on her face. Somewhere far below, the glow at the bottom of the shaft beat once, slowly, and she felt it in the floor.

Everyone on WAKE hated her. Seus hated her. Her friends were dead. April was gone.

*04 did this*, she told herself. And there was no one left who looked at her the way they used to.

### Chapter 51: Abriel

She couldn't sleep, so on the third night she went up.

The stairwells in the ruins of headquarters went up a long way past anything that was still a building. She climbed until the concrete walls on either side of her gave way to open air and wind, and then she climbed some more, and came out through a twisted fire door onto a roof that wasn't a roof anymore. It was the top of whatever was left: a broad, cracked platform, half of it gone, open on three sides to the night. From up here you could see the whole frozen city, the hanging districts turning in the dark, the seam pulsing overhead like a vein, and over all of it, low and huge, the red Moon.

Someone was already there.

He stood at the edge, looking up: a boy with pale hair in a white Umbral flight suit, his hands in his pockets, his weight on his heels, perfectly relaxed with a forty-story drop in front of his toes. He heard her on the stairs and turned around, and he smiled as if she were exactly who he had been waiting for.

"Oh," he said. "It's you. I've been waiting for you, Val."

She knew his voice.

She had heard it from the inside, through his own ears, at the top of a broken tower at the end of the world. She had heard it say *04 did this* and then cut out, half a breath before whatever came next. She had carried that sentence for months. She had held on to it in the Vigil's bay and on the shelter steps and in a hollow's capsule with Dasha dead on the concrete. He was the one whose eyes she had looked through. He was the voice she trusted without knowing why.

She waited for him to look at her the way everyone looked at her now, like a fire that might still be burning.

He didn't. He came over and sat down on the edge of the roof, close, his legs hanging over the drop, as if they had known each other for years. After a moment, because her legs had gone strange, she sat down beside him.

At the edge where he had been standing, the concrete was white with rime, in the shape of two shoes.

"You're not afraid of me," she said.

"No, why would I be?"

"Everyone else is."

"Everyone? That sounds exhausting." He looked up at the Moon. He had a way of looking at things as if he had just been told they existed and was delighted to find out it was true. "I'm the one who stopped it, you know. That night. I came down from the Morningstar and put the Sword through it."
