He held up one hand, fingers together, and drew it slowly down through the air, a long straight line from the sky to the city, the way a child draws a falling star. "To stop it, I had to touch it."

He turned his head and smiled at her sideways.

"So I touched you. You were holding someone you loved, and you were so happy. I don't think monsters are happy like that."

Val didn't say anything. She couldn't. Something had closed in her throat, right under the Halo, and wouldn't open.

He seemed to understand that. He didn't wait for an answer. He reached into the pocket of his flight suit and took out a can, small and warm, and set it on the concrete between them. Sweet coffee. The konbini kind, with the cartoon cow on the label, the kind that came in pairs on a dashboard on quiet afternoons.

She stared at it.

"I asked the man who runs this place what you liked," he said. "He said that. He didn't even have to think about it." He tilted his head. "He knows a lot about you, for someone who doesn't talk to you."

She picked up the can. It was warm through her gloves. She didn't open it. She held it in both hands in her lap, and the warmth went into her fingers, and she sat there holding it while the boy beside her watched the Moon.

"How do you know my name?" she said finally.

"Everyone knows your name." He smiled. "I knew it before that."

"Before what?"

He didn't answer. His name was Abriel, he said. He was the newest pilot under Commander Moriah's command, and he had come from the Moon.

"Nobody comes from the Moon," said Val.

"I did," said Abriel, as if that settled it.

He didn't explain more than that, and Val didn't ask. She was too tired to ask, and she found, sitting there, that she didn't want to. She didn't want this to turn out to be anything but what it was: a boy on a roof, a warm can, and someone who looked at her and didn't see the end of the world.

He was the first person since she woke who looked at her like that.

They sat on the edge of the roof until the Moon had moved a hand's width across the sky. He didn't talk much. Neither did she. When the cold finally drove her back toward the stairs, he called after her, not getting up.

"Come back tomorrow?"

She stopped in the broken doorway with the can in her hands.

"Maybe," she said.

"I'll be here," Abriel said. He turned back to the Moon. "I'm good at waiting."

In her room, she opened the can and drank it, cold by then, too sweet, all of it.

### Chapter 52: The Moon

It became a ritual: every night, the roof and the red Moon.

She would come up the long stairs after the ruins had gone quiet, and he would already be there, at the edge, looking up, with two cans in his pockets. He never asked where she'd been during the day. She never told him. She would sit down beside him, and he would hand her a can, and they would watch the sky. He held his own can all night and hardly drank from it. By the time she left, it was always furred white with frost where his fingers had been.

The sky over Hakobune was never dark, exactly. The veil of debris that had hung over the world since the Almost caught the light of the cracked Moon and glowed with it, a dim red haze from horizon to horizon. Through the gaps in it, here and there, real stars showed, very small and very far. And all night long, slow lights crossed it: pieces of the Morningstar, still falling, months later, burning up one by one in long patient lines, like matches struck very far away.

On the second night, Abriel started naming them.

"That one's the Ladle," he said, pointing at a cluster of stars that didn't look like a ladle at all. "That's the Sleeping Dog. And that long one, the one falling right now, is the Boy Who Forgot His Umbrella."

"Those aren't constellations," said Val.

"They are now. I named them." He looked delighted with himself. "Nobody else was using them. Your turn."

Val went very still.

*Your turn.* Small neat handwriting, pencil, on a page folded under a pillow.

Abriel noticed. He didn't ask. He looked at her for a moment, and then looked back at the sky, and waited, the way Sae used to wait on the hangar steps, a meter away, knees up, saying nothing. The falling light crossed half the sky and went out.

"Someone left me a note once," Val said. "That said that."

"Was it a good note?"

She thought about it for a long time.

"It was the only one I ever got," she said.

Abriel nodded slowly, as if that were a complete answer, and maybe it was. After a while, Val lifted one hand and pointed at a narrow line of stars low over the hills, three in a row, slightly crooked.

"That one's the Pencil," she said.

He laughed. It was a real laugh, startled and bright, the kind of laugh she had only ever heard from Haruto when Akari fell off something. "That's a terrible name."

"It's a pencil. Look at it."

He looked at it. "...It is a pencil."

"I know."

The next night, she named a round fuzzy patch of stars the Rice Ball, and didn't explain. He didn't ask her to. The night after that he named one the Commander's Car, and she looked at him sharply, and he only smiled at the sky.

Abriel told her stories, strange and tender ones. Some of them weren't stories. Val didn't know how he knew all of it, or why.

"The Church of the Saints says they fell fighting for us," he said one night, about the saints. "That they went up against Heaven for our sake, and lost, and that's why they're holy. And the people above Umbral have an older book. They call it the Testament. In the Testament, the saints have a different name." He drank from his can. "They call them devils."

"Which is it?"

"Neither." He pointed up, at the Moon. "You know the names. Sariel up there. Azazel in pieces in the belt. Semjaza under the ice. They weren't martyrs, and they weren't devils. They were rebels. Long before there were any people, they went up to Heaven to ask a question, and when nobody answered, they went to war. And they lost. They fell, and their bodies are still lying where they fell, with a sword in each one to keep it asleep." He turned the can in his fingers. "Nobody knows whose swords."

"What was the question?"

Abriel was quiet for a moment.

"Nobody remembers," he said. "That's the thing about a question nobody answers. After long enough, the silence is all that's left. Everyone remembers that there was no answer. Nobody remembers what they asked."

Val looked down at the can in her hands.

"You know about the children," he said, on another night. It wasn't a question.

"Someone told me," Val said. "The giants. They're the saints' children. They're born in their fathers' graves, out in the cold, and they fall toward the Root, because it's the only piece of their fathers on Earth. They're trying to get home." She had heard it in a voice coming out of Navis's walls, in a corridor turned to frost, and she had never told anyone. She told him. "Every one I ever killed."

"Yes," said Abriel. "Every one." He didn't say it to hurt her. He said it the way you'd say the sea was salt. "But it isn't the Root they want. The Root's only the way in. Their fathers wanted to get into Heaven so badly that the wanting had children. And the children think Heaven is home, because their fathers thought it would fix everything."

He looked up at the seam for a while.

"Some of them never fall, though. Some of them are born, and they just stay there, in the dark, where they were born. Waiting for someone to tell them where home is."

He talked about the Moon most of all.

He said it had been asleep for a very long time, longer than there had been people to look at it, and that it dreamed. He said it had dreamed of a son. He said the craters were where the dreams had pressed too hard, from the inside, trying to get out. He said that on nights like this one, when the Moon was red, you could almost hear it breathing, and he would go quiet and tilt his head, listening, and Val would listen too and hear nothing but the wind in the ruins.

He talked about the Moon the way people talk about someone they love and have never met. Val listened. It was like listening to a boy talk about his father's grave.

On the fifth night, she asked him.

"What was it like? On the Moon."

"Dark," Abriel said. "Cold. Very quiet. You'd think it would be lonely, but you have to know what people are to be lonely, and I didn't, yet." He turned the can around in his fingers. "The first thing I remember is the dark. I was lying on stone, at the bottom of a crater, inside the ribs of something enormous, in a place that had been made for me before I was there to see it. I didn't know what I was. I didn't have a name. I didn't know there was such a thing as a name."

He was quiet for a while.

"And then a girl came."

Val looked at him.

"On the Moon?"

"On the Moon." He smiled at the sky. "I don't know how. Nobody else ever came. But she was there, kneeling beside me in the dark. I don't remember her face. I've tried, every time. I remember that her hair was short, and that she'd been crying for a long time; you could tell, the way you can tell when it's been raining. She took my hand. She was the first thing I ever touched." He opened his hand on his knee and looked at it, as if the touch were still there. "And she asked me for something. Nobody had ever asked me for anything. I didn't know you could."

"What did she ask?"

Abriel turned his head and looked at her, for a long moment, in the red light.

"She asked me to make Valeria happy," he said.

The wind went across the roof. One of the slow lights crossed the sky behind his head and burned out.

"That's me," Val said. It came out barely a whisper. "That's... my name."

"I didn't know that, then. I didn't know who Valeria was. I didn't know what happy was. So I waited until I found out." He looked back up at the Moon, and his voice went soft, almost shy, the way people's voices go when they say something they've practiced alone. "I waited a very long time on the Moon. I'd have waited longer. I think that makes me a fool." He smiled at the sky. "I don't mind."

"Who was she?" said Val.

"I don't know. Someone asked me, and I said yes, and I never saw her again." He smiled. "Maybe she looked a little like you."

Val thought he was teasing her. She looked away, at the Pencil, low over the hills, and felt her face go hot under the hood, and was glad of the dark.

He wasn't afraid of her, and he didn't hate her. He didn't flinch when she spoke or watch her like a fire. He asked her questions and waited for the answers, and she found herself giving them.

About the school: Akari's punch, on the third day, square in the mouth, and how Akari had made it even afterward the only way she knew how, by saying *come meet him* and taking Val to the hospital to meet her little brother. Nanami's good mornings, every single day, to someone who never once said it back. Haruto's documentary, which he narrated in a nature-program whisper while following her down the hall, calling her *the subject*. About Alex's omurice, torn down the middle, the ugliest omurice in Hakobune, which she had eaten all of. About the eggs on her tray in Ararat on mission days, rolled soft and sweet the way Fumi used to make them, from a giant with frost on his neck who said he was a jinn with powers. About the other eggs, after he was gone, torn and burnt, from some new cook who didn't know how.

About April. Both of them. The one in the white room who had asked six questions and then a seventh, and the one in the hangar who didn't ask anything.

Abriel listened to all of it with his whole attention, the way April had listened in the white room, leaning in slightly, as if each answer were a gift being handed to him one at a time.

Lying awake afterward, in the narrow concrete room with the vent breathing on her, she thought he was the only person left in the world who understood her.

On the sixth night, they sat on the edge of the roof in a long, easy silence, and the Moon was very large, and she had her hood down for once because the wind had dropped. He was looking at her instead of at the sky. She noticed and didn't know what to do about it, so she kept looking at the sky.

"You're beautiful this time around," Abriel said.

All Val heard was the word *beautiful*, which she had never heard from anyone.

She didn't hear the rest of the sentence. She didn't think about it until much later, in a place outside time, where she had nothing to do but think.

A week passed. A week of Val quietly watching April from a distance, holding herself back from going to her. A week of star-gazing with Abriel. A week of peace, without exos, without hate, without pain.

She watched the girl with April's face in the hangar every morning, from the catwalk above Unit 00, with her hood up. The girl stood in front of the white unit for hours. She ate when a bell rang, exactly what she was given, at a table by herself, and stopped when the bell rang again. Once Val watched her hold a cup of tea in both hands until it stopped steaming. A thin ring of frost came up on the cup where her fingers were, fainter than the frost on Abriel's cans, and was gone a minute after she put it down. Once Val passed her in a corridor, close enough to touch, and the girl stopped and looked at her and said, "Valeria Kane," as a statement, as a fact, and then nothing.

Val stood there and waited for the question. There was always a question. There had always been a question.

None came. The girl walked on.

It was the loudest silence Val had ever heard.

* * *

On the fourth day, she asked about Yuki.

She hadn't meant to. She was on the catwalk above Unit 00 with her hood up, watching the girl stand, and the woman who looked after the girl came out of a side door below with a tablet under her arm and a can of the sweet coffee in her other hand. Val heard herself say the name out loud before she could stop it.

"Yuki."

Eve Sakaki looked up.

She had aged the way everyone in the ruins had aged, in the face and not anywhere else: the same lab coat over the same sweater, gone shapeless now, the same precise, tired way of standing, as if she were always about to be called away. She had stood beside Unit 00's cradle every morning for a year in Hakobune, with that tablet and that coffee, while a girl in bandages climbed in.

"That's not her," Eve said, with a nod at the girl below the white unit. "If that's what you're wondering. That one isn't mine. She came out of the sky. I only keep her cold."

"I know it's not her."

"Then what?"

Val didn't know. She stood on the catwalk with her gloves stuck to the frozen rail.

"Did she have anyone?" she said. "Family. Somebody who should have been told."

Eve looked up at her for a long time. Then she finished the coffee, and set the empty can on the edge of the cradle, very precisely, and said, "Come with me."

She took Val down. Below the hangar, below the levels where people lived, down a service stair that nobody had swept since the Almost, to a door with a keypad and no sign on it. Behind it was a long low room lit pale blue, and it was warm. It was the only warm room Val had found in the ruins.

Along one wall stood six tanks, tall glass cylinders full of something clear and very still. Five of them were occupied.

In each one, a girl floated, curled on her side, with her knees drawn up and her eyes closed and her short hair drifting. The same girl, five times. The same pale face Val had sat beside in Class 2-A for months, and seen bandaged on a pillow through a half-open door, and heard on a channel at four in the morning saying her name.

The sixth tank was empty. The label on it read *VESSEL LINE — SUBJECT 02*, and under that, in newer ink, *HAKOBUNE. LOST 2089.*

"Spares," Eve said. "In case the Kane girl broke. Or refused. Or went soft. In case you did anything they hadn't written down." She said it without any weight on it at all. "Two was the first one that woke up properly. They sent her to Hakobune to sit near you. In case."

Val looked at the face in the nearest tank.

"Whose face is it?" she said.

Eve put her hand flat on the glass.

"Root flesh to grow on. That's the easy part. You need a pattern for the rest." She didn't look at Val. "They took the cells from the only person they had who'd ever been through that door and come back as anything at all. Her body came back. The rest of her didn't." A small pause. "Yukimura. Sora Yukimura."

Val didn't move.

She had never known her mother's face. She had short hair and a pencil and the way Fumi said *an accident at work*, and nothing else. She had looked for it, at nine, in the mirror, and given up. And it had been here. It had sat two desks away from her for months by the window, and said *it's what I'm for*, and asked her what you say when you don't know what to say. Haruto had pointed his camera at the two of them in the corridor once and asked if they were related, and they had both said *no*.

"He used to come down here," Eve said. "After the Almost. Every night, for a year." She took her hand off the glass. "He'd stand right where you're standing. I'd be at the console. He never once turned around."

Then she went to the console, and typed something, and pressed a key.

The tanks drained. It took less than a minute. As the level fell, the girls inside them didn't drown and didn't wake. They came apart, gently, from the edges in, into pale flakes that drifted down through the emptying tanks like snow in a jar, until there was nothing in the glass but a little frost on the bottom.

Eve watched it all the way to the end.

"They're nobody," she said. "I made them nobody."

Val couldn't tell, then or later, whether she meant it as an apology.

"Go back upstairs, Kane," Eve said, and sat down at the console with her back to the empty tanks, and didn't turn around.

* * *

On the fifth morning she saw Abriel cross the hangar floor below her, on his way somewhere, and stop beside the girl in front of Unit 00.

"Good morning," he said to her, warmly, as if they were old friends.

The girl turned her head and looked at him and said nothing at all. After a moment, Abriel smiled and went on his way. When he came out onto the roof that night, Val asked him about it.

"Why do you say good morning to her? She doesn't say it back."

"She doesn't know what it means," Abriel said. "Nobody's ever taught her." He handed Val her can. "Somebody said it to you every day for months, didn't they? Before you said it back."

"I never said it back."

"Not yet," said Abriel.

* * *

Every evening before he went up, Abriel stopped at the vault.

Patrick kept the cans in the bottom drawer of the desk that didn't belong to the room. Nobody in the ruins knew where he got them. There hadn't been a konbini open in Hakobune since the Almost. Abriel took two every night, and Patrick never looked up from his reports while he did it.

One night, he did.

"Does she drink them?"

"All of it," said Abriel. "She lets it go cold first."

Patrick wrote something in a margin. The pen moved, and stopped, and moved again. Behind him the canvas leaned against the wall under its skin of frost, white, the way his canvases had been white for twenty-nine years.

"Who asked you to do all this?" Patrick said. "The roof. The cans. Her."

Abriel stood in the doorway with a can in each hand and thought about it, the way he thought about everything, as if it were the first time anyone had asked him.

"Someone I never saw again," he said. "Like you."

Patrick's pen stopped.

Abriel smiled at him, kindly, and went up the stairs to the roof. Patrick sat in the vault for a long time after he had gone, with the pen in his hand, looking at nothing. Then he capped it, and set it down beside the reports, parallel to the edge of the desk, and went back to work.

* * *

On the seventh night, the wind came back, and they sat closer together against it, and Val asked him.

She had seen the ruins from below, from the back of a salvage truck and the floor of a frozen shelter. She had seen the frost and the hanging districts and the burning mountain. But she had never asked anyone what had really happened that night, because no one had needed to tell her; everyone told her anyway, every day, with their faces. And he was the only one who had been there, inside it, with her.

"What happened to the world?" she said. "After you stopped 04?"

Abriel took her hand.

He didn't answer at once. He looked down at her hand in his, and then up at her face, and something passed over his own face that she didn't recognize then and would recognize later: the look of someone at the top of a staircase they have climbed many times before.

"You can't go back," he said. "Are you sure you want to know?"

"Yes, please."

### Chapter 53: You Did This

He led her up through the frozen ruins of headquarters, up a stairwell that went on long after the walls around it had fallen away, past floors that were only floors now, open to the wind.

It wasn't the stairwell to the roof. It was another one, on the far side of the ruins, in what had been the east tower, the tallest thing in Hakobune once, where the Commander's office had looked out over the harbor. The tower had broken in half during the Almost. The bottom half still stood, and the stairs went up through it, switchback after switchback, concrete slabs bolted to a steel spine, with nothing on either side but air and the long fall and the frost.

Abriel went first. He held her hand and climbed half a step ahead, and every few flights he looked back at her, to see that she was still there.

Halfway up, Val knew.

She knew the stairs. She knew the cold on her face, and the way the wind came across the open floors in long shoves that tried to push you sideways. She knew the steel railing, iced over, and the way your glove stuck to it if you held on too long. She knew the way he kept half a step ahead of her and kept looking back. She knew his hand.

She had been here before. She had been here months ago, lying on a hangar floor under headquarters with the sprinklers raining down on her, looking out through someone else's eyes. Climbing these stairs. Holding this hand. Looking back, every few flights, at a girl in a hood.

Her hood was up against the wind, the way it was everywhere she went now, and her stomach turned over, because she finally understood whose face she had never gotten a clean look at.

It had been her own.

She stopped on the stairs. Abriel stopped too, a step above her, and looked back, the way he had in the vision, the way he had every few flights. He didn't say anything. He waited.

She could have turned around. She thought about it, there on the stairs, with the wind pushing at her. She could let go of his hand and go back down and never know the rest. Keep the sentence she'd been carrying, the one that ended where the vision ended. *04 did this.* Keep it forever, the way she'd kept luck.

She didn't let go. She went on climbing.

At the top of the broken tower, where the floor simply ended in open air, he stopped.

The whole ruined world spread out beneath them. The hanging districts, turning slowly in the dark with their icicles dripping. The frost, going on to the horizon in every direction, over hills, over farms, over towns she would never know the names of. The seam in the sky, still pulsing, like a wound that wouldn't close. The red Moon. And far off, Fuji, still burning. No sign of life anywhere. Nothing. Just the cold ice.

It was the last frame of the vision. Exactly. Every detail. She had seen this view through his eyes so many times that standing in it now felt like stepping into a photograph of herself.

"This is what happened," Abriel said gently. "04 did this."

He turned to her.

Val held her breath on the half-second where the vision had cut out, the one she had been living inside since she woke. She heard her own heartbeat. She heard the wind. She heard the faint, slow drip of a hanging district a kilometer away. She stood in that half-second for what felt like a very long time, the way you stand at the edge of a step in the dark, with your foot out, feeling for the floor.

"And you did this, Val."

It wasn't an accusation. It was a fact, laid down between them like a stone.

"There was a sword in the Root," he said. "Since the white sky. It kept the door shut. That's all it does. Put a person and a giant and a piece of Semjaza together in one body, and you've made something that can open the door, and the sword goes to it to keep it shut. There isn't a door, Val. Not one you could touch. It opens wherever the thing that opens it is standing, right over its head." He looked down past the edge, toward the shaft, where the glow beat slowly in the dark. "That night, Navis had all three in it. You. What Navis had torn out of Penemue. And 04. So the sword came up the shaft for you. I saw it from the Morningstar. It stopped this far from Navis's neck." He held up his finger and thumb, a little apart. "A sword keeps a door shut, unless someone near it wants it open more. You wanted her back more than anything has ever wanted anything. So it turned around and went into you instead, like a key, and turned. And the sky opened right above your head."

Somewhere under it, she had always known. She had known on the shelter steps, saying *04 did this* over and over until it was only noise. She had known in the bay with Seus leaning over her. She had known in a hollow's capsule, feeling nothing, choosing nothing, so that nothing would be hers. She had built her whole life since waking on half a sentence, and she had known the whole time that it was half.

There was nothing left to hold on to. Not luck. Not 04. Not her father.

Val Kane sank down at the edge of the broken tower with the whole ruined world in front of her, and she broke.

She cried the way she hadn't cried at nine, or in the port, or anywhere in between: all of it at once, until she couldn't breathe. She cried for Seus, in the port, in her hands. For Akari and Haruto and Nanami, and an empty shelter, and a pencil in a frozen desk. For Yuki, in a white unit at four in the morning, holding a hangar door, with a face Val had sat beside for months and never known. For Dasha, mid-sentence, and Tom under the door, and Sae in the pass, and Gage in the street, and Kuze in the sky, finding her name. For April, holding her in the white room. For the April who stood in front of a machine and didn't ask anything. She was sick over the edge. She wanted to follow it down. She was too afraid to.

Abriel sat down beside her and held her, and he didn't tell her it was all right, because it wasn't.

He held her while she shook. He held her while the wind came across the top of the tower and tried to push them both off it. He held her while the falling lights crossed the sky one by one and burned out, and the Moon moved, and the cold came up through the concrete into their legs. He didn't say anything at all. He just stayed.

When she could hear again, he was still holding her hand. Her face was stiff with frozen tears.

"You're going to ask me to fix it," Abriel said.

She hadn't said anything. She couldn't have. But it was already there in her, the shape of it, rising up out of the wreck of her like the only thing left standing, and he looked at it the way he looked at everything, as if he had seen it before.

"Everyone thinks it can be undone." He looked up at the seam. "The door's still open. Heaven is outside time. Every day that ever was is still happening up there, somewhere. So it must be possible to go back to one of them and change it." He was quiet for a moment. "I've brought you up these stairs more times than there are stars, Val. Every time, you ask me to fix it. Every time, I say yes. I tell you about the swords, and how we'll take them up to the door and open it on your terms and put everything back the way it was. I believe it, every time. And every time, we end up right back here, on these stairs, looking at this."

She stared at him.

"So this time I'm saying no," Abriel said gently. "You can't undo it. I've tried."

She didn't understand half of what he'd said. She understood the no. It went into her the way the cold did, all the way down, and found nothing there to stop it.

"How many times?" she whispered.

"I stopped counting." He turned her hand over in his and looked at it, the way he had looked at his own hand on the roof. "You'd think it would be sad. It isn't. Every time, I get the roof, and the cans, and the stars. Every time, I get to name them again." He smiled at her. "Every time, I get you. It's the only life I've got. I don't mind it."

"Then what's the point?" Her voice cracked. "If it can't be undone. If nothing up there can be changed. What's the point of any of it?"

Abriel was quiet for a long time. A slow light crossed the sky above the seam and went out.

"There's one thing up there you haven't done," he said.

She looked at him.

"In the white room," Abriel said, "she asked you one more question. And you didn't answer it."

Val stopped breathing.

"I was there. When I put the Sword through, I felt her ask." He looked up at the long bright line in the sky. "Everything that ever happened is still happening up there. Which means she's still asking. She's been asking for seven years, in a white room that's going gray, waiting for you to say something." He squeezed her hand. "You can't change what happened, Val. But you could still answer her."

"Through the door," Val said.

"The Commander wants it opened." Abriel said it the way he had told her the names of the stars. "He has for a long time. After that night, both swords were still in 04, the one that turned for you and the one I left in the lock behind it. It took them seven years to cut them out. Now they're down in the Reliquary with the Root, where nobody can get at them. And they grew a body out of the Root, the only other thing on Earth made of what 04 was made of. That took seven years too." He nodded down at the ruins, toward the shaft. "It's finished. It's in hangar zero. He asked me to bring you to the swords."

"Why me?"

"He didn't say. He never does." He looked up at the seam again. "It's hungry. It eats wanting. Every time, you go up there to take something back, and it takes everything instead." He turned and looked at her. "So don't go up there to take. Go up there to give her something."

"And if I can't? If I want too much?"

"Then I'll be there," said Abriel. "I'm always there."

It would have been easier to talk. She knew that, the way you know things about other people. She could go down to the hangar and stand in front of the girl and say anything at all. She could find a radio and say Alex's name into it. She could knock on the vault door. Any of it would have meant opening her mouth and staying there while someone answered.

The door only meant going.

Val looked at the seam in the sky for a long time. She looked at it the way she had once looked at a girl in a white room: with everything in her.

Ruth Hermon brought him the mission order the next morning, in the vault, with her reading glasses on their chain and a game left unfinished on the board behind her.

"The Kane girl and the boy from the Moon," she said. "Hangar zero. The swords." She held out the pen, and didn't let go of it right away. "Do you know what she means to do up there?"

"Run the ops room, Ruth," Patrick said. "Don't ask."

"I've been not asking for twenty years, Patrick."

He took the pen. After a moment, she let him have it.

Patrick signed the mission order without asking Val a single question.

### Stinger

That night, in a room with no windows, seven old men and women sat at a long dark table and read.

There was one lamp, hung low, so that its light fell on the table and on their hands and on nothing above them. Sleeves. Reading glasses. A cup of tea going cold. In the middle of the table lay the Testament, open, an old book with a cracked spine, its margins full of dates in pencil, most of them crossed off.

"Four-seventeen," one of them read. "The Kane girl goes up."

"Has she asked?"

"Tonight. At the top of the east tower." A page turned. "The Moon child brought her to the stairs."

"As written."

"Sakaki drained the vessel line last night."

"Then there's only the one." A page turned. "There was only ever going to be the one."

"And the Commander?"

"He'll sign it in the morning. He won't ask her a thing." A dry sound that might have been a laugh. "He never has."

"The Enoch boy still holds her collar."

"Sixty kilometers. He's never pressed it."

"He won't," said someone at the far end. "He promised his mother."

There was a chair at that end of the table that nobody sat in. The lamp didn't quite reach it. There was dust on the seat.

"Helen asked us for one thing," the voice went on, "before she went through. Do you remember? She'd signed so many tests. She wanted to walk into the last one herself." A cup was set down. "She asked that her son look after the Kane girl."

"We granted it."

"It suited the schedule."

Someone turned back a great many pages, to where the margins had gone brown, and read without needing the light. "*One who has lost everything shall take up the arms of the fallen.*" A finger rested under the line. "It never gave a name. We looked for him for eighteen years. And then a boy climbed into a dying man's exo with his house on fire behind him, and all we had to do was read."

"We have only ever had to read."

"Fifty-two years," said the voice at the far end, quietly, the way you count something you don't like. "Half the world in three days. And then we finished the rest of it ourselves." Nobody answered that. They had all been young in the years after the white sky. They had all seen what people did to each other over what was left. "Every seal on Earth is a wall. Every wall is a war waiting for a reason. After the Rite there will be nothing left to fight over, and no one left to be alone."

"Salvation," someone said, as if reading it off the page.

"And after?"

It was asked very softly. For a moment nobody answered. Then the oldest of them turned to the end of the Testament, where the last page lay flat against the board. Below its last line there was nothing written, and after it there were no more pages.

"There is no after," she said. "The book ends."

Somebody leaned into the light and made a small neat mark in the margin of the Testament, in pencil, beside a date that was very nearly today.

"Four-seventeen," they read. "On schedule."

---

## Arc 14: The Third War on Heaven

> *And there was war in heaven: Michael and his angels fought against the dragon; and the dragon fought and his angels, and prevailed not; neither was their place found any more in heaven.*
>
> — Revelation 12:7–8

### Chapter 54: Twelve

Nobody briefed her. A technician came to her door in the morning with a clipboard and said, "Hangar zero, two hours," and left, and that was the mission.

She had told herself all night what she was going for. Not to undo anything. Abriel had said that on the tower, with her hand in his and the whole dead world in front of them: *You can't. I've tried.* She was going to answer a question. A question asked in a white room by a girl with short hair, still waiting somewhere on the other side of the seam for the answer Val hadn't given. She had said it to herself so many times in the dark that it had started to sound true.

Hangar zero was the deepest one, cut into the side of the shaft itself, so far down that the glow from the bottom lit the walls a faint, cold blue. The cradle hung out over the drop on steel arms, and in the cradle, waiting, was a unit she had never seen.

It was tall, taller than 04 had ever been, and narrow, and black. Not painted black: black all the way through, like glass that has been in a fire. Its arms were too long. Its head was smooth and small. Along its spine, where every other exo had one capsule, it had two, side by side, joined down the middle, so that two pilots would sit shoulder to shoulder inside it like passengers on a train. Its designation was painted on its shoulder in white: 12.

Abriel was already there, sitting on the edge of the cradle with his legs swinging over the shaft, as if it were the roof. He waved when he saw her.

Eve Sakaki stood at the cradle's control stand with her tablet, the way she had stood beside Unit 00 every morning in another life. She didn't look at Val. "Both capsules are live," she said, to the tablet. "Seals are good. It's never flown." She touched something on the screen, and the cradle's locks let go one by one along its arms with a sound like knuckles cracking. "Nothing grown out of that Root has ever come back the way it went up. I'm only telling you because nobody else will."

Across the hangar, Unit 00 stood in its own cradle with its capsule open, and the girl with April's face stood in front of it in her flight suit, waiting.

"Valeria Kane," the girl said, when Val came near. "I will accompany you to the door."

Val looked at her for a moment. The short hair. Sam's eyes.

"Okay," she said.

That was all either of them said. It was the longest conversation they'd had.

A voice came over the hangar speakers, mild and even, from somewhere far above in the ruins. She had heard that voice read her the weather. She had heard it say *Be careful* at the end of a hundred briefings. She had heard it say *Lieutenant, Proxy authorization* in a port, once, without rising at all.

"Valeria," Patrick said. "Have you eaten?"

Somewhere up there beside him, at the next desk in the ops room, Ruth Hermon would be sitting with her reading glasses on their chain, not asking.

She stood under Unit 12 with her hands in her parka pockets and didn't answer. The speaker hissed for a while, as if he were waiting. Then: "Launch when ready."

Abriel climbed in first, and Val followed.

The double capsule was close and dim and smelled of new resin. The two seats sat side by side with no wall between them, so close that her shoulder touched his when she strapped in. The walls were dark. Abriel looked over at her and smiled, the same smile as the roof, as if this were just another night and they were just going to look at the sky.

"Ready?" he said.

She wasn't. She nodded.

The link opened.

It opened in both of them at once, and it was nothing like 04, nothing like Navis, nothing like the hollow's empty dark. It was like stepping into a river with someone else and feeling the current take you both. She felt Unit 12 around her, huge and cold and new. She felt Abriel beside her inside it, not as a voice or a pressure but as a presence, warm and very large, larger than he looked, much larger, like looking at a boy and seeing the shadow of a mountain behind him.

The Halo around her throat began to blink red, sensing the spike. A thin high tone started up under her jaw.

Abriel looked at it. He reached over and touched it with two fingers, lightly, the way you'd touch someone's new scarf.

"Commander Moriah gave me the code for this," he said. "He gives me anything I ask for. I could take it off you."

"Then take it off."

He seemed to think about it. Then he took his hand away.

"No," he said. "If I take it off, there's nobody holding the other end."

"He's never pressed it."

"I know," said Abriel, and smiled, as if that settled it. "That's why I'm leaving it."

She didn't understand him. She didn't ask. The tone went on under her jaw, thin and patient, and he took her hand instead and squeezed it.

Unit 12 stepped off the cradle and went down.

It fell the way a stone falls into a well, past the broken levels of headquarters, past the scorched frame of the old drive wing, down into the blue, and the glow came up to meet them until the capsule walls were full of it. At the bottom of the shaft was the Reliquary: a basement the size of a cathedral, cut out of the bedrock under headquarters, with nothing in it but cold and the Root.

She had seen it once before, in the Almost, opening like an eye. It was closed now, curled in on itself, pale as the inside of a shell and the size of a house, and frost grew off it in long slow ferns up the walls of the shaft. Two swords stood in it side by side, driven in to the hilt.

One burned with a cold white fire that flickered, like a flame in a wind, always about to go out. She knew that one. She had watched it come up the shaft at her once, and stop a hand's width from Navis's throat, and hang there, and turn. The other burned perfectly still, like a flame that had forgotten how to flicker. That one had gone through her.

"Despair and hope," Abriel said. "That's what they call them upstairs. They like names." He looked at the two of them the way he looked at constellations. "Don't think about what you want, Val. Think about what you're going up there to give."

Unit 12 reached down with both hands and closed them on the hilts.

The swords fought. She felt it through the link, in her own palms: both of them straining against the grip, turning their points toward Unit 12's throat, the way they were made to, the way any sword turns on a thing like this. Then they felt her. She felt them feel her, the way you feel someone look at you in a dark room. The flickering one went quiet in Unit 12's right hand almost at once, as if it remembered her. The steady one took longer. It held its point at Unit 12's throat for a long moment, burning, deciding. Then it lowered.

They came out of the Root the way a splinter comes out: all at once, with a sound she felt in her teeth.

Unit 12 rose out of the ruins of headquarters with a sword in each hand and climbed toward the seam in the sky.

Sixty kilometers to the north, in a cloud bank over the mountains, the Vigil hung in the air on half power, the way it had hung there for twelve days.

Nobody aboard had asked why the commander kept the ship holding station within reach of the ruins of Hakobune, when there was a whole frozen world to hide in. Nobody had needed to ask.

On the bridge, at four in the afternoon, the small black switch on the corner of Alex's console chirped.

He picked it up. For twelve days its readout had shown almost nothing, only a faint carrier signal at the edge of its range, a heartbeat, steady, from somewhere inside the ruins. Now it was lit. The sync line on it was climbing. It went past the mark where Alex had set the soft limit months ago, and past the mark beyond that, and kept going, smoothly, as if there were no marks at all.

"Commander," said the sensor officer. "Something's coming up out of the ruins. One unit. Big. It's climbing. It's..." She stared at her screen. "It's going for the seam."

Alex looked at the switch in his hand. He didn't put it down.

"Seus," he said, on the open channel. His voice was perfectly calm. "Maggie. Launch."

### Chapter 55: Undo

The seam opened for them as if it had been waiting.

From the ground it had always been a line, a long bright crack down the middle of the sky. From inside Unit 12, climbing toward it, it was a door. It was taller than mountains and thinner than a hair, and along its edges the light didn't fall out of it so much as lean out, curious, like someone looking through a gap in a curtain. The closer they came, the more it seemed to widen to meet them, without moving, the way a face seems to come closer when it smiles.

They climbed through the red haze and came out above it, and for a moment, before the door filled everything, there were stars: real ones, hard and small and close, more of them than she had ever seen from the roof.

"Look," Abriel said, delighted, exactly the way he said it on the roof. "You can see the Pencil from up here."

She looked. It was there, low and crooked, three in a row. He looked as pleased as if he'd named it himself.

She had come to answer a question. She held on to that. But the closer they got to the door, the more she wanted something else.

She wanted to go back. She wanted to undo the white room, the light, the districts lifting, the shelter under the school. She wanted the morning before. She wanted Akari's punch and Nanami's good mornings and Haruto's camera in her face and Seus in the apartment next door complaining through the wall. She wanted the seventh day back, the first day back, a hangar full of strangers and a child's voice asking her for a name. She wanted it so much that it hurt in the link, a pressure in the chest of Unit 12 like a held breath, and Unit 12 climbed faster, as if her wanting were fuel.

In Unit 12's left hand, the steady sword began to flicker.

Halfway to the door, she understood with horrible clarity exactly who she sounded like.

*You'd burn the sky to see your daughter again.*

She had said that to a voice in Navis's walls, in a corridor turned to frost, to a man who had waited twenty-seven years to go back to one morning. A man who would burn the sky to see his daughter again. She had torn the core out of him for wanting this. She had killed him for wanting this, and now she was him.

Her hand tightened on Abriel's. He didn't say anything. He was looking up at the door, and his face was very calm, and very far away.

Below them, on the frozen plain, three units came out of the dark.

The first came fast and ugly, flying low over the ice on jets that coughed and stuttered: a patched-together thing, mismatched plates welded over mismatched bones, three hollows' worth of white armor and the long gray arms of some second-generation unit nobody had wanted. Its welds still glowed from the Vigil's hold. Its paint wasn't dry. It had been built in four days around a core that a boy had carried down from orbit in his arms, and it was flying because that boy had kicked it until it flew.

"Kane!" Seus was screaming over the open channel, his voice cracking with the cold and the speed. "Kane! Turn around, you idiot! That's not a door, it's a mouth!"

Val's breath stopped.

He was alive. She had known that for weeks. She had sat on the floor of the Vigil's bay with him standing over her. But she had not heard his voice say her name like that, high and raw and terrified, since a port, since a comm channel, since *It's okay, Val, it's okay.* He was coming after her.

"How do you know?" she said into the channel. It came out a whisper.

"Because it's laughing!" Seus shouted. "The thing in my eye! It knows what that is! It's laughing, Kane, and it's hungry! Turn around!"

The second unit came behind him, very old, rattling, orange under its scorch marks, climbing in long patient arcs. Its pilot was humming. It was something old, from before the white sky, and she hummed it the whole way up, and between the bars, very gently, she said, "Kiddo. Kiddo, come on down now. Come on."

The third unit came up out of the ruins behind Unit 12, white, with no number painted on it anywhere. Zero-Four, on her commander's orders, coming to make sure they went through.

Unit 00 turned in the air and dropped between Unit 12 and the two units climbing toward it, and spread its arms.

"Get out of my way!" Seus screamed at it.

"My orders are to ensure they reach the door," said Zero-Four. Her voice on the channel was perfectly flat. It was Val's voice, almost, at nine.

"Your commander is my *father*, and he's garbage! You're a— you're a copy of a—" Seus's patched unit slammed into Unit 00 in midair, and the two of them tumbled, white and gray, end over end above the plain. "Move!"

"Seus, go!" Maggie's unit came in from the side and caught Unit 00 by the arm and wrenched it away from him, still humming, a little breathless now. "The white one's mine. Go get her!"

He went. He came up out of the tumble and climbed, coughing jets and all, toward Unit 12, and Unit 12 kept climbing away from him toward the door, and the door kept widening without moving.

Inside the capsule, Val looked at Abriel.

"He came after me," she said.

"He did," said Abriel.

"He hates me."

Abriel turned his head and looked at her, and his face was gentle and sad and very old.

"People don't climb into the sky after people they hate," he said.

Val looked at the channel display, at Seus's call sign blinking on it, getting closer, getting closer. She could stop. She knew she could. She could take her hands off the controls, or say *turn around* to the boy beside her, and Unit 12 would turn, and she would go down to the plain and let Seus scream at her for the rest of her life.

Or she could go through, and undo it. And if she undid it, he would never hate her. He would never die in a port. He would never wake up with a patch on his eye and burns on his neck and a father who looked at him like a clerical error. None of it would happen. Nobody would ever have to climb into the sky after anyone.

She chose. She knew she was choosing, this time. Nobody else's button. Nobody else's hand.

She kept climbing.

Abriel didn't stop her. He only said, very quietly, almost to himself, "You always do." There was no blame in it at all.

It was a trap. It had always been a trap. And here she was, doing it for them.

Unit 12 reached the seam, and the swords went into Unit 12.

She didn't push them. They went on their own, out of Unit 12's hands and into its chest, the way keys go into a lock they were cut for. Both of them were flickering now, the same cold restless fire, so that she couldn't have told anymore which one had been which. Somewhere inside Unit 12, inside her, something turned.

The real Third War on Heaven began.

The door opened, right above their heads, where there was nothing to open. Not the half-open seam the Almost had left: wider, and wider, splitting the sky from the hills to the top of the world, as if someone were drawing back a curtain the size of the sky. What came through it this time wasn't light. It was cold. A cold so deep it had a sound, a high singing like a finger run around the rim of a glass, and where it touched the sky, the sky turned to glass. Val watched it happen through Unit 12's eyes. The red haze over the world went clear and hard and then cracked, in long white lines, like ice on a pond when someone steps on it. She knew that sound. She had heard it a hundred times through 04, when a Nephil's seal gave way under her hands. Now it was the whole sky. The clouds froze where they hung and fell, slowly, in pieces, a rain of crystal the size of houses, turning in the light as they came down.

On the cracked Moon, Sariel opened its eyes.

Val saw it. Everyone on Earth who was looking up saw it. In the long red crack down the face of the Moon, two lights opened, very far apart, very slowly, the way a sleeper opens their eyes in the morning and doesn't yet know where they are. They looked down. They looked down at the world, and at the door, and at the black unit hanging in it.

Then the flesh began to fall.

It came down over the frozen plain like snow: soft, pale, drifting flakes of something that wasn't snow. Saint flesh. Pieces of the belt, pieces of the Moon, pieces of the old dead fathers in the sky, shaken loose by the cold and falling to Earth in their billions, settling on the ice, on the ruins, on Seus's patched armor, like snow made of skin.

For fifty years the Church of the Saints had prayed for Heaven on Earth. This was what it looked like.

And through the link, through the door, through the cold, Val felt them wake. All across the solar system, in the belt, under the ice of Europa, deep in the stone of the Moon, the Nephilim that had not yet fallen began to stir inside their fathers. Thousands of them. She felt them the way you feel a crowd behind a wall: thousands of small, slow heartbeats starting up in the dark, one after another, like children waking in a dormitory at the sound of a bell. They turned toward the door. They turned toward home.

On the plain, a wave of the cold came down off the door like a wind off a glacier and hit the three units below.

Seus's patched unit was thrown across the plain like a toy, tumbling, shedding plates, and came down on its back in the snow of flesh, and lay still. Lizzie went down skidding, sideways, a long orange streak across the ice, throwing up crystal, and Maggie's humming cut off on the channel mid-bar. Unit 00 was struck out of the air by a falling shard of frozen cloud the size of a train, and broke, and fell, and its pilot fell with it.

The channel went quiet. Not silent. Quiet: a hiss, empty, very wide.

The Almost had broken the world. The Third War broke Val.

### Chapter 56: Final Form

The Halo at her throat was blinking.

It had been blinking slowly since the hangar, a patient red, in time with her heartbeat. Now it blinked faster. And faster. The tone came back, high and thin, not under her jaw now but everywhere, filling the capsule, because she wanted, and the door was taking everything she wanted and turning it into width.

Beside her, Abriel was changing.

She could feel it through the link. His sync had gone past anything Unit 12 had been built to measure: past human, past exo, past the line where the numbers on the walls simply stopped and turned into symbols Val didn't know. Something enormous was unfolding inside him, a shape far too large for a body. It was like being in a small room with someone and feeling them begin to stand up, and keep standing up, and keep standing up, until their head was through the ceiling and their shoulders were through the roof and they were still standing up.

Outside, through Unit 12's eyes, she saw it happen to the unit too. Crystal was growing down its black arms, white and branching, like frost racing across a windowpane. Its fingers lengthened. Over its small smooth head, a shape was forming, pale and blank and beautiful, like a mask carved out of ice. She had seen that mask before. She had seen it on giants falling over the river, over the harbor, over the plant. She had broken a hundred of them.

The capsule was getting cold.

Frost was forming on the walls, spreading from Abriel's side of the capsule toward hers in long white ferns. Her breath was smoking. His wasn't. His skin had gone pale and faintly clear at the edges, like the second skin of ice on a puddle, and under it, very faintly, there was light.

"It's all right," he told her from the seat beside hers.

His voice sounded farther away than it should have. As if he were speaking from the bottom of a well, or the top of a stair.

"I was always going to go back." He smiled at her. The light under his skin moved when he smiled. "I'm the last one, Val. The last child, and the last name in their book. The door needs one of us to open the rest of the way."

"You knew," Val said. "You knew what it was."

"I always know." He shook his head slowly, and frost fell out of his hair, and he was still smiling. "I come anyway. Every time."

"Why?"

"Because you come," Abriel said, as if it were obvious. "And I'd rather be here."

"Then stop. Stop it, turn around, we can—"

"I can't stop it. It's already got hold of me." He looked down at his hand, at the light moving under the skin. "It wants a giant. If I go through as that, it opens all the way, for all of them, and the sky comes down for good. If I go as me, it's only a boy going home. It doesn't open for a boy."

"Then go as you."

"I never manage it." He said it simply, without any pity for himself, the way he'd once said the sea was salt. "Every time, it gets too big. Every time, I end up as that."

She understood, then. All at once, the way you understand that a step is missing in the dark. Every time. Every turn of whatever this was, this was the moment: this seat, this cold. And every time he ended up as the thing she had spent seven years breaking, and the door opened all the way, and the stairs began again.

"No," Val said.

"Val."

"No. No, no, no. It's mine." The word came out of her like something torn. She had never said it. Not about anything. Not luck, not the plan, not 04. "I did this. You said so, on the tower. It's mine. I opened it. Take me instead, let it be me, give it to me—"

"It can't be you," Abriel said gently. "You're not one of us. It takes three kinds of flesh and a sword that's willing. You're the human. I'm the other thing. Unit 12 is the rest. And one of us has to walk through. It was never going to be you." He smiled again. "I didn't come up here to fix anything, Val. I just wanted to see the sky with someone first."

The frost reached her seat.

It came over the armrest and onto her hand, and it burned, the way cold burns, white and sudden. Abriel saw it and his face changed for the first time, a flicker of something like fear, not for himself.

"Let go," he said. "Val. You'll freeze. Let go of me."

She thought of a transport manifest, and a callsign crossed out in small neat handwriting, and a page folded under a pillow.

She didn't let go.

She tore the straps off her shoulders and climbed out of her seat and into his, into the cold, and put her arms around him.

"Oh," Abriel said softly, and he sounded, for the first time since she had known him, surprised. "You've never done that before."

The cold went into her all at once. It went through her flight suit and her skin and into her bones, into her teeth, behind her eyes. Her eyelashes froze. Her hair went stiff with frost. Her fingers went white, then numb, then nothing. And still she held on, with her arms around a boy who was turning into the thing she had been killing since she was nine, and she didn't let go.

"Everybody who stands next to me dies," she said into his shoulder. Her lips were stiff. The words came out in pieces. "Everybody. You know that, right?"

"I know," said Abriel. "I picked the seat anyway."

Through the link, she felt the enormous shape inside him pause.

It didn't stop. It was still unfolding, still standing up through the ceiling and the roof and the sky. But it paused, the way a tide pauses at the top before it turns, because something small and stubborn and frozen was holding on to the boy at the bottom of it.

"Oh," Abriel said again, softer. "That helps. Hold on, then. Hold on to me, and I'll stay me." He laughed, a little, and his breath didn't smoke at all. "I want to go as me. Not as that."

So she held on.

Sixty kilometers to the north, on the Vigil's bridge, the sync line on the small black switch had gone off the top of its scale.

Alex held it in his palm with his thumb resting on it, not pressing, the way he had held it for months. On the main screen, the long-range cameras showed the door, wide open now from the hills to the top of the world, and the black unit hanging in the middle of it with something white growing over its face.

"Commander." The sensor officer's voice was shaking. "The collar. If you cut her link... it might stop it. It might close."

Nobody on the bridge said anything. Everyone was looking at his hand.

*Would you sacrifice the city for one life?* a mild voice had asked him once, across an ops room, with a boy screaming over the speakers. He had stood down. He had chosen the city. And weeks later, under a beam, a woman with dust on her hands had held his face and told him to say no to her, one more time, and he had.

His thumb hung a millimeter above the switch.

"No," Alex said.

He said it to the sensor officer, and to the mild voice in the ops room, and to the sky. Then he closed his hand around the switch so that his thumb was nowhere near it, and held it against his chest, and watched the screen.

In the capsule, she held on.

She held on while the frost climbed her arms and her shoulders and her neck. She held on while the light under his skin got brighter and the mask over Unit 12's face finished forming, white and smooth and blank. She held on while the Halo's tone rose and rose at her throat, until it wasn't a tone anymore but a single held note, like the cold singing out of the door. She held on to him the way she had held April in the white room, with everything, and this time she knew what she was holding and she held it anyway.

He put his hand on the back of her frozen head, very gently.

"I've done this so many times," he said into her hair. "You don't know. Every time, I bring you to the swords and tell you they can fix it. Every time, you believe me, because you want it so much. And every time, I end up in this seat, and it gets too big, and the stairs start over." The light moved under his skin. "This time I didn't tell you that. I don't even know why. I just said answer her. And you climbed into the cold and held on to me." He laughed, very softly. "That's the only thing that's different. That's all it ever was."

She felt it go through him, through the link: the one thing he had wanted every time around, the only thing he had ever been for. That she should have what she wanted. The door open, the morning back, all of it undone. She felt him set it down.

"I don't want it open for you anymore," Abriel said. "I want you to answer her. That's all. Just answer her."

And Val noticed, the way you notice a noise has stopped, that she wasn't wanting the white room anymore either. She hadn't for minutes. She had only wanted him to stay him.

Inside Unit 12, deep in the lock where the two swords had gone in, one of them stopped flickering. It burned white and still again, like a flame that had forgotten how to flicker, and turned the other way.

"Okay," Abriel said quietly, the way you'd say it at the top of a long flight of stairs. "I'm going now. As me."

"No—"

"I'm going to remember this one," he said. "See you next time around."

He let go.

Not of her. Of the other thing: the enormous shape standing up through the sky. She felt him put it down, the way you'd set down something you've carried so long you forgot it was heavy. The mask over Unit 12's face cracked straight down the middle. The crystal on its arms went still. The shape inside him simply wasn't there anymore, the way a shadow isn't there when the light goes out.

He let out a breath, a long one, like someone reaching the top of a stair.

And then he wasn't there either.

He came apart in her arms. Not all at once: softly, from the edges in, the way frost comes off a window when the sun hits it. His hair first, into a fine bright powder. Then his shoulders. Then the weight of his head. He went up out of her arms as frost and light, through the open top of the capsule, up into the cold, rising, like snow falling in reverse, toward the door. Toward the Moon. Toward home.

Val held the place where he had been.

Above them, the door stopped. It hung wide open for a moment longer, very still, as if listening for someone who wasn't coming. It had been waiting for a giant. What came up to it was a boy, and a sword turning the other way inside him. Then, slowly, from the edges in, it began to close. It drew itself together across the sky, the way a wound closes, until there was only a seam again, and then less than a seam: a long pale scar from the hills to the top of the world, faintly lit, healed over, not gone.

Val watched it close through Unit 12's eyes. She had come all this way to answer a question. The door was closing, and she hadn't answered.

The crystal stopped falling. The flesh stopped falling. On the Moon, the two lights in the crack went dim, and then dark. In the belt and under the ice, the small heartbeats slowed, and slowed, and went still again, unfinished.

Unit 12 fell out of the sky with nobody flying it.

It came down onto the frozen plain on its back, hard, and slid a long way, and stopped.

Sixty kilometers to the north, the line on the small black switch had spiked once, higher than it had ever gone, at the moment he came apart in her arms. Alex's hand closed tighter around it. Then the line fell, and fell, and fell, and steadied, very low and very faint. A heartbeat. Hers.

He hadn't pressed it. He looked at it in his hand for a long time, at the small faint line that was still moving. Then he sat down where he was, on the floor of the bridge, in front of the whole crew, and nobody said anything.

An hour later the storm of falling crystal reached the mountains, tore the Vigil's port flight surfaces off, and drove the ship north, blind, for two hundred kilometers. By the time anyone had it under control again, the carrier signal had slid off the edge of the switch's range, and there was nothing on any channel from the plain but static.

When it was over, the sky was dark, darker than it had been in seven years, and the world was colder than it had been, and quieter. Val sat in the ruin of Unit 12, in the seat that wasn't hers, with her arms still frozen in the shape of holding someone and her hand still reaching toward the empty air in front of her, and something in her finally gave way, all the way down to the bottom.

### Chapter 57: The Walk

They found each other on the frozen plain afterward: three survivors in a dead world.

Seus came first, limping out of his ruined unit, bleeding from the scalp and still furious.

His patched armor lay on its back in the snow of flesh with its chest split open and steam coming out of the split. He climbed out of the capsule into a cold that took his breath away, a new cold, a worse one, and stood there in the soft pale drifts with the blood running down into his good eye. He wiped it away with his sleeve. He tried his comms. Nothing. He tried the WAKE frequency, the emergency band, Maggie's channel. Nothing. Static, and under the static, faintly, something that sounded like singing and wasn't.

He looked up. There was no sign of the Vigil anywhere in the sky. There was no sign of anything. The sky was dark and scarred and empty, and the Moon had gone dull, like a coal going out.

"Great," Seus said, to nobody. "Great. That's great."

He found Unit 12 by the long scar it had cut across the ice. It lay at the end of it, black and broken, with crystal frozen in branches down one arm and a white mask split down the middle over its face. Two swords lay in the ice beside it, where they had fallen out of its chest when the door shut. One burned with a cold fire that flickered like a candle in a draft. The other burned perfectly still. The thing behind his eye, which had been laughing all day, went quiet when he passed them, the way a dog goes quiet near a stove it has been burned on. He climbed up its side, slipping, swearing, and got the capsule open with a piece of broken plating.

Val was inside. She was sitting in the wrong seat, the left one, with frost all over her: in her hair, on her eyelashes, in a white crust over her flight suit. Her arms were up in front of her, curved, frozen in place in the shape of holding someone. There was nobody in them. At her throat, the collar blinked once, slowly, red, looking for a signal that wasn't there.

Seus stared at her.

"Kane," he said.

She didn't move.

He had to break the ice off her sleeves to get her arms down. He did it with the heel of his hand, swearing, chipping it off in white flakes, and when her arms finally came down they just dropped into her lap and lay there. He hauled her out by the back of her flight suit. She didn't resist. She didn't do anything. Her eyes were open, and there was no one behind them.

He dragged her down off Unit 12's side and set her on her feet in the snow of flesh, and she stood, the way a coat stands when you hang it on a hook, and he let go, and she sat down.

"Get up," Seus said. "Get up. Walk."

She didn't.

"Kane. I'm serious. Get up. It's minus forty and dropping and nobody's coming, do you understand me? Nobody is coming. Alex isn't coming. Maggie's—" He stopped. He looked across the plain toward the long orange streak on the ice, a kilometer off, where Lizzie had gone down. Nothing moved there. "Nobody is coming. Get up."

She didn't.

He swore, slung her arm over his shoulder, and walked for both of them.

He picked south, because the scar in the sky ran north, and he wanted it behind him.

A little way behind them came the girl from Unit 00. She had climbed out of her own wreck with her flight suit torn down one side and a cut across her forehead that she didn't seem to have noticed. Now she followed them across the ice at a distance, Sam Kane's face expressionless, because no one had given her any other orders.

Seus noticed her after the first hour. He stopped and turned around, with Val hanging off his shoulder.

"Stop following us," he said.

The girl stopped. "I have no orders."

"Then go somewhere."

"I have no orders to go somewhere."

Seus looked at her for a long time. The cut on her forehead. The torn suit. The face, which was so nearly Val's face that it made his teeth hurt.

"Fine," he said. "Then follow us. Whatever."

He turned around and kept walking. After a moment, the girl kept following.

He talked. He couldn't help it. He was carrying the person he hated most in the world across a frozen plain under a dark sky, and her feet dragged in the snow, and she didn't say anything, and if he didn't talk he would have to listen to the silence and the static that sounded like singing. So he talked.

"You don't get to do this," he told her. "You hear me? You don't get to just stop. I stopped once. I died. It's not restful. It's a year and a half of nothing and then you wake up and everyone's older and your exo's dead and your father has you escorted out of the building." He hitched her arm higher on his shoulder. "So you don't get to do it. Pick up your feet."

She didn't pick up her feet.

"You know what Alex said to me, when I woke up? The first thing? He said *welcome back*. Like I'd been on vacation. Like I'd gone to the beach." Seus laughed, and it hurt his ribs. "And then he said sorry. He's always saying sorry. You ever notice that? Sorry, sorry, sorry, and never for what." He walked. "You'd have liked my exo. Navis. It used to ask me if things were good. *Was that good?* After everything. After a fight. After a stupid dance on a harbor. And I never answered it, because I'm an idiot, because I thought there'd be time. And now it's dead. You killed it, forcing your way in. Or I did, a long time before that, by never answering it. I go back and forth." He walked. "Pick up your feet, Kane."

She didn't.

The dark came early. It always came early now. When it came, he found them a place: a piece of one of the hanging districts that had fallen, sometime since the Almost, and stuck in the plain at an angle, half buried. An apartment block, upside down. He kicked in a window and dragged Val through it, and they were standing on a ceiling, with the light fixtures sticking up out of the floor around them like strange plants, and the furniture all bolted to the roof over their heads, a table and four chairs hanging upside down in the dark like bats.

He broke one of the chairs down off the ceiling and made a fire out of it on the floor that had been a ceiling, in a bowl that had been a lampshade. He put Val next to it. She sat where he put her and looked at the fire with nobody behind her eyes, and he took off his WAKE jacket and put it around her, and swore at her for making him cold.

The girl from Unit 00 came in through the window after him and stood just inside it, at the edge of the firelight, with her arms at her sides.

"You can sit," Seus said.

"I have not been ordered to sit."

He stared at her. "Sit."

She sat, instantly, cross-legged on the ceiling, her back very straight.

"You didn't have to wait for me to say it," Seus said. "You can just sit. When you're cold. When you're tired. You don't need anybody to tell you."

The girl looked at him with Sam Kane's eyes. "I do not understand."

"Yeah," said Seus. "Me neither, most of the time."

He dug a ration bar out of the pocket of his flight suit, broke it in three, and pushed one piece into Val's frozen hand, where it stayed, and held out another toward the girl.

"I have not been ordered to eat," the girl said.

"Eat."

She ate it, in small, exact bites, looking at nothing.

"Next time," Seus said, "just eat it."

"If ordered," said the girl.

Seus looked at the two of them, the girl who wouldn't move and the girl who wouldn't stop following, one with his enemy's face and one with his enemy's father's face, sitting on a ceiling by a fire made out of a chair, and he put his head in his hands.

"Great," he said into his palms. "I'm a commander now."

He didn't sleep. Somebody had to keep the fire going, and somebody had to make sure Val kept breathing, and somebody had to watch the window in case something came through it. So he sat up all night, with his back against the upside-down wall and the cold in his bones, watching the two of them in the firelight, and every so often he put another piece of chair on the fire.

"Somebody has to sit up with the world," he muttered, around three in the morning, in a fair imitation of Alex's voice, and laughed at himself, and then didn't.

Toward dawn, the broken window went gray.

The girl from Unit 00 hadn't moved all night. Seus had his eyes half shut and a chair leg in his hand, ready for the fire. Val sat where he'd put her, beside the ashes, with his jacket around her shoulders and nobody behind her eyes.

The girl turned her head and looked at Val.

"Good morning," she said.

Seus's eyes came open.

Nobody had ordered it. He knew that, because he was the only one left who gave her orders, and he hadn't. She said it carefully, the way you'd read a word off a card you weren't sure was the right one. Somebody, Seus thought, had said that to her. Every morning. Until it stuck.

Val didn't answer. She looked at the ashes.

The girl waited a moment, as if there were a procedure for what came next and she couldn't find it. Then she turned back to the window and the gray light, and stood by.

Seus looked at the two of them for a long time.

"Yeah," he said finally, roughly, to nobody. "Morning."

---

## Arc 15: The Settlement

> *In the midst of the street of it, and on either side of the river, was there the tree of life, which bare twelve manner of fruits, and yielded her fruit every month: and the leaves of the tree were for the healing of the nations.*
>
> — Revelation 22:2

### Chapter 58: Found

They walked for two days.

On the third morning, a truck came over the ice.

Seus heard it before he saw it: an engine, coughing, very far off, which he decided for a long time was the static in his head. Then it came over a rise in the plain, slow and black against the gray: an old cargo truck with chains on its tires and a plow welded to the front, throwing up a fan of snow on either side. Painted on its door in uneven white letters was a name Val had never heard of. KASUMI.

It stopped thirty meters off. For a while nothing happened. Seus stood with Val's arm over his shoulder and the girl from Unit 00 a few steps behind him, and the truck's engine idled, and nobody got out, the way nobody gets out of a car when they've seen something on the road they don't understand.

Then the door opened, and a woman jumped down from the cab.

She wore a heavy coat patched at the elbows, and a medic's armband, white with a red cross faded to pink. She had a scar through one eyebrow and a ponytail that touched her collar. She came a few steps toward them through the snow and stopped, and stared at the three of them. Then she stared at Val.

"Kane?" she said. "Val Kane?"

It was Akari.

She was a grown woman now. They all were. Haruto came out of the back of the truck a second later, older and broader and bearded, with a camera around his neck. He let the camera drop on its strap and stood there with his mouth open. Akari was already crying, and angry about it, which was exactly like her.

"You idiot," she said, and crossed the last of the snow at a run, and pulled Val into a hug so hard it hurt. "You absolute idiot. We thought you were dead."

Val didn't hug her back. She didn't do anything. But her eyes moved to Akari's face, for the first time in two days.

Akari held on to her for a long time, with her face in the shoulder of Seus's old WAKE jacket, swearing into it. Then she let go and stepped back to look at her, and wiped her eyes with the heel of her glove, and turned to say something to the boy holding Val up.

She stopped.

Seus looked back at her. The eyepatch. The burns down his neck, shiny and pink. The jaw. Sixteen.

"Hi," he said.

Akari didn't move for a long moment. Then she walked up to him, very slowly, and took the front of his flight suit in both fists, and looked at his face from about ten centimeters away, as if she were checking it against something.

"You're sixteen," she said.

"Yeah. I get that a lot."

"You're dead. They said you were dead. They had a funeral, and they wouldn't let us in, and we stood outside the gate for an hour in our uniforms like idiots." Her voice was shaking. She turned her head and looked at Val, who looked at nothing. "*She* told me it was quick."

"It wasn't," Seus said. "Quick. Or dead. It's a long story."

Akari punched him in the shoulder, hard enough that he staggered, and Val staggered with him. Then she grabbed his sleeve and held on to it and didn't let go.

"I floated lanterns for you," she said. "Every year. Seven years. Seven lanterns, you jerk." She was crying again. "I want them back."

Seus opened his mouth and found nothing in it.

"...Sorry," he said.

Haruto had come up behind them in the snow. He was looking past all of them, at the girl from Unit 00, standing a few steps back with her torn suit and the cut on her forehead, Sam Kane's face blank under the gray sky.

"Who's that?" Haruto said slowly. "She looks like—"

"Don't," said Seus.

They had survived, all three of them.

They told it in the cab of the truck on the way back, with the heater roaring and Val wedged between Akari and the door, and the girl from Unit 00 sitting in the back on a sack of seed potatoes, straight-backed, looking at nothing. The shelter under the school had been evacuated before the ice sealed it. When the sky opened and the districts started to lift, Nanami had taken charge. Nobody told her to. She had gone from apartment to apartment in her pajamas with a flashlight, banging on doors, and gathered up the whole of Class 2-A and their families, and anyone else she could find, and walked them out of the city on foot, into the hills, in the dark, under a white sky, counting heads the entire way.

"She counted for three days," Haruto said. He was driving. His hands were steady on the wheel now, in a way they never had been. "Out loud. Every hour. A hundred and twelve. A hundred and twelve. A hundred and twelve. Then a hundred and eleven. Then a hundred and nine." He was quiet for a moment. "She still does it, sometimes. In her sleep."

They had lived out there ever since, in a settlement built in the ruins of an old farming town in a valley in the hills, with a few thousand other people who had made it out. There was a hot spring at the head of the valley, and a creek that ran down from it through the middle of the town, warm, the only running water for fifty kilometers that never froze. The steam came off it all day and all night and hung over the valley in a low white haze, and so they had named the place after that. Kasumi. Mist.

"Kōta's fifteen now," Akari said to Val, as if Val had asked. She hadn't. "He's still on oxygen at night, but he's fine. He's taller than me. He's going to lose his mind."

Seus, in the back with the potatoes and the girl, said very quietly, "Good."

Then he turned away toward the window so no one could see his face.

They came over the last rise at dusk, and Kasumi was below them: a valley full of steam, glowing from inside. Old tile roofs, half of them caved in and patched with tarps. Long greenhouses made of salvaged window glass, lit from inside like lanterns. Fields at the edges of the town, under rows of heat lamps on poles, glowing orange in the haze.
