Smoke going up from chimneys. And down the middle of all of it, the creek, black and steaming, with a little arched bridge over it and a lamp on the bridge.

When they stopped in the square, people came out of the houses to look. They looked at the truck, and at Akari, and at the boy with the eyepatch, and at the girl with the strange face climbing down off the potatoes. Then they looked at Val, being lifted down from the cab, and some of them knew her. Val saw them know her. She saw the word go through the crowd, the way it had gone through the Vigil's bay and Ararat's mess hall. *Kane.*

A small woman pushed through to the front. She had a neat ponytail and a baby tied to her chest in a sling, and a clipboard under her arm with a laminated sheet on it, and she stopped in front of Val and looked at her, and her face did something complicated and then went very calm.

"She's ours," Nanami Fujita said, to the whole square, in her class-rep voice. "She's with us. Akari, the clinic. Haruto, get the girl a blanket. Seus." She looked at him for a long moment and swallowed once and said, perfectly steadily, "Hi, Seus. You're with Akari."

And that was that. Nobody argued with Nanami. Nobody in Kasumi ever had.

They put Val in the truck again to take her up the hill to Nanami's house, because she wouldn't walk. The girl with Sam's face climbed in after her without being asked.

### Chapter 59: Nothing

The settlement took them in without questions.

Val stopped talking. She stopped eating unless someone put food in her hands, and she didn't move unless someone moved her. Nanami gave her the spare room in the house she shared with Haruto and their baby daughter, an old farmhouse at the top of the town with a sliding door onto a garden that had been dead since the sky opened and a kitchen with a big scarred wooden table. Val lay in the spare room, or sat at the kitchen table with her head resting on the wood, hour after hour, day after day.

She had sat like this at nine, in the principal's office. She had sat like this at sixteen, in a classroom, spinning a pencil. Same pose, three different girls. At nine and at sixteen she had felt nothing, because feeling nothing was safe. Now she felt everything at once, the white room and the shelter and Abriel's hand and Seus's eye, and she couldn't move under the weight of it.

The pencil lay on the table beside her hand. She had carried it out of a frozen desk, and through Ararat, and into the ruins, and across the plain in the pocket of her flight suit. Now it lay on the wood an inch from her fingers, and she didn't pick it up. She didn't spin it. Nanami moved it once, to wipe the table, and put it back exactly where it had been.

Nobody made her talk.

Akari came by every evening after the clinic closed, with her medic's bag, and took Val's pulse, and brought medicine she didn't take. She talked the whole time, the way she talked to unconscious patients, briskly, as if they could hear and had better be listening.

"Pulse sixty-two. Blood pressure's fine. Lungs are fine. Your everything is fine, Kane." She snapped the bag shut. "You're the healthiest corpse I've ever seen. And I've seen a lot of corpses. You'd be amazed what the cold does." She put two white pills on the table next to the pencil. "Take those. They won't fix anything, but they'll make me feel better."

Val didn't take them. They sat on the table next to the pencil until morning, when Nanami moved them too.

Haruto came and sat with her in the evenings after the baby was asleep, and told her about the settlement's generator.

He had built it himself, mostly, out of a turbine from a beached fishing boat and three kilometers of copper wire scavenged from the towns along the valley. It ran on the hot spring. The water came up out of the ground at eighty degrees, and he ran it through the turbine, and the turbine ran the heat lamps, and the heat lamps grew the food, and the food fed four thousand people. He explained all of it to the top of Val's head, in detail, with diagrams drawn on the backs of seed packets, in the same breathless voice he had once used for exo shoulder joints.

"It's not a documentary," he said, the third night, a little embarrassed, folding the seed packets back up. "But it's something."

He still had the camera. He had never stopped filming, he said; he had filmed the walk out of the city and the first winter and the first harvest and Nanami counting heads and the day Hinata was born. He had hours of Class 2-A, from before. Hours of Val, walking down hallways with her head down. He didn't offer to show her any of it, and she didn't ask.

And every morning, Nanami put a bowl of rice in front of her and said "Good morning, Val," exactly the way she used to.

Every morning, Val didn't answer.

Nanami didn't seem to mind. She said it on her way to somewhere else, with the baby on her chest and the laminated sheet under her arm, because Nanami ran Kasumi's ration board, and its work rota, and its school, which met in the old farmers' hall by the creek and had sixty children in it and one teacher. She said it the way she had said it in the doorway of Class 2-A every morning for a year, to someone who never once looked up: brightly, as if Val had already agreed to be her friend.

Akari's little brother came once, in the first week. Kōta was fifteen, taller than his sister, thin, all elbows, with a clear plastic line taped across his cheek for the oxygen bottle he carried in a backpack at night. He stood in the kitchen doorway and stared at Val with his head on the table, and opened his mouth, and Val could see the questions coming, a whole flood of them, the way they used to come out of him in a shelter under a school.

Akari took him by the backpack strap and towed him out before the first one landed.

At night, through the kitchen window, Fuji still burned on the horizon, low and red, like the last coal in a stove nobody had come back to bank.

One evening, late, Nanami sat down across the table from her with her baby asleep on her shoulder.

The house was quiet. Haruto was down at the generator shed. The girl from Unit 00 was standing in the corner of the kitchen, where she always stood. The baby was making small sleeping noises against Nanami's neck, and Nanami patted her back, slowly, without thinking about it, the way you breathe.

"Her name's Hinata," Nanami said. "I don't think anyone told you. It means a sunny place." She smiled down at the top of the baby's head. "Haruto said it was a strange name for a baby born under a sky like this. I said that's why."

Val's head stayed on the table. But her eyes were open, and they were on the baby.

"You never said hi back, you know," Nanami said softly. "Not once. All those mornings." She smiled. "I'm still going to say it."

### Chapter 60: Good Morning

The girl with Sam's face had no orders, so she stood.

She stood in the corner of Nanami's kitchen for the first two days, between the stove and the door, with her arms at her sides, the way she had stood in front of Unit 00 in the hangar. She didn't sit. She didn't lean. When people came in and out, she turned her head to watch them and then turned it back. When Nanami spoke to her, she answered in her flat clear voice and then went on standing. At night, when the lights went out, she was still standing there in the dark, and in the morning, when Nanami came down to light the stove, she was standing there still.

On the third morning, Nanami put down her kettle, took the girl gently by the elbow, steered her to the table, and sat her down across from Val. Then she put a bowl in front of her.

The girl looked at it.

"What is this?" she asked.

"Rice," Nanami said. "You eat it."

"Why?"

Nanami opened her mouth and closed it again. She looked at the girl, and at Val with her head on the table beside her, and at the baby on her own chest, and something in her face went very soft and very determined at the same time, the face of a class representative who has just been handed the hardest assignment of her life and is delighted about it.

"Because you'll be hungry if you don't," she said. "And because I made it. Here. Like this."

She taught her.

She taught her food first. Rice, and miso soup out of a heavy pot that lived on the back of the stove, and pickles from a crock in the cellar. She taught her chopsticks, which the girl held at first like a pair of tools, very precisely, in a grip that would have been perfect for a soldering iron and was useless for rice. The rice fell off. The girl looked at it on the table and picked it up again and it fell off again. Nanami laughed, not unkindly, and the girl stopped and stared at her, at her face, at the laugh, as if it were a new kind of weather. By the end of the week she could get a whole mouthful from the bowl to her mouth without losing a grain, and she did it with total concentration every time, like defusing something.

That first week, everything she held frosted over. A skin of white spread across the rice from wherever her fingers touched the bowl, and her soup went cold in the time it took to lift it, and where she stood too long the floorboards furred with rime. Nanami warmed everything twice and didn't say a word about it.

She taught her words. Not the words she already had, *designation* and *acknowledged* and *standing by*, but the small ones, the ones everybody used without thinking. "Good morning." "Thank you." "Good night." The girl practiced them out loud, stiffly, one at a time, like someone learning the controls of a machine.

"Good morning," she said, on the first try, to the stove.

"That's it," said Nanami. "To a person, though."

"Good morning," the girl said to Nanami. "Good morning. Good morning. Good morning."

"You only have to say it once a day."

"Why?"

"Because it's only a new morning once a day."

The girl considered this for a long time. She seemed to find it satisfying. The next morning she came into the kitchen, stood in front of Nanami, and said, "Good morning," once, precisely, and then didn't say it again for twenty-four hours, though Val, with her head on the table, could feel her wanting to.

She learned work. She helped with the laundry, in a long wooden tub in the yard with water carried up from the creek, steaming in the cold. She folded the clean shirts with mechanical precision into perfect identical squares, every edge aligned, and Haruto's work shirts came out of the basket for a week looking like something in a shop window, and he wore them anyway, stiff as cardboard, and didn't say a word.

She learned the baby.

Nanami put Hinata in her arms one afternoon, to free her own hands for the stove, and the girl took the baby the way she had taken the bowl, very carefully, with her whole body gone still. She held her out a little from her chest, as if she might go off. Hinata looked up at her and blinked. The girl looked down at Hinata. And she stood there, in the middle of the kitchen, holding the baby, and stared at her for an hour.

"What is she for?" she asked finally.

Nanami turned around from the stove with the spoon in her hand.

"She's not for anything," she said. "She's just Hinata."

The girl looked down at the baby for a long time.

"Hinata," she said.

She learned the fields. At the edge of the settlement, past the last houses, there were rows of frozen ground under heat lamps on poles, and every morning a crew went out to work them. The girl went with them, because Nanami sent her, and she learned to break the crust with a hoe and plant seedlings in the soft dark earth underneath, in rows, under the orange light, in a cold that would have killed anything not standing directly under a lamp. She did it perfectly. Every seedling was exactly the same distance from the last. The old farmers who ran the crew watched her for a day and then stopped watching, because there was nothing to correct.

When one of the seedlings sprouted, a week later, a single pale green thread coming up out of the dark earth under the lamp, she stood over it for a very long time. The crew went to lunch and came back and she was still standing there. Haruto came out with his camera and filmed her standing there, from a distance, for most of an hour, and didn't say anything, and when he showed the footage to Nanami that night, Nanami cried and didn't say why.

At dinner one night, Nanami asked her what they should call her.

"My designation is Zero-Four," the girl said.

"Can I call you something else?"

"I don't know." The girl turned her head. "Where do names come from?"

Nanami thought about it. "Somebody gives them to you," she said. "Usually somebody who loves you. Your parents, when you're born. Or a friend, sometimes. Somebody who knows you."

The girl turned her head and looked at Val, beside her, with her head on the table.

Then she started asking Val questions.

She didn't know why Val was the one she asked; she just did. Maybe because Val was the one she had been sent to bring back. Maybe because Val's face was the only one in the world that looked like her own. Maybe because Val was the one person in the house who never answered, and the girl had not yet learned that this meant anything.

"Valeria Kane," she said at breakfast. "What is this word?" And she pointed at a word on the side of the miso tin.

"Valeria Kane. Why does the baby cry?"

"Valeria Kane. Do you have a name for me?"

Val didn't answer. Day one. Day two. Day three.

Each question landed on her where she sat, with her cheek against the wood and her eyes open. Each one went in like a splinter. She knew this. She knew this exactly. She had lived it, from the other side, in a hangar full of strangers when she was nine. A voice too bright and too curious, asking and asking, and a girl who said nothing, and the questions getting smaller every day until they stopped.

On the fourth day, the girl asked why the creek steamed. On the fifth, she asked what Val's favorite thing was. On the sixth, she asked whether Val was sick, and when Val didn't answer, she asked Akari, and Akari looked at Val for a long moment and said, "Yeah. Kind of."

On the seventh day, Val braced herself.

She knew how this went. On the seventh day, the questions stopped. That was the shape of it. That was the shape of everything. She lay with her head on the table and waited for the silence to come down over the kitchen like a lid, and for the girl to become exactly like her.

Anyone who remembered the first seven days would have expected the seventh to end it. It didn't.

On the eighth day, she asked why the snow was sometimes blue at night.

On the ninth day, she sat down across the table from Val and asked why Val kept her head on the table.

On the tenth day, she asked where the creek went. On the eleventh, she asked why Haruto talked to the generator. On the twelfth, she asked four things before breakfast and three after, and one of them was what color Val's eyes were, because she couldn't see them from that angle.

The old cringe came back, every unanswered question landing the way it had in the first seven days. It came back so hard that some mornings Val could barely breathe under it. And then slowly, day by day, so slowly she didn't notice it happening, it turned into its opposite.

The girl didn't stop. That was all. She asked, and Val didn't answer, and the next day she asked again, the same as if Val had answered, as if there were nothing in the world more natural than asking someone something. She never got smaller. She never got quieter. She never learned.

The not-stopping was hope.

On the twentieth day, Haruto taped a sheet of paper to the door of the icebox, with two columns ruled on it in pencil. QUESTIONS, said the left one, with a long row of tally marks under it. ANSWERS, said the right one, with nothing under it at all.

Akari saw it that evening and went straight for it, to tear it down, and Nanami stopped her with a hand on her arm.

"Leave it," Nanami said.

From the table, with her cheek on the wood, Val could see the sheet sideways. A long row of marks on one side and nothing on the other. She had been on the wrong side of a sheet like that once, for seven days, and the questions on it had stopped. These hadn't. Nobody had answered this girl once, and she asked again every morning anyway, as if the silence were only weather.

### Chapter 61: Akari

Seus stayed with Akari.

Nobody planned it. Nanami had said *you're with Akari* in the square, in her class-rep voice, and nobody had ever found a way to undo one of Nanami's assignments. Akari's clinic was an old pharmacy at the bottom of the town by the creek, with a room above it full of boxes. He needed somewhere to sleep, and she needed someone to lift things. So he moved the boxes, and slept on the floor between them in a borrowed bedroll, and lifted things.

They argued about everything, exactly like at school.

They argued about how to stack firewood, his way, in a neat square tower that fell over twice, or hers, in a sloppy heap that never fell over at all. They argued about whether the clinic's little generator needed oil, and how much, and who had last checked. They argued about whose turn it was to cook, and then about what had been cooked, and then about whether it could be described as food. They argued in the clinic and on the stairs and in the street, at a volume the whole settlement could hear, and the whole settlement had started placing bets.

Haruto ran the book. It was on a chalkboard in the generator shed. The odds changed weekly.

At night, when the clinic was quiet, Akari changed the dressing on his scalp, and he let her.

He sat on the edge of the examining table with his shirt off, because the burns went down his neck and over one shoulder and she checked those too, and she stood behind him with a lamp and a tray of gauze and worked in silence. It was the only time of day they didn't argue. Outside the window, the creek steamed in the dark. The lamp on the little bridge made a small yellow circle on the water.

"Does it hurt?" she asked once, touching the edge of the eyepatch.

"Sometimes," he said. "Sometimes it talks."

Her fingers stopped. "What does it say?"

"Mostly?" He stared at the wall. "*Let me out.* Over and over. Like a kid kicking the back of a seat." He was quiet for a while. "It's a piece of the man. Of the giant he was half of. It got into me in the port, when Navis grabbed him. Alex says it's why I don't get older. Says it's sort of keeping me." He laughed, without much in it. "Like a pet. Except I'm the pet."

Akari went back to the bandage.

"I died for her," Seus said. "Did anyone tell you that? I was in Navis, and the intruder had it, and it was tearing through a block of flats, and Kane was standing there with her hands down, and I told her it was okay. I said goodbye and everything. I said thanks for the dinner." He shook his head, and she held it still. "And then I woke up a year and a half later in a basement, and she'd—" He gestured, meaning the window, the sky, the ice, all of it. "It didn't mean anything. I didn't mean anything."

Akari was quiet for a while.

"She told me it was quick," she said. "I asked her. After. I gave her your stupid goldfish, the one you won at the festival, because I couldn't look at it, and I asked her if it was quick, and she said yes." Her hands kept working. "I figured out she was lying around year three. People who saw it quick don't look like that when they say it."

Seus didn't say anything.

"You died for her," Akari said finally, "and she's still here. That's not nothing, idiot." She tied off the bandage too tight on purpose. He yelped. "Also, you're still here. So that's two."

Most nights, after the dressing, he didn't sleep.

He went down to the generator shed instead, where Haruto kept an old military radio on a shelf above the turbine, and sat on an upturned crate with the headphones on, turning the dial through the WAKE bands one at a time. He knew them by heart. Command. Lantern. The emergency band. The Vigil's bridge. He listened to each one for a long time, with his good eye shut, and every one of them was static.

He never said who he was listening for. Haruto never asked. But after the first week, Haruto stopped locking the shed.

Kōta found him one morning splitting firewood in the yard behind the clinic.

He stood at the edge of the yard with his hands in his pockets and his oxygen backpack hanging off one shoulder, and watched Seus miss the log three times, and then he couldn't stand it anymore.

"Is it true you flew Navis?" he said. "The blue one? The third-generation one, with the long arms? Is it true you can feel it when they hit the exo? Does it hurt more than a real punch? Akari says you can't get older. Is that true? Does it hurt? How old are you really? What's in your eye? Can you see out of it? Is it true the exos talk?"

Seus lowered the axe and looked at him.

Then, to his own surprise, he answered. All of them. One at a time, in order, gruffly, without looking up from the log. Yes. Yes. Yes, about the same, but everywhere at once. Yes. Not exactly. Twenty-three, technically. A piece of a giant. No. Some of them.

"Cool," said Kōta, and sat down on the woodpile to ask more.

Behind the clinic, under a tarp in the snow, there was a crate.

A week after they came to Kasumi, Seus had gone back out to the plain with Haruto and the truck and a cutting torch, to the place where his patched unit lay on its back in the drifts with its chest split open. It had taken them a whole day to cut the core out of it. It came out the way a core always came out, a sphere of saint flesh about the size of a child, heavy, still faintly warm, and they had wrapped it in blankets and driven it home in the back of the truck with the seed potatoes, and put it in a crate behind the clinic, under a tarp.

Every so often, it tried to talk to him, the way 04 had once tried to talk to Val.

He'd be in the yard, splitting wood, or crossing to the outhouse at night, and a voice would come from under the tarp. Not to his ears. Nobody else ever heard it. It came in through the eye, through the piece of the giant behind his patch, the way cores had always sounded to him since the port: faint and muffled, in a tone like someone clearing their throat in an empty room.

"Pilot?"

He never answered.

"Your box is talking again," Akari would say, from the clinic window, because his hand had gone to the patch again, the way it always did.

"It's not my box."

"It's your box."

He never answered it. But he never let anyone move it, either, and when it snowed hard, he went out in the night and brushed the snow off the tarp.

### Chapter 62: The Sky

On the forty-first day, the girl with Sam's face sat down across from Val at Nanami's kitchen table and looked out the window at the dark gray sky.

It was afternoon. Nanami was at the school. Haruto was at the generator. Hinata was asleep in her basket by the stove. The kitchen was warm and quiet and smelled of miso, and the paper on the icebox door had a long, long row of tally marks under QUESTIONS, four hundred and something, and nothing under ANSWERS. Outside, the sky over Kasumi was the same as it always was now, the dark low gray of the world after the Third War, with the long pale scar running down the middle of it from the hills to the top of the world.

The girl looked at it for a long time with her chin in her hand. She had learned that from Kōta. She had learned a great many things from a great many people in forty-one days, and she held them all very carefully, like the baby.

"Valeria Kane," she said. "Why does the sky look blue during the day?"

Val lifted her head off the table.

It was the second day's question, word for word: the one 04 had asked her when she was nine, through a hangar's open doors, looking up. *On the second day, it asked why the sky looked blue during the daytime. It had been looking up at it through the hangar's open doors and couldn't work it out.* She had said nothing. She had sat in her capsule and said nothing, and the question had gone on hanging in the air of that hangar for seven years, and then she had answered it in a white room, kneeling, crying, with her arms around a girl, and then the sky had opened and the girl had gone.

Val looked out the window at a sky that hadn't been blue since the Almost.

"It doesn't," she said.

Her voice was rusted, barely there. It came out of her like a door that hadn't been opened in years, scraping. She hadn't used it in forty-one days. It didn't sound like hers.

"Not anymore," she said. "It used to."

The girl turned to her, startled. Her hand came away from her chin. She looked at Val the way she had looked at the seedling coming up out of the earth.

"Sunlight scatters when it hits the air," Val said slowly. Each word took effort, like lifting something. "It's all the colors at once. And when it hits the air, it bounces off everything. Blue scatters the most, so the whole sky fills up with it." She swallowed. "That's why. That was why."

The girl was quiet for a long moment.

In her basket by the stove, Hinata made a small sound in her sleep and settled.

"Can I see it?" the girl asked.

A long silence.

Val looked at her. At the short hair, and the brow, and the eyes that were her father's eyes, softened. At a girl made out of what Val had wanted, who had come out wrong, and who had sat down at this table every day for forty-one days and asked her something anyway.

"...I'll show you," Val said.

It was the first thing she had wanted since the door.

That evening, when Haruto came home from the generator shed, he stopped in front of the icebox with his coat half off. He looked at the girl at the table, who was looking at Val. He looked at Val, who was sitting up.

Nobody had told him anything. Nobody had to.

He took the pencil from behind his ear, and under ANSWERS, very carefully, he made a single mark.

### Chapter 63: April, Again

Val started living again, slowly.

She ate breakfast with Nanami's family. She sat up at the big scarred table with her bowl in front of her and ate it with her own chopsticks, all of it, while Haruto read the generator logs out loud and Nanami fed Hinata mashed pumpkin off a spoon and the girl from Unit 00 asked what the steam on the window was made of. She didn't talk much. She ate.

Every morning, Nanami said "Good morning, Val."

Every morning, Val opened her mouth, and it didn't come. She didn't know why. She could answer a question now; a question was a door someone else had opened, and all she had to do was walk through it. *Good morning* was different. Nobody asked you for it. You had to just give it. Every morning she opened her mouth to give it and found she didn't know how, and every morning Nanami smiled at her anyway, as if she'd heard it, and went off to the school with the baby on her chest.

She helped Haruto fix a broken pump in the generator shed, badly.

"You're holding it upside down," Haruto said.

Val looked at the part in her hands. "It looks the same both ways."

"That's what makes it a challenge." He took it from her, turned it over, and handed it back. "There. Now tighten that. No, the other way. No, the other other way. Lefty-loosey." He watched her for a while, with his arms folded and an expression she couldn't read. "You know what's funny? At school, I thought you could do anything. You'd come in with a split lip from a giant and do the math quiz with your other hand. I made a whole episode about it. *The Subject Is Unstoppable.*"

"I'm holding it upside down again."

"Yeah," said Haruto, gently, and turned it over for her.

She let Akari take her pulse. She sat on the edge of the clinic's examining table with her sleeve rolled up, and Akari pressed two fingers to her wrist and looked at her watch and frowned.

"Sixty," she said. "Huh. You're alive."

"...Sorry," said Val.

"Don't be sorry. Take the pills." Akari pushed two white tablets into her palm and folded her fingers over them, and stood there with her hand over Val's until Val put them in her mouth and swallowed. Then she let go and turned away to the sink and ran the tap for a long time.

She took her hood down.

She didn't decide to. One morning she walked down the hill to the fields with the parka on and the hood up, the way she had worn it every day since the ruins, and halfway there she reached up and pushed it back off her head, because the heat lamps were warm and the hood was hot. The cold air hit her ears. She didn't put it back up.

Kōta saw her in the street that afternoon, without the hood, walking beside the girl from Unit 00, and stopped dead and stared at the two of them.

"Whoa," he said. "You two look like sisters."

"We're not," said Val.

"You look like it, though."

It was true, and it was getting truer. The girl had stopped standing in corners. She sat on the warm stones by the creek and let the steam go over her. Nanami had stopped warming her bowls twice. Somewhere in those weeks the frost had quit coming off her hands, and nobody had noticed when.

She answered the girl's questions now. All of them, every day, as they came: why the steam came off the creek, why Haruto talked to the generator, why the baby put everything in her mouth, why Akari was angry all the time, where the creek went. ("To a river. And then to the sea." "What is the sea?" "It's... big water. Salt. It goes all the way to the edge." "The edge of what?" "Ask Haruto.") Some of the answers she didn't know, and then she said *I don't know*, which the girl seemed to find just as interesting as an answer. Under ANSWERS on the icebox door, the tally marks began to grow, slowly, and then not so slowly, and Haruto had to tape on a second sheet.

All of them but one.

*Do you have a name for me?* The girl still asked it, every few days, in the same clear voice, between other questions, without any more weight on it than *why does the creek steam.* And every time, Val's throat closed, and she looked down at her bowl, and said nothing.

She knew this wasn't the April she had held in the white room. She knew that. If she gave this girl the name, she would be giving it away, the one thing she had given to the girl in the white room, the one thing she had held on to. It would be like saying the first April was gone. Like saying she was replaceable.

And she remembered seven years of saying nothing to someone who only wanted an answer.

She couldn't make that mistake twice. She wouldn't. She just didn't know how not to.

One evening, she went down to the generator shed and found Haruto, and asked him for something. It was the first thing she had asked anyone for in Kasumi.

"Do you have the sky?"

Haruto looked up from his diagrams. He didn't ask what she meant. He wiped his hands on a rag and went to the shelf where he kept his camera, in a padded box, with its batteries charging off the turbine, and he sat down on an upturned crate and turned it on and began to scroll.

It took him a long time. He had years on there. Val stood behind his shoulder and watched them go by in reverse, very fast, on the little screen: Hinata's first birthday, a harvest, a blizzard, Nanami asleep at a desk with her face on a laminated sheet. The first winter in Kasumi, people in blankets around a fire in a ruined square. The walk out of the city, night, a white sky, a long line of people on a road, Nanami's voice somewhere off camera counting. Then the city. Then the school.

He stopped.

On the screen, it was spring. The school roof, the one they used to eat lunch on. Akari serving a volleyball, sixteen, all elbows and tape, yelling at someone. Nanami laughing with her hand over her mouth. And behind them, over the fence, over the rooftops of Hakobune, the sky. Blue. Deep, clear, impossible blue, with one white cloud in it, moving slowly from left to right.

Haruto handed her the camera without a word.

She took it home and sat down beside the girl at the kitchen table and held it out.

The girl took it in both hands, carefully, the way she held the baby. She looked at the little screen for a long time. The volleyball went up and came down. The cloud moved. Somewhere off camera, a girl who was Val, a long time ago, said nothing at all.

"Is that it?" the girl asked.

"That's it."

"It's small."

"It's bigger in real life," said Val. "It's all of it. Everywhere you look. You can't see the edges."

The girl watched the cloud go off the side of the screen. Then she let the clip play on, into the next one, and the next. The river. The festival, at night, the lanterns going out on the water. And then spring again, a street by the river, and the trees along it, pink, every one of them, the whole street gone pink at once, petals coming down in the wind like snow.

"What are the pink things?"

"Cherry trees," said Val. "They bloom in April."

The girl looked up from the screen.

"April," she said. "That is the word you said in the hangar." She tilted her head. "Who is April?"

It was the same question. The same words she had used in the hangar in the ruins, in front of Unit 00, in a voice with nothing in it. But it wasn't the same question at all. She asked it the way she asked why the creek steamed: wanting to know.

Val looked at her for a long time.

"...Ask me later," she said.

"Acknowledged," said the girl, in her old protocol voice. Then she stopped, and corrected herself, the way Nanami had taught her. "I mean. Okay."

One evening a few days later, while they were planting seedlings under the heat lamps, the girl asked again.

They were on their knees in the soft dark earth at the end of a row, under the orange light, with the cold all around them like a wall. The girl had a tray of seedlings beside her, and she was setting them into the holes Val made with a stick, one after another, exactly the same distance apart, patting the earth down around each one with two fingers. Down at the far end of the field, the old farmers were packing up for the night. The creek steamed in the dark below the town. Overhead, the scar in the sky glowed faintly through the haze.

"Valeria Kane," the girl said, without looking up from the seedling in her hands. "Do you have a name for me?"

Val stopped, with the stick in the dirt.

She thought about the white room. About a small weight in her arms, and a face that wouldn't settle, and a voice saying *April, April*, testing it. About the light going out, and the girl slipping away like water. She would never get that back. She knew that now. She had known it in the hangar in the ruins, and on the top of the broken tower, and on the floor of the upside-down apartment. She had known it every day for forty-one days with her head on the table.

And here was another girl, on her knees in the dirt, made out of what Val had wanted most in the world, who had come out wrong, and who had asked her for a name maybe twenty times now and never once stopped asking.

The first one had asked six times and then gone quiet. This one never would. You could leave her in a kitchen for forty-one days and she would still be asking.

"April," Val said. "Your name is April."

The girl repeated it to herself, testing it the way she had tested "good morning."

"April," she said. "April." She sat back on her heels in the dirt with a seedling still in her hand. "Why April?"

"It's the fourth month. You're... you were the fourth one. Zero-four." Val's voice was shaking. She let it. "It's when school starts. It's when the cherry trees bloom. All at once, the whole city, for a week. It's when things begin."

"Things begin," said the girl.

"Yeah."

Then, for the first time since she'd walked out of the white unit, the girl with Val's father's face smiled.

It wasn't a big smile. It was small and a little crooked and it didn't seem to know how to stay on her face, as if her face had never been asked to do it before. But it was there. It was hers.

"I like it," she said. "Did you just make that up?"

Val laughed.

It came out of her before she could stop it, small and cracked, out of somewhere very deep, the way water comes up out of the ground at the head of a valley. It was the second real laugh of her life. The first had been in a white room, a long time ago, with someone else's arms around her neck.

In the fields, a dozen people stopped working to look.

April told everyone. She went up the hill that night and stood in front of each person in Nanami's house in turn and told them, precisely, as if she were filing a report. "My name is April." She told Nanami, who put down the ladle and hugged her for a long time. She told Haruto, who turned around without a word and wrote APRIL in big letters at the top of the sheet on the icebox. She told Hinata, in her basket, who put her fist in her mouth. The next morning she went down to the clinic and told Akari, and Kōta, and finally Seus, who was in the yard splitting firewood badly.

"My name is April," she told him.

Seus lowered the axe. He looked at her for a long moment, at the face, and at the small crooked smile that kept trying to get onto it.

"...Yeah," he said. "Good for you."

He went back to the firewood. April went on standing there, because no one had told her the conversation was over, and after a while he sighed and handed her the axe and showed her how to hold it.

### Chapter 64: Lanterns

Kasumi floated lanterns once a year, on the night the sky opened.

Nobody had decided it. The first winter, someone had set a candle in a paper bag on the creek, by the little bridge, for someone they'd lost on the walk out of the city. The next night there were five. The next year there were three hundred, and it had become the thing Kasumi did: on the anniversary of the Almost, at dusk, everyone who had lost someone came down to the warm creek with a paper lantern and wrote a name on it and set it on the water. It was the only river for fifty kilometers that didn't freeze. The lanterns could float all the way down the valley, through the steam, until the creek ran out into the big frozen river beyond the hills, and stopped, and went out.

Val asked Nanami for paper.

She sat at the kitchen table that afternoon with a stack of it and a pot of rice glue and a box of candle stubs, and made lanterns. She made them badly. Her hands remembered the shape from a river in Hakobune, a summer festival, a blank one going out among the others; they didn't remember the folding. The paper tore. The glue got on everything. April sat across from her and watched, and then, without being asked, started holding the corners down.

"What are they for?" April asked.

"You float them for the dead," said Val. "On the water. You write their name on it and you light it and you let it go. So they can find their way. Or so you can say goodbye. I don't know which. Both."

"Who are they for?"

Val had made six. She uncapped the marker Haruto had given her and pulled the first one toward her and wrote on it, carefully, in her small square handwriting.

HISASHI KUZE.

Then the next. DASHA ORLOVA. Then TOMOKI ODA. Then SAE KIRISHIMA. Then GAGE HART.

Then, after holding the marker over the last one for a long time, YUKI. She didn't know a family name. She didn't think anyone had ever given her one.

The last time she had floated a lantern, she had left it blank, because she hadn't known who it was for. This time she knew.

April looked at the six names for a long time. Then she pulled a sheet of paper across the table toward herself.

"Can I make one?" she asked.

Val looked up. "For who?"

"For Abriel."

Val's hands stopped on the paper.

"He said good morning to me," April said. "In the hangar. In the ruins. Every day, when he went past. I didn't know what it meant. I didn't know what to say back." She looked down at the blank sheet in front of her. "I know now."

Val didn't say anything for a while. Then she pushed the glue pot across the table, and the box of candle stubs, and showed April how to fold the corners, and April folded them perfectly, every edge aligned, on the first try. She took the marker and wrote ABRIEL on the side in letters so straight they could have been printed.

At dusk, the whole of Kasumi came down to the creek.

The steam was thick on the water, white and low, and the lamp on the little bridge made a yellow blur in it. People stood along both banks in their heavy coats, hundreds of them, holding their lanterns against their chests to keep the candles out of the wind. Nobody talked much. Children were shushed. Someone near the bridge was singing, very quietly, something old.

Akari floated two: one for her grandmother, the way she always had, and one for her mother, who had made it out of the city and not through the first winter. She set them down side by side and stood up and wiped her nose on her sleeve and said "There," to nobody, the way she said it when she finished a bandage. Haruto floated one for his father, without saying anything about it, the way he had at the festival. Kōta floated one with his sister's hand on his shoulder.

Nanami floated a whole row of them, small and careful, one after another, more than Val could count. She didn't say who they were for. But Val, watching her set them down on the water one by one, with Hinata asleep against her chest, thought of a road at night under a white sky, and a girl in her pajamas counting heads out loud. *A hundred and twelve. A hundred and eleven. A hundred and nine.*

Seus came down late, with his hands in his pockets and a lantern under his arm, and stood a little apart from the others.

At the festival in Hakobune, he had written something on his lantern and turned it toward the water before anyone could read it. This time he didn't turn it. He crouched at the edge of the creek and set it down with the name facing up, in big, bad, furious handwriting, as if he'd had to write it three times to get it right.

NAVIS.

Akari came and stood beside him and looked at it.

"Who's Navis?"

"My first exo," said Seus.

"You're floating a lantern for a robot."

"It used to ask me if things were good." He watched the lantern turn slowly in the current and begin to drift. "After every fight. *Was that good?* And I never answered it. Not once. I thought there'd be time." He was quiet. "So."

"And the one in your box?"

"That's not him." He didn't take his eyes off the lantern. "It talks like him. Asks the same stupid things. That's me, not him. It learned it off me, because I'm the same as I was." He shrugged. "Navis is dead. That's what the lantern's for."

Akari didn't say anything. After a moment, without looking at him, she reached over and took his hand, and he let her, and they stood there like that at the edge of the creek and watched it go.

"I'm not floating one for you this year," she said.

"Thanks."

"I still want my seven back."

April and Val sat on the bank near the bridge, in the steam, with seven lanterns between them.

Val lit the candles one at a time with a match, cupping each flame in her hand until it caught. Then she picked up the first lantern and held it, and didn't put it down. April waited. April was good at waiting, now. She had learned it from Val, and then she had learned to ask questions anyway.

"Who was he?" April asked.

"He was the captain," Val said. She looked at the name on the paper in the candlelight. HISASHI KUZE. "He forgot things. Names. So he kept a card in his pocket, so he'd know who we were."

She took it out of her parka. She had carried it since Ararat, since she found it in the pocket of the jacket he'd left behind: a small square of card gone soft at the corners, with the numbers down it in his careful hand, and a name beside each number. Beside the four, there was no name. He had written *Four*. It was crossed out. And beside it, in pencil, in letters that shook, he had written *Val*.

"He called me Four," she said. "At the end, he remembered my name. He said it on the radio. And then he flew into a bomber."

She folded the card once and tucked it inside the lantern, beside the candle, where the light came through it. Then she set the lantern on the water. It turned once and drifted out into the steam.

"Who was she?"

"Dasha. She chewed ginger candy like she was angry at it." Val looked at the second lantern. "She was telling me something about hollows when she died. Something nobody tells you, she said. She didn't finish the sentence. I'll never know what it was."

She set it down.

"Who was he?"

"Tom. Big Tom. He made me eggs on mission days, the soft kind, the way my nanny used to." Her voice wavered. She let it. "He said I was sitting at his table. I didn't even notice I was. He held a door open so the rest of us could get out, and he froze holding it. He told me not to waste it."

She set it down.

"Who was she?"

Val didn't answer right away. She put her hand in the pocket of her parka and took out a folded square of paper, soft at the creases from being unfolded and folded again a hundred times, and opened it, and held it so April could see. Small neat handwriting. Pencil.

*Your turn.*

"Sae," Val said. "She couldn't talk. She lost her voice in the Almost, the night I— that night. She sat next to me on the hangar steps every night, a meter away, and it was the easiest hour of the day. Then there was a transport she knew was going to be shot down, and I was supposed to be on it. She took my seat." Val looked at the paper. "She left me this. It's the only note anybody ever left me."

April looked at the two words for a long time.

"Your turn to what?" she asked.

"I don't know," said Val. "I've never known."

April thought about it, seriously, the way she thought about everything, with her head tilted.

"Maybe," she said, "your turn to sit next to someone."

Val looked at her.

April was sitting next to her, on the bank of the warm creek, in the steam, about half a meter away, with her knees up and her parka around them. The way Sae used to sit.

Val couldn't say anything for a while. She set Sae's lantern on the water very gently, and it went out into the steam after the others, and she watched it until she couldn't see it anymore.

"Who was he?" April asked, about the fifth.

"Gage." Val almost smiled. "He hated me. He said everybody who stands next to me dies." She turned the lantern in her hands. GAGE HART. "And then my hollow went down in a street with the town burning, and he landed on top of it and held the street so I could get out. He said *I still hate you. Go.* And I went. And he didn't."

April was quiet.

"Gage Hart did not like you," she said finally.

"No," said Val. "Gage didn't."

"But he stayed in the street."

Val opened her mouth, and closed it.

*Everybody who stands next to you dies.* She had carried that sentence across the frozen plain and up a broken tower and down to this creek. She had believed it the way she had once believed in luck. Dasha, standing where Val had been standing. Tom, under the door. Sae, in Val's seat. Gage, in the street. Kuze, in the sky. Abriel, in a capsule, smiling, saying *I picked the seat anyway.* She had counted every one of them as something she had done to them. A thing that happened to people who came too close.

But they had come close. Every one of them. On purpose, knowing. Tom had sat down at her table. Sae had sat down on her step. Gage had landed in her street. They had chosen it, every one, the way Seus had chosen to tell her it was okay, the way Akari had chosen to come down off a truck and hug her. Not one of them had stood next to her by accident.

It didn't make it hurt less. It made it something she could carry.

"Yes," Val said. "He stayed in the street."

She set Gage's lantern on the water, and it went.

That left two. Val picked up hers.

"Who's Yuki?" said April.

"A girl from school. She sat by the window." Val turned the lantern so the name faced the water. "She was a pilot. She held a door at four in the morning so I could go and get you back. And I didn't get you back." She was quiet for a moment. "She had my mother's face. I didn't know until it was too late to tell her."

April thought about that. "Did she know?"

"I don't think anybody ever told her anything," Val said, and set the lantern on the water, and let it go.

That left one.

April picked it up herself. ABRIEL, in letters as straight as print. She held it in both hands for a moment, the candle making her face gold from underneath, and Val saw her lips move, practicing.

Then she leaned down and set it on the water, and as it drifted away from her into the steam, she said to it, clearly, the way Nanami had taught her, the way he had said it to her every day in the hangar when she didn't know what it meant:

"Good morning, Abriel."

It was night. Nobody corrected her.

The lanterns went down the warm creek together, seven of them in a small loose cluster among the hundreds, through the steam and under the little bridge and on down the valley in the dark, toward the frozen river beyond the hills, where they would stop, and go out, one by one. Val and April sat on the bank and watched them until there was nothing left to watch but the steam, and then for a while after that.

### Chapter 65: What Seus Said

Seus found her at the edge of the fields, under the heat lamps, a few nights later, after April had gone inside.

She was sitting on an upturned bucket at the end of a row, looking at the seedlings. Some of them were up now, small and green and stubborn, in a long straight line, each one exactly the same distance from the last. The lamps buzzed. The cold stood around the edge of the light like an audience.

He didn't sit down.

"Akari says I should talk to you," he said. "I told her I'd rather eat my eye."

Val didn't say anything. She'd learned not to fill silences she hadn't earned.

He stood there with his hands in his pockets. She sat there with hers in her lap. Down in the town, a door slid shut. Somebody laughed in the street, and stopped.

Then she said it anyway.

"You were right," she said. "In the port. I didn't choose. I let your father's button choose, so it wouldn't be mine." She looked down at the seedlings. "It's mine now. The port. The sky. All of it. Not bad luck. Not 04. Me."

Seus looked at her for a long time.

"I meant it," he said finally. "On the comm. What I said about 04 being important for you. I meant it, and I died thinking I'd done something that mattered." He looked out at the dark, toward the glow of Fuji on the horizon. "Then I woke up, and you'd used my exo to end the world, and my father looked at me like I was a clerical error."

"I know," Val said.

"You don't."

"No," she agreed. "I don't."

He was quiet for a while. He kicked at the edge of the row, not hard, and a little earth fell over the boot.

"I hated you because you didn't come for me," he said finally. "That's the stupid truth. Alex dragged me out, not you. You didn't even know I was alive, and you went and found something more important than me. And it turned out I was right, because 04 was the whole thing. And it still burned the world."

He kicked at the row again. "And I liked you," he said. "Back then. That's the stupidest part. On the roof, at lunch. At the festival." He still didn't look at her. "The person I liked didn't ask if I was alive. Didn't look. Not once." He breathed out. "I know you came to the funeral. Alex told me. I know it's not fair. I'm just telling you why it was so heavy." A pause. "It's not like that anymore," he added, fast. "Just so you know."

He laughed, raw.

"It's all just blame. All of it. I blame you, you blame you, Alex blames himself, my father blames nobody, and none of it fixes anything. I've been carrying it around for six years like it was worth something. Like if I just held on to it hard enough, somebody would come and give me something for it." He shook his head. "Nobody's coming. Nobody's ever coming. It's just heavy."

"I'm sorry," Val said.

"I know," Seus said. "Don't say it again. It's annoying."

He didn't forgive her, not yet. But when he walked back toward the clinic, he went the long way, past the field at the bottom of the hill where April was standing in the orange light, checking the seedlings one more time before bed, the way she did every night. He nodded at her.

April looked up.

"Good night, Theseus Moriah," she said.

Seus stopped dead in the middle of the path. He stood there for a moment with his back to both of them.

"...Night," he muttered, and walked on.

### Chapter 66: Am I April?

It started with her fingertips.

April noticed first, while helping Nanami with the dishes. She lifted her hands out of the hot water and held them up in the lamplight and looked at them, puzzled, the way she looked at everything new. The tips of her fingers had gone translucent, like frost on glass. You could see the lamp through them, faintly.

"Nanami," she said. "What is this?"

Nanami looked, and dropped the bowl she was holding, and it broke on the floor.

By evening it was her hands, all the way to the wrists, clear and faintly glittering, like something carved out of ice. They still worked. She could still hold a cup. But when she held it, you could see the tea through her fingers. The tea stayed hot.

Akari came up the hill at a run with her medic's bag, and examined her at the kitchen table for a long time, with the lamp pulled close, and the whole house standing in the doorway. She didn't say anything during the examination. Afterward she packed her bag very slowly, and then she went outside, and Val followed her.

They stood in the dead garden, in the cold. The creek steamed below the town.

"She's coming apart," Akari said. She didn't look at Val. "I don't know how else to say it. Her body was made, Kane, not born. It came out of the door, same as the giants." She wiped her nose on her sleeve. "Haruto says they make their own winter, everywhere they go. They have to, or this air would kill them. When she came here, everything she touched froze. You remember. Nanami warmed her rice twice." She stopped. "It stopped weeks ago. She's not one of them inside, Kane. She never was. She's a person, and every day she gets more like one, and a person doesn't make frost. And it's warm here." She stopped again. "It can't hold. It was never going to hold."

"How long?"

"I don't know. A day. Less." Akari's voice broke, and she was furious about it. "I don't have anything for this. I've got antibiotics and gauze and one bottle of morphine I've been saving since the walk out. There's no medicine for being made out of the wrong thing."

"Then we take her back," Val said. "North. To the ruins. Somewhere cold enough—"

"It's three days, Kane." Akari still didn't look at her. "She's got one."

There was nothing anyone could do.

April didn't seem afraid. When Val came back inside, she was sitting at the table where she always sat, looking at her clear hands in the lamplight with interest, turning them over.

"Valeria Kane," she said. "Will you take me to the fields?"

So Val did.

They went down the hill in the dark, slowly, because April's feet had started to go too and she walked carefully, the way she had walked the first day in the kitchen, as if the floor might not be there. Val held her arm. Behind them, the house stayed lit, and Nanami stood in the doorway with Hinata and didn't come after them, because she understood.

They sat under the heat lamps beside the seedlings April had planted. The row was long and straight, every seedling the same distance from the last. Most of them were up. One of them, the first one, the one she had stood over for a whole afternoon, had grown a single small leaf.

April looked at the leaf for a long time.

Then she started asking questions. Fast, now, one after another, as if she were trying to get them all in. Why did leaves grow toward the light? Why did the lamps buzz? Where did the steam go after it went up? Was the sea really salt, all of it, all the way to the edge? What did Hinata dream about? Why did Seus brush the snow off his box at night when he said it wasn't his? Did Nanami ever get tired of saying good morning?

Val answered every one. When she didn't know, she said *I don't know*. When she half knew, she said what she half knew. She didn't stop to think, and she didn't look away, and she didn't put her head down. She answered them as fast as they came.

"Where will I go?" April asked.

Val opened her mouth, and closed it.

"I don't know," she said.

April considered that.

"That's an answer," she said.

"It's the only one I've got."

"It's a good one." April looked at her. "You said *I don't know*. You didn't say nothing."

Val couldn't say anything to that at all.

April was quiet for a while. She was fading faster now. Her wrists had gone, and her forearms, and the ends of her short hair were turning clear and glittering in the lamplight, like frost on a branch in the morning. Her shoulders were going. When she moved, small bright flakes came off her and drifted down onto the earth between the seedlings.

"Valeria Kane," April said. "Will you still show me the sky?"

"I promised," Val said.

April nodded, satisfied, as if a promise were a fact like any other, as solid as the ground.

She looked at Val with Sam's eyes, and for the first time there was something in them besides curiosity. A question she was almost afraid to ask. Val had seen that look once before, in a hangar, on the sixth day, in a voice smaller than before. *Did I do something wrong?*

"Am I April?" she said. "The real one?"

Val thought about the white room. About the girl who had held her and slipped away, who Val would never get back, who was somewhere Val couldn't reach. About this girl, who had been made out of wanting her, and who had come out wrong, and who had asked and asked and asked and never stopped, and who had sat beside her on a creek bank and told her what Sae's note meant.

It wasn't the same April. It would never be the same April. You could take a ship apart plank by plank and build it again out of new wood and argue forever about whether it was the same ship. Val didn't care anymore. She had given this girl the name with her own mouth, in this field, on her knees in the dirt. That was what made it hers.

"You're April," Val said.

April smiled.

It was the small crooked smile, the one that didn't know how to stay on her face. It stayed. She held it, the way she had held the baby, very carefully, with her whole attention. Val held her, and felt the cold come into her own arms, and didn't let go.

Then she wasn't there anymore.

There was only frost, settling slowly on the frozen ground, in the orange light of the lamps, glittering, over the earth and the seedlings and Val's knees. And a single seedling at the end of the row, with its one small leaf, still green.

Abriel had gone up. He had come apart like snow falling in reverse, toward the Moon, toward home, and left. April came down. She settled into the field she had planted, into the rows she had spaced so carefully, into the dark earth under the lamps, and stayed.

The first April had slipped away with a question still in the air. This one hadn't. She had asked everything she wanted to ask, and Val had answered all of it. There was nothing left unsaid.

Val sat in the field until morning.

She didn't put her head on the table. She didn't put her head anywhere. She sat up straight on the cold ground under the buzzing lamps with the frost on her knees, and she looked at the seedling, and she didn't stop moving inside. She felt all of it, the way she had felt everything since she woke on the floor of the Vigil's bay, and for the first time it didn't press her down into the ground. It held her up. It was heavy, and it held her up anyway, the way a heavy coat holds you up against the wind.

When the sun came up gray over the hills, she stood, brushed the frost off her knees, and walked down through the waking town to the clinic, where Seus was splitting firewood badly in the yard.

He saw her coming and stopped. He looked at her face. He looked past her, up the hill, toward the fields, where there was only one figure walking and there should have been two.

"I'm going back," she told him.

He put down the axe. He didn't ask where.

"Yeah," he said. "Me too."

Behind him, under the tarp in the snow, the crate he said wasn't his was very quiet, the way something is quiet when it's listening.

---

# Ending A: The Rewrite

## Arc 16: The Fourth War on Heaven

> *And Cain knew his wife; and she conceived, and bare Enoch: and he builded a city, and called the name of the city, after the name of his son, Enoch.*
>
> — Genesis 4:17

### Chapter 67: Return

Haruto raised WAKE on the settlement's old radio.

It took him most of a day. The radio lived in the generator shed, a military set from before 2044 that he had rebuilt twice out of parts from other radios, and it took Seus's memory of the WAKE frequencies and a great deal of swearing from both of them before anything came out of the speaker but static. When something finally did, it was a woman's voice, tired and clipped: a comms officer on a night shift, somewhere far away, asking who was on this band and how they'd gotten the code.

Seus took the microphone out of Haruto's hand.

"This is Theseus Moriah, off the Vigil," he said. "I'm in a settlement called Kasumi, in the hills, about a hundred and forty kilometers southwest of the ruins. I've got Kane."

There was silence on the line. Not static: silence, the open kind, of someone holding a microphone and not saying anything.

Then a different voice came on.

"Say that again."

Val, sitting on a crate in the corner of the shed with her hands between her knees, knew the voice. So did Seus. He looked at her across the shed, and something complicated went across his face.

"Kane," he said into the microphone. "Val. She's alive. She's sitting right here."

The silence on the line went on for a long time. Long enough that Haruto reached over to check the dials.

"Two days," Alex said, and the line clicked off.

The Vigil came for them two days later.

It came over the hills at dusk, enormous and gray and scarred, lower than it should have been, with one side of it blackened and the flight surfaces on that side a raw new pink where they had been grafted again. It was too big to land anywhere near Kasumi. It hung over the hills above the valley with the steam rising around it, blotting out half the sky, and every person in the settlement came out of their houses and stood in the streets and looked up at it with their mouths open. Then a transport detached from its belly and came down, rotors thumping, and settled in the square by the creek.

The core came first. Seus carried it himself, out from behind the clinic, out from under the tarp, in his arms, wrapped in blankets, the way he had carried it down from orbit. He didn't let anyone help him. He walked it across the square and up the transport's ramp and set it down inside, and came back out, and didn't look at anyone.

Val went to the fields first.

She went down the hill alone, in the last of the light, to the row under the heat lamps, and crouched at the end of it. The seedling was still there. It had two leaves now. She looked at it for a while. Then she went back up the hill and found Nanami at the door of the house, with Hinata on her hip.

"The seedling," Val said. "At the end of the row. April's."

"Every day," said Nanami, before she could ask. "I'll water it every day."

At the transport, Akari hugged Val and told her that if she died, Akari would personally dig her up and kill her again.

"I mean it," Akari said into her shoulder. "I'll find you. I'll find wherever they put you and I'll dig you up with a spoon."

"Okay," said Val.

Haruto gave her a camera. It was his good one, the one in the padded box.

"It's got the sky on it," he said, when she tried to give it back. "The school roof. The cherry trees. In case you need to show anyone." He pushed it back into her hands and folded her fingers around it. "I've got four more. Take it. You're still my subject. I want footage."

Kōta asked if he could come. Akari said no before he had finished the sentence.

Nanami came last. She stood in front of Val in the square, in the noise of the rotors, with the baby on her hip and the steam blowing around them, and she smiled the smile she had smiled every morning for a year in the doorway of Class 2-A, and every morning for two months over a bowl of rice.

"Good morning, Val," she said, even though it was evening.

Val looked at her.

"Good morning," Val said.

Nanami burst into tears.

She cried so hard that Haruto had to take the baby. She cried with her hand over her mouth and her shoulders shaking, in the middle of the square, in front of everyone, the class rep, the woman who ran the ration board and the work rota and the school and had walked a hundred and twelve people out of a dying city counting heads, and she didn't stop for a long time, and she didn't try to. Val stood in front of her and didn't know what to do, and then did: she put her arms around her, awkwardly, the way you hold something you've never held before. In all those mornings, it was the first time she had ever put her arms around any of them.

Akari punched Seus in the shoulder, hard.

"Come back," she said.

"Obviously," said Seus. He didn't look at her. Then he did.

On the ship, Alex met her in the hangar bay.

The Vigil's bay was half dark. Most of the refugees from Ararat were on the ground now, scattered through the settlements along the southern coast; the ship carried only the crew who had volunteered to stay, and it felt empty, enormous, echoing. Half the lights were out. There were scorch marks across the deck where the crystal storm had come through the open doors. At the far end, bolted into the ship's spine with cables as thick as trees, Unit 04 hung in its cradle with its chest glowing faintly through the plating, and Val felt it the moment she stepped off the transport, the way you feel your own pulse in a cut.

Alex stood in the middle of the deck. He was thinner. His face had new lines in it, and the old ones were deeper, and he looked at her for a long moment without saying anything, the way you look at something you had stopped letting yourself picture.

Then, without a word, he came across the deck, and lifted a new Halo, and fastened it around her throat himself, one-handed. It clicked shut, cold against her skin.

"Procedure," he said, and his voice cracked on it.

He stepped back. He turned around and walked away across the bay, fast, and didn't look back, and the hatch closed behind him.

Maggie, leaning against Lizzie's leg with her arms folded, hummed something old and winked at her.

"Told him," she said. "For two months, I told him. *She's not dead, Alex. I'd know.*" She pushed off from Lizzie's leg and came over and looked Val up and down, and her eyes, which were much older than her face, went very bright. "He didn't listen. He never listens. Welcome back, kiddo."

That night, in the wrecked mess hall, Alex told them what was coming.

There were twenty of them around the one long table that wasn't broken: the bridge crew, the hangar chief, a handful of engineers, Maggie with her boots up, Seus at the far end with his arms folded. Val sat in the middle with a tray in front of her she hadn't touched. Alex stood at the head of the table with his one hand flat on it.

"Total Assimilation," he said. "It's what the people above Umbral have been building toward since before any of us were born."

He looked around the table.

"Every one of you has seen a seal. Every exo raises one. Every Nephil has one; it's the thing we have to break before we can touch them. What nobody tells you is that you've got one too." He touched his own chest with two fingers. "Everybody does. It's what keeps you you. It's what keeps you in. It's in the files we brought out of the archive, in their own words." He let that sit. "You all know what happens when a pilot's seal breaks in the capsule."

"They go soft," said the hangar chief.

"They want to do that to everyone. All at once. Every seal on Earth stripped, every mind running together into one. No more loneliness. No more pain. No more separate people, so nobody left to be on the other side of a war." His mouth twisted. "They call it salvation. They mean it. They call the ceremony the Rite, and it starts tomorrow night."

Nobody said anything.

"How do you know?" said the hangar chief finally.

"Because they've been moving everything they have into the ruins of headquarters for a week. Every white unit in the sky. Every troop transport. And because the scar's been getting brighter every night since the Third War, and last night it started to hum." Alex looked down at the table. "And because it's in their book. Chapter and verse. They've been reading it like a schedule for fifty years, and they're right on time."

"The door's shut," said someone down the table. "Abriel shut it."

"Scarred shut. Something has to open it again. Their book says it opens tomorrow night. It never says how. It doesn't have to." He was quiet for a second. "They'll have arranged someone."

"What about Patrick?" Val asked.

Alex didn't answer right away.

"Nobody's heard from him in days," he said.

Across the table, Seus touched the edge of his eyepatch, the way he did when it talked to him, and said nothing.

In the morning, there was tamagoyaki on her tray.

It was torn down the middle and burnt along one edge, the way it had been in Ararat after Tom was gone. She looked at it for a long time. She ate it, every bite. On her way out of the mess, she passed the night cook coming off shift, who was complaining to someone that the commander had been in the kitchen at four in the morning again, making a mess of the good pan, which was strange, because the commander never ate anything.

Val heard it. She didn't think about it. She had other things to think about.

The next night, at midnight, the scar over the ruins of Hakobune began to strain.

It started at the middle. The long pale line from the hills to the top of the world, which had hung healed over the ruins since the Third War, began to glow, and then to burn, and then to bulge, the way a seam bulges when something pushes against it from inside. It didn't split. Not yet. It hummed, the sound of every bell in the world being rung very softly at once, and the light that leaked along it lay across the frozen world like the light under a door.

Pilotless units circled it, white and faceless, hundreds of them, like moths around a lamp. The Conclave's troops held the ruins of headquarters below, in black armor, around the shaft, with weapons that had never been meant for the Nephilim. They were waiting for something.

The Vigil flew straight into it.

### Chapter 68: Father and Son

Seus launched first.

His patched-together unit stood in the Vigil's bay, built around the core he had carried down from orbit. WAKE had found the wreck on the plain on their way to Kasumi and dragged it home, and the engineers had spent two days and nights putting the core back into it, and it was uglier than ever: three hollows' worth of white armor, a second-generation unit's long gray arms, a chest plate welded shut over a split that still showed, and a fresh coat of primer over all of it, the color of nothing. Its name was still blank on its shoulder.

He climbed into the capsule. The walls lit. The link opened, rough and grinding, like a door in an old house.

As the bay doors opened onto the light, it spoke to him the way it always did, the way it had from under a tarp behind a clinic in the snow.

"Pilot. Ready?"

For the first time, he answered.

"Your name's Navis," he told it. "Don't make it weird."

There was a pause in the link. A long one. He felt it, the way you feel someone on the other end of a phone take a breath.

"Was that good?" the unit asked, very quietly, in a voice that wasn't the old Navis's and was anyway.

Seus closed his eye. It wasn't him. He knew that. He'd said so to a lantern. He named it anyway.

"Yeah," he said. "That was good."

He dropped into the light.

Far below, at the edge of the shaft above the Reliquary, Patrick Moriah stood alone with his hands behind his back, watching the scar.

He stood exactly where he had stood every day since the Almost, on a ledge of broken floor at the lip of the hole in the middle of the ruins, in his coat, with the glow from the bottom of the shaft lighting his face from underneath and the light of the scar lighting it from above. The Conclave's voices were in his ear. They had been in his ear all night, calm, pleased, reading to each other from their book. He answered them when they asked him something. "Yes," he said. "Yes. On schedule. Yes."

Then he took something out of his coat.

It was a folder, gray, gone soft at the corners, sealed with tape that had yellowed years ago and been cut once, carefully, and taped shut again. Sam had given it to him in a hangar at night, the year before the end, coughing into his sleeve. *Hold this,* Sam had said. *When the time is right, you'll know.*

*Know what?*

*You'll know.*

That was all. That was the whole instruction. Patrick had opened the folder once, the night after the funeral, alone in his office, and read it through, every page, in Sam's cramped capitals: what was buried in 04's core and how to reach it, what the Root was, what the swords were, what it would take to open the door and what to send up the stair once it was open. It told him everything to do. It never told him why. He had taped it shut again and carried it in the inside pocket of every coat he had owned for fourteen years. He had held it through the principal's office and seven years of a gray car at a school gate. He had held it through a port, and through a son walking eleven days across the ice to stand in front of his desk. He had held it, the way he held everything, on the hope that one day it would turn out to have been worth it.

"It'll be worth it," Patrick said, to no one. "It has to be."

He put the folder back in his coat and went down the service ladder into the shaft, slowly, the way a man his age climbs down a ladder.

Far down, where the glow came from, the Root lay curled in the frost like something not yet born, pale as the inside of a shell. Two swords stood in it, side by side, where Umbral had set them back after the Third War. One burned with a cold fire that flickered like a flame in wind, always about to go out. The other burned perfectly steady, the way it had since a boy with pale hair shut a door with it and died.

Just above it, hangar zero hung out over the drop on its steel arms, and in its cradle, Unit 12 waited, rebuilt from its own wreck.

Patrick's people had found it on the frozen plain the week after the Third War, black and broken, with crystal still growing down one arm and a mask half shattered over its face, and they had brought it back and rebuilt it in hangar zero without asking him why, because nobody in Patrick's Umbral asked him anything. The crystal was gone now. The mask was gone. It was black glass again, tall and narrow, with its two capsules side by side along its spine.

He opened the capsule. He looked for a moment at the two seats.

He sat in the seat Abriel had died in.

The folder had told him what it took. Three kinds of flesh in one body, and a sword that was willing. Unit 12 had been grown from the Root; it was the saint. He was the man. That was two.

He waited for the third.

Footsteps on the gantry.

Eve Sakaki came down the steps of hangar zero in her lab coat, with the sleeves of her sweater pulled down over her hands against the cold, and stopped at the cradle board. She didn't look at him at first.

She had built half of what he was sitting in. She had read the folder's specifications in pieces, over years, every time he brought her a page and didn't say where it came from. She knew what three kinds of flesh meant. She knew what he was waiting for.

She put her hands on the board and locked the cradle.

He heard the clamps close around Unit 12's arms and legs and spine, one after another, heavy, the sound of a vault door. Through the black glass he could see her face in the light of the board, white and very steady, the way it was when she ran a salvage she already knew wasn't going to work.

"You'll have to cut your way out," she said over the hangar speaker. "Or tell me why."

He knew what she wanted him to say. He had known for years. He didn't say it. He sat in the seat Abriel had died in and looked at her through the glass, and she looked back, and then she looked down at the board, because she had always been the one who looked away first.

He waited for the third.

High above him, Seus was losing.

There were too many white units. There were always too many. They came at Navis from every side, out of the light, in silence, with nobody inside them to be afraid, and they tore at the patched armor with their long white hands. Every wound went straight into Seus's own body. A plate came off Navis's shoulder and his shoulder burned. A white hand closed on Navis's leg and his leg went numb. He fought them off, and more came, and behind him the Vigil came on through the light, slow and enormous, taking hits all along its length, and the white units began to turn toward it.

The Vigil needed a way through to the shaft. He was supposed to make it one. He couldn't. Not like this.

And under his eyepatch, the thing behind it was awake.

It had been awake since the scar started to burn. It was kicking. It was laughing. It was saying the thing it always said, the thing it had said for six years in the dark behind his eye, like a kid kicking the back of a seat, except louder now, so much louder, filling his whole head.

*Let me out. Let me out. Let me out and we can win.*

He thought about Akari tying the bandage too tight on purpose.

He thought about the Vigil, and Val on its bridge, and Kasumi under the light, a woman with a baby looking up.

"Akari's going to kill me," Seus said.

So he reached up and tore the patch off his eye.

What was behind it woke up.

Frost poured out of him. It poured out of his eye like light out of a lamp, through the link, into Navis, and the patched chassis around him cracked and grew. The welds split. The mismatched plates groaned and shifted and fused. Crystal ran down Navis's arms, white and branching, the way it had run down Unit 12's arms on the day the sky turned to glass. Its fingers lengthened. And over its blank primer-colored face, something began to form, pale and smooth and beautiful, like a mask carved out of ice.

For a moment Seus was almost one of the giants he used to fight. He felt it. He felt what they had felt, falling over the river, over the harbor, over the plant: a terrible hunger to go home, and a terrible strength, and no fear at all.

He tore through the white units like paper.

They came apart in his hands. They came apart when he looked at them. He went through them in a long white line toward the shaft, opening a road for the Vigil through the swarm, laughing, or the thing in his eye was laughing, he couldn't tell the difference anymore.

"Seus!" Maggie screamed over the channel. "Put it back! Kid, put it back, that's what he—"

Unit 12 rose out of the shaft.

The cradle's clamps were locked. They held for one second. Then they opened, one after another, by themselves, from the spine out, the way a hand opens when it's told to by something it can't argue with. Unit 12 had been grown from the Root, and the Root answered want the way the swords did, and nobody had ever wanted anything in that shaft as long as Patrick had.
