On the gantry, Eve Sakaki stood at the dead board with her hands still on the keys and watched it go.

It came up out of the ruins of headquarters black and silent, and the white units parted for it.

He had known Seus would tear off the patch. He had counted on it. He had known it from the moment he saw it, in the doorway of his office in the ruins, the year after the Almost, when a boy who should have been dead came in out of eleven days of ice and stood in front of his desk with a black patch over one eye. He had known what was behind it. Abriel was gone. Only one Nephil was left in the world, and it was in his son's eye.

On the open channel, Seus heard breathing.

He knew that breathing. He had grown up at the other branch, across the sea, with his mother, and his father had called on his birthday most years. Not every year. Most. And every time, before he said anything, there had been this: a long slow breath on the line, in and out, like a man standing at the edge of cold water deciding whether to go in. Seus had learned to wait through it. He had learned to count it. Sometimes it went on so long that his mother took the phone away.

Navis turned in the light, crystal-armed, half masked, and faced the black unit rising toward it.

"Dad?" said Seus.

The breathing went on. In, and out.

"Dad. I know it's you." His voice came out wrong, too young. "Say something."

In, and out.

"Say something. Just once."

In Unit 12's capsule, in the seat where Abriel had died, Patrick Moriah sat with his hands on the controls. His jaw was working. His eyes were open. He was looking at the half-masked thing in front of him, which had his son inside it, and his mouth opened, once, as if something were going to come out of it.

Nothing did.

Unit 12 reached out and took Navis.

It didn't strike it. It simply opened, the way a mouth opens, the black glass of its chest unfolding in long petals, and closed around Navis and the boy inside. The frost pouring out of Seus's eye poured into it instead, into the black glass, into the Root-grown flesh and the man in its seat, and the light from Seus's eye went out of the sky.

Unit 12 went back down into the shaft with what it had taken, fast, past the broken levels and the scorched frame of the old drive wing, down into the blue, until it hung above the Root.

At the bottom of the shaft, the swords tore loose from the Root.

They came up out of the frost end over end, like two sparks going up a chimney, the way the first of them had once come up the same shaft for Navis, to stop it. They stopped a hand's width from Unit 12's glass and hung there, side by side, the flickering one and the steady one, and their light lay across the black glass and across the man inside it.

Patrick felt them look at him. There was no other word for it.

He didn't know what they saw. Fourteen years of a folder in his coat. Twenty years of holding. And under all of it, older and plainer than any plan, the thing he had wanted since a hangar at three in the morning: for it to turn out to have been worth something.

The swords turned, and went into Unit 12, the way keys go into a lock they were cut for.

Inside it, the steady one began, very slightly, to flicker.

And the door opened.

It opened right above Unit 12, deep in the shaft, where there was nothing to open, and it kept opening: straight up the shaft and out of the ruins and along the old scar to the top of the sky. The scar split from the middle outward, the way a seam splits when something pushes against it from inside, and widened, past where it had been in the Almost, past where it had been when Unit 12 touched it the first time, until it filled the horizon, a wall of light from one edge of the world to the other. And at the bottom of it, deep in the shaft where it had begun, just above Unit 12, the light ran down into the heart of it, into the place where everything was going to go. It opened quietly. It opened the way a door opens in a house where someone is sleeping.

Total Assimilation poured out of it, and up toward it.

In Patrick's ear, the Conclave's voices had stopped reading. They were saying his name now, in the tone of men who had just understood that something was wrong.

"Commander Moriah. The door is open. You are to hold it from the shaft. Going through is not on the schedule. You are not authorized to—"

"I held," Patrick said. "The time is right."

He cut them off.

Patrick never said a word to his son. He never had.

Then Unit 12 went into the light at the bottom of the door, carrying what it needed, with the two swords burning inside it like lamps carried into a dark house. Behind it, the way down stayed open.

* * *

In what was left of the ops room, three levels up the wall of the shaft, Ruth Hermon sat alone at the deputy's desk.

She had not been alone when the door opened. There had been eleven of them, technicians in heavy coats with their breath smoking in the air, and when the light came up the shaft and in through the cracked windows they had looked up from their consoles one after another, the way people look up when someone says their name, and come apart, gently, from the edges in. Their chairs were still turning a little. Their screens were still lit.

Ruth didn't get up. A long time ago Patrick had told her to run the ops room and not ask, and she had run it, and she had not asked. There was nobody left to run it for now. She sat with her reading glasses on their chain and her hands folded on the desk and watched the feeds go white one after another, the coastal towns, the settlements, the last relay stations, and waited for her turn, the way you wait at the end of a long exam for the last student to hand in her paper.

It didn't hurt. She felt her own edges go, the way she had always imagined the test chair must have felt to the people who sat in it, and was a little surprised to find she wasn't afraid.

At the end there was a young woman standing in the doorway of the ops room, with short hair and a pencil behind her ear, looking at her the way she used to look up from the front row of a lecture hall when she had found the mistake in the proof before anyone else.

"Yukimura," Ruth said.

Then the chair turned a little, by itself, and there was nobody in it.

### Chapter 69: The Trigger

From the bridge of the Vigil, Val watched it happen.

She stood at the long window with her hands flat on the glass, with the light of the scar pouring over everything, and watched a black shape rise out of the ruins and open like a flower and close around Navis. She heard Seus on the channel. She heard him say *Dad.* She heard the breathing. She heard it stop.

Then the door opened, and filled the sky, and the black shape went back down into it.

On the monitors along the bridge, the feeds came in: settlements, coastal towns, the last relay stations still working. Across the frozen world, people were looking up. In streets and fields and on rooftops, they stopped what they were doing and lifted their faces to the light, and a look came over them, peaceful, almost relieved, like someone setting down a heavy bag at the end of a long walk. And then they began to come apart. Slowly, gently, into frost and light, from the edges in, the way frost comes off a window when the sun hits it: every seal on Earth going soft at once. They rose. Thousands of them. Millions. Rising off the earth like snow falling in reverse, toward the door.

Val knew that. She had seen it, in a capsule, in her own arms. It was how Abriel had gone.

Somewhere, in a valley full of steam, a woman with a baby on her hip was looking up.

The Vigil was coming apart.

The road Seus had opened through the swarm was closing behind them. The white units had found the ship. They were all over it, on its back, on its flanks, tearing at the grafted pink flight surfaces with their long hands, and the whole ship shuddered under her feet with every hit. Alarms were going off all over the bridge, one on top of another, until they stopped meaning anything. Somebody was shouting damage reports. Below the bridge window, in the hangar bay, Unit 04 hung in its cradle in the ship's spine, and the cables that fed the ship from its core were tearing loose, one by one, and whipping across the bay floor, throwing sparks.

Alex knew they wouldn't make it all the way down. Val could see it in the way he was standing.

He came and stood beside her at the window.

"He went down," Alex said. "Patrick. He took Seus with him."

Val didn't say anything. She couldn't.

Then Alex held out his hand.

The Halo's trigger sat in his palm. The small black switch. She had watched it in his hand on the bridge as she walked across the bay toward a white unit, with his thumb on it. She had felt it every day in Ararat, on every mission, a millimeter from her life. He had held it for a year and never pressed it. He was holding it out to her.

"You decide what's important for you," he said.

It was the answer to her question, years late. She had screamed it in a capsule in a port, alone, with her comms off, so nobody would hear. *Who the fuck decides what's important for me?* Somebody had heard. Alex read every log. He had found it in the capsule's audio weeks later, alone, at four in the morning, and never said a word about it. *You do.* You always should have.

Val took it.

It was lighter than she'd expected. She closed her hand around it and looked down through the bridge window, at the bay, at Unit 04 hanging in its cradle with its chest glowing through the plating. She had felt it since the transport. She had felt it every minute since she came aboard, the way you feel your own pulse. And under the cradle, under the suppression, under whatever the Morningstar and a war and a door had done to it, under the place where the rage was sleeping, there was something else. Something small. She didn't know what it was. She only knew it was there, the way you know someone is in a dark room before they speak.

"April," she said.

Alex looked at her for a long moment. Then he nodded, once, as if she had told him something he already knew.

"Then go get her," he said.

He put his hand flat on the command console and pulled the cradle release.

Down in the bay, the Enoch cradle around 04's core opened like a hand letting go. The last cables tore loose all at once with a sound like a building coming down. And every light on the Vigil went out.

The consoles died. The alarms died. The deep hum that had been coming up through the floor since Ararat, the sound of the ship breathing, stopped. In the sudden silence, the emergency lights came on, dim and red, and the whole bridge was the color of the inside of a closed eye. The ship lurched. Its nose dipped. Without the core, it had no power to fly, only its long grown wings, and it began to fall forward and down, slowly, in a long tilting glide, toward the ruins.

"We'll never make it to the shaft like this," Alex said. "So I'm going to do something stupid."

He smiled. And for a moment he was twenty-three again, in an apartment kitchen, burning omurice one-handed and laughing at himself while a girl at the table watched.

"Go," he said. "The bay. Go."

She turned to go. She got as far as the hatch.

"Val. Wait."

She stopped.

When she looked back, he was standing at the dead console in the red light, with his one hand on it, and his face was doing something she had never seen it do. It was coming apart. Not like the people under the door, gently. All at once.

"There's no time," he said, "so just listen. Seus. I got him out. Not you, not Umbral, not luck. Me and Aoi. After the port, his vitals spiked, once, I saw it. We took him. Aoi signed the report that said *unrecoverable* so nobody would come looking, and we kept him in a room under headquarters that wasn't on any map, and every night one of us sat with him. For months. She sat with him more than I did." He was talking fast, too fast, the words falling over each other. "When the sky opened, she went down to get him out, and the ceiling came down on her, and she told me to take him. So I took him. I carried him up eleven floors. I went back for her and the stairs were gone." His voice broke and he kept going through it. "She's still down there. Under the ruins. Lieutenant Sena. Aoi. You knew her voice. You knew her at the postcard wall. You never knew she sat with him every night."

The ship shuddered. Somewhere aft, something tore.

"Your father wrote to you," Alex said.

Val went very still.

"Postcards. Letters. Every month, until a month before he died. The Conclave held every one. There was a box. Aoi found it, in the mail office. She was going to give it to you on a Sunday, with soup, the right way. She left a note on it. *For Val. Do it right.* She never got her Sunday." He shut his eyes. "Then I found it. In the archive, after the codes. Filed right beside them, with Patrick's initials on the tag. I had it for months. In my desk. I didn't give it to you. I was afraid of what it would do to your numbers. I was afraid of the Halo. I was afraid of— I was afraid. And it burned. In Ararat. They're gone, Val. All of them."

She didn't move. She didn't breathe.

"And my mother signed the test that killed yours," Alex said. "Helen Enoch. Door Project, Test 2075-11. *Approved.* It's her signature on it. I've been forging that signature since I was eight. She went through the door herself, in '87, and she didn't come back. The last thing she asked for was that I look after the Kane girl. I thought she meant be kind to you. I think she meant pay you back." He opened his eyes and looked at her across the red bridge. "I'm sorry. I'm sorry. I should have said all of it a year ago. I should have said it every day."

Val stood in the hatchway with the trigger in her fist.

Somewhere, later, she would be angry. She knew that. She would be angry about the box, and the desk, and the months, and the fire in Ararat, and a woman under four floors of rubble who had once taught her to roll an omelette. She would be angry in the way you're only angry at people who could have done better, because they mattered.

But not now. Because he had told her. After a year of saying nothing, of talking to her only through lieutenants and trays and switches, with the ship coming apart around them and the world coming apart around the ship, he had finally said it. All of it. Out loud. With words. The way Maggie had said someone had to, eventually.

Alex looked at her. Then he laughed, very softly, the old laugh, the one from the kitchen.

"Hey. Remember the corridor at your old school? The morning of the plant? I asked you why you even still went to school, and you said—" He shook his head. "I wanted to say something, that day. I didn't."

He smiled at her across the dark bridge.

"Normal's what you do best, right?" Alex said. "So go be normal. You've got school in the morning."

Val looked at him for a long moment.

"Thank you," she said.

It was the first time she had ever said it to him.

Alex stared at her. His mouth opened, and nothing came out, and he swallowed, and tried again.

"...You're welcome," Alex said.

She ran.

She ran down through the dark ship, through red corridors full of noise, down a ladder, down another, sliding the last half with her hands on the rails, out onto the catwalk above the hangar bay. The bay doors were open. The light of the door poured in through them, blinding, and the white units were out there in it, wheeling. And in the middle of the bay, Unit 04 hung half out of its open cradle with the torn cables trailing from its chest, swinging, tilting toward the open doors as the ship tilted, held by the last of its moorings.

She went over the catwalk rail and dropped onto its shoulder and slid down onto its chest, and grabbed hold of the plating over the core, and held on.

The last mooring snapped.

Unit 04 slid out of the open bay into the sky with Val clinging to its chest, and fell.

On the bridge, alone, in the red light, Alex Enoch took the controls.

The crew was gone. He had sent them aft to the escape pods with a single order, the way he'd sent the town to the ramps, in a voice so calm none of them had argued. The ship was dark and silent and falling, and it handled like a barge in a storm, but it had wings, long grown wings of living tissue, and living tissue remembered how to glide. He found the angle. He found the line. Below him, through the window, the ruins of headquarters came up out of the light, and the shaft in the middle of them, and the Conclave's troops ringed around it in their black armor, and the white units swarming at its mouth to hold it, and the path of light going down.

White units hit the ship. He felt every one through the deck. He didn't turn.

He thought about a grocery list stuck to a refrigerator with a magnet shaped like a fish. Round handwriting. *Eggs. Rice. Soup stock. Keep practicing.*

He thought about a beam across a room, and dust coming down in long gray sheets, and two dirty hands on his face.

*Then come back,* she had said. *Take him.*

He had taken him. He had come back. The stairs had been gone.

"I'm coming back," Alex said, to the window, to the light, to the ruins coming up to meet him. "You said to. I'm late. I'm always late."

The Vigil came down over the Reliquary.

It came down the way the Morningstar had come down on Fuji, slow and enormous, burning. It came down across the ring of black armor around the shaft and the troops scattered and didn't scatter fast enough. It came down across the swarm of white units at the shaft's mouth, and swept them into the ruins like leaves. Its long spine broke across the lip of the shaft and tore it open, wide, wider, the old concrete and the frost and the broken floors of headquarters all going down together, so that the narrow path Unit 12 had gone down became a road. And the ship broke apart over the Reliquary, over the sub-levels and the forgotten rooms and a door that wasn't on any map, and burned.

Alex Enoch went down with it.

### Chapter 70: Go Get Her

Maggie caught her in midair.

Lizzie came out of the wreckage of the Vigil a second before it hit, out of the open bay doors in a shower of burning plating, rattling, smoking, missing paint and half its armor and impossibly alive. She came in under the falling shape of Unit 04 in a long screaming curve and caught it under one arm, hard, like catching a sack of rice, and Val was nearly shaken off its chest and wasn't.

Maggie was laughing in the capsule. She was laughing the way she laughed at everything, and tears were streaming down her face.

"Hang on, kiddo!" she shouted over the speakers. "Last stretch!"

Below them, the Vigil lay broken across the ruins of headquarters, burning, from one side of the shaft to the other. The shaft was open. Wide open, torn wide, a road of light going straight down out of the ruins into somewhere that wasn't anywhere, with the frost of a whole species falling up past it toward the sky.

Val looked at the burning ship for one second. Then she put her face against 04's plating and held on.

Maggie flew Lizzie straight down the path Unit 12 had left open.

Down through the light. Down through the white units, which came at them from every side, pale and silent, reaching. Down through the frost-snow of a whole species coming apart, the slow bright flakes of people rising past them on every side, millions, so many that it was like flying down through a blizzard falling the wrong way. Lizzie twisted through all of it with 04 under her arm, an old orange unit older than anyone flying, rattling like a can full of bolts.

A white unit caught Lizzie's free arm and tore it off at the shoulder. Maggie screamed, because she felt it, and kept humming. A white hand closed on Lizzie's leg, and the leg came off, and Maggie screamed again, and kept humming, louder, something old, from before the white sky, a song Val had heard her hum a hundred times and never learned the name of. Lizzie fell more than flew now, tumbling, righting herself, tumbling again, and Maggie kept her pointed down the path, down and down, toward the bottom, where the light was brightest.

At the bottom of the path, where the light was brightest, Lizzie finally gave out.

Val felt it happen. The old unit shuddered all over, the way a horse shudders before it goes down, and the rattling stopped. They were falling slowly now, the three of them, Lizzie and 04 and Val, drifting down through light so bright it had no color.

Maggie let go of 04.

She did it on purpose. She swung Lizzie's last arm, the one holding 04, in a long slow arc, and opened Lizzie's hand at the top of it, and sent 04 tumbling the last few meters down into the heart of the Rite, into the place where everything was going.

Val turned in midair, clinging to 04's chest, and looked back.

Lizzie hung above her in the light, broken, one-armed, one-legged, with her capsule cracked open, and Maggie was leaning half out of it, seventeen and older than anyone, with her hair blowing around her face and tears on it, grinning.

"You asked me once who told me to keep an eye on you," Maggie called down to her through the light. "Go find out."

Val looked at her: this girl who had stayed seventeen for decades. Who had brought her food on a tray three times a day in Ararat when no one else would come near her, and sat on the floor, and talked about nothing, and never once asked about the Almost. Who had stood at a bay door with her arms folded and said *careful, kiddo.*

She didn't have time to say any of it. She hoped Maggie knew. She thought, looking up at that grinning face, that Maggie probably did.

Then she climbed into Unit 04.

The capsule was dark and cold and smelled of frost and old metal. She dropped into the seat. The hatch closed over her. In her right hand was the trigger to her own Halo, and in no one else's.

She linked with it by her own choice.

She reached for 04 the way she had reached for it a thousand times, the way she had reached for it in the dark of a corrupted mass with Navis's hands, the way she had reached for it in a white room. She braced for the wall. For the rage, waking. For the white room, or the gray, or nothing.

It wasn't any of those.

The core was quiet.

It was like walking into a house after a fire. She could feel the shape of everything that had been there: the room Sam had built and locked, the white room, the corners where a girl had sat very still for four hours in 2082. All of it scorched. All of it empty. The rage was still there, she could feel it, far down, under the floor, asleep, coiled, enormous, the way a fire sleeps in a coal. But on top, where the rooms had been, there was only the burned floor, black and quiet and swept clean.

And in the middle of the burned floor, something new.

It was very small. It was the way the seedling had been, the first day: a single pale thread coming up out of black earth. It had no shape yet. It had no memories. It didn't know where it was or what it was or that there had ever been anything here before it. It was the last thing left, after everything else had burned down. It was what was left when you burned a core down to nothing.

It turned toward her.

"Who are you?" it asked.

It was a child's voice, small and clear, with nothing in it at all. No fear. No hope. Just the question.

Val thought of a white room going gray. Of a small weight slipping out of her arms like water. Of a question she hadn't been able to answer, the last one, while the light went out. *Did you come to save me? Are you a hero?* She had thought of the port, and her hands, and her father on a talk show with his chest full of medals. She had thought of everything a hero was supposed to be and everything she wasn't. And she hadn't been able to say yes.

She didn't think about any of that now. She thought of a seat taken on a transport. A door held up in the snow. A street held. A name remembered on the radio. None of them had been heroes. All of them had picked the seat anyway.

She clutched her chest, tears falling from her eyes.

"I'm your hero," Val said.

The small thing in the burned house was quiet for a moment. It seemed to turn the word over, the way you'd turn over a stone you'd found, to see what was under it.

"...Who am I?" it asked.

"April," said Val.

She held the trigger in one hand and the controls in the other, and she felt the small thing in the core settle against her, the way a cat settles against a warm leg, without deciding to.

"Hold on," Val told it. "We're going in."

Unit 04 fell into the light.

---

## Arc 17: Rooms

> *In my Father's house are many mansions: if it were not so, I would have told you. I go to prepare a place for you.*
>
> — John 14:2

### Chapter 71: Rooms

Heaven was outside time, and it was built from memory.

Penemue had told her that much, in a corridor turned to frost: everything that ever happened is still happening there. Every day that ever was. What he hadn't told her, because maybe he hadn't known, was what it was made of. It was made of people. Every mind that had ever slipped outside time, in a white sky or a War on Heaven or a test that went wrong, was here, and everything those minds remembered was here with them, kept exactly, down to the dust. And tonight, with the Rite pulling every mind on Earth up through the door, there were more of them arriving every second. Heaven was filling up with rooms.

It had rules, though nobody had ever written them down. She would learn them the way you learn the rules of a house in the dark: by walking into the furniture. Nothing in it weighed anything except what it weighed to someone. There was no up or down except the ones people remembered. And it gave you what you wanted most. It couldn't help it. Whatever you wanted hardest, it showed you, or built for you, out of whatever was nearest, and it couldn't tell what had happened from what you wished had happened. Neither could you, unless you knew how to look. The real rooms had something wrong in them: a crack, a scuff, a stain, frost in a corner. The ones somebody had only wished for were perfect, and cast no shadows. And the ones in between flickered, like a picture on a bad screen, as if they hadn't decided yet.

Unit 04 fell out of the light and landed on a floor.

It was wooden. It was polished, and scuffed in a long familiar path from a door to a desk, and it went on in every direction farther than she could see, and every board in it was as wide as a road. Light came in from the side, from a window so huge its frame vanished into haze, and the light lay across the floor in a long gold slant, and in the slant, dust was turning. Each mote was the size of a car. They drifted past 04's head, slow and shining.

She knew this room.

At the far end of it, the size of a cliff, was a desk. And at the desk, in a chair as tall as a tower, sat a nine-year-old girl with short hair, with her arms folded on the wood and her head resting on them, perfectly still, the way she had sat while a man in a rumpled coat explained the rest to the top of her head. She was enormous. She was the size of the world. She didn't move. She would never move. She would sit like that, here, forever.

Val looked at her from inside 04 and couldn't breathe.

"Val," said the small voice in the core. "Val, where are we? What is that?"

"It's okay," Val said. "It's just a room."

Unit 12 was waiting for her in the middle of it.

It stood in the gold slant of light between 04 and the desk, black and tall and narrow, with the dust turning around it. It didn't move when she landed. It didn't raise its hands. And Patrick's voice came out of it, calm and tired, the same voice that had once knelt to her eye level in this room and started to tell her that her father was a great man.

"Go back, Valeria," he said. "You don't have to see this."

She hit him.

She came across the floor of the principal's office in three strides and drove 04's fist into Unit 12's chest so hard that the black glass rang like a bell, and Unit 12 went backward through the dust, and through the wall.

They fought through the rooms of their life together, and every one of them was a room he had been in, or should have been.

The wall of the principal's office broke open onto his apartment.

She knew it at once: the neat bare living room of a man who didn't spend much time in it, the low table, the two chairs, the window over the expressway. They crashed through the living room wall in a shower of plaster the size of boulders, and Unit 12 caught itself on the dining table, and the table was the one where they had eaten two months of silent dinners when she was nine, every night, with him asking about school and food and television and her answering in single words until he ran out. Unit 12's shoulder hit the hall closet as it went past.

The closet burst open.

Blank canvases spilled out across the floor by the dozen. White and empty, stacked edge to edge, dozens and dozens of them, more than any closet could hold, pouring out like water from a broken dam, sliding across the floor around their feet. A box of dried-out paint tubes fell after them and broke open, and the tubes rolled away, hard as stones. 04 stepped on a canvas, and it crunched. Unit 12 stepped on another. Neither of them could get out of the way. There were too many.

"You were going to paint," Val said. "You told him. In the hangar, at three in the morning. You were going to go to the mountains."

"I was twenty-five," said Patrick.

He came at her through the canvases, and she met him, and they went through the apartment's outer wall and out into the light.

The next room was the school gate.

It was the gate of Hakobune Municipal No. 3, in the afternoon, with the cherry trees along the wall bare and the shadows long. And parked across from it, at the curb, the size of a building to them now, was a small gray car with its engine running. Two cans of coffee sat on the dashboard, going cold. The sweet one was on the passenger side.

Two giants fought around it. 04 swung, and Unit 12 ducked behind the car, and the car didn't move, didn't shake, didn't even rock on its springs, as if nothing in this room were allowed to touch it.

"You picked me up," Val shouted. "Every day. Seven years. Every quiet day. You never missed one."

"Yes," Patrick said.

"Why?"

He didn't answer. Unit 12 came over the top of the car at her, and she caught it, and they went down together onto the street, and behind them the gray car went on idling at the gate, waiting for someone to come out.

The next room was a dinner table.

It was her own kitchen, in the apartment near headquarters, the night of the dinner. Four plates. Four glasses. Chopsticks laid neatly on their rests. Three dishes in the fridge under plastic wrap. A torn tamagoyaki on a plate by itself. And over all of it, smoothed flat with both hands, a white tablecloth she had bought with her own money, the first thing she had ever bought for anyone.

They fought across it. Unit 12 went down onto the table, and the plates didn't break, and the tablecloth didn't wrinkle.

"You said you'd come," Val said.

"I know."

"You said it in the car. I asked you, and you said *I'll come.*"

"I know," said Patrick.

The last room was a hangar at three in the morning.

It was dark, a cathedral, with the work lights off and the status panels blinking green along the gantries and the city's glow lying across the floor in long pale stripes from the high windows. The exos stood in their bays like saints in alcoves. And in front of Unit 04's empty bay, small, so small down there on the floor between them that she almost stepped on him, a man in a loose tie stood holding a can of coffee in both shaking hands, shouting at a machine.

*Why won't you tell me everything?*

The shout went up into the dark and came back down, smaller, and smaller, and then nothing. And then it started again. It would always start again. It would go on starting forever.

Val stopped. Unit 12 stopped too, across the hangar from her. They both looked down at him.

"You asked him to tell you everything," Val said. "I was there. I heard you. And he never did, so you just kept going?"

"What else was there?" Patrick said.

She was better than him.

She had always been better than him. He had sat in a chair and watched her fight since she was nine; he had learned it from a screen, every movement, every habit, every tell, and it wasn't the same. It had never been the same. She drove Unit 12 back across the hangar floor, through the pale stripes of city light, and it couldn't keep up with her, and its guard broke, the long black arms coming apart a fraction of a second too late, and for one moment she had him. She had 04's hand drawn back over the open center of its chest, where the capsule was, where he was.

"Val," said the small voice in the core. "Who is that?"

Val hesitated.

"Someone I know," she said.

"Is he a bad man?"

She didn't know. She stood there with 04's fist drawn back over the black glass and found that she didn't know, and that was the moment.

Unit 12's hand closed on 04's shoulder.

And Patrick did the thing he had done once before, in a port, to his own son.

He engaged Proxy.

04 went out from under her.

It went the way it had gone in the port, all at once, like a floor opening. One moment it was her body. The next it wasn't. She pulled at the controls and nothing happened. She screamed at it and nothing happened. And under her, under the burned floor of the core, she felt the rage wake up.

It had been asleep. She had felt it sleeping, far down, coiled in the coal. Now it opened its eyes. It came up through the floor of the core like something coming up out of deep water, enormous, the old fury, older than her, older than 04, twenty years deep, and it took the body she was sitting in. It took 04's arms. It took 04's legs. It filled the capsule with heat until the walls glowed. She was in the middle of it, trapped, the way she had been trapped in the port with her hands crushing Seus, and she screamed.

The small thing in the core screamed too.

She felt it get pushed down. The rage came up over it like a wave over a child on a beach, and it went under, small, terrified, with nothing to hold on to. *Val. Val. Val.* It didn't have any other words yet.

"Hold on to me," Val said. She couldn't move her hands. She couldn't move anything. She reached for it anyway, inside, the way she had once reached for a girl in a white room. "Hold on to me. I'm here. I'm still here."

It held on.

"This was always going to happen," Patrick said.

### Chapter 72: The Fourth War

At the center of Heaven was a stair, and at the top of the stair was a throne.

The stair rose out of the middle of all the rooms, out of everything, white and impossibly long, going up and up into a light so bright it had no color, so that you couldn't see the top except as a place where the light was thickest. Every room in Heaven was built around it the way a city is built around a cathedral. Every memory faced it. It was the one thing in Heaven that nobody remembered building.

Unit 12 climbed toward it, carrying the Nephil it had taken from Seus's eye, with the two swords burning inside it, both flickering now.

Val could see it, from inside the Beast, through 04's eyes, which weren't hers anymore. Unit 12 went up the first steps slowly, the way Patrick had gone down the ladder in the shaft, the way a man his age climbs anything. In the open center of its chest, where it had closed around Navis, something was glowing: a cold white light, small and hungry, the same light that had been behind a black patch for six years. The last child. Going home.

When it reached the foot of the stair, the light at the top of it parted.

Heaven gives you what you want most, and the last child wanted home. The light at the top of the stair drew back like a curtain, the way it had been built to, a long time ago, for a child coming home. And there, at the top, Val could see it now, small and far and absolutely clear: a chair.

Then Patrick drove the Beast up the stair.

Val was inside it. So, through Proxy, was he. From an ops room, Proxy could move a body and nothing more; it worked through whatever was left of the nature in the core. The rage was something else. To drive the rage, Patrick had to reach down into the core himself, in person, and take hold of it with his own mind. So he was in there, with her, in the heat, in the capsule that wasn't a capsule anymore. And so was everything Sam had left in the core: the rage, and under the rage, the memories that fed it.

They poured over both of them as the Beast climbed.

He had read the folder, every page of it, once. And for the first time in twenty years, Patrick saw what he had been holding.

They watched it together.

Val felt him see it. She felt it go into him, the way cold water goes into a cracked jar. She saw it with him, because there was nowhere else to look, because the memories were the walls now, and the floor, and the light.

Sam's plan had never really been about Umbral. Umbral was only the door. It always had been. The people above Umbral wanted Heaven open, for their Rite, for their one mind forever; let them. Let them spend fifty years and a white sky and a city and a hundred pilots getting it open. And when it opened, go through it first. Take the door. And send the Beast up the stair, all the way up, to the top, to kill whoever sat on the throne.

Whoever had let a giant fall on a house where two little girls were waiting for their brother to come home from school. Whoever had let Sora die in a test chamber at two in the afternoon, alone, with nobody holding her hand. Whoever had watched all of it from up there, and said nothing. Not once. Not one word. It was a war on God, a Fourth War on Heaven, and Sam Kane had built it alone, in a hangar, at night, over years, coughing blood into his sleeve, with the cancer eating him from the inside.

Val felt Patrick understand it. She felt him understand that this was what he had held. That this was what the brushes had gone into the closet for.

The Conclave burned first.

They were here. Of course they were here. They had been here all along, presiding over their Rite from inside the light, the voices that had never shown their faces, and now Val saw them: old men and women at a long table at the foot of the stair, in a room built from fifty years of their own memory, with their book open in front of them. They looked up when the Beast came. They had time to look up. The fire touched them, and they went up like paper in the middle of their own ceremony, the book and the table and the long room and all, a single bright flash, and then ash, drifting up the stair.

That part of the plan worked.

The Beast climbed on.

It reached the top of the stair. The light was so thick there that it had weight, like water. And in the middle of the light, on a plain white step, stood the throne.

It was empty.

There was no one there. There was no one sitting on it, no one standing behind it, no one hiding in the light around it. The chair had the look of a chair nobody has sat in for a very long time, or ever: no wear on the arms, no dent in the seat, nothing. Val couldn't tell which. Maybe someone had been there, once, and said nothing. Maybe no one had ever been there at all. The saints had climbed this stair to ask their question, and heard nothing, and gone to war over it either way.

Sam's war had been against an empty chair.

And the war didn't stop.

That was the worst of it. The rage had been built to climb the stair and kill whatever sat at the top, and there was nothing at the top, and so it simply went on. It went sideways. It went out. Heaven was made of memory, every mind that had ever slipped outside time, and the Beast's fire went through it room by room, the way fire goes through a house when nobody is home to put it out.

The principal's office caught. Val watched the nine-year-old at the desk vanish into the flames, still not moving.

The school gate caught. The small gray car burned at the curb with its engine running.

Then, at the very edge of everything, a small room caught.

It was a laboratory. A test chamber, white, with a window into a control booth and a chair in the middle bolted to the floor. And in the room, in the chair, as if she had been sitting there since 2075 waiting for someone to come and get her, was a woman with short hair, cut above the neck. She looked up when the fire reached the door. She had a pencil in her hand, and she was turning it through her fingers, over and under, the way you turn a pencil when you've been waiting a long time.

"Sora," Patrick whispered.

He had known her. He had sat at their table, Sam and Sora's, in a small apartment by the harbor, eating her bad curry and laughing at Sam's worse jokes. He had held Valeria on his knee once, when she was a baby, for about four minutes, terrified. Sam's revenge for Sora was climbing up the stairs of Heaven in front of him, and it was going to burn Sora too.

Then they saw the last thing in the core.

It was a hangar at night, in the last year, a long time before the end and not long enough.

Sam was in it, alone, too sick to stand without leaning on something. He leaned on Pip's leg. He was gaunt and gray and he had stopped going to the talk shows months ago, and he breathed the way the very sick breathe, with his whole body, with a sound in it. He was looking at Pip, at the core in its chest, at the locked room he had built inside it.

He had changed his mind.

Val saw it in his face the way you see weather in the sky. Somewhere in the last year, he had changed his mind. Maybe it was Pip asking about his daughter. Maybe it was a postcard with a lighthouse on it, with no answer. Maybe it was the cancer, which makes some people braver and some people more afraid and some people, for a while, very clear. He didn't want the war anymore. He didn't want the Beast. He wanted to unbuild it.

And he couldn't.

He had run out of ways to undo what he'd built. The rage was locked in the core, behind a door he had locked from the inside, and he had built it too well; he couldn't reach it anymore, not without breaking the core, and if he broke the core there would be nothing left for his daughter to pilot when the Conclave came for her, and they would come for her. The missions never stopped long enough for him to find Patrick alone and tell him to quit, and every room in headquarters had the Conclave's ears in it, and Patrick was the only man alive the Conclave didn't watch, because Patrick didn't know anything. If Sam told him, they would know. So he didn't.

He had given Patrick the folder the spring before, when he still meant every word in it. *Hold this.* He couldn't take it back now without saying why, and if he said why, they would hear.

So he did the one thing he still could.

He couldn't reach the rage. But he could put something next to it.

All that last year, in Pip's capsule with the walls dark, he had been building something small in another corner of the core, beside the thing he had built to burn Heaven. Not a weapon. Not a plan. A voice: curious, the kind of thing that keeps asking when nobody answers. He was hoping it would grow big enough, someday, to sit on the rage.

He didn't know if it would work. He didn't know how far any of it would go. By then he was too sick to know much of anything, except that he had to try.

"Patrick will finish it," Sam said to no one, or to Pip, who was far above him in the hangar and couldn't hear. "He's better at holding on than I am. He'll hold this till it kills him. He'll take it all the way up." He breathed, the way the very sick breathe. "So she has to grow. If she grows, there's nothing down there for him to wake. He'll reach in for the fire and find a little girl asking questions."

He put his hand flat on Pip's leg, as high as he could reach, toward the place where the small new thing was.

"Grow," he said. "Please."

Patrick let go of Proxy.

He did it all at once. Val felt his mind let go of the rage the way a hand lets go of a rope it has been holding so long it forgot it was holding anything. 04 came back to her, her arms and her legs and her eyes, all at once, so suddenly she nearly fell.

The Beast stopped on the top step, in front of the empty chair, and the fire stopped with it.

Below the stair, inside Unit 12, one of the two swords stopped flickering. Its flame stood up straight and still, the way it had burned in its case before anyone wanted anything from it.

Slowly, the rage drew back into itself. It came down off the stair, step by step, a great shape of heat and smoke and old fury going down out of the light, into the center of Heaven, where all the rooms met. And in the center of Heaven, there was a house burning. A small house, in a district by the harbor, two stories, with a laundry pole on the balcony, burning the way it had been burning since the day a giant fell on it, when a boy of seventeen came home from school. The rage went down to it. It went into the fire. And it sat down in the fire, the way a tired man sits down in his own kitchen, and went still.

Patrick sat in Unit 12 for a long time without moving.

Val could hear him breathing on the channel. In, and out. A long time.

"It was stupid," he said at last. "All of it. A war against an empty chair. He gave up his daughter for it. I gave up my son for it." He laughed once, and it didn't sound like a laugh. "And he saw it in time. He had a year left, and he spent it trying to stop himself. I had twenty years. I had Theseus. I had you, every quiet afternoon at that gate. And I held. I held on to the hope that it would all be worth it, because if it wasn't, I'd done all of it for nothing."

On the screen of 04's wall, Unit 12's head bowed. In the capsule, she knew, he had put his face in his hands.

"He was stupid, Valeria," Patrick said. "I was so much stupider."

Val looked at Unit 12, the black machine that had swallowed Seus. She could feel the hungry white light in its chest, dimmer now. And around it, in it, held, something else. Someone.

"He's in there with you," she said. "Seus."

"I know."

"Go talk to him."

Patrick was quiet.

"I don't know how," he said.

"Neither did I," Val said.

### Chapter 73: The Train

It was a late train, nearly empty, the overhead lights flickering. Outside the windows there was nothing but light.

Seus sat on the bench he had sat on at sixteen, the day he came into Hakobune for the first time, with his two duffel bags at his feet. The same bench, the same car, the same advertisement over the door for a cram school that had closed before the white sky. The same scratch on the window where someone had carved a heart with a key. He had looked at that heart for three hours on the way in, the first time, because he didn't want to look at anything else.

He didn't know how he had gotten here. The last thing he remembered was the black glass closing, and the light going out of his eye, and the breathing on the channel, in and out, and nothing.

Across from him, someone sat down.

It took Seus a long moment to understand that it was his father.

Patrick Moriah sat on the opposite bench with his hands on his knees and his coat buttoned, the way he sat at his desk, the way he sat everywhere. He looked at the floor of the car. He looked at the duffel bags. He looked, for a moment, at Seus's face, at the eye that had no patch on it now and nothing behind it, and then away.

Neither of them spoke for three stations. They had never been good at it.

The train stopped at each one with a long sigh of brakes, and the doors opened, and the platforms outside weren't platforms. The first one was a kitchen in an apartment across the sea, with a woman standing at the stove with her back to them, and a phone on the counter, off the hook, and a small boy sitting beside the phone, waiting, counting something. The second was a training yard in the rain, with a boy of twelve in a too-big flight suit being knocked down and getting up and being knocked down, and nobody in the stands. The third was a funeral in a gray room on the fourth floor of a building in Hakobune, with a photograph on an easel and a row of folding chairs that nobody sat in, and a man at the front reading a statement about duty.

The doors closed. The train went on.

"You killed me," Seus said at last.

"Yes," Patrick said.

"Twice."

"Yes."

"Why?" Seus's voice cracked on it. "You picked her up every day. Seven years. You never missed one. You never even came to the station for me."

"I was at the station," Patrick said.

Seus stared at him.

"In the car. Across the street." Patrick looked at his hands. "I watched you come out with your bags and look around for me. I watched you look at every car. I watched you wait. Twenty minutes. You checked your phone eleven times. Then you picked up your bags and went to find a cab." He was quiet for a moment. "I couldn't get out of the car."

The train rocked.

"She wasn't mine, Theseus," Patrick said. "It didn't cost me anything to be kind to her. You were mine. Every time I looked at you, I had to look at what I was doing. So I didn't look."

"And when I came to you? After I woke up?" Seus's hands were shaking on his knees. "I walked eleven days. I didn't have boots for the first four. I came all the way to the ruins and stood in front of your desk, and you looked at me for a whole minute, and you had them walk me out."

"I saw the patch," Patrick said. "I knew what was behind it. I knew I would need it. That's why I sent you away." He didn't look up. "I couldn't look at you and know that."

"I tore it off," Seus said. His voice was barely there. "You didn't make me. I did that. The door. Everyone out there coming apart—"

"I counted on it," Patrick said. "I sat in the dark at the bottom of the shaft and waited for you to."

The train rocked. The lights flickered. Outside, the light went past.

"I used to paint," Patrick said after a while. "Landscapes. No people in them. People leave, and mountains don't." He almost smiled. "I should have painted you."

Seus looked out the window at the light for a long time.

He thought about the phone on the counter, off the hook, and a small boy beside it counting a long slow breath on the line, in, and out, waiting through it, because if he waited long enough, sometimes, after the breathing, there would be a voice. *Happy birthday, Theseus.* Three words. Sometimes four. And then a click.

When he spoke again, his voice was very small, the voice of a boy who had asked one question his whole life and never gotten an answer.

"Was any of it good?" he said. "Anything I did?"

Patrick looked up.

He looked at his son. Not at the eye, or the burns, or the place where the patch had been. At him. For the first time, maybe, in Seus's whole life, his father looked straight at him and didn't look away, and Seus saw it cost him something, and saw him pay it.

"You were good, Theseus," his father said. "You were always good. It was me."

The train began to slow.

Outside, a platform slid into view, ordinary and gray: Hakobune Central, under its old curved roof, with the evening coming down and the vending machines lit along the wall. The station where a sixteen-year-old had once come out with two duffel bags and stood under the clock and waited for someone who never got out of the car. Across the street, through the station's glass doors, Seus could see the curb. There was no gray car there now.

"This is your stop," Patrick said.

"What about you?"

"I'm staying on."

Patrick reached over, awkwardly, the way a man does something for the first time, and put his hand on his son's head. He left it there. It was heavy and warm and not very steady. Seus sat very still under it and didn't breathe.

"Go home, Theseus," his father said.

Seus picked up his duffel bags. He stood. He got off. The doors closed behind him with a long sigh, and he turned around on the platform and looked back through the window.

His father was still sitting on the bench, with his hands on his knees. And as the train began to move, he lifted one hand, in a small, stiff wave.

It was the first time Seus had ever seen him wave.

Then the train pulled away, into the light, and was gone. And Seus woke up in Navis on the frozen ground at the edge of the shaft, in the ruins, in the snow, with the light of the door pouring down on him and the burning wreck of the Vigil a hundred meters away.

The crystal was gone from Navis's arms. The mask was gone from its face. The patch was gone, and his eye was just an eye. He could see out of it. It didn't talk. It didn't kick. It was quiet in there for the first time in six years, because what had lived in it was gone, carried up a stair in his father's hands, and he sat in the capsule in the quiet with his hands over his face, and he was crying, for the first time since he was small.

"Pilot?" said Navis, softly, after a while. "Are you damaged?"

"No," Seus said into his hands. "No. I'm okay. I'm good."

---

## Arc 18: Heaven

> *And I saw the dead, small and great, stand before God; and the books were opened.*
>
> — Revelation 20:12

### Chapter 74: Heaven

When the fire went out of the rooms, Val saw everyone.

Not the way she had seen them before. The way they truly were.

It was the Rite, she understood later. Every mind on Earth was being pulled up through the door, into the light, into the one mind the Conclave had wanted, and every one of them was passing through here on the way. For a moment, a long moment outside time, they were all in Heaven at once, all of them, the living and the dead, the ones coming apart and the ones who had come apart years ago. And when the fire went out and the smoke cleared, she could see into all of them, the way you see into a lit room from a dark street.

Every one of them was where they had most wanted to be. That was the last thing the Rite did, before the edges went: it gave each person the one thing they wanted, whole, so they would let go of everything else. In a moment they would run into each other and be nobody. Until then, each of them was more themselves than they had ever been allowed to be.

And she was awake to see it. The Rite had taken everyone who hadn't chosen to come. The few who had come in on their own stayed themselves: the ones who had been here long before the Rite began, and the ones who had walked in with their eyes open. And her.

She saw Alex.

She saw him in a kitchen on the Vigil at four in the morning, alone, with one light on over the stove, cracking eggs one-handed into a bowl. Picking out the shell with chopsticks, swearing at it under his breath. Pouring the egg into the pan in thin layers and trying to roll it, the way someone had shown him once, and tearing it, and doing it anyway. The night cook coming in at five. *Mission day. Her tray.*

She saw him before that, in a corridor in Ararat, stopping a giant with frost on his neck and pressing a ration chit into his palm. *Mission days. Don't tell her.*

Val stood in the light and understood.

It had been him. The eggs. All of them. The soft sweet ones, rolled the way Fumi used to roll them, on the mornings she went out with Lantern: his eggs, his ration, through Tom's hands. And after Tom, the torn ones. The burnt ones. The ones from some new cook who didn't know how. Him. In a kitchen at four in the morning, with one hand, getting it wrong, every time, and doing it anyway, because it was the only way he knew to say anything.

She saw him in a tatami room at night with a box on the low table and a yellow note on it, and his hand over his eyes. She saw him carry a boy up eleven flights of stairs in the dark. She saw him go back down, and find the stairs gone. She saw him blame himself for all of it: for Ross, for Seus, for the Halo, for every hour he hadn't been fast enough, every word he hadn't been brave enough to say. *Somebody has to sit up with the world,* he had said once, naming a ship. And he had. Every night, for years. Beside a hospital bed in a room that wasn't on any map. Beside a box he couldn't open. Beside a stove at four in the morning. Sitting up with the dead and the nearly dead and the ones he couldn't talk to.

And beside him, at last, she saw Aoi.

Lieutenant Sena. The voice on the comms every day for a year: *Good morning, Kane-san. Try to bring the suits back in one piece.* Val had never once looked at her face. She looked now. It was a round face, tired, with a crooked smile and a smudge of ink on the cheekbone from the mail office, and her hair tied up badly with a pen stuck through it. She was younger than Val had imagined. She was much younger than Alex had looked, in the end.

Val saw her in the little office down the hall from mission control, finding a box in the back of a cabinet. Reading the label. KANE, S. — OUTGOING — HOLD. Sitting with it on her lap for a long time, with her hand flat on the lid. Writing a sticky note in round handwriting, and crossing it out, and writing it again. *For Val. Do it right.*

She saw her at a comms desk in a port, with her eyes closed, and then open, typing a code one key at a time, and taking her hands off the console as if it had burned her.

She saw her in a room under headquarters, every night, for months, sitting beside a bed in the dark with a boy in it who didn't wake up. Holding his hand. Telling him about her day. Reading him the postcards off the back wall of mission control, one at a time, the beaches and the lighthouses and the city squares that didn't exist anymore. *This one's Lisbon. Nobody's been to Lisbon in fifty years. It's supposed to be yellow.*

She saw a beam across a room, and dust coming down in long gray sheets, and two dirty hands on Alex's face. *Take him.*

And now, in a small room in Heaven, the two of them sat at a little table in a kitchen. Alex had a plate in front of him with an omurice on it, torn down the middle and dark at one edge. Aoi had a spoon. She was eating it. She chewed, very seriously, with her eyes narrowed, while Alex watched her with an expression Val had never seen on his face, an expression like a man watching a scoreboard.

Aoi swallowed.

"It's edible," she announced.

Alex laughed. He put his face in his hand and laughed until his shoulders shook.

Aoi looked up, then, across the room, across the light, and saw Val standing there. And she smiled the crooked smile, and lifted her spoon a little, like a toast.

"Good morning, Kane-san," she said.

Val's throat closed. She made it open.

"Good morning, Aoi," she said.

She saw Seus. Who had died for her and was angry because it mattered so much. Who had lain in a basement for a year and a half with someone holding his hand and never known whose. Who had carried her across a frozen plain and sat up all night by a fire made of a chair and called himself a commander. Who was sitting in the snow at the edge of the shaft right now, crying into his hands, with his eye quiet for the first time in six years.

She saw Akari, Haruto and Nanami, who had never once stopped saying good morning. She saw them in a valley full of steam, looking up at the light, beginning to come apart, slowly, peacefully, with the baby in Nanami's arms going bright at the edges. She saw Nanami's lips moving. Counting heads.

She saw Lantern.

They were in a hangar with its roof painted black, sitting on crates in a loose half circle, the way they had sat for Kuze's briefings. Kuze was at the front, with a cup of coffee, and his hands weren't shaking. He didn't have his card. He looked around at them one by one, slowly, the way a man counts his children in a crowd, and he said each name, out loud, without looking at anything.

"Dasha. Tom. Sae. Gage."

He looked up and saw Val, at the edge of the light.

"Val," said Kuze, and smiled.

Dasha was chewing a ginger candy. She waved at Val with the torque driver, the big one, the one with the red handle.

"Hey, Four! I never finished telling you. The thing nobody tells you about hollows." She pointed the driver at Val. "They're never really empty. That's it. That's the whole thing. You can cut out every scrap of the saint, every receiver, every nerve, and something stays anyway. Little habits in the meat. Dropping the shoulder before a turn. Afraid of heights. You can't get it all out. Nothing's ever really empty." She grinned around the candy. "Not even you, kid. You tried, though. I'll give you that. You tried so hard."

Big Tom was sitting at a table, his own table, with his rice cooker. There was no frost on him anywhere. His arms were bare and brown and ordinary, and he looked smaller without it, and younger, and very tired, and happy. He pushed a rice ball across the table toward the empty chair across from him, the way he always had, without saying anything.

Gage stood a little apart from the others, turning the beads on his wrist. Val saw him, now, the way he truly was: a boy whose whole family had gone up in the light on the night of the Almost, mother and father and two little brothers, out of their apartment window, while he was on a night shift at the yard. A boy whose priest had given him somebody to hate, because hating somebody was easier than being scared of the sky. Who had landed in a burning street anyway.

He looked at her for a long moment across the hangar. He didn't say *I still hate you.* He didn't say anything. He gave her a nod, short and stiff, the way Seus nodded at people he wasn't ready to forgive, and went back to his beads.

And Sae sat on the end of a crate, a meter from everyone, with her knees up and her coat around them and her notebook in her lap.

She looked at Val. She opened her mouth.

"Your turn," Sae said.

Val had never heard her voice. It was low and a little hoarse, as if it hadn't been used in a long time and was surprised to still be there. It was nothing like Val had imagined, and exactly right.

Sae smiled at her. Then she looked back out at the valley that wasn't there, the way she always had, and was quiet.

She saw Palindrome.

He was standing at the head of a long white corridor that smelled of disinfectant, in a facility that wasn't there anymore, and he wasn't alone. There were children all down the corridor behind him, two dozen of them, in gray program smocks, most of them younger than Val: a boy with a bandage on his wrist, two girls holding hands, a girl with her eyes shut who turned her face toward Val anyway, as if she could see straight through the light. Nobody had sewn anything into any of them. Not here. Palindrome walked at the front, and he was fourteen. His face had no scar. It was only a face, a little lopsided, a boy's face, the age he had been when the program ended, before every reversal took its years out of him. Here he had never gotten old. He was walking them out, slowly, in a line, counting heads under his breath, the way someone had once walked a plant full of workers out past a fence and left them standing in the parking lot, unhurt.

He saw Val. He didn't smile the polite smile.

"You asked me why I evacuated the plant," he said. "Nobody else ever asked."

Then he turned back to the children and went on counting, and walked them out of the light.

She saw Maggie.

She saw a hangar at night, in 2082, with an old orange unit in its bay and a dying man leaning on its leg. *Mags. I need you to do something for me. My kid. Valeria. Watch out for her. Not now. Later, when they come for her. Keep an eye on her.* And a girl of seventeen who had been seventeen for almost twenty years already, who had every reason in the world to say no to him, saying yes.

And long before that, she saw why. A battle over the harbor, a lifetime ago, a Fall, and two young pilots flying wing together: a boy of nineteen with a crooked grin, and his wingman, a freckled girl of seventeen in an orange unit. She saw the boy break formation, suddenly, without a word, to chase a kill he wanted, and leave the girl alone in the open. She saw the girl take the hit meant for both of them. She saw the link between Maggie and her unit close around her in that moment like a fist, to keep her alive, and hold her, and never let go again. That was why Maggie had never aged. That was the price of the hit Sam Kane had left her to take alone.

And Maggie had forgiven him. Not because he asked. He never asked. She had just decided to, one day, the way you decide to put down something you've carried too far. And then she had said yes to him, and flown out to the edge of the belt, and waited, and come back.

She saw Pip.

Not the core she had climbed into at nine. Before that. The same hangar, the same night, in the next bay over from Maggie's: an old exo kneeling under the work lights with its capsule standing open and nobody in it, and a voice in the walls, gentle and a little formal, the voice that had asked a dying man every night whether he shouldn't be asleep.

"Hello, Valeria," Pip said, the way he had said it once before, in a capsule, to a nine-year-old with a pencil in her fist.

"Hi, Pip."

"I asked him once if I could meet you. He said he didn't really know you." A pause, the kind a machine doesn't need to take. "So we only met the once. For the link. I read you, at the end, before I went." The work lights hummed. "You spin the pencil. You don't like it when people kneel to you. You like the rolled omelettes. The sweet ones." The voice went quieter. "He always wanted to know that. I found out too late to tell him."

"Why did you go?"

"He'd made someone else. For you. Someone small, who'd ask you things." The voice was gentle about it, the way you're gentle about something you made peace with a long time ago. "There wasn't room for both of us. She needed to grow."

Val was quiet for a while. Then she went back to the thing he had wanted to know, because it seemed like the right thing to give him.

"Yes," she said. "The sweet ones."

"Good," said Pip, pleased, the way a child is pleased to have gotten something right. "Then we've met."

She saw Helen Enoch.

She sat at one end of the Conclave's long table, or what was left of it: a line of ash in the light, with the book gone and the other chairs gone, and only hers still standing, because she had left it before the fire came. A thin woman in a gray cardigan, with Alex's straight eyebrows and her reading glasses pushed up into her hair. Val saw her sign a page at that table, in 2075, with the same pen she signed everything with. She saw her, twelve years later, take her grown son's face in her hands and ask him for one thing, the night before she walked down to the Root on her own feet, to see where she had sent Sora Yukimura. *Look after the Kane girl.*

He had. Badly, and every day.

Helen Enoch looked across the light toward a small kitchen where her son sat watching a woman eat his cooking. She didn't go to him. She sat at the end of a burned table with her hands folded, the way you sit outside a room you aren't sure you're allowed into, and watched.

She saw Abriel, a son going home. Far off, at the very edge of the light, a pale-haired boy was walking away from all the rooms, toward a door she couldn't see, the way you walk toward a house where someone is waiting.

She saw Patrick. On a train that had stopped moving, somewhere out in the light, sitting alone on a bench with his hands on his knees. His grief for his son, held so tightly for so long that it had become a kind of stone. And behind the stone, she could see it, if she looked: a landscape. Mountains, and a valley, and a river, and the light coming down over all of it, the way it does in the evening. A painting he had never painted. There were no people in it.

There was a space in the foreground where people could go.

And then, at the very edge of everything, past Patrick's train, past all the rooms, in a place where the light went thin and dark and cold, she saw a door she didn't recognize. It wasn't one of her rooms. It wasn't one of anyone's she knew.

Someone was waiting there.

### Chapter 75: The First Time

She went through the door at the edge of everything, and it was cold.

Not the cold of the frozen world, or the singing cold that had come out of the door in the Third War. An older cold. The cold of a place where there has never been any air, where the dark isn't the absence of light but a thing of its own, patient and enormous. She stood on stone, at the bottom of a crater so deep its walls went up out of sight. And rising out of the stone all around her, arching overhead, pale and faintly glowing, were ribs. Each one as tall as a tower. They curved up out of the floor of the crater and met far above her head like the frame of a cathedral nobody had finished, and between them was the black sky, with no stars in it, and hanging in the black, small and white and very far away, the Earth.

She knew where she was. She had heard it described on a roof, under a red Moon, by a boy who talked about it the way people talk about someone they love and have never met.

She was on the Moon, inside Sariel's ribs. She was in its grave.

In the middle of the stone floor, in a hollow worn smooth, as if it had been made to fit him before he was there to fit it, a boy lay curled on his side. He was small, and pale-haired, and naked as something just hatched, and he was shivering. His eyes were open. He was looking at the dark with no expression at all, because he didn't have any yet. He didn't have a name. He didn't know there was such a thing as a name.

And kneeling beside him on the stone, holding his hand, was a girl.

She had short hair, cut above the neck. She wore a gray parka, too big for her, with the hood pushed back. Her face was turned away, toward the boy, so that Val couldn't see it, and she was crying, quietly, the way you cry when you've been crying for a very long time and there's nothing left in it but the shape.

"Please," the girl said to the boy. Her voice was rusted, barely there. "Please. Make Valeria happy."

Val stood in the dark and tried to see her face, and couldn't.

"Hello again," said someone beside her.

She turned.

Abriel was sitting on a ledge of rock a little above her, with his legs hanging over the edge, the way he had sat on the roof of the ruins every night for a week. He was in his white flight suit. There was no Halo at his throat. His hair was pale and untidy and his face was exactly the same, and he was smiling at her the way he had smiled the first night, as if she were exactly who he had been waiting for.

"Is that me?" Val said. She pointed, uselessly, at the girl on the stone. The short hair. The parka. It could have been. It could have been her mother, at sixteen. It could have been anyone with short hair who had lost everything.

"I don't know," Abriel said. "I never saw her face. I've tried to remember it, every time around. I can't. I remember her hand. I remember what she asked." He looked down at the stone. "That's the first time."

"The first time what?"

He looked down at the girl and the boy for a long moment before he answered.

"This has all happened before, Val," he said. "Not exactly. Close. The world ends, and you end up here, outside time, at the end of everything. And you find this room." He tilted his head at the girl. "The first time, she was alone. Everyone she knew was gone, and she was alone in Heaven at the end of the world, and the only other thing awake in all of it was a boy on the Moon who didn't have a name yet." He smiled, a little sadly. "So she came to me. She knelt down and held my hand. She was the first thing I ever touched. And she asked me for one thing."

"Make Valeria happy," Val whispered.

"And then the world started over. And I woke up in the dark with her hand still warm in mine, and I waited." He looked up at the roof of the grave, at the pale ribs glowing in the rock. "I waited, and I came down, and I found you, and I tried. And I failed, and the world ended, and you came up here, to Heaven, and you asked it to take everything back. Every time. And Heaven gives you what you want most. So it did." He shrugged. "And every time, I'd told you there was a way to fix it. A sword, a door, one more try. I thought that was helping. You never remember. I always do. That's how I knew you on the roof. That's why I said—"

"This time around," said Val.

"You were beautiful this time around." He said it the same way he had said it on the roof, simply, as a fact. "You let yourself feel things. You cried at the top of the tower. You held on to me in the capsule when it was killing you. You named a constellation after a pencil." He laughed, the startled bright laugh. "That's never happened before. Not once."

"How many times?"

"I stopped counting." He looked down at his hands. "It doesn't matter. It's always the same ending. I always die at the door, and you always break, and you always come here, and you always ask to take it back."

Val looked at him.

She looked at him for a long time, there in the dark of the grave, with the girl crying on the stone below them and the small boy shivering in the hollow. And she saw it.

She had seen it on a ledge above a shaft, in a man in a coat on a ledge, holding a dead man's plan for years because somebody had told him *hold*. She had seen it in a hangar at night, in a man leaning on his unit's leg, building a war out of a promise he had made to two little girls in a burning house. She had seen it in a voice in Navis's walls, waiting twenty-seven years to go back to one morning. A man who had been given one instruction, once, and had built his whole life around keeping it, without ever stopping to ask whether it was still what anyone wanted.

Abriel was the same. He didn't even remember who had asked him. He had held the request through every time around anyway. He had waited on the Moon for it and fallen out of the sky for it and died at the door for it, smiling, over and over, and he had never once asked himself if there was anything else he wanted. It wasn't that he didn't love her. It was that the love had turned into a room he couldn't get out of.

He was like Patrick. He was like her father.

"You did it," Val said.

Abriel looked up. "Did what?"

"Made me happy. On the roof. Every night, for a week." Her voice shook. She let it. "The coffee. The constellations. The Ladle and the Sleeping Dog and the Boy Who Forgot His Umbrella. I sat on the edge of a roof at the end of the world, and I was happy. I didn't know that's what it was. I'd never had it before, so I didn't know what it was called. But it was." She swallowed. "You did it. You made Valeria happy. So you're done."

Abriel stared at her.

For a moment, he looked the way the girl from Unit 00 had looked when Nanami laughed: as if he were seeing a new kind of weather.

"...Did I?" he said.

"Yes."

He laughed. It came out of him startled and wet, and he put his hand over his mouth. Then the laugh went away, and something else came into his face, something unsure, something she had never seen there.

"I don't know what I'm for," he said slowly, "if I'm not for that."

Val thought of a kitchen in a valley full of steam. A girl with her father's face holding a baby very carefully, staring down at it for an hour. *What is she for?*

"You're not for anything," Val said. "You're just Abriel."

He was quiet for a long time.

"Then what do I do?"

"What do you want?"

Abriel opened his mouth, and closed it. She watched him look for it, the way you look in a pocket for something you're sure you put there. Nobody had ever asked him. In all the times around, nobody had asked him once.

"I don't know," he said finally. "I've never seen the Moon from the ground in the daytime. You can, you know, sometimes. It's very pale. Like a fingerprint on glass." He thought. "I've never had ice cream. You told me Haruto talked about it at lunch. On the roof. That he had opinions." He smiled a little. "Friends, maybe. Ones who aren't waiting for me to save them. Ones who don't know anything about any of this."

"Then go do that," said Val. "Go sit in the sun. Eat ice cream. Make friends who aren't me." She held his eyes. "And don't look up. Don't wait for anyone. Don't wait on the Moon."

She climbed down off the ledge.

She crossed the stone floor of the grave in the dark, to the hollow where the small boy lay shivering, and the girl in the gray parka kneeling beside him didn't look up. She let go of the boy's hand as Val came, the way you hand something over at the end of a long shift, and stood, and stepped back into the dark with her face still turned away, and was gone. Val never saw her face. She found she didn't need to.

Val knelt down where she had been.

She took the small cold hand in hers. The boy turned his head on the stone and looked at her with eyes that didn't know anything yet.

"You don't have to make anyone happy," Val told him. "You don't have to wait for anybody. Be happy. That's all. That's the whole thing."

The boy blinked at her.

And something in the grave changed. She felt it before she saw it. Far off, at the rim of the crater, the black began to pale, and a thin line of light came over the edge. Not the colorless light of Heaven. Sunlight. The slow lunar dawn, which comes once a month and takes days to arrive, had chosen this moment to begin. It came down between the great ribs in a long slanting line and fell across the stone floor, and across the boy, and he lifted his face into it and closed his eyes, the way a cat does in a sunny window.

When Val looked up at the ledge, Abriel was standing.

He was looking up at the dawn.

"This never happened," he said. "Not once. Not in all the times."

Val felt it the way you feel ice let go on a river: somewhere far below everything, something that had been turning over and over for longer than she could think about had stopped. It had broken here first. In a grave on the Moon, for the one who had been here at the very start.

"You told me it always ends the same," Val said. "You were right. I couldn't undo it." She looked at the line of daylight on the stone, on the small boy's face. "You were wrong that nothing could change."

Abriel looked down at her, and he smiled, and it was a different smile from any of the ones she knew. It wasn't waiting for anything.

"Can I tell you something?" he said. "The first time around, I'd known you a week. I loved you anyway. I don't know why. I knew how it ended. I chose it every time." He said it simply, the way he had said she was beautiful, as a fact. "I'm not telling you so you'll do anything about it."

"I know," said Val.

He was quiet. Then, carefully, like someone trying out a word in a language he had only just started learning: "If I find you out there. After. Is that all right?"

"You don't need me to say yes," said Val. "That's the whole point."

Abriel looked at her for a long moment. Then he laughed, the startled bright laugh, as if she had shown him a new constellation.

"Good," said Abriel, and he meant it.

He turned and walked away along the ledge, into the line of daylight, toward wherever it came from. He didn't look back. It was the first time she had ever seen him walk toward something without looking back to see if she was still there.

### Chapter 76: If Only

After the grave, Heaven showed her the rooms that had never been.

She didn't go looking for them. She walked out of the Moon's dark into the light again, and they were simply there, all around her, the way the real rooms had been: kept exactly, down to the dust.
