But nobody had remembered these. Nobody could have. They had never happened. They were made of the things people had almost said, and almost done, and wanted to, and didn't. Heaven kept those too. It turned out that Heaven kept everything.

She walked through them one at a time.

In the first room, it was evening at Hakobune Central, under the old curved roof, with the vending machines lit along the wall. A sixteen-year-old boy stood under the clock with two duffel bags at his feet, looking at every car that went past the glass doors. Across the street, at the curb, a small gray car sat with its engine running. And then the car's door opened, and a man in a rumpled coat got out. He stood beside the car for a moment, with one hand on the roof, as if he had forgotten how to let go of it. Then he crossed the street, through the traffic, without looking, and came in through the glass doors, and stopped in front of his son.

"Theseus," Patrick said.

He bent down and picked up one of the duffel bags. Just one. He didn't say anything else. They walked out to the car together, the boy carrying the other bag, neither of them looking at the other, and that was the whole room. That was enough.

In the next room, it was night, in an apartment kitchen near headquarters, with a goldfish turning slowly in a glass bowl in the middle of the table. A girl sat on one side with her hands in her lap. A young man with one arm sat on the other, and he was leaning forward with his hand flat on the table, and he was saying it.

"He's alive. Val. Listen to me. Seus is alive. His vitals spiked, I saw it, and Aoi and I— we've got him. He's not awake. He might not wake. But he's alive, and you didn't kill him, and I'm not going to let you think you did for one more night."

Val watched her own face on the girl at the table. She watched it do something she had never seen it do.

In the next room, there was a balcony at night, and a boy and a girl on it, and the boy was telling the girl what he had found in 04's logs. He told it calmly. He didn't shout. She listened to all of it without walking away, and when he was done she said *okay*, and *thank you for telling me*, and they stood at the railing for a while looking at the city. And the next night there was a dinner. A white tablecloth. Three dishes heated up out of the fridge. And a man in a rumpled coat sitting at the end of the table across from his son, eating, in silence, for a long time. Then he put down his chopsticks.

"This is good," Patrick said.

Seus nearly dropped his bowl.

In the next room, there was a white room going gray at the edges, and a girl holding another girl, and the small one looking up and asking her last question.

"Did you come to save me? Are you a hero?"

"Yes," said Val.

And the light didn't go out.

In this one, she'd said yes right away. In hers, she'd said it in a burned house, to a girl with no name yet, years late. It had still been yes.

She walked through all of them. She went slowly. She looked at every one for as long as she wanted to, and nobody hurried her, and the rooms didn't fade. And as she went, she began to notice something. They connected. Every if-only opened onto another, further back. Every silence had grown out of an earlier silence, the way a crack in a windshield grows out of another crack, all the way back to a single point where something small had struck the glass.

She followed it back. Past the white room. Past the seven days. Past the hangar full of strangers and the principal's office. Back and back, to the last room, the smallest, the first.

It was a doorway.

It was the front door of a house on the east side of Hakobune, a narrow house with a persimmon tree in the yard, on a summer evening, with the cicadas going. The door was open. In the hallway, holding on to the apron of a woman Val knew better than her own face, Fumi, was a girl of six with short hair, half hiding behind the apron, looking out.

At the door stood a thin man in a coat.

He was thirty-four. His face was the face from the talk shows and the posters, but younger and more tired, and less sure of itself, and he was looking at the girl behind the apron as if she were something he had been told about and had never quite believed in. Behind him, a step back, on the path, stood another man, holding a set of car keys, looking at the ground: younger than Val had ever seen him, with no gray in his hair, already standing like someone who sat in front of screens for a living.

Sam knelt down on the doorstep so he was at the girl's eye level. The girl didn't like it. Val remembered that she didn't like it.

"Hi, Valeria," he said.

The girl said nothing.

He reached into his coat and took out a pencil. Scuffed metal, with a logo on the side. He held it out to her, on his open palm, the way you'd hold out food to a bird.

"This is for you," he said. He seemed about to say more. His mouth opened, and Val saw a name start in it, two syllables, and stop. He closed his mouth.

"Say thank you, Val-chan," said Fumi.

"Thank you," said the girl, to the floor, and took the pencil.

Sam smiled. It was a careful smile, as if it might break.

Then, because he couldn't think of anything else, because he wanted to know one thing about her, one real thing, to take with him, he asked.

"What's your favorite food?"

The girl looked at the floor.

She didn't answer. She held the pencil in both hands against her chest and looked at the floor of the hallway, and the cicadas went on outside, and the man knelt on the doorstep and waited. Behind him, on the path, Patrick shifted his weight and looked at his watch, and then away from it, ashamed.

"That's okay," Sam said finally, gently. "Next time."

He stood up. He said goodbye to Fumi. He went down the path, and Patrick opened the car door for him, and the car drove away. And the girl went on standing in the hallway with the pencil against her chest.

Val knew the rest. She had lived it. For months afterward, a girl of six had gone to the mailbox every day, after school, before anything else. Every day. And it held bills, and flyers, and nothing else, because somewhere in a little office down the hall from mission control there was a box filling up, month by month, with postcards stamped HELD. And one day, still six, the girl had stopped checking. She had decided, somewhere before she could remember deciding anything, that when you asked for something, nothing came back. That questions didn't get answered. So why answer them?

That was the first one. The first question nobody answered. Every silence in her life had grown out of this one: the seven days, the seven years, the mirror she had made of 04, the head on the table, the port, the white room. All of it, from a doorway on a summer evening, and a man who said *next time*, and a girl who looked at the floor.

And then the room showed her the other way.

The same doorway. The same cicadas. The same man kneeling on the step with the pencil on his palm. *What's your favorite food?* And this time, the girl behind the apron looked up.

"Omelettes," she said.

It came out small and fast, the way children say things they've decided to say before they can stop themselves.

Sam laughed. It burst out of him, surprised and delighted, the laugh of a man who has been handed something he didn't know he was allowed to have.

"Me too," he said. "The rolled ones? Sweet?"

The girl nodded, very seriously.

"Next week," said Sam, "I'll bring some."

And he did. Val could see it, down the hallway of that room, through a door into another and another: the next week, and the one after, a thin man at the door with a bento box, getting a little less careful every time, a girl getting a little less hidden behind the apron, a mailbox that wasn't empty. A whole life, unspooling from one word.

Val stood in the last room and looked at all of it.

None of it had happened. Not one room. Patrick had never gotten out of the car. Alex had never told her. The dinner had never been eaten. The light had gone out in the white room before she could answer. The girl in the doorway had looked at the floor.

And every one of them had wanted to. That was what Heaven was showing her. Every one of them had had the answer inside them the whole time, ready, at the edge of their mouths. Patrick with his hand on the car roof. Alex leaning forward at the table. Aoi with her box. Her father on the doorstep with a name starting in his mouth. Herself, at six, at nine, at sixteen. They had all wanted to say it. They had just been afraid. Like her. Sometimes the answer came late. Sometimes it never came at all.

It didn't happen. It could have. And somehow, standing there, that was enough.

Val smiled.

It wasn't a laugh, surprised out of her. It wasn't the crooked thing that had happened at the corner of her mouth on the hangar steps when Sae wrote *same thing*. It was a smile she chose. It came up slowly, from somewhere very far down, and stayed, and she let it stay. She stood in the doorway of a house that didn't exist anymore, in front of a six-year-old girl who had looked at the floor, and smiled at her, the way you'd smile at someone you were finally going to forgive.

She could have stayed. Heaven kept everything, and it would have kept her too: in a doorway on a summer evening, with a man laughing on the step and a whole life unspooling down the hall. Nobody would have stopped her.

She turned around and walked on.

They were waiting for her past the last room.

Not in a room. On nothing, in the light, the way people wait along a road for someone they know is coming. A long line of them, on either side, all the way down, as far as the burning house at the center of everything. She didn't know how long they had been standing there. She thought, from their faces, that it had been a while.

Nanami was first. Of course she was. She had Hinata on her hip, and the baby was asleep, and Nanami's face did the thing it had done every morning for a year in the doorway of Class 2-A, as if she had already decided to be glad, whatever happened.

"Good morning, Val," she said.

"Good morning," Val said.

Nanami stepped aside.

Akari had her arms folded and her chin up, the way she stood when she was trying not to be the first to look away. "Good morning," she said, gruffly, and then looked away first, and stepped aside.

Haruto had his camera up, and lowered it, and said it without it. Kōta said it out of breath, as if he had run all the way here, and he had color in his cheeks, and nothing taped to them, and no tank hissing behind him.

Tom said it with a rice ball in his big hand, held out, the way he always had. There was no frost on him anywhere.

Sae said it out loud.

Val had never heard her voice. It was low, and a little hoarse, as if it had been folded up somewhere for a long time and only just taken out. "Good morning," said Sae, and then looked faintly surprised at herself, and almost smiled.

Kuze said it without looking at a card. Gage said it as if it cost him something, and turned a bead in his fingers, and paid it anyway. Dasha said it with a ginger candy in her cheek. Aoi said it in her comms voice, warm and a little rough, with a pen stuck through her hair, the way she had said it over the speakers every morning when Val was fifteen, before Val knew she was a person and not a voice.

Alex said it with both his arms. He didn't seem to know what to do with the second one. He put it in his pocket.

Maggie winked. Palindrome, fourteen, said it gravely, with a line of children behind him who all said it too, raggedly, not at the same time. Abriel said it smiling, with frost on his shoulders. A woman with flour on her hands said it, and Val's chest went tight, because it was Fumi. An old woman in a cardigan said it, with her reading glasses on a chain, and as she said it she looked past Val, down the line, toward the end of it, as if there were someone there she had been looking for for a long time.

"Good morning," they said. One after another. All the way down.

Val said it back. Every time. To every one of them.

Each of them, when she had said it, stepped aside.

And the last of them stepped aside, and the line opened onto the burning house, and in front of it, a woman with short hair and a pencil.

### Chapter 77: Sora

At the center of Heaven, the house was still burning.

It stood where all the rooms met, at the foot of the white stair: a small house by the harbor, two stories, with a laundry pole on the balcony, burning the way it had been burning since a day in 2062. The rage was inside it. Val could see it through the windows, a great dark shape sitting down in the fire, the way a tired man sits down in his own kitchen. And somewhere in there with it, deeper in, small, she could feel something else.

At the edge of the burning house, a woman was waiting.

Her hair was cut short above the neck, the way Val's was. Val knew her. She knew her from a cracked print in a plastic sleeve that she had carried from the nanny's house to Patrick's apartment to her own, and had never once been able to take out of the sleeve. And she knew her from a girl in a white unit who had worn that face every day for months, in a classroom, in a hangar, without either of them knowing whose it was.

The girl was here too. Yuki stood a little behind Sora, a little to one side, the way a child stands behind an apron, with her hands folded in front of her and her face, Sora's face, turned toward Val. She was wearing a lab coat with the sleeves pushed up, and there was ink on her fingers, and in her hand, turning over and under through her fingers the way you turn something when you've been waiting a long time, was a pencil.

Scuffed metal. A worn logo on its side.

Val looked down at her own hand.

She hadn't taken it out. She didn't remember taking it out. But it was there, in her fingers, the way it had always been there, in a principal's office, in a classroom, in a frozen desk. The same pencil. Exactly the same, down to the scratches. And the logo on its side, worn half away by her own thumb, was one she had walked past a hundred times without looking at, on a plaque behind a glass wall in the warp-drive wing.

*Space Transit Research Division.*

"Hi, Valeria," said her mother.

Val couldn't say anything at all.

Sora Yukimura smiled at her. It was a lopsided smile, a little shy, and Val had never seen it before, and she knew it from the inside of her own face.

"You kept my pencil," Sora said.

"I didn't know it was yours." It came out a whisper. "Nobody told me. He just gave it to me. At the door."

"I know. I watched." Sora held up her own pencil, the memory of it, and turned it so the end caught the firelight. "It's a good pencil. I stole it from the supply room my first week in the division. Look." There were small dents in the metal at the end, close together, in two neat rows. "I chewed it when I was thinking. Terrible habit. Your father used to take it away from me in meetings."

Val looked at the end of her own pencil.

The dents were there. Small, close together, in two rows. She had seen them every day of her life. She had run her thumb over them in every big moment, in the principal's office and the classroom and the shelter steps. She had thought they were scratches.

"I thought those were scratches," Val said.

Her mother laughed. And Val, hearing it, had the strangest feeling: that she had heard it before, somewhere, from the inside. Maybe in a dream. Maybe in herself, once, by a field, under a heat lamp, when a girl asked her if she had just made something up.

"You spin it," Sora said. "Over and under. I used to do that."

"I know," Val said. "I mean, I didn't know. I just do it."

"I know," said her mother. "I watched that too."

Sora Yukimura had worked on the door, years before anyone called it that. She told Val about it standing there at the edge of the fire, simply, the way you'd tell someone what you did for a living. She had been a physicist in the Space Transit Research Division, on the project everyone at headquarters called the warp drives. She had believed they were for the stars. She had believed it right up until the afternoon in 2075 when she sat down in the chair in the test chamber for Test 2075-11, for a routine calibration, with a two-year-old at home with a nanny and a husband on patrol over the harbor. Somebody far above Umbral had decided that the door needed a mind to go through it first, to see what was on the other side. Somebody far above Umbral had decided it should be hers. A woman named Helen Enoch had signed the approval at the bottom of the page.

Her mind had gone through the door that afternoon, and it had never come back. It had been here ever since. Outside time. Watching.

"They were never engines," Sora said. "The drives. We built them around the Root, and they leaned on the door, like a crack leans on a window. They open one way." She turned the pencil over. "Nothing comes back through a door like that."

"What is this place?"

"Thought. Memory. Everything anyone ever kept." She said it the way she might have explained a result at a meeting, a little apologetically, as if it were obvious once you saw it. "There's no physics here. It does what you want, if you want hard enough. It shows you what happened, and it shows you what you wished had happened, and it can't tell the difference. Most people can't either." She tapped the dents at the end of the pencil. "The real ones always have something wrong with them. That's how you tell."

"I saw all of it," Sora said. "Everything. You at the nanny's. You at nine, with your head on the desk. The seven days. The port." Her face did something. "The white room. I saw all of it, and I couldn't do anything. That's what it's like, in here. You can see everything out there. You can't touch any of it." She looked at Val. "Except now. Now you're here."

She held out her hand.

"Do you want to see him?" she asked. "The way I saw him?"

Val took her mother's hand.

Through her mother's eyes, Val finally saw her father.

She saw him at twenty-six, in the canteen at headquarters, at the end of a long table, alone, with his chest full of medals and his tray full of nothing he wanted, the most famous pilot in the world eating by himself. She saw a woman with short hair sit down across from him without asking, because it was the only seat left, and start talking about the stars. She saw him listen. She saw him meet Sora and change, slowly, over months, the way ice changes in spring: become someone gentle, someone who laughed at himself, someone who held a baby on his chest on a couch at two in the morning and hummed to her, badly. Someone afraid of being alone, for the first time in his life, because now he had something to lose.

She saw him lose her, and drown.

She saw him come home from patrol to a phone call. She saw him sit on the floor of a small apartment by the harbor with his back against the door for a whole night. She saw him look at a two-year-old asleep in a crib and not be able to go in. She saw him hand her to a nanny. She saw him go back to the hangar, and climb into Pip, and stay there.

She saw him find out, years later, from a file he wasn't supposed to see, that the test had been arranged. She saw what that did to him. She saw him build the rage and the plan, in a hangar at night, behind Pip's back, a room in the core with the door locked from the inside, and make his daughter its instrument. The one piece the whole thing needed.

And then she saw him try to change again.

She saw him in the hangar at night, the last year, sicker every week, sitting in Pip's capsule with the walls dark. Building something else. Not the rage. Something small, beside it, in another corner of the core. Not himself; he was careful about that. A new nature. A child, bright and too loud and curious about everything, shaped to whatever he thought his daughter was like, and whatever he thought she would need. He started it off with every question he had ever wanted to ask her and never had. *Do you have a name for me? Why is the sky blue? Why is your hair like that? Do you have a mom? What's your favorite food? Did I do something wrong?* Then he let it grow its own. He built it in, piece by piece, night after night, for a girl he didn't know, so that when she sat in this capsule someday, something in it would be glad she was there. Something in it would ask. Pip watched him do it, and understood what it meant, and didn't say anything.

The voice that asked her for a name on the first day had never been her father. It was what her father thought she would need. And then it had become itself.

He ran out of time.

Val stood at the edge of the burning house, holding her mother's hand, and looked at her father the way her mother had looked at him: all of him, all at once, the medals and the floor of the apartment and the rage and the locked room and the small bright voice in the corner, asking.

He had not been good. He had not been evil. He had tried.

Before he came, her mother let go of Val's hand and turned to the girl behind her.

Yuki looked back at her. Two of the same face, in the firelight, one of them a little older, one of them a little more careful with it, as if it were something borrowed that she was afraid of breaking.

"You don't have to wear my face anymore," Sora said.

Yuki didn't answer. She lifted one hand and touched her own cheek, lightly, the way you touch a bandage to see if it's still there. Then she lowered her hand, and stayed exactly where she was, at Sora's side, and nobody asked her to move.

"He's coming," said her mother softly, to Val. "He's been waiting a long time to talk to you." She took Val's hand again and squeezed it. "Be patient with him. He's very bad at it."

### Chapter 78: Sam

He came out of the fire to meet her.

Not the Beast: Sam. Thirty-seven, gaunt, in a flight suit with the sleeves rolled up, standing in front of the burning house as if he had been waiting there for a very long time. He had the crooked grin from the posters, but it wasn't on his face now; it was somewhere behind it, waiting, the way the rage had waited at the bottom of the core. His face now was only tired, and afraid, and looking at her.

He stopped a few steps away. He didn't come closer. He looked at her the way he had looked at a six-year-old behind an apron.

"Valeria," he said.

She didn't say anything. She didn't know how to start, and neither did he, and they stood there in front of the fire, two people who were very bad at it.

Then he told her everything.

He told it standing up, with his hands at his sides, the way you'd give a statement. He didn't sit down. He didn't look away from her. She thought he might never have told anyone all of it, all at once, in order, and she thought that maybe he had been practicing it here, outside time, for years, the way Alex had practiced *you're welcome*.

"It was my house," he said, and tipped his head at the fire behind him. "That one. In the harbor district. I was seventeen. I was walking home from school, and the sirens went, and I started running, because my sisters were home. They were six and four. My mother was at work. And I came around the corner and a giant was standing in our street." He looked at the house. "It wasn't attacking anything. I know that now. It was just walking, the way they walk. Going home. And our house was in the way."

"There was an exo in the street. A second-generation unit, torn open. The pilot was lying half out of it, dying. And the core was still running." His jaw worked. "I pushed him out. With my hands. He was holding on to the seat, and I pushed him out of his own capsule, and I climbed in. I didn't know anything about how it worked. I didn't know about assimilation, or natures, or any of it. I just wanted to hit something." A breath. "His name was Penemue, later. Before that, he had another name, and a little girl called Mio, who called his exo *Pipstick* because she couldn't say *pilot* or *ship*. I flew under that name for twenty years. I never once asked where it came from."

"The rage," Sam said. "You've felt it. You know. It was the best thing I'd ever felt. It was the only thing that held me up. Everything else, the funerals, my mother, the medals, the talk shows, all of it was just noise around it. And the people above Umbral loved it. They have a book, Valeria. It said someone like me would come. Somebody who'd lost everything, climbing into a dead man's machine. They didn't make me. They didn't have to. They just watched me climb in, and recognized me, and made sure I never climbed out." He looked down. "When I finally became a problem, they started putting something in my food. Slowly. Over years. By the time I knew, it was the cancer, and there was nothing to do about it."

"And Sora." He glanced at her mother, standing at the edge of the light, and his whole face changed, softened, the way a cold room softens when someone opens a door. "She sat down at my table because it was the only seat. And she talked about the stars for an hour, and I didn't understand a word, and I didn't want her to stop. She made me afraid of being alone. Do you understand? Nobody had ever done that. I'd been alone since I was seventeen and it had never once scared me."

"Then I lost her," he said, "and I drowned. And much later, I found out the test that killed her had been arranged. And I drowned worse."

He was quiet for a moment.

"I came to see you twice," he said. "Did you know that? You might not remember."

"The doorway," said Val. "The pencil."

He nodded, slowly. "The first time, I brought you her pencil, from her desk at the door project. I'd taken it the day after. I'd been carrying it around for four years. I was going to tell you whose it was. I had it all planned. *This was your mother's. She chewed the end when she was thinking.*" He shook his head. "I couldn't say her name. I got to the doorstep and I opened my mouth and I couldn't say it out loud. So I just gave it to you. And you said thank you. And then I asked you what your favorite food was, and you looked at the floor."

He laughed a little, without much in it.

"And I said *next time*. And I went home and sat in the car for an hour and Patrick didn't say anything." He made himself look at her. "You were six. Six-year-olds look at the floor. I was the grown-up. I heard you not answer and decided it meant no."

Val had carried that doorway her whole life as the place where she had failed. It had never been her silence. It had been what he did with it.

"The second time," he said, "you were eight. You had a fever. A bad one. Fumi called the hangar, I don't know how she got the number. I came in the middle of the night. You were asleep. You slept the whole time, and I sat beside your bed, and put a cloth on your head, and changed it when it got warm. All night. And when it got light, and your fever broke, I left. Before you woke up." He looked at her. "Because I didn't know what I'd say. Because if you woke up and looked at me the way you looked at the floor, I didn't think I could stand it."

Val didn't move.

"The other times," Sam said. "There were other times. I'd stand across the street from your school, at the gate, when you came out. I'd watch you walk to the nanny's car. You always walked with your head down. You always had that pencil." He was very quiet now. "I never crossed. Not once. It was a two-lane street, Valeria. I'd fought forty giants with my own hands, and I couldn't cross a two-lane street. Because if I loved you, I could lose you. I'd already lost everyone. I was a coward. That's the whole of it."

"And I wrote you," he said. "Every month. Postcards, mostly. Letters, when I had more to say, which wasn't often, because I didn't know what to say. A lighthouse. A harbor. The ocean, from the Morningstar, once, at dawn." He swallowed. "You never wrote back. I thought you didn't want to. I told Pip you were busy. You were nine." He shook his head. "I never knew they were held. I didn't find that out until I was in here."

"And the facility," he said. He didn't look away from her. That was the hardest part to watch: that he made himself not look away. "Twenty-six kids. They told me to make sure nobody testified, and I did it. I went through that building room by room. I missed one in a tank in the basement. One reversed my shot." His hands stayed at his sides. "I told myself they'd never have been people again, after what was done to them. That I was letting them go. I was lying. I knew it then."

"The plan," he said. "You've seen it. You were inside it. I built a war on God because I wanted someone to blame for my sisters and for Sora, and I couldn't find anyone big enough, so I went looking for the biggest thing there was. And I built it around you. My own kid." His voice broke for the first time. "And then I changed my mind. Too late. Sick, with a year left. I'd built it too well. I couldn't reach it without breaking the core, and you'd need the core. And I couldn't tell anyone, because every room had their ears in it. So I made something else, next to it. Something small. I didn't put me in there. I'd done enough. I tried to make what you'd need." He breathed. "I didn't know it would go as far as it did. I didn't know anything. I just hoped she'd grow big enough to sit on the rest of it. That's the last thing I did. I hoped. I'd never done that before either."

"The night I died," Sam said, "the sky was burning over the harbor. A Fall. I was in Pip. I knew I wasn't coming back from that one; I was too sick. And do you know what I thought about? At the very end?" He almost smiled. "Not Sora. Not the plan. Not the Conclave. I thought: I never found out what she likes to eat."

He stood in front of her with the fire behind him and his hands at his sides.

"I'm sorry," he said. "I'm so sorry, Valeria."

Val looked at him. She looked at him for a long time.

She thought she would feel something enormous. Rage, maybe, the old rage, coming up through the floor. Or the other thing, the thing that had come up out of her in the white room, all at once, like a dam breaking. Neither came. What came was quieter and harder to name. It was the feeling of looking at a person, finally, all of a person, at once, with nothing between you and them. The doorway and the fever. The postcards and the facility. All of it.

"You asked me something," Sam said. His voice was very careful, as if it might break. "In there, in the core, I couldn't ask you anything. I could only listen. But I've wanted to ask you something since I got here. Since the first postcard." He swallowed. "Did any of it ever reach you?"

Val thought about a mailbox with nothing in it. About a box under four floors of rubble, and a box in the drawer of a desk in an inn on a hill, burning. About eighty-four postcards she would never read.

And she thought about a voice in a hangar on the first day, too loud and too bright, asking her for a name. A voice that wanted to know why the sky was blue, and why her hair was short, and whether she had a mom, and what her favorite food was. A voice that had been glad she was there. A child in a white room with her arms around Val's neck. A girl in a kitchen in a valley who never stopped asking.

"It did," Val said. "Through 04. Through April."

Sam closed his eyes.

He stood there with his eyes closed and his hands at his sides, and something went out of his shoulders, something he had been holding up for a very long time, and he let it go.

She had waited her whole life to meet her father. Here he was.

### Chapter 79: Out of the Fire

The April she had named was inside the fire.

Val could feel her in there. Not the way you feel a thing through a link: the way you feel someone in the next room when you've known them all your life. Small. Frightened. Deep in the burning house, in the middle of it, held fast by twenty years of her father's rage, the way a child is held by someone who won't let go of her hand in a crowd. The child from the white room. The one who had asked six questions, and then waited seven years, and then asked a seventh. She had never gone anywhere. When the light went out in the white room, she had slipped down into the core, into the dark, and the rage had closed over her, and held her, and she had been there ever since, under everything, waiting for someone to come.

And Val was not the only one who felt her.

Someone came walking out of the rooms of memory, out of the light, out of a field.

She came out of a row of seedlings under heat lamps, with the orange light behind her and frost still glittering on her shoulders. But her shoulders weren't fading. Her hands weren't clear. She was whole, solid, exactly as she had been on the first morning at Nanami's table, with her short hair and her face made out of two faces. The second April. Her body had come apart in a field in Kasumi. Her mind had not. It had gone back through the door, the way it had come out of it, and it had been here, in Heaven, in the rooms of the people who remembered her. In Nanami's kitchen. In Haruto's camera. In a field, beside a seedling with two leaves.

She walked past Val without stopping. She looked at Val as she went past and gave her the small crooked smile, the one that didn't know how to stay on her face.

"Good morning, Valeria Kane," she said.

And she walked straight into the burning house without hesitating.

Val started after her. Her father's hand stopped her: not grabbing, just resting on her arm, very light.

"Wait," Sam said. "Let me."

He looked at the house. At the shape in the fire, sitting in its kitchen, with its back to them. It was the size of the house. It was the shape of a man, sitting, with its head down. It had his face. Val could see that now, through the flames: a face like the posters, like the talk shows, like a boy of seventeen in a flight suit two sizes too big, squinting past a camera at something still burning behind him.

Sam looked at the thing in the fire wearing his face.

And he simply stopped holding on.

He didn't fight it. He didn't go in. He stood at the edge of the fire with his hands at his sides and let go of it, the way you let go of a rope you've been holding so long your hands have forgotten they're holding anything. Val felt it go. Everything that had held the rage together for twenty years, every night in the hangar, every medal, every postcard with no answer, every word he never said: he let it all go at once. And the shape in the fire lifted its head, and looked at him, and seemed, for a moment, very tired, and very young.

Then it lay down in the flames, and the fire guttered.

It sank. It went down from a roar to a crackle to a glow, the way a fire goes down at the end of a long night, and in the middle of the burning house, in what had been a kitchen in the harbor district in 2062, in the last of the light, the Aprils found each other.

The second April was kneeling on the floor, among the embers, holding someone. A child, nine, maybe ten, with short hair and bare feet and a face that wouldn't settle, curled up small in her arms, blinking, as if she had just woken from a very long sleep.

"Hey," said the second April to the first. "Hey. You're April, right?"

The child looked up at her.

"I'm April," she said. "Are you?"

"Yes," said the second April. "Valeria Kane told me. In a field."

And then something came out of Val.

She felt it go. It had been with her since the burned house in 04's core, since *Who are you?* and *I'm your hero*. It had held on to her through the Proxy and the stair and the fire, small and frightened, and she had held on back. Now it let go of her, gently, the way a seedling lets go of the seed, and went across the embers to the two girls in the middle of the floor. It wasn't a girl yet. It was barely anything. A small light, new, with nothing in it but two questions. The third April. The one that had grown up out of the black floor after the rage burned everything else away.

It went to them, and the other two opened their arms, and it went in between them.

The child who had asked seven questions. The girl who had asked a hundred. And the small new thing, who had only asked two, *Who are you?* and *Who am I?*, and had gotten an answer to both.

They held each other in the embers. The one Val had named in a white room. The one Heaven had built out of frost because Val wanted her back so badly, with April's face and none of her memories. And the one asleep in 04's core all along, starting over from nothing. Curiosity, and the want to live, and whatever came next. They had always been the same girl, the way a river is the same river.

For the first time, 04 was whole.

Val walked into the burning house.

The embers didn't burn her. She crossed the floor of a kitchen in the harbor district where two little girls had been waiting for their brother to come home from school, and the ash was soft under her feet, and the light was going down. In the middle of the floor, there was only one girl now. Short hair. Bare feet. A face that had finally settled into something Val could look at straight on. It was a little like her father's, and a little like her own, and a little like her mother's, and most of all it was nobody's but April's.

April looked up at her.

"Hey," said April. "You're Val, right?"

It was the first thing she had ever said. In a hangar full of strangers, too loud and too bright, a long time ago. *Hey, you're Val, right? How cool is that?* And a nine-year-old in a capsule had said nothing at all.

"Yeah," Val said. "I'm Val."

She held out her hand. April took it.

At seventeen, Sam had run into that house and climbed into a dead man's exo. His daughter walked out of it holding a hand.

---

## Arc 19: The Rewrite

> *And he that sat upon the throne said, Behold, I make all things new.*
>
> — Revelation 21:5

### Chapter 80: The Rewrite

She refused it.

The Rite was still pulling. She could feel it all around her, in every direction, the whole weight of it: every mind on Earth rising through the door, every seal giving way at once, coming apart, pouring up into the light to become one mind. Nanami counting heads in a valley full of steam. Akari with her hand on her brother's shoulder. Kōta. Hinata, in her mother's arms, going bright at the edges. And all of them, every one, was offering her the same thing, in all their voices at once, warm and close and gentle as a hand on the back of the neck.

*Stay. Stay with us. You'll never be alone again. No more loneliness. No more pain. No more separate people. Just us. Forever.*

She could see where it went. Past the rooms, past the perfect evenings and the kitchens and the long tables, every person she had just seen whole was running into the next one, the way a pilot runs when the seal goes: warm, soft, with no edges left, into one bright sea. It wasn't death. Nobody in it was dead. Nobody in it was anybody. It didn't think. It didn't want anything. It would never have to say a word to anyone again, because there would be no one else to say it to. It just was.

She had seen every one of them for exactly who they were, all the way down, in the last few minutes. The Conclave had given her that, without meaning to. It still wasn't the same as being with them.

Val Kane had spent her whole life refusing connection. She had folded herself small and put herself away. She had answered nothing and asked nothing and kept her head on the table year after year so that nobody could reach her and nothing could be taken. And now she was being offered the ultimate connection, the end of every wall, the one thing that would mean she never had to be alone again.

She said no.

Not to protect herself, this time. Not because closeness was dangerous, or because wanting things killed people, or because it was safer to be nobody.

She said no because she had finally learned what it was worth to be close to people and still be separate from them. Because Sae had sat a meter away on the hangar steps, and it had mattered that it was a meter, and that it was Sae. Because Tom had pushed a rice ball across a table, and it had mattered that there was a table, and someone on the other side of it. Because Nanami had said good morning, every day, to someone who never said it back, and it had mattered, it had mattered more than anything, that Val was someone else. Someone who could say it back.

"You can't say good morning to someone," Val said, to the light, to all of them, "if they're you."

Everything here was kept. Nothing here had ever begun.

So she rewrote the world instead.

She didn't know how. She did it anyway, the way she had once pulled a core out of a corrupted mass, past every limit, without a plan. She stood in the middle of a house that had stopped burning, holding April's hand, and she reached out through Heaven, through all the rooms, through every mind that had ever slipped outside time, for the whole frozen broken world.

Heaven listened to her. She wondered about that, later, the way you wonder about luck: why her. Far below everything, at the bottom of a shaft under a dead city, the Root had wanted the door for a million years and never once gotten through, and never once gotten an answer. When she reached, it felt her reach, and it gave her its dream, the way a sword turns.

What came into her free hand was a book.

Heaven made whatever people believed in. It had made a white room once, and a girl out of frost, and a hundred rooms of if-only. And people believed in stories. They believed a world could be written down. So Heaven gave her one to write in: a plain book with a soft cover, like a school notebook, and every page in it was blank.

She thought of the other book, open on a long dark table, every page of it full, every margin scheduled in pencil all the way to the end. This one had nothing in it at all.

She opened it on her knee. April took hold of her sleeve instead of her hand, and Val took her mother's pencil out of her pocket.

Every other time, she had come this far and tried to take it back. Heaven was made of memory, and memory could only give back what had been, so the world had come back the same every time, and everything had happened again. This time she didn't take anything back. She gave something instead.

Heaven had only ever been asked to give things back. Nobody had ever handed it something.

She didn't write Tom back, or Sae, or Aoi. She couldn't. She wrote one thing, on the first page, in small careful letters, and let the rest grow from it, the way a seedling grows.

*No silence is the last word.*

She left every other page blank. No schedule. No loop. No one holding anything for anyone.

Then she laid the pencil in the fold of the book, the way you mark your place, and let go of it. It was the last thing she had from before. It could stay before.

Her first free act had ended the world. Her last gave it back.

But a door that big couldn't be closed from the outside. It had to be held shut from in here.

She felt it as soon as she'd done it. The new world was forming, out past the edge of the light, she could feel it coming together the way you feel the morning coming before you open your eyes. But the door was still open behind it. It would always be open, unless someone closed it. Shut from out there, it would only be a door with Heaven leaning on it, the way Heaven had leaned on Hakobune for fifty years: the Falls, the frost, the wanting coming through every crack. Shut from in here, there would be nothing on the other side pushing.

And someone had to keep the keys. Down in the light behind them, inside Unit 12 where it had come to rest, the two swords were still burning, one steady and one flickering. As long as they were anywhere in the world, somebody would find a want big enough to turn them.

Someone had to stay outside time and close the door from in here, and take everything that didn't belong in the new world with them when they did: the two swords, the rage, the exos and the giants and whatever had lived inside them. The saints could sleep. Nobody would come for their swords again. Someone just had to stay behind and hold it shut.

Val understood. She let go of April's hand.

She stood up to go.

Her mother put a hand on her shoulder.

Her father stepped in front of her.

And last of all, out of the light, from a train that had stopped for good somewhere out at the edge of everything, Patrick came walking. He came slowly, the way a man his age walks anywhere, with his coat buttoned and his hands empty, and he stood beside them.

"You've done enough," Patrick said.

Sam turned and looked at him. The man who had held his crumbs for twenty years. Who had put his brushes in a closet because Sam came to his door with a look on his face. Who had been called Kane's dog in the corridors for two decades and never once complained. Who had carried a sealed folder in his inside pocket until it nearly killed everyone, because Sam had said *hold*, and never said anything else.

"Patrick," Sam said. "I'm sorry. For all of it. For never telling you."

"I didn't need an apology, Sam," Patrick said. "You tried to stop yourself, and I never even looked up. I needed you to tell me." He almost smiled. "You just did."

Sam looked at him for a long moment. Then he laughed, a short surprised breath of a laugh, and put his hand on Patrick's shoulder, and Patrick let it stay there.

They had a little time while the new world formed.

The four of them sat at the edge of the light, on nothing, together, like people waiting on a bench for a train. April sat at Val's feet with her back against Val's knees, the way she had sat on the bank of the creek, and looked out at the light, and asked what was out there. The book lay closed on the bench between them. Sora had taken the pencil out of it, and turned it over once, and found her own tooth marks, and smiled, and kept it. With her other hand she held Val's. She didn't say much. She held it as if she were learning the shape of it, and every so often she squeezed it, as if to check it was still there. Patrick sat a little apart, at the end, with his hands on his knees, looking out at the light the way he used to look at a landscape: as if he were already working out how to paint it. With people in it, this time.

After a while, without looking away from the light, Patrick said, "Valeria. Have you eaten?"

Val laughed. She couldn't help it.

"No," she said.

Sam turned his head and stared at him. "You asked her that? Every day?"

"Every day," said Patrick.

"For seven years?"

"Every quiet day."

"Did she ever say yes?"

Patrick considered it.

"Never once," he said.

Sora laughed so hard she had to let go of Val's hand.

And then, when it was quiet again, Sam turned to his daughter. He looked at her for a long moment, the way he had looked at her from a doorstep on a summer evening, with something in his hands he didn't know how to give.

He asked her the fifth day's question. It had always been his question.

"What's your favorite food?"

"Omelettes," Val said.

Sam laughed. Really laughed, all the way, with his head back, the crooked grin from the posters finally all the way out on his face where it belonged. It sounded exactly the way she had always imagined it would. She had imagined it a lot, it turned out. She hadn't known that until she heard it.

"Me too," he said. "The rolled ones. Sweet."

"He's lying," said Sora. "He likes them burnt."

"I like them burnt," Sam admitted.

Val reached into the pocket of her flight suit and took out Haruto's camera.

She turned it on. She scrolled back, past years of footage, past Hinata and the harvest and the walk out of the city, to a school roof in the spring. She held the little screen out so April could see it. The volleyball went up. The cloud moved across the blue.

"That's the sky," Val said. "I promised I'd show you."

April looked at it for a long time, very seriously, with her chin on Val's knee.

"It's small," she said.

"It's bigger in real life."

"How much bigger?"

"All of it," said Val. "Everywhere you look. You can't see the edges."

April looked up at her.

"Show me," she said.

The new world was ready. Val could feel it, out past the edge of the light, waiting, the way a morning waits on the other side of a curtain. She stood up. She put the camera back in her pocket. Then she reached up and unclasped the Halo from her throat, and set it down on the bench, with its trigger beside it. Nobody had to hold it anymore. Not even her.

She held out her hand, and April took it, and stood.

She looked at the three of them, on their bench at the edge of everything. Her mother, with her short hair and her pencil. Her father, with his crooked grin. Patrick, with his hands on his knees and a landscape in his eyes.

"Will I remember?" she asked.

"Some of it," said her mother. "The way you remember a dream."

"Is that enough?"

Her father smiled at her.

"Ask me later," he said.

Then Val walked on, out of the light, holding April's hand.

The three of them stayed behind, on the inside, with the swords, and closed the door, and watched her go. Sora held up the pencil, like a toast. Sam waved with his whole arm, the way a man waves at a school gate when he's arrived embarrassingly early. And Patrick, at the end of the bench, lifted one hand, in a small, stiff wave.

* * *

The door closed behind her without a sound.

When she looked down, her hand was empty. April had gone on ahead, the way a little kid runs ahead on a path once she can see where it goes. Val didn't call after her.

She was standing in a field. She knew it: the rows of dark earth at the edge of Kasumi, the heat lamps on their poles, the frost lying over everything, glittering, the way it had settled the night a girl came apart into the rows she had planted. Except there was no cold here, and the lamps weren't needed, and they burned anyway. At the end of the row, the seedling had four leaves.

Out past the edge of the field, the new world was beginning. She could feel it the way you feel a morning through a curtain.

She sat down at the end of the row, beside the seedling, and didn't go.

She had given it to them. That was the whole thing. You didn't hand someone a world and then take a place in it. So she sat with her knees up and her arms around them, and looked at the light coming up over everything, and for the first time she could remember she didn't have to do anything next. She let herself rest. It was beautiful. She hadn't known it would be.

Something came down out of the light, more falling than flying, and landed in the field with a groan of old metal.

Lizzie was a wreck. One arm, one leg, the paint mostly gone. She came down onto her one knee in the rows, carefully, so as not to crush the seedling, and the capsule popped, and Maggie leaned out of it and looked down at her.

"There you are," Maggie said. "Why aren't you with the ones you saved?"

"That part wasn't for me."

"Says who?"

Val opened her mouth, and found she didn't have an answer.

"You can't give somebody a world," Maggie said, "and then not be in it, kiddo. Who's going to say good morning back?"

Val looked at the seedling for a long time.

"Who sent you?" she asked. "Last time, somebody asked you. You told me to go find out. I did."

"I know." Maggie rested her chin on the edge of the hatch. "Nobody asked me this time."

She held out Lizzie's hand, palm up, in the frost.

Val got up, and climbed into it, and sat in the big orange palm with her legs crossed. It was the second time Lizzie's hand had caught her. Lizzie stood up, groaning, and turned toward the morning.

"We'll forget all this, won't we," Val said.

"Probably." Maggie was humming already, something old, off-key. "Seventeen was getting old anyway."

Somewhere on the way across the field, Val forgot. She didn't feel it go. It went the way a dream goes in the first minute after you open your eyes, and it didn't leave a hole behind.

## Epilogue: The Normal Thing to Do

> *And God called the light Day, and the darkness he called Night. And the evening and the morning were the first day.*
>
> — Genesis 1:5

Theseus Moriah was the only one who remembered.

He had worked that out in the first week. He had woken up in his own bed, in a small apartment on a hill above a harbor, with a school uniform hanging on the back of a chair and both his eyes and no burns on his neck, and he had lain there for a long time staring at the ceiling, waiting for something to talk to him from behind his eye. Nothing did. Then a man's voice had called from the kitchen, mild and a little distracted, the voice of someone reading a newspaper: "Theseus. Eggs or rice?"

He had stood in the kitchen doorway for a full minute without answering. His father had looked up from the paper over his reading glasses and said, "Well?", and Seus had said, "Eggs," and gone back to his room and sat on the floor with his back against the door for a while.

Then he had tested it.

He had gone up to Akari Harada at school, at the shoe lockers, on the first morning, and said, "You punched me in the shoulder. At a truck. In the snow. You said *come back*." She had looked at him as if he had grown a second head. Then she had punched him in the shoulder anyway, because he was being weird. Some things carried over.

He had told Haruto Sakai, at lunch, that he had once raised a flying ship on a radio he built out of other radios. Haruto had said, "That's sick. Is that a movie?" and written it down.

He hadn't needed to test Nanami. On the first morning she had said "Good morning!" to him in the corridor outside Class 2-A, on his way past to 2-B, brightly, as if he had already agreed to be her friend, and he had said it back, and she had looked mildly surprised and pleased, and gone on to the next person.

Nobody remembered. Not the white sky, or the ice, or the door, or the giants falling over the river. Not the port. Not the plain. Only him.

He didn't know why. He had a theory, the kind you have at three in the morning: that he had gotten off a train before the end, and woken up on the frozen ground at the edge of the shaft with his eye quiet, outside Heaven, when Val rewrote the world. That he was the only one who had been inside and walked back out before the door closed.

That was the half that made sense. The other half came later at night, and he never said it out loud: that she had left it up to him. That everyone else had chosen in their sleep, and he was the only one awake enough to be asked. He'd said yes. He'd have said yes every time.

Either way, it didn't matter. He had decided, around the end of the first week, that it didn't matter.

So every morning, he walked the long way to school.

It took forty minutes instead of ten. He went down the hill and along the river and through the station and around by the park, and he didn't hurry, and he didn't tell anyone why. It was a route. A patrol. A watch. Somebody had to sit up with the world, a man had told him once, and that man didn't remember saying it, so Seus did it for him.

The first stop was a gallery on the hill.

It was a narrow white building with big windows, and there was a sign in the window this week, hand-lettered: PATRICK MORIAH, LANDSCAPES. OPENING FRIDAY. Seus stopped in front of it every morning and looked in. The walls were hung with paintings, most of them big, most of them mountains and valleys and rivers in the evening light, with the light coming down over all of it the way it does at the end of a day. There were no people in any of them.

Except one.

It hung at the back, the biggest canvas in the room, the one his father had finished last month and wouldn't talk about at dinner. It was a train platform at dusk, under an old curved roof, with vending machines lit along the wall. And on the platform there were two people: a man in a coat and a boy with two duffel bags at his feet. The man's hand was on the boy's head.

*I dreamed it,* his father had said, embarrassed, when Seus asked. *I don't know. I just dreamed it.*

The opening was tonight. Seus was going to go. He was going to roll his eyes at the wine and the speeches and the people from the university saying *luminous*. One of them would be a tired woman in a sweater who came to every one of his father's openings and never bought anything, and who would stand in front of the painting at the back a little longer than anyone else, without saying a word. Seus knew her face from somewhere else, a lab, a long time ago. She didn't know his. He was going to stand in front of the painting for a long time too, and he was going to stay the whole night.

The second stop was a house by the river.

It was small and ordinary, with a garden out front that someone was trying very hard to grow vegetables in and failing. A man was in the garden with a tennis ball, playing with a big shaggy brown dog. He threw the ball one-handed, every time, with his other hand in his pocket, out of some habit he couldn't have explained. He had both arms. Seus had to stop walking for a second, every morning, when he saw that. *Little habits in the meat,* Dasha used to say in the hangar, about the hollows, to anyone who would stand still long enough. You cut the mind out of a thing, and something stays anyway.

"Ross!" a woman shouted from the doorstep. "Ross, drop it! Not the tomatoes! Alex, he's in the tomatoes!"

"They weren't going to make it anyway," Alex called back.

The woman on the doorstep laughed. It was warm and a little hoarse, and Seus had heard it, once, coming out of a comms speaker in another life, telling him to bring the suit back in one piece. She had a pen stuck through her hair. The kitchen window behind her was open, and something in there smelled like burnt eggs.

Alex Enoch was learning to cook. He was very bad at it. Aoi Sena said she would marry him the day it was edible. They had been engaged for a year.

Alex didn't know why he had named the dog Ross. He'd just liked it. He said it suited him.

The third stop was a crosswalk outside the elementary school, where an old man in a yellow vest held a stop sign, and his hands didn't shake.

"Good morning, Haruka," he said, as the children went past him. "Good morning, Ren. Good morning, Daichi, tie your shoe, you'll break your neck. Good morning, Mei. Good morning, Sota."

He knew every one of their names. He didn't have a card. He didn't need one.

The fourth stop was the station. There was a rice-ball stand by the east entrance, a little cart with an awning, run by the largest man Seus had ever seen, with bare brown forearms and a voice that came from the bottom of a well. He made the rice balls by hand, one after another, and wrapped them in seaweed, and gave the commuters exact change without looking. Next to the cart, a woman in a headscarf was arguing with the vending machine. She had its front panel off and a screwdriver in her teeth, and she was chewing a ginger candy around the screwdriver, which shouldn't have been possible.

"It's the coin mechanism," she was telling the big man. "Nobody ever checks the coin mechanism. That's the thing nobody tells you about these machines—"

"Dasha," said Tom, patiently. "Customers."

Seus bought a rice ball every morning. It was always good.

The fifth stop wasn't a stop. It was a young man coming the other way along the river path, every morning, at almost exactly the same time, with two little boys running ahead of him and a string of plain wooden beads wrapped twice around his wrist. The boys were fighting about something. The man was telling them to stop fighting, without much hope.

"Gage!" one of the boys yelled. "He said Mom said I can't have the—"

"Mom did say it," said Gage. "Walk."

The sixth stop was a music school above a bakery, on the corner by the park. Every morning at a quarter past eight, a window on the second floor was open, and someone was singing scales. A girl's voice. Low, and a little hoarse, as if it hadn't been used much and was surprised to still be there, and getting better every week. Seus stopped under the window for exactly one scale, every morning, with his hands in his pockets. He didn't know her. He had never seen her face. He knew exactly who she was.

Then Kōta Harada came past, every morning, at a run, with his whole junior soccer team strung out behind him in a long noisy line, all knees, healthy and loud. He was asking his coach four questions at once. There was nothing taped to his cheek. He had never been on a respirator in his life.

And the last stop was the park.

On the first bench, by the gate, a gray-haired man sat every morning with a newspaper he never got to read, because his grown daughter was drawing on his forearm in ballpoint pen. It was always the same drawing: a stick-figure robot with big square fists. Under it, in careful capitals, the way she must have written it when she was five, she wrote PIP.

"Mio," he said, without looking up. "People can see that."

"Good," said Mio.

There was another bench, by the pond, in the sun. And most mornings, on the bench, there was a pale-haired boy eating ice cream with two friends, much too early in the day for ice cream, laughing at something one of them had said. He had chocolate on his chin. He didn't know it. His friends were about to tell him, and then decided not to, and laughed harder.

It was daytime. The Moon was up, over the trees, very pale, like a fingerprint on glass. It was whole. There was no crack in it anywhere. Seus still checked, most mornings.

The boy didn't look at it. He was too busy laughing.

Seus walked past the bench every morning without stopping. The boy never looked up at him either. Once, a few weeks ago, their eyes had met, and the boy had smiled at him, politely, the way you smile at a stranger in a park, and gone back to his ice cream. Seus had walked the rest of the way to school feeling something in his chest he didn't have a name for, and had decided it was good.

Then he went to school.

A sixteen-year-old girl sat in the back row of a classroom, spinning a pencil through her fingers, over and under.

It was a scuffed metal pencil with a logo worn half off its side, from the space agency where her mother worked, building engines for the stars. Her mother had stolen it from the supply room her first week, she said, a long time ago, and then given it to Val on the first day of high school, as a joke, and Val had kept it. There were tooth marks at the end, small, in two rows. Some of them were her mother's. Some of them, lately, were hers. She chewed it when she was thinking. It was a terrible habit.

The door slid open.

It was only the bell.

"Good morning," Val said, as the class rep went past her desk.

"Good morning, Kane-san," said Nanami Fujita, on her way out, as if it were the most ordinary thing in the world.

There was a new girl in the seat by the window. She had come that morning, at homeroom: a transfer student, quiet, with short pale hair and her hands folded on the desk, and the teacher had written her name on the board, YUKI, and a family name nobody in the room had ever heard, and the room hadn't gone quiet at all. Her face didn't look like anyone else's. It was only her own.

She was still sitting there, looking out the window, when everyone else had gone. Val stopped by her desk on the way out. She didn't know why.

"Hi," Val said.

The girl looked up, surprised, as if nobody had ever said it to her first before.

"...Hi," she said.

In the corridor, Akari and Seus were arguing about whose fault it was that the volleyball had gone through the gym window, and Haruto was filming them. Akari said it was Seus's serve. Seus said it was Akari's face, which had distracted him. Akari hit him in the shoulder. Haruto narrated all of it in a nature-program whisper.

Seus caught Val's eye as she passed and gave her a nod, the way he had every day for a month, for no reason she could name.

After lunch there was a fire drill. The school's safety officer walked them out, the way he did every term: a lean, quiet young man in a gray jacket, twenty-one or so, not much older than the seniors, with a clipboard and a face that was exactly the same on both sides, with no scar anywhere on it. He counted every class out through the side doors and across the field without once raising his voice, and he didn't let anyone go back for a bag. Nobody ever got hurt on his drills. He seemed to think that was the whole point of him.

At the end of the day, she waited at the school gate.

She stood where she always stood, by the wall, under the cherry trees, which had finished blooming a few weeks ago and were green now. Her bag was over her shoulder. Her pencil was in her hand. The sky over the city was blue, deep and clear, with one white cloud in it, moving slowly from left to right.

Across the courtyard, Seus stood by the bicycle rack and watched her, the way he had every afternoon for a month.

He hadn't crossed. Not once. It was thirty meters of courtyard. He had stood in front of a door into Heaven and torn the patch off his eye and ridden a train with his father, and every afternoon for a month he had stood by the bicycle rack and watched her wait for her ride, and he hadn't crossed thirty meters. He knew exactly whose footsteps he was standing in. A man on the other side of a two-lane street, every afternoon, watching a girl come out of school with her head down, never crossing. Never once.

He crossed.

He walked across the courtyard with his hands in his pockets and stopped in front of her by the wall, under the green cherry trees. She looked up at him. There was nothing in her face but mild surprise, the way you look at a classmate from another class who has never spoken to you.

"Hey," Seus said. "Kane."

"Hi."

He looked at her for a long moment. He had planned this. He had planned it in his head every night for a month. He'd had speeches. He didn't use any of them.

"Do you know me?" he asked.

Val looked at him. For half a second something moved in her face, far back, the way a shadow moves across the bottom of a pond. A city on a fallen angel. A boy on a balcony. A frozen plain, and someone's arm over someone's shoulder, and a voice saying *pick up your feet*. Then it was gone.

"Sorry," she said. "Knowing's not my thing."

Seus laughed.

It came out of him all at once, startled, and he didn't try to stop it. It was exactly what she had said to him, a long time ago, in a world that didn't exist anymore, the first day they met, in a hangar, in front of her exo. *Do you even know who I am?* *Sorry. Knowing's not my thing.* He had hated her for it for a week.

He could have told her everything. He'd thought about it, every night. He could tell her about the port and the sky and the plain and the train, and the letters, and a house on fire, and she would look at him the way Haruto had looked at him, and maybe, some night, it would come back to her, like a dream does sometimes in the afternoon.

He didn't.

He had watched his father hold on to something for twenty years. He had watched Abriel hold on to something for longer than that. He had held on to blame for six years himself, like it was worth something, and nobody ever came to give him anything for it. It was just heavy.

So he let it go. All of it. Right there, at the gate.

"Then let's start over," Seus said. He took his hand out of his pocket and held it out. "I'm Theseus. Seus."

Val looked at his hand. Then she shifted her pencil to her other hand and shook it.

"Val," she said.

A car horn tooted at the curb.

Her dad was waiting at the school gate: older than thirty-seven, ordinary, a little gray, embarrassingly early, waving with his whole arm out the driver's window. He had parked right at the gate. Not across the street. Right at the gate, in the spot by the curb where the teachers had asked parents not to park, with two wheels on the line.

Her mother sat in the passenger seat with her hair cut short above the neck and a pencil behind her ear. She raised a hand to Val through the windshield, and then saw Seus, and raised her eyebrows at Val, and Val pretended not to see.

In the back seat, Val's little sister was already talking before Val got the door open.

She was six. She had short hair, like Val's, and she had been asking questions since the day she learned to talk, and she had never once stopped.

"Val. Val. Val. Why is the sky blue?"

Val looked up.

The sky was blue. All of it. Everywhere you looked. You couldn't see the edges.

"Sunlight scatters," she said. "Blue scatters the most."

"Why?"

"Ask me later, April," Val said, and got in, and pulled the door shut.

Her sister laughed, because she always did ask later, and Val always answered. It was their game. It had been their game for as long as either of them could remember.

"Aunt Maggie's coming to dinner," her mother said, turning around in her seat. "She found her first gray hair this morning. She called me crying. Brace yourself."

"Happy crying or sad crying?" Val said.

"Both. She says it's the best thing that ever happened to her."

"She's going to talk about it all night," said her father. "My sisters are coming too. They'll egg her on."

"She's going to talk about it for a year," said her mother. "And Professor Hermon's coming. My old adviser. She'll ask about your grades."

"We're having omelettes," her father said to Val in the rearview mirror, and grinned, the crooked grin, a little shy, as if he wasn't sure he was allowed to. "The rolled ones. I'm doing them. Don't let your mother tell you I burn them."

"He burns them," said her mother.

"I like them burnt."

"I'm doing them," said April. "Fumi next door showed me how to roll them. She says mine tear."

"Torn ones taste the same," Val said, and didn't know where it had come from.

The car pulled away from the gate. April had her face pressed to the window, looking up at the blue, and was already asking the next thing. Why did clouds float. Why did the car hum. Why did Val always answer later, every time, even the boring ones.

Val thought about it.

"It's the normal thing to do," she said.

Out the back window, she could see Seus at the gate, getting smaller, with his hands back in his pockets. He watched the car go all the way down the street. Then he turned and walked off the other way, up the hill, toward a narrow white gallery with a painting at the back of it. The man who painted it wasn't the man who had waved from the train. Seus knew that. He stayed the whole night anyway.

Sometimes Val remembered another world, the way you remember a dream: a city built on a fallen angel, a voice in the walls of a machine, a white room, a gray car at a gate. A creek that never froze. Lanterns going out on the water, one by one. It didn't leave a hole. It left a warmth, the way a cup does in your hands after you've set it down. Whether she was the same girl who had lived there, nobody could say. She didn't mind.

"It's the normal thing to do," she had said once, a long time ago, in a corridor, as a performance. Now it was just true.

---

# Ending B: The Real World

## Arc 15B: The Settlement

*Arcs 1–15 run as in the shared text until "Val sat in the field until morning." in Chapter 66.*

### Chapter 66B: Am I April?

Val sat in the field until morning.

She put her head down on her knees. Not on a table; there was no table out here. On her knees, in the frost, the way she had put it on a school desk for seven years so nobody could reach her. She stayed like that under the buzzing lamps and felt the cold come up out of the ground into her, and let it.

There was nothing left unsaid. That was the worst part. April had asked everything she wanted to ask, and Val had answered all of it, and it hadn't kept her. Answering didn't keep anyone. Tom had pushed rice balls across a table every morning. Sae had written *Your turn* and taken a seat. Kuze had remembered her name. Abriel had gone up like snow falling in reverse. Every one of them had picked the seat, and every one of them was gone, and here was the last of them, lying on the earth in a fine glittering layer, over the rows she had planted.

*Everybody who stands next to you dies.* Gage had said it quietly, in a hangar, a long time ago. He hadn't been shouting. That was what made it true.

And under that, colder, something she had never let herself think in words: they had all left her. Every one of them. Her mother had gone to work one afternoon and never come home. Her father had stood on a doorstep and decided the floor was an answer, and watched her from across the street for three years and never crossed it. Patrick had driven her home every quiet day for seven years and never once told her anything true. Alex had kept a box of her father's letters in his desk for months and let it burn. Aoi had taught her to roll an omelette and never told her what else she was doing for her. Yuki had said one thing she wanted, once, at four in the morning, on an open channel, and died of it. Maggie had been late. Abriel had gone up into the sky smiling. And now April. Every one of them had sat a meter away, and then they had gone, and not one of them had ever asked her if she wanted them to.

Somewhere near dawn, something in her went flat and still and very clear, the way the plain had been clear the morning after the Third War: nothing left on it but ice.

Heaven could make a girl out of wanting. It had done it once. It had made her out of frost and out of how badly Val wanted her back, and she had sat at a kitchen table for forty-one days and asked about the sky.

If it could do that, it could give her back. It could give all of them back. It could take all of it back: the plant, the port, the white room, the plain, the field. Every bit of it. All the way back to a principal's office, and a pencil, and a man in a rumpled coat kneeling on the carpet. She had been the reason the door opened once. She could be the reason again.

It was the first plan she had ever had that was only hers. It felt like the first warm thing in a long time.

That should have frightened her. It didn't.

When the sun came up gray over the hills, she stood, brushed the frost off her knees, and walked down through the waking town to the clinic, where Seus was splitting firewood badly in the yard.

He saw her coming and stopped. He looked at her face, and something in his own face closed, the way a door closes on a draft. He looked past her, up the hill, toward the fields, where there was only one figure walking and there should have been two.

"I'm going back," she told him.

He put down the axe. He didn't ask where. He didn't ask why. Maybe he already knew. Maybe he didn't want to.

"Yeah," he said. "Me too."

Behind him, under the tarp in the snow, the crate he said wasn't his was very quiet, the way something is quiet when it's listening.

---

## Arc 16B: The Fourth War on Heaven

> *And Cain knew his wife; and she conceived, and bare Enoch: and he builded a city, and called the name of the city, after the name of his son, Enoch.*
>
> — Genesis 4:17

### Chapter 67B: Return

Haruto raised WAKE on the settlement's old radio.

It took him most of a day. The radio lived in the generator shed, a military set from before 2044 that he had rebuilt twice out of parts from other radios, and it took Seus's memory of the WAKE frequencies and a great deal of swearing from both of them before anything came out of the speaker but static. When something finally did, it was a woman's voice, tired and clipped: a comms officer on a night shift, somewhere far away, asking who was on this band and how they'd gotten the code.

Seus took the microphone out of Haruto's hand.

"This is Theseus Moriah, off the Vigil," he said. "I'm in a settlement called Kasumi, in the hills, about a hundred and forty kilometers southwest of the ruins. I've got Kane."

There was silence on the line. Not static: silence, the open kind, of someone holding a microphone and not saying anything.

Then a different voice came on.

"Say that again."

Val, sitting on a crate in the corner of the shed with her hands between her knees, knew the voice. So did Seus. He looked at her across the shed, and something complicated went across his face.

"Kane," he said into the microphone. "Val. She's alive. She's sitting right here."

The silence on the line went on for a long time. Long enough that Haruto reached over to check the dials.

"Two days," Alex said, and the line clicked off.

The Vigil came for them two days later.

It came over the hills at dusk, enormous and gray and scarred, lower than it should have been, with one side of it blackened and the flight surfaces on that side a raw new pink where they had been grafted again. It was too big to land anywhere near Kasumi. It hung over the hills above the valley with the steam rising around it, blotting out half the sky, and every person in the settlement came out of their houses and stood in the streets and looked up at it with their mouths open. Then a transport detached from its belly and came down, rotors thumping, and settled in the square by the creek.

The core came first. Seus carried it himself, out from behind the clinic, out from under the tarp, in his arms, wrapped in blankets, the way he had carried it down from orbit. He didn't let anyone help him. He walked it across the square and up the transport's ramp and set it down inside, and came back out, and didn't look at anyone.

Val went to the fields first.

She went down the hill alone, in the last of the light, to the row under the heat lamps, and crouched at the end of it. The seedling was still there. It had two leaves now. She looked at it for a while. Then she went back up the hill and found Nanami at the door of the house, with Hinata on her hip.

"The seedling," Val said. "At the end of the row. April's."

"Every day," said Nanami, before she could ask. "I'll water it every day."

At the transport, Akari hugged Val and told her that if she died, Akari would personally dig her up and kill her again.

"I mean it," Akari said into her shoulder. "I'll find you. I'll find wherever they put you and I'll dig you up with a spoon."

"Okay," said Val.

Haruto gave her a camera. It was his good one, the one in the padded box.

"It's got the sky on it," he said, when she tried to give it back. "The school roof. The cherry trees. In case you need to show anyone." He pushed it back into her hands and folded her fingers around it. "I've got four more. Take it. You're still my subject. I want footage."

Kōta asked if he could come. Akari said no before he had finished the sentence.

Nanami came last. She stood in front of Val in the square, in the noise of the rotors, with the baby on her hip and the steam blowing around them, and she smiled the smile she had smiled every morning for a year in the doorway of Class 2-A, and every morning for two months over a bowl of rice.

"Good morning, Val," she said, even though it was evening.

Val looked at her.

She didn't say anything.

She could feel it there, the way you feel a word on the tip of your tongue: two words, small, easy, the ones she had said back every morning for weeks in a kitchen that smelled of miso. They wouldn't come. There was a field above the town with frost on it, and the words had gone into it with everything else.

Nanami kept smiling. It was a good smile. It had had a lot of practice at not being answered. She reached out and straightened the collar of Val's parka, the way she straightened the laminated schedules on the school wall, and stepped back, and didn't cry, and Val got on the transport without looking back at her.

Akari punched Seus in the shoulder, hard.
