"Come back," she said.

"Obviously," said Seus. He didn't look at her. Then he did.

On the ship, Alex met her in the hangar bay.

The Vigil's bay was half dark. Most of the refugees from Ararat were on the ground now, scattered through the settlements along the southern coast; the ship carried only the crew who had volunteered to stay, and it felt empty, enormous, echoing. Half the lights were out. There were scorch marks across the deck where the crystal storm had come through the open doors. At the far end, bolted into the ship's spine with cables as thick as trees, Unit 04 hung in its cradle with its chest glowing faintly through the plating, and Val felt it the moment she stepped off the transport, the way you feel your own pulse in a cut.

Alex stood in the middle of the deck. He was thinner. His face had new lines in it, and the old ones were deeper, and he looked at her for a long moment without saying anything, the way you look at something you had stopped letting yourself picture.

Then, without a word, he came across the deck, and lifted a new Halo, and fastened it around her throat himself, one-handed. It clicked shut, cold against her skin.

"Procedure," he said, and his voice cracked on it.

He stepped back. He turned around and walked away across the bay, fast, and didn't look back, and the hatch closed behind him.

Maggie, leaning against Lizzie's leg with her arms folded, hummed something old and winked at her.

"Told him," she said. "For two months, I told him. *She's not dead, Alex. I'd know.*" She pushed off from Lizzie's leg and came over and looked Val up and down, and her eyes, which were much older than her face, went very bright. "He didn't listen. He never listens. Welcome back, kiddo."

That night, in the wrecked mess hall, Alex told them what was coming.

There were twenty of them around the one long table that wasn't broken: the bridge crew, the hangar chief, a handful of engineers, Maggie with her boots up, Seus at the far end with his arms folded. Val sat in the middle with a tray in front of her she hadn't touched. Alex stood at the head of the table with his one hand flat on it.

"Total Assimilation," he said. "It's what the people above Umbral have been building toward since before any of us were born."

He looked around the table.

"Every one of you has seen a seal. Every exo raises one. Every Nephil has one; it's the thing we have to break before we can touch them. What nobody tells you is that you've got one too." He touched his own chest with two fingers. "Everybody does. It's what keeps you you. It's what keeps you in. It's in the files we brought out of the archive, in their own words." He let that sit. "You all know what happens when a pilot's seal breaks in the capsule."

"They go soft," said the hangar chief.

"They want to do that to everyone. All at once. Every seal on Earth stripped, every mind running together into one. No more loneliness. No more pain. No more separate people, so nobody left to be on the other side of a war." His mouth twisted. "They call it salvation. They mean it. They call the ceremony the Rite, and it starts tomorrow night."

Nobody said anything.

"How do you know?" said the hangar chief finally.

"Because they've been moving everything they have into the ruins of headquarters for a week. Every white unit in the sky. Every troop transport. And because the scar's been getting brighter every night since the Third War, and last night it started to hum." Alex looked down at the table. "And because it's in their book. Chapter and verse. They've been reading it like a schedule for fifty years, and they're right on time."

"The door's shut," said someone down the table. "Abriel shut it."

"Scarred shut. Something has to open it again. Their book says it opens tomorrow night. It never says how. It doesn't have to." He was quiet for a second. "They'll have arranged someone." He didn't look up from the table. "And there's one more line. The last one in their book. It was in the files we brought out of the archive, at the very bottom, under the directive number they've been using for twenty years." Then he did look up, down the long table, at her. "*Four-seventeen. The Kane girl goes up.*"

"What about Patrick?" Val asked.

Alex didn't answer right away.

"Nobody's heard from him in days," he said.

Val heard every word of it and kept one sentence. *The Kane girl goes up.* Through a door into the place that gave you what you wanted most. Alex was looking at her as if he were afraid of it. She wasn't. She sat in the middle of the table with her tray untouched and felt something in her chest go still and sure, the way it had in the field. She didn't say anything. Nobody asked her what she wanted. Nobody ever had, except a girl made of frost.

Across the table, Seus touched the edge of his eyepatch, the way he did when it talked to him, and said nothing.

In the morning, there was tamagoyaki on her tray.

It was torn down the middle and burnt along one edge, the way it had been in Ararat after Tom was gone. She looked at it for a long time. She didn't eat it. She left it on the tray, torn side up. On her way out of the mess, she passed the night cook coming off shift, who was complaining to someone that the commander had been in the kitchen at four in the morning again, making a mess of the good pan, which was strange, because the commander never ate anything.

Val heard it. She didn't think about it. She had other things to think about.

The next night, at midnight, the scar over the ruins of Hakobune began to strain.

It started at the middle. The long pale line from the hills to the top of the world, which had hung healed over the ruins since the Third War, began to glow, and then to burn, and then to bulge, the way a seam bulges when something pushes against it from inside. It didn't split. Not yet. It hummed, the sound of every bell in the world being rung very softly at once, and the light that leaked along it lay across the frozen world like the light under a door.

Pilotless units circled it, white and faceless, hundreds of them, like moths around a lamp. The Conclave's troops held the ruins of headquarters below, in black armor, around the shaft, with weapons that had never been meant for the Nephilim. They were waiting for something.

The Vigil flew straight into it.

### Chapter 68B: Father and Son

Seus launched first.

His patched-together unit stood in the Vigil's bay, built around the core he had carried down from orbit. WAKE had found the wreck on the plain on their way to Kasumi and dragged it home, and the engineers had spent two days and nights putting the core back into it, and it was uglier than ever: three hollows' worth of white armor, a second-generation unit's long gray arms, a chest plate welded shut over a split that still showed, and a fresh coat of primer over all of it, the color of nothing. Its name was still blank on its shoulder.

He climbed into the capsule. The walls lit. The link opened, rough and grinding, like a door in an old house.

As the bay doors opened onto the light, it spoke to him the way it always did, the way it had from under a tarp behind a clinic in the snow.

"Pilot. Ready?"

For the first time, he answered.

"Your name's Navis," he told it. "Don't make it weird."

There was a pause in the link. A long one. He felt it, the way you feel someone on the other end of a phone take a breath.

"Was that good?" the unit asked, very quietly, in a voice that wasn't the old Navis's and was anyway.

Seus closed his eye. It wasn't him. He knew that. He'd said so to a lantern. He named it anyway.

"Yeah," he said. "That was good."

He dropped into the light.

Far below, at the edge of the shaft above the Reliquary, Patrick Moriah stood alone with his hands behind his back, watching the scar.

He stood exactly where he had stood every day since the Almost, on a ledge of broken floor at the lip of the hole in the middle of the ruins, in his coat, with the glow from the bottom of the shaft lighting his face from underneath and the light of the scar lighting it from above. The Conclave's voices were in his ear. They had been in his ear all night, calm, pleased, reading to each other from their book. He answered them when they asked him something. "Yes," he said. "Yes. On schedule. Yes."

Then he took something out of his coat.

It was a folder, gray, gone soft at the corners, sealed with tape that had yellowed years ago and been cut once, carefully, and taped shut again. Sam had given it to him in a hangar at night, the year before the end, coughing into his sleeve. *Hold this,* Sam had said. *When the time is right, you'll know.*

*Know what?*

*You'll know.*

That was all. That was the whole instruction. Patrick had opened the folder once, the night after the funeral, alone in his office, and read it through, every page, in Sam's cramped capitals: what was buried in 04's core and how to reach it, what the Root was, what the swords were, what it would take to open the door and what to send up the stair once it was open. It told him everything to do. It never told him why. He had taped it shut again and carried it in the inside pocket of every coat he had owned for fourteen years. He had held it through the principal's office and seven years of a gray car at a school gate. He had held it through a port, and through a son walking eleven days across the ice to stand in front of his desk. He had held it, the way he held everything, on the hope that one day it would turn out to have been worth it.

"It'll be worth it," Patrick said, to no one. "It has to be."

He put the folder back in his coat and went down the service ladder into the shaft, slowly, the way a man his age climbs down a ladder.

Far down, where the glow came from, the Root lay curled in the frost like something not yet born, pale as the inside of a shell. Two swords stood in it, side by side, where Umbral had set them back after the Third War. One burned with a cold fire that flickered like a flame in wind, always about to go out. The other burned perfectly steady, the way it had since a boy with pale hair shut a door with it and died.

Just above it, hangar zero hung out over the drop on its steel arms, and in its cradle, Unit 12 waited, rebuilt from its own wreck.

Patrick's people had found it on the frozen plain the week after the Third War, black and broken, with crystal still growing down one arm and a mask half shattered over its face, and they had brought it back and rebuilt it in hangar zero without asking him why, because nobody in Patrick's Umbral asked him anything. The crystal was gone now. The mask was gone. It was black glass again, tall and narrow, with its two capsules side by side along its spine.

Eve Sakaki was waiting for him in hangar zero.

She stood at the cradle console in her lab coat, with her sweater sleeves pulled down over her hands against the cold and an empty can of the sweet coffee he always left in the lab sitting on the console beside her. She didn't look up when he came off the ladder.

"I locked it," she said. "All eight clamps. I changed the codes an hour ago. You'd need mine, and you don't have them."

Patrick looked at her. He looked at Unit 12, held in its clamps over the drop.

"Eve," he said.

"I read the folder too." Her voice was very even. "The week after the funeral. You fell asleep at your desk, and I opened it, and I read every page, and I taped it shut again the way you do. I know what's buried in 04. I know what you're going to send up the stair." Her hands were shaking, under the sleeves. Her voice wasn't. "I've done everything you ever asked me. I grew a girl out of a dead woman and a piece of a saint because you asked. Then I watched you sit by her bed. I'm not doing this one."

He didn't answer her. He never had. He went past her, to the cradle.

He opened the capsule. He looked for a moment at the two seats.

He sat in the seat Abriel had died in.

The folder had told him what it took. Three kinds of flesh in one body, and a sword that was willing. Unit 12 had been grown from the Root; it was the saint. He was the man. That was two.

He waited for the third.

High above him, Seus was losing.

There were too many white units. There were always too many. They came at Navis from every side, out of the light, in silence, with nobody inside them to be afraid, and they tore at the patched armor with their long white hands. Every wound went straight into Seus's own body. A plate came off Navis's shoulder and his shoulder burned. A white hand closed on Navis's leg and his leg went numb. He fought them off, and more came, and behind him the Vigil came on through the light, slow and enormous, taking hits all along its length, and the white units began to turn toward it.

The Vigil needed a way through to the shaft. He was supposed to make it one. He couldn't. Not like this.

And under his eyepatch, the thing behind it was awake.

It had been awake since the scar started to burn. It was kicking. It was laughing. It was saying the thing it always said, the thing it had said for six years in the dark behind his eye, like a kid kicking the back of a seat, except louder now, so much louder, filling his whole head.

*Let me out. Let me out. Let me out and we can win.*

He thought about Akari tying the bandage too tight on purpose.

He thought about the Vigil, and Val on its bridge, and Kasumi under the light, a woman with a baby looking up.

"Akari's going to kill me," Seus said.

So he reached up and tore the patch off his eye.

What was behind it woke up.

Frost poured out of him. It poured out of his eye like light out of a lamp, through the link, into Navis, and the patched chassis around him cracked and grew. The welds split. The mismatched plates groaned and shifted and fused. Crystal ran down Navis's arms, white and branching, the way it had run down Unit 12's arms on the day the sky turned to glass. Its fingers lengthened. And over its blank primer-colored face, something began to form, pale and smooth and beautiful, like a mask carved out of ice.

For a moment Seus was almost one of the giants he used to fight. He felt it. He felt what they had felt, falling over the river, over the harbor, over the plant: a terrible hunger to go home, and a terrible strength, and no fear at all.

He tore through the white units like paper.

They came apart in his hands. They came apart when he looked at them. He went through them in a long white line toward the shaft, opening a road for the Vigil through the swarm, laughing, or the thing in his eye was laughing, he couldn't tell the difference anymore.

Then they stopped coming apart.

He didn't see what changed. One moment they burst under his hands like frost off a window, and the next they didn't. They took the hits and kept coming. The ones he had torn in half came on in halves, crawling up the light toward him, and there were more behind those, and more behind those, all of them turned toward the light pouring out of his eye the way moths turn toward a lamp. It wasn't him they wanted. It was what was in him.

They got Navis by the arms.

Five of them. Then ten. They held it the way you hold something down to look at it, and then they began to take it apart. Not fast. They took the left arm at the shoulder, and Seus felt his own arm go, and screamed. They took the right. They opened the crystal on Navis's chest with their long white fingers, the way you open a shell, and he felt that too, every plate, through the link, into his own body, until there was nothing he could feel that wasn't being torn.

On the open channel, everyone heard him. The bridge of the Vigil heard him. Maggie heard him, swearing, trying to reach him through the swarm and not getting close.

He screamed for his father. He didn't mean to. He hadn't said that word out loud in years.

"Seus!" Maggie screamed over him. "Hang on! Kid, put it back, that's what he—"

In hangar zero, the clamps held for one second.

Then the black glass moved inside them, the way a held breath moves, and all eight clamps sheared at once with a sound like bells breaking. Eve's codes didn't matter. No code had ever mattered to a want that size. She was still at the console with her hand on the release, pressing it, as if pressing hard enough would put the clamps back.

Behind her, black armor came down the service ladder. They had been told to keep the commander's road clear, and she was standing in it. They didn't say anything. They shot her at the console, and she sat down against it with her hand still on the useless release, and the empty coffee can rolled away across the deck, and she watched the black unit go.

Unit 12 rose out of the shaft.

It came up out of the ruins of headquarters black and silent, and the white units parted for it, and let go of what was left of Navis, and drew back.

He had known Seus would tear off the patch. He had counted on it. He had known it from the moment he saw it, in the doorway of his office in the ruins, the year after the Almost, when a boy who should have been dead came in out of eleven days of ice and stood in front of his desk with a black patch over one eye. He had known what was behind it. Abriel was gone. Only one Nephil was left in the world, and it was in his son's eye.

On the open channel, Seus heard breathing.

He knew that breathing. He had grown up at the other branch, across the sea, with his mother, and his father had called on his birthday most years. Not every year. Most. And every time, before he said anything, there had been this: a long slow breath on the line, in and out, like a man standing at the edge of cold water deciding whether to go in. Seus had learned to wait through it. He had learned to count it. Sometimes it went on so long that his mother took the phone away.

Navis turned in the light, crystal-armed, half masked, and faced the black unit rising toward it.

"Dad?" said Seus.

The breathing went on. In, and out.

"Dad. I know it's you." His voice came out wrong, too young. "Say something."

In, and out.

"Say something. Just once."

In Unit 12's capsule, in the seat where Abriel had died, Patrick Moriah sat with his hands on the controls. His jaw was working. His eyes were open. He was looking at the half-masked thing in front of him, which had his son inside it, and his mouth opened, once, as if something were going to come out of it.

Nothing did.

Unit 12 reached out and took Navis.

It didn't strike it. It simply opened, the way a mouth opens, the black glass of its chest unfolding in long petals, and closed around Navis and the boy inside. The frost pouring out of Seus's eye poured into it instead, into the black glass, into the Root-grown flesh and the man in its seat, and the light from Seus's eye went out of the sky.

Unit 12 went back down into the shaft with what it had taken, fast, past the broken levels and the scorched frame of the old drive wing, down into the blue, until it hung above the Root.

At the bottom of the shaft, the swords tore loose from the Root.

They came up out of the frost end over end, like two sparks going up a chimney, the way the first of them had once come up the same shaft for Navis, to stop it. They stopped a hand's width from Unit 12's glass and hung there, side by side, the flickering one and the steady one, and their light lay across the black glass and across the man inside it.

Patrick felt them look at him. There was no other word for it.

He didn't know what they saw. Fourteen years of a folder in his coat. Twenty years of holding. And under all of it, older and plainer than any plan, the thing he had wanted since a hangar at three in the morning: for it to turn out to have been worth something.

The swords turned, and went into Unit 12, the way keys go into a lock they were cut for.

Inside it, the steady one began, very slightly, to flicker.

And the door opened.

It opened right above Unit 12, deep in the shaft, where there was nothing to open, and it kept opening: straight up the shaft and out of the ruins and along the old scar to the top of the sky. The scar split from the middle outward, the way a seam splits when something pushes against it from inside, and widened, past where it had been in the Almost, past where it had been when Unit 12 touched it the first time, until it filled the horizon, a wall of light from one edge of the world to the other. And at the bottom of it, deep in the shaft where it had begun, just above Unit 12, the light ran down into the heart of it, into the place where everything was going to go. It opened quietly. It opened the way a door opens in a house where someone is sleeping.

Total Assimilation poured out of it, and up toward it.

In Patrick's ear, the Conclave's voices had stopped reading. They were saying his name now, in the tone of men who had just understood that something was wrong.

"Commander Moriah. The door is open. You are to hold it from the shaft. Going through is not on the schedule. You are not authorized to—"

"I held," Patrick said. "The time is right."

He cut them off.

Patrick never said a word to his son. He never had.

Then Unit 12 went into the light at the bottom of the door, carrying what it needed, with the two swords burning inside it like lamps carried into a dark house. Behind it, the way down stayed open.

### Chapter 69B: The Trigger

From the bridge of the Vigil, Val watched it happen.

She stood at the long window with her hands flat on the glass, with the light of the scar pouring over everything, and watched a patched blue unit come apart in the hands of the white ones, piece by piece, an arm and then an arm, like something being opened to see how it worked. She heard Seus on the channel. She heard him scream. She heard him say *Dad.* She heard the breathing. She heard it stop.

Then a black shape rose out of the ruins and opened like a flower and closed around what was left of him, and went back down, and the door opened and filled the sky.

On the monitors along the bridge, the feeds came in: settlements, coastal towns, the last relay stations still working. Across the frozen world, people were looking up. In streets and fields and on rooftops, they stopped what they were doing and lifted their faces to the light, and a look came over them, peaceful, almost relieved, like someone setting down a heavy bag at the end of a long walk. And then they began to come apart. Slowly, gently, into frost and light, from the edges in, the way frost comes off a window when the sun hits it: every seal on Earth going soft at once. They rose. Thousands of them. Millions. Rising off the earth like snow falling in reverse, toward the door.

Somewhere, in a valley full of steam, a woman with a baby on her hip was looking up.

The transports came alongside at the same moment.

They came out of the light on either side of the Vigil, long and black and low, the way they had come over the mountains at Ararat, and they didn't fire. They latched on. Val felt it through the deck, a long grinding shudder down the length of the ship, and then another, and then the alarms changed their note.

"Boarders," somebody said, almost wonderingly. "They're boarding us."

They came in through the cargo locks and the gun ports and the tears in the hull the white units had made, in black armor with no patches and no names and no faces behind the visors. Humans. The same as on the night of the raid, seven years ago. They moved through the ship as if they had its plans, and they did. They didn't fumble at a single hatch. They didn't take prisoners. They didn't need any.

Val heard it come up through the ship toward the bridge. Not the way the white units sounded, the long screech of hands on plating. A worse sound. Short, flat bangs, close together, and then quiet, and then more of them, closer. Someone screaming a name on the intercom, and then not. The hangar chief's voice, once, very angry, and then not.

People were killing people. On a ship that was falling, under a sky that was coming apart, with every seal on Earth going soft outside the windows, they had come aboard to shoot the crew of a flying ship, because a book said so.

Alex had a pistol.

She had never seen him hold one. He held it in his one hand by the bridge hatch, badly, the way you hold something you were trained on years ago and never thought you would use, and when the hatch opened and a black visor came through it, he shot it. He looked down at what he had done for one second, white to the mouth. Then he grabbed Val by the wrist.

"They're not here for the ship," he said. "They're here for you. *The Kane girl goes up.* Come on. Come on, Val."

She let him pull her.

She let him the way she had let him make her bento for a year: without helping. Her legs moved. That was all. He dragged her off the bridge and down the emergency ladder and along a corridor in red light, past a hatch where the night cook lay on his face in the galley doorway, the man who had complained about the good pan, and past others she didn't look at, and the bangs were behind them and then in front of them, and Alex stopped, and shot, and pulled her on.

At the top of the bay stairs, something hit him.

He made a small sound, surprised, the way you'd make a sound if someone bumped into you in a crowd. He didn't fall. He kept going down the stairs with her wrist in his hand, slower, and when they reached the bay floor he let go of her and leaned on 04's cradle and slid down it, slowly, until he was sitting on the deck with his back against the steel and his one hand pressed to his side.

It came away dark.

"Huh," Alex said.

The bay was empty. Above them the open doors poured light. Unit 04 hung half out of its cradle in the ship's spine, with the feed cables trailing from its chest, swinging as the ship swung. Somewhere above, on the stairs, boots.

"There's no time," Alex said, "so just listen."

She stood over him and didn't move.

"Seus. I got him out. Not you, not Umbral, not luck. Me and Aoi. After the port, his vitals spiked, once, I saw it. We took him. Aoi signed the report that said *unrecoverable* so nobody would come looking, and we kept him in a room under headquarters that wasn't on any map, and every night one of us sat with him. For months. She sat with him more than I did." He was talking fast, too fast, the words falling over each other, and his breath was wrong. "When the sky opened, she went down to get him out, and the ceiling came down on her, and she told me to take him. So I took him. I carried him up eleven floors. I went back for her and the stairs were gone." He shut his eyes. "She's still down there. Aoi. You knew her voice. You knew her at the postcard wall. You never knew she sat with him every night."

The ship shuddered. Somewhere aft, something tore.

"Your father wrote to you. Every month, until a month before he died. The Conclave held every one. Aoi found the box and left a note on it. *For Val. Do it right.* Then I found it. In the archive, after the codes, with Patrick's initials on the tag. I kept it in my desk for months. I was afraid of what it would do to your numbers. I was afraid of— I was afraid. And it burned. In Ararat." He opened his eyes. "And my mother signed the test that killed yours. Helen Enoch. *Approved.* She went through the door in '87, and the last thing she asked was that I look after the Kane girl. I thought she meant be kind to you. I think she meant pay you back." He coughed, and it was a bad cough. "I'm sorry. I should have said all of it a year ago."

Val stood over him with her hands at her sides.

Every word went onto the list. Aoi. The box. The letters, every month, burning. A signature on a test. She filed each one away, neatly, the way you pack for a trip, because every one of them was a thing she was going to take back.

Alex held up his hand. The Halo's trigger sat in his palm: the small black switch he had held for a year and never pressed.

"You decide what's important for you," he said.

It was the answer to a question she had screamed into a capsule in a port, alone, with her comms off. He had found it in the audio log weeks later and never said a word.

She took it, because he was holding it out, and because she didn't know what else to do with her hands.

He smiled at her. It was the old smile, the kitchen one, burning omurice one-handed.

"Normal's what you do best, right?" he said. "So go be normal. You've got school in the morning."

She looked at him for a long moment.

She opened her mouth.

Nothing came. It was the old nothing, the one she had kept on a school desk for seven years with her head on top of it so nobody could reach her. She had thought it was gone. It had only been waiting.

Alex waited too. One second. Two. Then he nodded, slowly, as if that were an answer too. Maybe, to him, it always had been.

He reached up past her with his one hand, to the red lever on the cradle's side, the manual release, and pulled it.

The cradle opened like a hand letting go. The last cables tore loose all at once with a sound like a building coming down. And every light on the Vigil went out.

In the red dark, Alex let his hand fall back into his lap. He didn't lift it again.

Boots on the stairs.

She didn't decide anything. She would think about that later, in a place where there was no later: that she never decided. There was a dead man on the floor at her feet and men with guns coming down the stairs and an open capsule above her, and her body went up the cradle's side and into it the way water goes downhill, because it was the only place left to go.

The capsule was dark and cold and smelled of frost and old metal. The hatch closed over her. In her fist was the trigger to her own Halo, and in no one else's.

The core was quiet. It was like walking into a house after a fire. And in the middle of the burned floor, something small turned toward her.

"Who are you?" it asked.

She thought of a seat taken on a transport. A door held up in the snow. A street held. A name remembered on the radio. A girl coming apart into a field she had planted. A man on a hangar floor who had waited two seconds for a word. All of them gone. And above her, past the light, was a place that gave you what you wanted most.

Her eyes were dry.

"I'm going to take it all back," Val said.

The small thing turned the words over, the way you'd turn over a stone to see what was under it. It didn't know what they meant. Neither, quite, did she.

"...Who am I?"

"April," Val said.

The Vigil tilted, nose down, with nobody flying it. Unit 04 slid out of the open bay into the sky, and fell.

Behind it, the ship went down across the ruins of headquarters the way the Morningstar had gone down on Fuji, slow and enormous, burning, with nobody at the controls. It broke its back across the lip of the shaft. It took the troops with it, and the crew who were left, and a man sitting against an empty cradle in the dark with his hand in his lap. Nobody steered it anywhere. Nobody chose a line. It just fell.

### Chapter 70B: The Kane Girl Goes Up

Maggie caught her in midair.

Lizzie came up out of the swarm in a long screaming curve, rattling, smoking, missing half her armor, and caught Unit 04 under one arm, hard, like catching a sack of rice.

"Got you, kiddo!" Maggie shouted over the speakers. She was laughing. She was crying. "Got you. We're not going in there. Not like this. Hang on."

She turned Lizzie south, away from the door, and ran.

She got four hundred meters.

The white units came from every side at once, the way they had come for Navis, not fast, not angry, only many. They didn't bother with Lizzie. They went past her, around her, through her, for the thing under her arm. A white hand took Lizzie's free arm and tore it off at the shoulder, and Maggie screamed, and kept flying. A white hand took her leg, and the leg came off, and she screamed again, and kept humming, louder, something old, from before the white sky. She held on to 04 with Lizzie's last arm as if that one arm were all she had left in the world.

They opened Lizzie's fingers one at a time.

Val felt each one go. She felt the old orange unit shudder all over and let go, not because it wanted to but because there was nothing left to hold with, and she felt Lizzie fall away beneath her, tumbling, one-armed, one-legged, toward the ruins, and Maggie's humming on the channel went to static in the middle of a bar.

Then the white units carried her up.

Dozens of them. Then a hundred. They took 04 by the arms and the legs and the shoulders and lifted it up through the light, gently, the way you would carry something holy, up past the ruins and the burning ship and the rising frost of a whole species, up under the door. And there they spread out around it in a great ring in the sky, arms out, wings of light open behind them, every one of them turned inward, and held it there at the center, arms wide, like a thing nailed up to be looked at.

On every channel at once, calm, pleased, seven old voices read the last line of their book.

*"Four-seventeen. The Kane girl goes up."*

Inside the capsule, Val sat with the trigger in her fist and didn't move.

"Val?" said the small thing in the core. "Val, where are we going?"

She didn't answer.

The Rite had a door. It had a sea, rising. It had every seal on Earth going soft at once and every mind running toward the light.

It still needed a center. Something for all of it to turn around. One person, whose want the whole sea would take the shape of.

It had one.

---

## Arc 17B: Hold

> *In my Father's house are many mansions: if it were not so, I would have told you. I go to prepare a place for you.*
>
> — John 14:2

### Chapter 71B: Proxy

Heaven was outside time, and it was built from memory.

It was made of people. Every mind that had ever slipped outside time, in a white sky or a War on Heaven or a test that went wrong, was here, and everything those minds remembered was here with them, kept exactly, down to the dust. And tonight, with the Rite pulling every mind on Earth up through the door, there were more of them arriving every second.

It had rules, though nobody had ever written them down. Patrick had read them in a folder in Sam's handwriting, which was not the same as knowing them. You learned them the way you learn the rules of a house in the dark: by walking into the furniture. Nothing in it weighed anything except what it weighed to someone. There was no up or down except the ones people remembered. And it gave you what you wanted most. It couldn't help it. Whatever you wanted hardest, it showed you, or built for you, out of whatever was nearest, and it couldn't tell what had happened from what you wished had happened. Neither could you, unless you knew how to look. The real rooms had something wrong in them: a crack, a scuff, a stain, frost in a corner. The ones somebody had only wished for were perfect, and cast no shadows. And the ones in between flickered, like a picture on a bad screen, as if they hadn't decided yet.

Unit 12 came down out of the light onto a floor that wasn't a floor, with the two swords burning inside it and the wreck of a patched blue unit folded in its chest like something held in a fist. The boy inside the wreck wasn't moving. Patrick didn't look at him. He hadn't looked at him at the station, either.

At the center of everything was a stair. It went up and up into a light so bright it had no color, and at the top of it, very small and very far and absolutely clear, was a chair. Every memory in Heaven faced it. And high above the stair, under the door, at the center of a ring of white units with their arms spread wide, Unit 04 hung in the light like a thing nailed up to be looked at, with a girl inside it who had stopped moving too.

The last page of the folder was very simple. Sam had always been better at the simple parts.

*Wake the rage. Take it up the stair. Whatever is in the chair, kill it.*

There was no distance in Heaven. Nothing was far from anything except what someone remembered as far. So Patrick didn't have to cross the sky to reach 04. He only had to want to, the way he had wanted it for fourteen years in the inside pocket of every coat he owned.

He engaged Proxy.

From an ops room, Proxy could move a body and nothing more; it worked through whatever was left of the nature in the core. The rage was something else. To drive the rage, you had to reach down into the core yourself, in person, and take hold of it with your own mind. So he did. He went down into 04 the way he had gone down the ladder into the shaft, slowly, the way a man his age goes down anything.

It was like walking into a house after a fire.

He knew the shape of it from the folder: the room Sam had built and locked, far down, where the rage was kept. He could feel it there under the burned floor, enormous, coiled, asleep, twenty years deep, the way a fire sleeps in a coal. He could feel the girl in the capsule, too, above him, sitting very still with something in her fist. She didn't speak to him. He didn't ask her to.

He reached down through the floor for the fire.

And something small turned toward him.

It was sitting right on top of it. That was the first thing he understood. On the burned floor, directly over the place where the rage was sleeping, the way a child sits on the lid of a box to keep it shut, there was something very small and very new, a single pale thread coming up out of black earth.

"Who are you?" it asked.

Patrick stopped.

It was a child's voice, small and clear, with nothing in it at all. No fear. No hope. Just the question. Nobody had asked him that in twenty years. Not the Conclave, not his people in the ruins, not the girl in the gray car. Everybody had always known exactly who he was. Kane's dog. The commander. The man in the chair.

"Patrick," he said, after a while.

The small thing considered that.

"Are you a bad man?" it asked.

"I don't know," said Patrick.

It seemed to accept that. It didn't move off the lid.

And under it, under the fire, under the locked room, at the very bottom of the core where nobody would ever have looked unless they came down here in person with their own mind, he found the last thing Sam had left.

It was a hangar at night, in the last year, a long time before the end and not long enough.

Sam was in it, alone, too sick to stand without leaning on something. He leaned on Pip's leg. He was gaunt and gray and he had stopped going to the talk shows months ago, and he breathed the way the very sick breathe, with his whole body, with a sound in it. He was looking at Pip, at the core in its chest, at the locked room he had built inside it.

He had changed his mind.

Patrick saw it in his face the way you see weather in the sky. Somewhere in the last year, he had changed his mind. Maybe it was Pip asking about his daughter. Maybe it was a postcard with a lighthouse on it, with no answer. Maybe it was the cancer, which makes some people braver and some people more afraid and some people, for a while, very clear. He didn't want the war anymore. He didn't want the Beast. He wanted to unbuild it.

And he couldn't.

He had run out of ways to undo what he'd built. The rage was locked in the core, behind a door he had locked from the inside, and he had built it too well; he couldn't reach it anymore, not without breaking the core, and if he broke the core there would be nothing left for his daughter to pilot when the Conclave came for her, and they would come for her. The missions never stopped long enough for him to find Patrick alone and tell him to quit, and every room in headquarters had the Conclave's ears in it, and Patrick was the only man alive the Conclave didn't watch, because Patrick didn't know anything. If Sam told him, they would know. So he didn't.

He had given Patrick the folder the spring before, when he still meant every word in it. *Hold this.* He couldn't take it back now without saying why, and if he said why, they would hear.

So he did the one thing he still could.

He couldn't reach the rage. But he could put something next to it.

All that last year, in Pip's capsule with the walls dark, he had been building something small in another corner of the core, beside the thing he had built to burn Heaven. Not a weapon. Not a plan. A voice: curious, the kind of thing that keeps asking when nobody answers. He was hoping it would grow big enough, someday, to sit on the rage.

He didn't know if it would work. He didn't know how far any of it would go. By then he was too sick to know much of anything, except that he had to try.

"Patrick will finish it," Sam said to no one, or to Pip, who was far above him in the hangar and couldn't hear. "He's better at holding on than I am. He'll hold this till it kills him. He'll take it all the way up." He breathed, the way the very sick breathe. "So she has to grow. If she grows, there's nothing down there for him to wake. He'll reach in for the fire and find a little girl asking questions."

He put his hand flat on Pip's leg, as high as he could reach, toward the place where the small new thing was.

"Grow," he said. "Please."

Patrick stayed where he was, in the burned house, with his hand on the floor over the fire and a small thing sitting on the lid in front of him, asking nothing now, only waiting.

*Why won't you tell me everything?*

He had shouted it at a machine at three in the morning, in a hangar, with a can of coffee shaking in his hands. He had carried the plan in his coat for fourteen years and never once found the reason in it, because the reason wasn't in the folder. Sam had put the reason here. In the one place Patrick would have to come in person. In the one form Patrick couldn't put down a ladder and climb past.

*He'll reach in for the fire and find a little girl asking questions.*

Sam had told him everything after all. He had just told him in the last place either of them would have looked, in a voice small enough that it would have to be listened to.

Patrick let go of Proxy.

He did it all at once, the way a hand lets go of a rope it has held so long it forgot it was holding anything. The fire stayed asleep. The small thing stayed where it was, on the lid, swinging its feet, if it had feet. Up in the ring, 04 hung still in the light, and the girl inside it didn't move, and didn't know anything had happened at all.

Inside Unit 12, one of the two swords stopped flickering. Its flame stood up straight and still, the way it had burned in its case before anyone wanted anything from it.

"It was stupid," he said at last. "All of it. A war against an empty chair. He gave up his daughter for it. I gave up my son for it." He laughed once, and it didn't sound like a laugh. "And he saw it in time. He had a year left, and he spent it trying to stop himself. I had twenty years. I had Theseus. I had you, every quiet afternoon at that gate. And I held. I held on to the hope that it would all be worth it, because if it wasn't, I'd done all of it for nothing."

In Unit 12's capsule, he put his face in his hands. In the other capsule, at the center of the ring, Val didn't move.

"He was stupid, Valeria," Patrick said. "I was so much stupider."

Then, at last, he looked down at what he was holding.

### Chapter 72B: The Last Page

In the ruins of headquarters, in what was left of the ops room, Ruth Hermon sat alone at the deputy's desk.

The others had gone an hour ago, one by one, at their consoles, the way everyone everywhere was going: a look of relief, and then the edges, and then the light. She had watched each of them go and written down the time, in pencil, on the back of a duty roster, because somebody should. Her go board sat on the desk beside her, a game against herself she had never finished. The screens showed nothing she could do anything about.

She had run the ops room. She hadn't asked. She had kept a pot of tea warm for a girl with a borrowed face, and stood at the back of that girl's funeral, and kept not asking, for twenty years, for a man who had been not asking for longer.

Her hands were going bright at the edges. She looked at them without much surprise. Then she looked up.

Someone was standing in the doorway of the ops room. A young woman with short hair and a pencil behind her ear, the way she had stood in the doorway of a seminar room at the Space Transit Research Division thirty years ago, late, out of breath, with a question she couldn't wait to ask.

"Yukimura," Ruth said.

She didn't say anything else. She put the pencil down on the roster, beside the last time she had written, and went.

* * *

At the foot of the stair, in a room with no windows built out of fifty years of their own memory, seven old men and women sat at a long dark table and read the last page of their book.

The lamp was low. You could see hands. Sleeves. A pair of reading glasses. One chair at the table was empty, and had been for nine years, and nobody looked at it.

"Four-seventeen," one of them read. "The Kane girl goes up."

"On schedule."

A pencil crossed it out. It was the last line. There was nothing under it.

Somebody turned the page anyway, out of fifty years of habit.

It was blank.

They looked at it for a long time, the seven of them, in the low light. Nobody said anything. Then one of them, at the far end, an old woman with a pencil still in her hand, said what none of them ever had.

"And after?"

Nobody answered her. There was nobody left to read it to them.

Their seals went, one by one, at the table, in the order they were sitting. They didn't scream. They didn't look afraid, exactly. They looked like people who had been waiting a long time for a train and had just understood that it wasn't the train they had wanted. Then they came apart, gently, into frost and light, the way everybody was coming apart, and rose, and were gone.

The book lay open on the table under the lamp, at the blank page, with nobody to read it.

### Chapter 73B: The Train

It was a late train, nearly empty, the overhead lights flickering. Outside the windows there was nothing but light.

Seus sat on the bench he had sat on at sixteen, the day he came into Hakobune for the first time, with his two duffel bags at his feet. The same bench, the same car, the same advertisement over the door for a cram school that had closed before the white sky. The same scratch on the window where someone had carved a heart with a key. He had looked at that heart for three hours on the way in, the first time, because he didn't want to look at anything else.

He didn't know how he had gotten here. The last thing he remembered was the black glass closing, and the light going out of his eye, and the breathing on the channel, in and out, and nothing.

Across from him, someone sat down.

It took Seus a long moment to understand that it was his father.

Patrick Moriah sat on the opposite bench with his hands on his knees and his coat buttoned, the way he sat at his desk, the way he sat everywhere. He looked at the floor of the car. He looked at the duffel bags. He looked, for a moment, at Seus's face, at the eye that had no patch on it now and nothing behind it, and then away.

Neither of them spoke for three stations. They had never been good at it.

The train stopped at each one with a long sigh of brakes, and the doors opened, and the platforms outside weren't platforms. The first one was a kitchen in an apartment across the sea, with a woman standing at the stove with her back to them, and a phone on the counter, off the hook, and a small boy sitting beside the phone, waiting, counting something. The second was a training yard in the rain, with a boy of twelve in a too-big flight suit being knocked down and getting up and being knocked down, and nobody in the stands. The third was a funeral in a gray room on the fourth floor of a building in Hakobune, with a photograph on an easel and a row of folding chairs that nobody sat in, and a man at the front reading a statement about duty.

The doors closed. The train went on.

"You killed me," Seus said at last.

"Yes," Patrick said.

"Twice."

"Yes."

"Why?" Seus's voice cracked on it. "You picked her up every day. Seven years. You never missed one. You never even came to the station for me."

"I was at the station," Patrick said.

Seus stared at him.

"In the car. Across the street." Patrick looked at his hands. "I watched you come out with your bags and look around for me. I watched you look at every car. I watched you wait. Twenty minutes. You checked your phone eleven times. Then you picked up your bags and went to find a cab." He was quiet for a moment. "I couldn't get out of the car."

The train rocked.

"She wasn't mine, Theseus," Patrick said. "It didn't cost me anything to be kind to her. You were mine. Every time I looked at you, I had to look at what I was doing. So I didn't look."

"And when I came to you? After I woke up?" Seus's hands were shaking on his knees. "I walked eleven days. I didn't have boots for the first four. I came all the way to the ruins and stood in front of your desk, and you looked at me for a whole minute, and you had them walk me out."

"I saw the patch," Patrick said. "I knew what was behind it. I knew I would need it. That's why I sent you away." He didn't look up. "I couldn't look at you and know that."

"I tore it off," Seus said. His voice was barely there. "You didn't make me. I did that. The door. Everyone out there coming apart—"

"I counted on it," Patrick said. "I sat in the dark at the bottom of the shaft and waited for you to."

The train rocked. The lights flickered. Outside, the light went past.

"I used to paint," Patrick said after a while. "Landscapes. No people in them. People leave, and mountains don't." He almost smiled. "I should have painted you."

Seus looked out the window at the light for a long time.

He thought about the phone on the counter, off the hook, and a small boy beside it counting a long slow breath on the line, in, and out, waiting through it, because if he waited long enough, sometimes, after the breathing, there would be a voice. *Happy birthday, Theseus.* Three words. Sometimes four. And then a click.

When he spoke again, his voice was very small, the voice of a boy who had asked one question his whole life and never gotten an answer.

"Was any of it good?" he said. "Anything I did?"

Patrick looked up.

He looked at his son. Not at the eye, or the burns, or the place where the patch had been. At him. For the first time, maybe, in Seus's whole life, his father looked straight at him and didn't look away, and Seus saw it cost him something, and saw him pay it.

"You were good, Theseus," his father said. "You were always good. It was me."

The train began to slow.

Outside, a platform slid into view, ordinary and gray: Hakobune Central, under its old curved roof, with the evening coming down and the vending machines lit along the wall. The station where a sixteen-year-old had once come out with two duffel bags and stood under the clock and waited for someone who never got out of the car. Across the street, through the station's glass doors, Seus could see the curb. There was no gray car there now.

Seus looked down at his hands.

They were going bright at the edges.

It didn't hurt. That was the strange thing. It came up out of his fingers like frost coming off a window when the sun hits it, gently, from the edges in, and the light came through them, and he could see the bench through his own palms. Outside the windows, everyone was doing it. The whole world was. The Rite had found the train.

"Dad," he said.

Patrick reached over, awkwardly, the way a man does something for the first time, and put his hand on his son's head. He left it there. It was heavy and warm and not very steady.

"I'm here," Patrick said.

He had never said it. Not on a birthday call, not at a station, not across a desk. He said it again, because once wasn't enough, because he wanted to say it until it was true. "I'm here, Theseus. I'm here."

He didn't try to stop it. He couldn't. He had held on to the wrong thing for twenty years with everything he had, and it hadn't stopped anything. So he held on to this one, the right one, for as long as it lasted, which wasn't long.

"Don't get off," Seus said. His voice was coming apart too, going thin and bright. "Don't— you never get out of the car."

"I'm not getting off," said his father. "I'm staying right here."

The doors of the train opened onto the platform at Hakobune Central, under the old curved roof, with the vending machines lit along the wall, and nobody got off.

When they closed again, there was nothing under Patrick's hand.

He sat on the bench with his hand still in the air, where his son's head had been, for a long time. Then he put it down on his knee, next to the other one, the way he sat at his desk, the way he sat everywhere. The train went on into the light.

He stayed on it.

Somebody had to.

---

## Arc 18B: The Center

> *And I saw the dead, small and great, stand before God; and the books were opened.*
>
> — Revelation 20:12

### Chapter 74B: The Center

She was at the center of everything.

She could feel it the way you feel the middle of a room in the dark. The ring of white units around her, arms spread, holding 04 up in the light. The door over her head, wide open, from one edge of the sky to the other. The stair below, going up out of Heaven toward a chair. And under all of it, rising, the sea: every mind on Earth with its seal gone soft, running together, turning toward her the way water turns toward a drain. She was what it was turning around. She was the low place in the world.

Then her seal went.

It didn't hurt. It was like taking off a coat you had worn so long you thought it was your skin.

And everything that had ever hurt her came in.

Not all at once, this time. In order. Over and over. The way a recording plays in an empty hangar at three in the morning, from the beginning, again, again.

*"Valeria Kane to the principal's office."*

*"I know you may never have really known your father, Valeria, but I want you to know that he was a great m—"*

*"I heard it hurts. Whether it accepts you or not."*

*"I'm deleting myself."*

*"Hey, you're Val, right? How cool is that? Could you give me a name?"*

*"Did I do something wrong?"*

Six days. Six questions. Silence after every one, and the silence was hers, and she heard it now, all six of them, from the other side, the way the voice in the capsule must have heard it: a girl sitting in the dark not answering, while something small and hopeful asked and asked and finally stopped.

*"My little brother was on a respirator when your plant went dark. No thanks to you."*

*"You really are not your father, are you?"*

*"Who the fuck decides what's important for me?"*

*"Pilot attributes outcome to. Pilot has no comment."*

*"You stood there. You stood there with your hands down."*

*"Her father loved the city. She burned the sky."*

*"Everybody who stands next to you dies."*

*"Valeria. I think this is what I want."*

*"And you did this, Val."*

And under every one of them, the tone. The Halo's tone, thin and patient under her jaw, a millimeter from her life, every day for a year.

They came again. Louder. Faster. They weren't in order anymore. The plant and the port and the white room, the shelter steps and the tower and the frozen plain, all of it at once, on top of itself, every accusation anyone had ever made of her and every one she had ever made of herself, and she couldn't tell anymore which were which.

And then she was saying it back.

She hadn't known it was in her. It came up out of her in the noise, in her own voice, at all of them, at every face she had ever been afraid of.

*You never crossed the street.*

*You never told me anything. Seven years in a car and you never told me one true thing.*

*You kept the box. You kept it in your desk and let it burn.*

*You never showed me your face.*

*You were late. You were always late.*

*You went up into the sky and left me there.*

*You chose to die for me. I never asked you to.*

*You went to work one afternoon and never came home.*

*You said you'd come. You said it in the car. I bought a tablecloth.*

*You left. All of you left. Every one of you sat a meter away and then you went, and not one of you ever asked me if I wanted you to.*

The two kinds of voices ran together until there was only one noise, enormous, her own and everyone's, accusing and accused, around and around the center of the world, with her in the middle of it like the still place in a storm. It went on and on. It was every bad day she had ever had, all at once, forever, and there was no desk to put her head on, and no car to get into, and no one coming.

Then it stopped.

All of it. At once. Like a held breath.

And in the silence, gently, from everywhere, in every voice she had ever known and millions she never would, everyone asked her the same thing at the same time.

*What do you want?*

Nanami's voice, bright, in a doorway. Akari's, rough. Kuze's, tired, reading it off a card. Tom's, with his mouth full. Aoi's, warm and a little hoarse, over a comms speaker. Alex's, from a hangar floor. Yuki's, flat and careful, the way she asked everything, as if it were the first time anyone had let her. Patrick's, from a car with the engine running. Her mother's. Her father's, from a doorstep on a summer evening, with something in his hands. All of them. Asking.

It was the one thing she had wanted her whole life. For somebody to ask. Not *have you eaten*, not *are you injured*, not *pilot has no comment*. That. Nobody ever had, except a girl made of frost. And here they all were, every person on Earth at once, finally, at the very end, asking her.

She answered them.

"I want it to stop," Val said. "All of it. Everyone. I want it to stop."

Heaven gave you what you wanted most. It couldn't help it.

And far below, at the bottom of the shaft, something heard her.

The Root.

It had lain in the Reliquary for fifty years, curled in the frost like something not yet born, pinned under Hakobune for most of them with a sword through it: the piece of Semjaza that had carried its waking. Semjaza, the saints' leader, the only one of them that had ever been able to open the door. Long before there were people to give it a name, it had stood in front of that door and asked its question, and heard nothing, and gone to war, and lost, and wanted, for a million years, to go back up and ask again. It had never gotten through. It had never gotten its answer.

It felt her want come down the shaft. Nothing had wanted anything that much since it had.

So it gave her its dream.

It rose. Out of the Reliquary, up the shaft, past the broken levels and the scorched frame of the old drive wing, unfolding as it came, the way a fern unfolds, pale as the inside of a shell, until it stood up out of the ruins under the open door and kept on standing up: a giant, white, taller than the door, taller than the sky, with its hands cupped together under the ring the way you cup your hands under running water.

It had April's face.

Val didn't know why. Maybe because April was what she had wanted most once, in a white room, and the Root had been listening then too. Maybe because April had come out of 04, and 04 had come out of it. It didn't matter. It was April's face, small and serious and very close, looking down at her as if it were about to ask her something.

It reached up. The white units let go. And the giant closed its hands around Unit 04, very gently, the way April had once held a baby in a kitchen in Kasumi, and lifted it out of the ring, and held it against its chest, and carried her want up to the door the way a sword turns in a lock.

Below it, the world went.

Everywhere at once, now. Not slowly from the edges in. All of it. Every seal on Earth that hadn't gone yet went in the same instant, in the coastal towns and the settlements and the ruins and the hot-spring valley in the hills, and every person in all of them came apart into frost and light and rose, the way snow would rise if the world turned over. Not into the giant. Past it. Each of them on their own, toward the door, running together on the way into one bright sea.

It was what she had asked for.

It was the Conclave's book, all the way to the last line. They had never been able to force it. They had only arranged the road and waited for a girl at the end of it to want the right thing badly enough. She had.

*The Kane girl goes up.*

She went.

---

## Arc 19B: Face to Face

> *For now we see through a glass, darkly; but then face to face: now I know in part; but then shall I know even as also I am known.*
>
> — 1 Corinthians 13:12

### Chapter 75B: Soft

She sat in it.

There was nothing to sit on, and no she to do the sitting, and she sat in it anyway, for a long time, the way you sit at the bottom of a warm bath until the water goes still around you and you can't tell where it stops.

Nothing hurt. That was the first thing. For the first time since a principal's office, nothing hurt anywhere. Nobody was late. Nobody was waiting. Nobody was asking her anything, because there was nobody to ask and nobody to be asked. Nobody would ever stand next to her again, because there was no next to. Nobody would die of it.

She had wanted this. She had wanted it in a field, with her head on her knees. She had wanted it on a school desk for seven years, with her arms folded and her cheek on them and the class going on above her like weather.

She let it be what she had wanted.

It was very quiet. Not the quiet of a room with nobody in it. The quiet of there being no room. Somewhere, very far off, or very close, it was impossible to tell, the frost of a whole species was settling, and none of it made a sound.

She stayed there a long time. She didn't count it. There was nothing to count it with.

The world went flat.

It happened slowly. Everything she had ever seen went flat around her, like a picture of itself: the stair, the chair, the city, the field. A picture, and then a drawing of the picture. Then the lines of the drawing.

Then fewer lines.

A horizon.

A line.

She was a line.

she was

a

—

*Val.*

Nothing.

*Val, it's morning.*

There was no one there. There was no one anywhere. That was what she had wanted, and it was there, all around her, finally, perfect: no one. And she reached, without meaning to, from the bottom of wherever she was, for the small stupid thing she had learned to reach for in a kitchen that smelled of miso, two words, *good morning*, the way you reach for a light switch in the dark of your own house.

There was nobody to say it to.

It had nowhere to go. It came up out of her and there was nothing on the other side of it, no doorway, no class rep, no kitchen, and it just stayed there, the word, not said, the way a postcard stays in a box in an office for seven years because nobody will ever come to collect it.

She didn't cry. There was nothing to cry with. She only held it, the word with nowhere to go, the way you hold a cup of tea that's gone cold because putting it down would mean admitting nobody is coming back to drink it.

### Chapter 76B: The Interrogation

*Why are you here?*

The voice didn't come from anywhere. There was nowhere for it to come from.

"I wanted it to stop."

*Why?*

"Because it hurt."

*What hurt?*

She didn't answer. It answered for her.

*Everything you touched.*

Then there were more of them.

They weren't the voices from the center, accusing, all at once. These were slower. Patient. They took turns. They sounded like everyone she had ever known, and they sounded like her, and after a while she couldn't tell the difference, and after a while she stopped trying.

*There was a line about you in a book before you were born.*

"I know."

*Seven old men and women read it to each other like a train schedule. The Kane girl goes up. It was written down before your mother ever met your father.*

"I know."

*Your father built a war around you. He put you in the middle of it. You were the piece the whole thing turned on.*

"I know."

*A man carried a folder about you for fourteen years. He drove you home every quiet day so he would know where you were when it was time.*

"I know."

*They grew a spare. In a tank. In case you broke. They didn't even need you. They only needed someone with your blood who would go up when she was told.*

She didn't say anything to that.

*A boy lived through the same years over and over, more times than anyone can count, and every single time, it ended at you.*

*A man with prayer beads said it to you once, in a hangar, quietly. He wasn't shouting. That was how you knew it was true.*

*Everybody who stands next to you dies.*

She saw them. She couldn't not. Not the way she had seen them at the center, all at once, a storm. One at a time, held up to her, the way a teacher holds up a test with the wrong answers circled in red.

A man in a flight suit too big for him, on a poster, smiling for a city.

A woman in a chair in a test chamber, with a pencil behind her ear, on an ordinary afternoon.

A boy in a capsule in a port, saying it was okay.

A girl in a white unit with the numbers on its hull, at four in the morning, holding a hangar door with its seal cracking like ice on a pond.

*She held it for you. So you could get to the unit. So you could go and get the other one.*

*Did you get the other one?*

"No."

*So she died for nothing.*

"Yes."

A big man under a door in the snow. A small woman with a notebook, in the window seat of a transport, with her callsign written in where Val's had been. An old man climbing toward the bombers in his shirtsleeves. A girl on an apron in the middle of a sentence. A boy with beads. A woman under a beam with dust on her hands. A man on a hangar floor against an empty cradle, waiting one second, two.

A girl made of frost, coming apart into a field she had planted.

*Everybody who stands next to you dies.*

"Yes."

*What would have happened if you had never stood anywhere?*

**IF YOU HAD NEVER BEEN BORN.**

She looked at it. It was what she had always thought, under everything, under the desk and the car and the word *okay*. She had never said it. Nobody had ever asked.

She said it.

"I shouldn't have been born," Val said. "If I'd never been born, none of this would have happened."

The voices were quiet.

*Do you want to see?*

"Yes."

### Chapter 77B: Without

It was white.

Not the white of a room. Not the white of snow, or a sky the morning after something terrible. White the way a page is white before anyone has written on it. It went on in every direction and it didn't end, because there was nothing for it to end against.

There was no up and no down in it. There was no floor. There was nothing to hold her and nothing to hold her back. She could go anywhere. She could be anything. There was no one to tell her she couldn't, and no one to need her to, and no one to be late for.

This was the world without her. Heaven had made it because she asked, the way it made everything. No Kane girl. No line in a book with her name on it. No daughter for a man to build a war around. Nothing for anyone to stand next to.

For a moment it was the most restful thing she had ever seen.

Then she looked closer.

Because there was a world in it. There had to be. Taking her out of it didn't take everything else out with her. And she looked at that world, the one she wasn't in, the way you look at your own house through a window from outside, at night, with the lights on.

A white sky came in 2044. It came anyway. She hadn't been born yet. Nobody asked her.

A Fall came down on a district by the harbor in 2062, and a boy of seventeen came around a corner and saw his house. He climbed into a dead man's machine. He would have done that anyway. His sisters were dead before anyone had thought of her.

A woman sat down in a chair in a test chamber in 2075, for a routine calibration, and didn't come back. The test had been arranged. It was arranged in this world too. It didn't need a daughter at home to be arranged.

The man who had been a boy of seventeen sat in a hangar at night and built a war on God out of what was left of him. He built it around something else, without her. Around nothing. It burned just the same.

And the line in the book was still there. *The Kane girl goes up.* There was a tank in a basement with a girl in it who had a lost woman's face, and in this world there was nobody for her to be the spare of. So she wasn't the spare. She was the Kane girl. They lifted her into the sky in a white unit with the numbers still on its hull, and she went up alone, because she had been made to, and nobody had ever asked her what she wanted, and she didn't know she was allowed to want anything at all.

Val watched her go. She watched it all the way to the end.

It came anyway. All of it. The white sky and the war and the door. The saints had gone to war before anyone was born. Her father's grief was older than she was. The book had been written before her mother ever met her father. None of it had needed her. She had never been the reason for any of it.

She had only been there.

And then she looked at the other things. The things that weren't in the book.

Nanami Fujita stood in the doorway of Class 2-A every morning and said *good morning* to the desk in the back row by the window, where nobody sat. She did it for a year. She did it forever. The desk never said it back, because there was nobody at it, and Nanami never found out that one day somebody would have.

A small voice in the walls of a machine asked for a name, and asked, and asked, and nobody was there to hear it, and nobody ever called it anything.

There was no white tablecloth. Nobody went out with her own money and bought one and smoothed it flat with both hands.

A big man pushed a rice ball across a table every morning to the empty seat across from him, and it sat there until it went cold.

A note in small neat pencil, two words, lay folded under a pillow on a cot nobody slept in.

On the back of a card in an old man's breast pocket, beside the number four, there was nothing. He never had to remember anyone's name there. He never got to.

A boy with a respirator in a hospital bed asked four questions at once, and nobody came in with a box of trading cards and sat down and answered them.

A boy in a port, in a blue exo, said *it's okay* to someone who wasn't there.

Val stood in the white and looked at all of it.

It wasn't true.

What she had believed under everything, for her whole life, wasn't true. If she had never been born, the worst things would have happened anyway. They would have happened to someone else, or to no one, or to everyone. And some of the only good things she had ever seen would never have happened at all.

### Chapter 78B: Glass

Then she saw them.

Not the way she had seen them at the center, all at once, a storm. Not held up to her like tests with the wrong answers circled. One at a time, slowly, the way you see faces through a window when your eyes get used to the dark. The seals were gone. All of them, everyone's. There was nothing between her and anyone anymore. She could see straight into them, the way you see through glass.

None of them said anything to her. They couldn't. They were asleep in the warm, the way she had been. She could only look.

And this time, when she looked into each of them, she saw something she hadn't looked for.

She saw herself.

She saw Nanami, who said good morning first because she was afraid that if she waited, nobody would say it to her. Who had counted heads on a dark road for three days and lost three and never found out their names. And in Nanami, looking out at the back row, Val saw a quiet girl by the window. A girl who might answer, someday. Who was worth saying it to every morning until she did.

She saw Akari, who was angry all the time because angry was the only thing in her that didn't shake. And in Akari she saw a girl with blood on her lip who had looked at it and said *okay*, and who Akari had never once forgiven for not hitting back, because it would have been so much easier if she had.

She saw Gage, praying to nobody every night and getting nothing back. And in Gage she saw the devil's daughter. The thing he could hate instead of God. It was a hard thing to see in somebody's eyes. It was in there. She looked at it until she could.

She saw Tom, who had decided at ten, in a tank, that if he only had so many mornings he would spend them feeding people. And in Tom she saw a kid who hadn't eaten. Just that. A kid who needed feeding, who sat down across from him every morning, who took the rice ball.

She saw Sae, who had lost her voice in the middle of a note and spent every day after it afraid of being useless. And in Sae she saw two words in small neat pencil: *same thing.* Someone the same as her. Someone who was afraid of the same things, a meter away on the hangar steps.

She saw Kuze, who had forgotten Hayashi's face first and never forgiven himself. And in Kuze she saw a number, Four, crossed out, and a name written in beside it in a hand that shook. *Val.* He had worked to keep that one. Of all the names.

She saw Seus, a small boy beside a phone, counting a breath on the line; a boy at sixteen under a station clock; a boy who had liked her, a little, the stupid way you like someone at sixteen, and who had blamed her because it was easier than missing her. And in Seus she saw the genius. The girl in the hangar who had said *Sorry, knowing's not my thing* to someone who had come a long way to be known.

She saw Haruto, who was afraid of forgetting what people looked like. And in Haruto she saw his subject. A girl walking down a hallway with her head down, and a camera on her, because somebody should keep it.

She saw Aoi, sorting mail nobody would ever claim, because she couldn't stand that anything addressed to a person should stop at a desk. And in Aoi she saw a girl at a kitchen counter whose omelettes tore. *That's how people will know it's yours.*

She saw Alex, forging his mother's signature at eight, and finding the box, and putting it in his desk and taking it out again every night for months. And in Alex she saw the one he had promised to look after and had been terrified, every day, of failing. A girl he made eggs for at four in the morning because he didn't know any other way to say he was sorry.

She saw Patrick, who stopped painting because a blank canvas could never be wrong, and held a folder for fourteen years because letting go would mean it had all been for nothing. And in Patrick she saw a girl in the passenger seat of a gray car, with a can of sweet coffee going cold in her hands, every quiet day for seven years. The one thing he knew how to give that didn't need any words.

She saw Maggie, seventeen forever, laughing because if she ever stopped she would never start again. And in Maggie she saw a kid. *Kiddo.* Somebody's kid, that somebody had asked her to keep an eye on, and she had, and somewhere along the way it had stopped being because she was asked.

She saw Abriel, around and around, every loop ending on the same stair, never knowing what he wanted, only happy when she was. And in Abriel she saw a girl on a roof under the constellations he had named for her. *You're beautiful this time around.* He had meant it every time.

She saw the Conclave, seven frightened old people with a book, and in them she saw nothing but a line. *The Kane girl.* It was the smallest picture of her anyone had. She looked at it anyway, because it was in there too.

She saw Yuki, made in a tank to be somebody else in case somebody else broke. And in Yuki she saw the person Yuki had been made in case of. Not a burden. Not a rival. The one person in the world she had been made to be, and had been so curious about, every day, across a classroom, without ever saying so.

She saw Ruth Hermon at her go board, playing against nobody. And in Ruth she saw Sora's daughter. The child of the best student she had ever had, grown up, with her mother's hair.

She saw Eve Sakaki in her lab at three in the morning with a can of someone else's coffee. And in Eve she saw the original. The real one. The one the spares had all been copies of, and none of them had been able to be.

She saw Fumi, in a narrow house with a persimmon tree, with a two-year-old on her hip who wasn't hers and who she had loved anyway, every day, for seven years, and been paid for, and would have done it for nothing. And in Fumi she saw Val-chan. A small girl holding on to an apron. Just that.

She saw her father. She saw the doorway from his side: a summer evening, cicadas, a pencil in his hand, and a girl of six behind an apron, looking at the floor. And in him she saw what he had seen: not a girl refusing him. A girl he was so afraid of loving and losing that he took the floor for an answer and walked away. He had wanted her so much it frightened him. That was in there too. It had been in there the whole time.

And she saw April. All three of her. And in April she saw the one who had answered. *I'm your hero.*

None of them were the girl she had carried.

That girl was a thing she had made out of the worst of it, alone, in a principal's office, and kept on a desk for seven years with her head on top of it so that nobody could see her and say otherwise. And here was everybody else, all of them, saying otherwise. Not the same thing. Different things, contradictory things, a quiet girl and a devil's daughter and a genius and a kid who needed feeding and a hero. They didn't add up. They weren't supposed to.

She started to build herself out of them.

Not out of one. Out of all of them, the way you build a house out of whatever boards you have, and the boards don't match, and the house stands up anyway.

### Chapter 79B: The Spare

Someone was standing in the white.

She had short hair, and a face Val had sat across a classroom from for most of a year without knowing whose it was. She knew now. She had seen it in a tank, and on a woman in a test chamber, and in a gray car's rearview mirror, the way you see a face you only half remember, when you're tired.

Yuki looked at her the way she had always looked at her: straight, and curious, and without any idea how to start.

"They made me so that if you broke, it wouldn't matter," Yuki said. "I was supposed to be you, if they needed one."

She thought about it, the way she thought about everything, very seriously, as if there might be a wrong answer.

"I couldn't be," she said. "Nobody could."

### Chapter 80B: The Floor

Somebody drew a line.

It came out of nowhere, a single line across the white, under her, the way a child draws the ground first, before anything else, so the house has somewhere to stand. And because the line was there, there was an under. And because there was an under, there was a her, standing on it.

It was a floor.

That was all. A floor, under her feet. An edge. A place where she stopped and the rest of everything started. She had wanted, more than anything, for there to be no edge, so nothing could get in. Now she stood on one and found that it was the only reason she could stand at all.

You couldn't be anyone without an edge. You couldn't stand next to anyone without one. There had to be a you, and then a meter, and then them.

She looked down at the line under her feet for a long time.

*It's okay for me to live.*

She didn't say it out loud. She didn't need to. It was the first thing in her life she had known all the way down, the way you know your own handwriting.

And for a moment, on the white, there was a world.

A school gate under green cherry trees.
